Sefer Bereishit

The Book of Genesis

Parshat

Bereishit

Who Is A Good Wife?

Yosef · October 5, 2018

In this weeks parsha, G-d creates the entire universe and all that fills it in seven days. The focal point of all of creation, however, was the sixth day of creation – the creation of man. We are all familiar with how Adam found that every creature except for himself was given a mate. Hashem then decreed that it was not good for man to be alone and decided to give him a wife. He put Adam to sleep and fashioned Eve out of Adam's own rib.

The wording used in the mission statement of the creation of woman is somewhat puzzling. Hashem said, "I will make him a helpmate against him." Rashi is bothered by the contradiction from one word to the next – is she a helpmate or is she against him? Rashi explains that it all depends on which type of wife one gets. If one is meritorious he receives a helpmate, and if not, he receives an adversary, or one who is against him.

I'd like to share a novel explanation of these words, written by the Netziv, Rabbi Naftali Tzvi Yehuda Berlin (1816-1893). He quotes the verse in Proverbs: "All the ways of man are straight in his eyes." Most of us are full of flaws and we make countless mistakes throughout our lives, but it is incredibly difficult to recognize our own shortcomings, much less dig ourselves out of the mud. It is for this reason that we need outside input to set us straight. But who can be that person? Who knows us well enough to recognize exactly what changes can bring about our perfection, yet possesses the sensitivity to help us rather than hurt us with their criticism. It is for this reason that G-d created two genders and the institution of marriage. A spouse is meant to fulfill this role. Man's wife is so close to him that she can know precisely what makes him tick, and yet she possesses the love and care to build instead of break.

This is the meaning of the verse. "I will make for him a helpmate against him." When will she be his helpmate, helping him to reach perfection of character and greatness of spirit? When she is against him, meaning when she points out his flaws and helps him to grow, all in her own special way of sensitivity and softness.

According to this, the goal is to find a wife who is not just there to be his fan club, but rather one who, through telling him when he is wrong, will thereby truly help him in the mission of life.

We should look for such a quality both in our spouses and in our circle of friends. Let's try to find those people in our lives that are able to constructively criticize us and avoid the yes-men who will only lead us deeper into our own darkness. And most of all, let us work on ourselves to be among those who appreciate criticism and don't send our true helpmates away with our inability to admit fault.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

A Piece of G-d

Yosef · October 25, 2019

I once heard a story of a seemingly regular older woman who was walking with her grandchild when suddenly a car spun out of control and trapped the child underneath. With her precious grandchild's life on the line, she acted out of desperation and attempted the seemingly futile task of lifting the car off of the child. And, as if by a miracle, the impossible happened and she succeeded in saving the child.

Perhaps most remarkable, though, was the postscript of the story. She was shaken to the core by the latent power which she had witnessed in herself and had never known about. After realizing how much she could accomplish, she decided to achieve something else which she thought she never could. She went back to college, and finally got the masters she had always dreamed of.

From where did she draw such super-human strength during the car accident? How could it be that someone who just yesterday struggled to take out her garbage was now able to move thousands of pounds? And what did she learn which finally empowered her to chase and capture her dreams?

And I think we all know that this is not an isolated story, but one that has repeated itself, in different ways, too many times to count. People acting out of desperation find within themselves capacities, whether physical, intellectual, or emotional, that are all but endless. Where does it come from?

When man was created, in this week's Parshah, the Torah writes, "And He blew into man a living soul." The Zohar comments on this that "one who blows, blows from within himself", meaning that every soul is a piece of G-d Himself.

The words of Rabbi Zevi Trenk ring in my ears as he described the incredible import of this Zohar. "If you say you can't, you're saying He can't!" Because our soul is no less than a piece of infinity, nothing short of godliness, we are nearly limitless in our true capacities. We need only to believe in our greatness to unlock all the limitless potential that lies within.

To further illustrate, there is a prayer which we say every morning which begins with the declaration, "The soul which You placed within me is completely pure." Rabbi Shimon Schwab points out that even if one were to commit the worst sin, he would still wake up the next morning and be expected to recite this prayer. The soul which You placed within me is totally pure. What does that mean and how can it be recited across the board by every living person? Has he not tainted his soul through his misdeeds? The answer, says Rabbi Schwab, is the same. Our soul is hewn from the loftiest heavens, from a place so holy and otherworldly that it is untaintable from the physical realm. Its loftiness doesn't allow it to ever lose that purity, no matter how far one strays.

If we would only recognize the greatness of the human spirit, a greatness so immensely powerful and godly, we could achieve anything. So let us believe in ourselves, believe in our godly spark, and believe in the unconditional and pristine purity of our souls. Let us never sell ourselves short or let past failure stunt our forward growth. Let us achieve the impossible.

Good Shabbos, Yosef

The Creation of Rest

Yosef · October 16, 2020

We all know that on the seventh day of creation G-d created rest. But what does it mean to create rest? Isn't rest just a lack of activity; not a proactive creation unto itself?

The Beis Halevi (1820-1892) explains that the way things seem to the naked eye is really a mistake. We think that for six days there was creation and afterwards it was finished. Now it seems as if things just go by themselves, running off of their original creation so many years ago. This, says the Beis Halevi, is a fundamental misconception. In truth, Hashem is constantly creating the entire world anew, to the point that if he would just stop creating for one second, the entire universe would cease into oblivion. It wouldn't need to be destroyed - it would simply come apart at the seams.

According to this understanding, nothing less was created on Shabbos than on the rest of the days of creation. Even on Shabbos everything was created anew for every second of the day. If so the term 'rest' refers not to a lack of creation but to the recreation of things exactly as they were before. So yes, 'rest' required creation just like all other facets of the world.

And this is one of the central messages of Shabbos, the day commemorating the constant recreation of the universe,. To understand that nothing is running on auto-pilot. It is on the Shabbos that we remember and realize that Hashem is perpetually and persistently involved in our world. He does not play a backseat roll, but is always running the entire show.

In this light, the world we live in takes on a whole new significance. When we walk out in the morning and see the beauty of a new day – the rising sun, the music of the birds, the leaves rustling in the breeze – it is our loving father making it all for our sake. When we face a challenge – a difficult person, a concern about our livelihood, a problem with our health – it is again our loving father orchestrating it all for our ultimate benefit. And so it is with every facet of the world which we live in; G-d doesn't have a cool and passive relationship with us, but a constant and intimate connection which weaves itself into even the most minute details.

So let us just realize his persence, feel his love and involvement, and reciprocate with an effort to build our bond to the Almighty from our side of the fence as well.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Hope in the Darkness

Yosef · October 13, 2023

We stand at an impossibly dark hour for our people. An hour of boundless pain and suffering, of tremendous fear, of unspeakable atrocities. The only thing that flows more copiously than the rivers of Jewish blood, is the oceans of Jewish tears.

I heard the question asked, how could it be that God granted such uncanny success to a party of such depravity and evil? One would have to be blind and deaf to think that such a bloodbath was the product of Hamas planning and IDF error. Not one part of the story makes any sense by normal human terms. So how could the Kind and Compassionate One grant these savages the divine assistance necessary to carry out their horrible intentions?

And the answer is that Hashem didn't help them. He sent them.

And if Hashem sent them, then we know and believe that behind all the darkness lies a light at the end of the tunnel. A light that is not in spite of the darkness, but which will be born of the darkness itself.

Yes, it is a dark, painful, and terrifying hour -- but it is not an hour of despair. The darkest part of night is the moment preceding dawn, and the shadows bring with them the hope for daybreak.

So why the darkness? Why must we endure such unbearable pain?

We don't know the ways of Hashem, and we can't profess to pinpoint the answer to that difficult question. But, in a general sense, we can look towards history for a frame of reference.

In Egypt, the Jews suffered terribly for generations, but somehow they held out and would have been there for the entire 400-year sentence foretold to Avraham. Until Pharaoh contracted leprosy and began to bathe daily in the blood of our children! This tragedy was too much to bear, and the Jewish people let out an unprecedented cry -- a wail that rent the heavens. And, as that darkest moment brought us to such a cry, Hashem began the process of redemption.

Then, as Moshe reappeared on the scene bearing the message of salvation, the people were relieved. But, as he approached Pharaoh, the situation only grew worse, with Pharaoh refusing to supply the people with straw for their bricks. It was an impossible task, and again we screamed out, this time with the prayer that heralded in the beginning of the ten makos.

And this, says Reb Tzadok (Tzidkas Hatzaddik ch. 151), is the process by which Hashem brings the blessing and bounty of rain. Rain is the magnificent, life-giving phenomenon that sustains the entire planet, yet it is always prefaced and produced by dark and threatening clouds. Why? Reb Tzadok explains that when we need the rain and we don't quite have the merits by which to deserve it, Hashem prepares the antidote with the darkening storm clouds. As the darkness thickens and the thunder begins to roar, we turn our eyes heavenward and are compelled to cry out to Hashem and to hope for His mercy, and thereby we merit the rain.

Now too, as we grope in these darkest of times, we can find solace in the knowledge that it is brought upon us by none other than our Loving Father. We can easily see past all the ridiculous political rhetoric that tries to view these unnatural events through an atheistic lens. We can choose to see the dawn about to break, and we can indeed merit that salvation if we truly feel the pain of our brothers and seize the moment to cry our hearts out to Hashem. And let's not think small, about returning to life as usual, but rather about the ultimate salvation with the coming of Moshiach speedily in our days.

Good Shabbos and may we hear only good news,

Yosef

Parshat

Noach

Noah · October 20, 2017

This week's parsha is that of my namesake and I have always taken it personally. There is an ongoing theme found in the commentary throughout this parsha that has always been difficult for me to understand and accept. Noach is contrasted to Avraham in multiple places and this almost always results in Noach being viewed as the lesser of the two. I'm sure this wouldn't have bothered to such an extent if my little brother didn't carry the name of the superior biblical example but, alas, this has haunted me. To make matters worse, as great as Noach is made out to be, he is not the Father of the Jewish People like Avraham is. Noach is seen as the patriarch for the world but the especially coveted title of Father of our Nation is reserved for Avraham. Understanding the relationship between these great men can reveal a profound lesson about what it means to be righteous, what it means to be a Jew, and where these principles intersect.

The first words of this week's Parsha say, "These are the offspring of Noach, Noach was a righteous man, perfect in his generations; Noach walked with God." From the Torah's words alone it seems like Noach is the best of the best. In fact, no other person in the Torah is described as "righteous." But, of course, the sages could not take these praises at face value and instead compare them to the exaltations of Avraham. The Torah relates, "Avraham walked before God." While Noach was in lock step with the creator, Avraham was running ahead, enthusiastically looking for the next challenge. Rav Eliyahu Dessler zt"l writes, in an effort to explain this discrepancy, that while Noach is called a man of righteousness, Avraham is a man of kindness. Sure, Noach performed acts of kindness in his Ark, feeding the animals, caring for his family, and ultimately saving humanity, but Noach was motivated to perform these acts because they were the right thing to do. His service did not stem from an overflowing desire to give but was rather a reaction to the needs of others. Avraham, in contrast, did not perform his acts of kindness out of a sense obligation, rather he performed them because of a burning desire to give, regardless of whether it was necessary.

Next, the sages question what is meant by the seeming redundancy of the words, "in his generations." If he was truly a perfect person why does he need to be compared to the sinners of his age? Just say that he's perfect and leave it at that! Rather, the sages say that had he lived in Avraham's generation, which was not nearly as sinful, Noach would not have been special. His greatness was only in direct comparison to the scum running around the world at the same time. While Avraham's greatness is described in an absolute sense, Noach's exists only in relation to those around him.

Finally, to drive the inadequacy of Noach home, the Sages analyze another verse. It says that Noach went into his Ark "Because of the waters of the flood." Rashi says, "Noach was also lacking faith in God, and he believed and didn't believe that the flood would come, so he did not enter the Ark until the waters pushed him into it." This Rashi is especially hard for me to swallow. Noach committed more than a century of his life to building the Ark and preparing for his famous voyage. How can Rashi disregard all of this commitment and in a split second proclaim that Noach lacked faith?

I once heard an alternative understanding that flips Rashi's explanation of Noach's lack of faith on its head and emphasizes our responsibility as Jews. Instead of punctuating Rashi's comment as is typically done, the comma is moved over slightly to reveal a powerful message. Instead of punctuating the verse

"אף נח מקטני אמנה היה, מאמין ואינו מאמין שיבא המבול ולא נכנס לתיבה עד שדחקוהו המים"

Which means that Noach himself was lacking in faith, the comment is changed to read,

".אף נח מקטני אמנה היה מאמין, ואינו מאמין שיבא המבול ולא נכנס לתיבה עד שדחקוהו המים"

Which instead means, "Noach even believed in those who lacked faith, and (because he was confident they would repent) did not enter the Ark until the water pushed him." According to this explanation, Noach did not lack faith, instead, he had too much faith in those around him. Rather than doing what Avraham was famous for and spreading the belief in God and objective morality to others, he sat by and just believed they would repent. This criticism illustrates just how important it is for us to engage the outside world by demonstrating how a people held to higher standard behave. Noach's lack of eagerness to enter the Ark was not a result of too little belief, rather too little ambition to spread this belief to others.

Furthermore, in regard to Noach righteousness existing only relative to his times, we see that a Jew must never compare himself to the world around him when assessing his morality. It is not enough to hold ourselves to the arbitrary and subjective measures of morality that our society imposes upon us. We must always strive to refine our character far beyond what the standards of today are and instead fight to be as loving and compassionate as our forefathers. Noach's relative righteousness is a high standard for the rest of the world but it is a Jew's responsibility to lift the standard, to be a moral light to the nations.

Finally, to understand the relationship between these two characters, we have to understand that they are built upon each other. Avraham only existed and was able to become the Father of our Nation because of Noach's unending commitment to doing what was right. While Noach sets the ideal for the humanity, Avraham sets the ideal for what a Jew can be. The Talmud explains that there are three traits that are the sign of a Jewish Soul. Tractate Yevamot 79b says that the three signs of members of the Jewish People are that they are, "merciful, bashful, and doers of acts of kindness." The kindness referred to here is the same kindness that Avraham exemplified, whereas the righteousness of Noach is not described as an "inherently Jewish" quality. While the rest of the world is commanded to obey the Seven Mitzvot of Children of Noach and to accept these guidelines as the standard for morality, for a Jew our obligation extends much further. We must approach each situation with a burning desire to do what is good and right because of our love for others. It is not enough to be merely do the correct thing, we must exceed expectations and be an example for others.

On a personal level, I feel much better. I now appreciate that my brother's namesake was not in competition with mine, rather he took the values and example that Noach set and put a fresh passion and motivation behind it. Avi makes a huge Kiddush Hashem at his school and on the street, defining to many of his peers what a Jew is. His high ethical standard and passion for helping others is evident to all who meet him. In him, I see shades of Avraham Avinu, excelling with fervor and raising a bar I can only hope I had something to do with setting. God willing, we should all merit to share in Avraham's (and our Avi's) example of being a source of light and inspiration to all those who see us.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Noach's Rainbow

October 12, 2018

Noach and his family lived shortly after the creation of the world, in an era consumed by crime and violence. God instructs Noah to build an ark because he wants to drown the world but let some cream rise to the top. Over the course of the next 120 years, Noach builds a great big ark in his front yard. All the meanwhile his neighbors watch, making fun of him and skeptically hoping they would not get stuck in the rain. Although God made it so a lifetime could pass before the winds and rain started nobody headed Noachs warning and nobody repented. Noach was instructed to gather animals two by two for reproduction in the future after the flood. Noach's wife and family sealed themselves inside the ark and for forty days and nights, their vessel rose over the mountains until the earth could not be seen and all inhabitants man, beast and fowl were consumed by deep waters.

For a hundred and fifty days the ark bobbed lazily into decent where it settled on mount Arat. Noach dispatched a raven and multiple doves out across the water, one solar year from their arrival at mount Arat one dove returns with an olive branch. Noach is instructed to open the arc and populate the soil. Noah makes an offering to god and god promises by the sign of a rainbow that he will never drown the globe again. Noach grows a vineyard, and settles into the family life, drunkenly embarrassing his children. For ten generations Noach's children speak one language and live in a shared culture until their dispersal. With epic cooperation, they plan and attempt to build a structure that elevates them to the level of God. God floods their minds with babel and Noach's lineage is dispersed into seventy nations each with unique language and culture. This summarizes the time between Noach and Abram.

Rainbows have a very distinct symbolism in Judaism, though we are instructed to make a blessing after seeing one we are told not to see them, not to look at them at all. What kind of opportunity is this, one of blessing or one that reminds us of past crime resulting in global disaster?

A Rainbow is a sign that we are sinning, however, there have been generations that did not see a rainbow. The rule of King Chizkiyahu, the era of the Men of the Great Assembly, the generation of Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai and the generation of Rabbi Yehoshua ben Levi. When this happens it means someone on the earth is so righteous that he acts as a foundation stone for the world. In a conversation between Rabbi Yehoshua ben Levi and Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai in Gan Eden, Rabbi Shimon asked Rabbi Yehoshua if the rainbow had been seen in his lifetime. When Rabbi Yehoshua modestly hid his greatness by saying that it had, Rabbi Shimon said, “Then you’re not ben Levi!” the lesson is some greatness hides and some greatness can’t help it. When acting behind the scenes we have more room for error. When we step into the spotlight because we can't help it we will undoubtedly take a blow to our pride. This is a rare time god seemingly acts beyond his intent.

"And the Lord repented that He had made Man on the earth, and it grieved Him in His heart. And the Lord said, "I will destroy man whom I have created from the face of the earth, both man and beast, and the creeping thing and the fowls of the air, for I repent that I have made them."

This quote from before the flood shows Hashem having regret for creating all of man. How can god have grief or regret if he has already seen the show? He is beyond time, He knows the end before the beginning! The Midrash on these verses tells a story. A heretic asked R. Joshua ben Korchah: "Don't you Jews say that G-d knows the future?"

Rabbi Joshua answered, "Yes."

"Why then," continued the heretic, "is it written 'grieved Him in His heart?"

Responded R. Joshua, "Was a son ever born to you?"

"Yes," said the heretic.

"What did you do?"

"I rejoiced." "But didn't you know that one day he will die?"

Replied the man, "One rejoices when it is a time for rejoicing, and one mourns when it is a time for mourning."Said R. Joshua, "So it is with G-d.

Rashi adds "Although it was known to Him that they will sin and be destroyed, He nevertheless created them for the sake of the righteous who will descend from them."

When God chooses to perform miracles or permanently alter the world we live in he finds extreme measure. It takes many minor to add up to a flood of destruction. The bracha for seeing a rainbow is Blessed are You, Lord our G-d, King of the universe, who remembers the covenant, and is faithful to His covenant, and keeps His promise.

The rainbow arc is the same arc as a bow and arrow. Stretched over the horizon the arrow would load facing the heavens. A sign of forgiveness and weapons at rest.

A rainbows main meaning is that although we have been treated harshly in the past we recognize we are the produce that lived through a harsh weathering. It is impossible for us to revert to bad ways as it is impossible for God to flood the earth again. The rainbow stands testimony to renewal, healthy only to those who are established enough to grow.

Some have found the rainbow to have different signs The prophet Ezekiel described a vision in which he had seen the divine presence “like a rainbow in the clouds on a rainy day, with a corona around it…” in response some said it would even be appropriate to bow when seeing a rainbow. Yet it is still impolite to stare, as it is Hashem's glory. The Zohar says its a sign of the era of Moshiach.

Rainbow of Unity

November 1, 2019

In this week's Torah Portion, Parshas Noach, there are two major events that take place.

The more famous one is the incredible story of a man named Noach who was instructed by Hashem to build an ark. The people of his time were acting in terrible ways and deserved to drown in a flood. Only Noach, his family, and two of every species of animals (except for the kosher ones, of which seven were selected) would be saved. Why did the entire world need to be destroyed and all the people wiped out? Because they were sinning against each other. Specifically, they were stealing from each other and performing acts of sexual immorality.

The other event that takes place in this week's reading is the story of the Tower of Babel. In this event, the citizens of the world decided that they wanted to build a tower that would reach the heavens. To what end? To fight with God Himself! Their punishment was that they all suddenly spoke different languages and couldn't understand each other. With no way to communicate any longer, the project was abandoned and they dispersed to the four corners of the Earth.

This seems rather strange if one takes a close look. When the people were sinning against each other, the entire world needed to be obliterated. But when they attempted to fight and defy God Himself, they only needed to be dispersed and separated so they could no longer rebel against Him?

Perhaps the story could be better understood if I share one of my own. When I was a little girl, I shared a room with four brothers. One night, we were sent to bed and given permission to read on our own until we fell asleep. A brother who was not particularly interested in reading took a puzzle to his bed. It soon became too difficult to put it together on a surface that was not wide and flat so he moved it to the floor and invited us one by one to come and help him. I was very engrossed in a book and was the last one to join. Only moments later, my father walked into the room to check on us. Imagine his surprise when he saw us not in bed at all but with the light on and working on a puzzle! But then his face softened.

He said, "Had only a few of you been out of bed I probably would have been very angry, but you are all together and collaborating and there is no greater joy for a parent than to see his children working in unity."

It happened to have been that that night was the 9th of Av, the saddest day in the Jewish calendar. It is the anniversary of when the Holy Temple was destroyed. We are taught that the reason why it was destroyed was because the Jews were not getting along with each other and were acting hateful towards one another. It was a time that was especially important to my father that his children love each other and he was very happy to see that.

That is the explanation to our earlier conundrum. When the humans of the Earth were sinning in their interactions with each other, they didn't deserve to live. They needed to be completely wiped out. But when they were acting in unity, even against Hashem, they can live. God loves all of his creations, and what he wants from us is to love each other.

Let us try our best to love, not judge. To help, not deride. To support, not tear down. Let us do right by each other and Hashem will shine His blessings down upon us.

Good Shabbos

Believe in Me

October 23, 2020

Noach is approached by God and informed of his intention to destroy the earth. As an exceptionally righteous man in a wicked world, he alone has been selected to build a boat that will ensure the continuation of humanity. He does not argue or ask questions, "Noach did so, just as God commanded him, so he did."

Then the rain starts to fall. At 600 years old Noach gathers his family in the foyer to depart their home and enter the vessel he has dedicated himself to creating. They have their bags packed, the animals are caged away. Their house is empty and life savings have gone to gopher wood. Looking out the front door window they see the drops start getting bigger. Noach's wife Naamah is standing beside her three sons and daughters-in-law. It is silent. There is palpable anticipation hanging in the air. Everyone is waiting for Noach to open the door and lead the way into the ark. But he is standing there frozen, gaze transfixed on the puddles rapidly accumulating in the front lawn.

Rashi alludes to this mysterious moment. After 600 years of faithful service, and the commitment and investment that goes into an ark, why would Noach be hesitating now? According to Rashi, "Noach was of those who lack faith, he believed and didn't believe that the flood would come and did not enter the Ark until he was pushed by the water." Despite demonstrating his faithfulness and listening to God's every word consistently through this whole process, somehow he still lacked some deeper belief. Even though he had reoriented his entire life around this goal when the moment of truth arrived, he choked.

This perspective on Noach's moment reveals that true faith is expressed in the less significant daily decisions we make and confirmed on the occasions that require complete trust. Building the ark demanded the former, which Noach was comfortable with, but when his emuna required blind faith he couldn't give himself and his family over. From an outside perspective, it seems unbelievable that Noach would go so far only to stall at the finish. But I know that I expend an inordinate amount of energy on goals that I believe are important while not allowing that belief to propel me to take a risk or make a significant change.

While I was here in Yeshiva I heard another take on this dramatic moment. Instead of reading Rashi the way it is translated above, by moving a comma (and ignoring a little grammar) his comment can be read, "Noach also believed in those who were lacking belief so he did not enter the Ark until he was pushed by the water."

In this reading, Noach had so much faith in those around him that he believed they would come to their senses and abandon their sinful ways. His faith in humanity was so great it could overshadow all of the toil of building the ark and the torrential rains. Clearly, we can see the peril of having too much faith in harmful people and not allowing ourselves to get away until we're in over our heads.

I hope that I can internalize the lessons of this episode from both perspectives. I want the courage to take a leap of faith and the sense to know when I am letting my faith in others drag me down.

Shabbat Shalom,

2021

Noach is a patriarch that we can all emulate. In this weeks Parsha Rashi tells us that Noach had doubts about the flood and never entered until it started raining. Noach is not credited with birthing a nation, or leading his people to the promised land. Unlike Moshe and Avraham Noach remains an unsung hero of humanity, and not just humans but life on earth.

Noach's strengths are in surviving and persevering through harsh criticism and doubt. Noach's super power is not letting even his own doubts distract him from the job. Noach's job was not pretty and much of his day on the ark was devoted to tending to animals.

There is a Yiddish saying Fun a kasha shtarbt men nit -- "Nobody ever died of a question." This saying is coming to teach us that while we are torn or doubtful we cannot become paralyzed. Noach was faced by the doubts of all his neighbors and he had his own doubts but the stakes were too high not to act.

The lesson of Parsha Noach is one of survival and perseverance through criticism and doubt. Noach's people speak one language and belong to one culture for ten generations until his descendents build a monument to their own invincibility. This is their ultimate downfall as God is forced to split their tongue and scatter them throughout the land. Noach's gift of action in face of doubt is a double edged sword but a gift nonetheless. Assertive people bring life into a room with them. Noach's ability to be assertive saved humanity.

Who Knows?

Yosef · October 28, 2022

During the Great Flood, the entire world was destroyed. Every person, every animal, every building – everything except those housed within Noach's ark. When Noach finally opened the door of the ark, after a full year inside, he stepped out onto the dry ground of a new world and he was filled with an overwhelming sense of gratitude. Gratitude for the precious gift of life and for the priceless opportunity to walk the face of Hashem's beautiful world. As an expression of thanks, Noach built an altar and brought an offering to Hashem from all of the kosher animals.

I can't tell you exactly why, but this offering elicited a great amount of satisfaction from Hashem. Maybe it was the level of gratitude with which it was fueled, maybe it was because it marked a drastic change from the downward spiral the world had been following up until that point, but whatever the reason it gave Hashem great pleasure.

The Torah tells us that at that moment a very great thing happened – Hashem swore that he would never again destroy the world in its entirety. No matter how low the world would sink, no matter how deep mankind would trudge into the quagmire of iniquity, the world would not be destroyed. What a monumental accomplishment Noach had achieved! Who knows how many times this promise has saved the world from destruction? Who knows how heavily all of mankind depends on Noach's offering that day?

Yet the Ramban comments that, at the time, no man was aware of the great waves being made in heaven. Hashem swore, but he did not communicate His promise to Noach or to anyone else. Finally, thousands of years later, when Moshe wrote the Torah, Hashem revealed to him what had taken place upstairs as Noach brought his offering, and that is how we know about it today.

For me, this is wildly encouraging. Noach had just won a victory for the ages, he had just made one of the greatest accomplishments in the history of mankind, yet he never knew about the fruits of his labor.

Sometimes we put our heart and soul into achieving some greatness of spirit. It could be a pure and heartfelt prayer, a mitzvah which we struggle to fulfill to completion, or an insult which we manage to swallow without lashing back. It could be a kind act or a kind word or a million other things. And we wonder if we really made a difference in the world. Usually we'll never find out, but let's remember the offering of Noach and realize that we very well might have just scored a point which can sustain the whole world, which can bring Moshiach closer, which can elicit great pleasure from Hashem. We may not know about it, but we're no worse off than Noach, whose offering sustains us in every generation without his knowledge.

Just keep striving for greatness. You never know what you may be achieving.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Avi · October 19, 2023

This week has been particularly difficult for Jews around the world. This weeks Parsha however does not focus on Jews and it instead focuses on Noach the only righteous man in a world steeped in violence and backwards corruption. God speaks to Noach and instructs him to build an ark because God is going to end humanity with a great flood.

Noach follows God's instructions and gathers a pair of every living creature his direct family and the tides began to rise. It rained for forty days and nights and the water churned for 150 days. Once the water calmed Noach sent doves to find dry land. One year after the beginning of the flood Noach and his family were finally instructed by God to leave the ark and repopulate the drying land.

Noach builds an alter and offers sacrifices to God. God pledges to never erase humanity again and the rainbow is the sign of His testament. God commands Noach about the sanctity of life and murder is designated as a capital offense. While man is permitted to eat meat he is not allowed to eat living flesh.

Noach plants grapevines and becomes drunk on wine. Two of his three sons Shem and Yapheth are commended for covering up their fathers embarrassment. Ham is criticized for taking advantage of his fathers shame.

Noachs descendants live as a single people with one language and culture for ten generations. They unite in the effort to build a tower to the heavens as a symbol of their invincibility. God humbles them by splitting their tongue making it impossible for them to understand eachother. They abandoned their project and divided into seventy nations.

The Parsha concludes by recounting the ten generations between Noach and Abram (Abraham) and Abram's journey to the land of Cannan.

They said to one another: ". . . Let us build for ourselves a city, and a tower whose top shall reach the heavens; and we shall make for ourselves a name, lest we be scattered over the face of the entire earth . . ."

G-d dispersed them from there across the face of the earth, and they stopped building the city. Therefore its name was called Babel (confusion), for there G-d confused the language of the world . . . (Genesis 11:3-9)

Why did God destroy the tower of Babel? After God destroyed humanity their culture was one based on survival. They built a shrine to the cause of survival and that was the beginning of their descent. The cause of survival is hollow and the purpose of human life goes far beyond survival. All too often we Jews find ourselves fighting for our very survival and in times like this we must remember the lesson of the tower of Babel. Our purpose must not be a higher wall and stronger tower rather we must give meaning to our survival by reiterating the "why?" of our existence. We must give our name meaning, our city significance and crown our towers with a higher purpose. I pray that Jews around the world sanctify Gods name by being exemplary humans. Shabbat Shalom.

Parshat

Lech Lecha

Yosef · October 27, 2017

Our forefather Avraham was given ten great tests in his lifetime. These were the defining moments in his life and served as the catalysts for his becoming the father of the entire Jewish nation. In the beginning of Parshat Lech Lecha we find one of these tests; the test to leave his family and his homeland in order to travel to the land of Israel.

However, it is curious to note that when he was commanded to set out, he was guaranteed by Hashem that it would be to his benefit. He was told that at the other end of his journey he would be blessed with children, wealth, and honor. So what exactly was the big test? Doesn't everyone leave their home in search of these much-coveted blessings?

Rabbi Moshe Feinstein explains that the test here was not about the actual journey, it was about the mindset. Avraham knew very clearly that G-d could do anything at all. He had already experienced numerous miracles which defied all laws of nature. This awareness could have led Avraham to a very simple question -- why must he travel so far in order to receive G-d's blessings; couldn't G-d give them to him right there and then? The test Avraham was faced with was to accept the decree of the Almighty without asking questions. To realize that G-d has a master plan, and that we are not meant to understand everything.

This was the greatness of Avraham, and it behooves all of us to learn from our great forefather and incorporate this attribute into our daily lives.

We often want a particular thing, be it a job, an item, or a relationship, and we convince ourselves that the only way we can be happy is if we have that thing. But, if you're anything like me, then you know that more often than not we don't actually receive our wishes. At that point the natural reaction is to feel a great deal of frustration and sadness, or even anger and depression. We are absolutely certain that we would have benefited from that thing and we can't see any good reason for it not going the way we'd like.

It is here that we are charged with the task of "tamim tihyeh im Hashem Elokeichem", "you shall be wholehearted with G-d". This means to trust Him and accept that He knows what is for our ultimate good much much better than we ever could. We need to realize that in our human frailty our vision is grossly jaded and short-sighted. We see such a small part of the picture that it is ludicrous to think that we know best. Who then can we rely on? On He who knows and controls all of creation, He who can see the ultimate outcome of any given scenario, and He who loves us unconditionally and wants only our very best.

I heard a beautiful story last week that really drives this point home. First, let me introduce Rabbi Yehoshua Hoffman to those who don't know him. Yehoshua was once a regular guy, like you and me. But when he was twenty-two years old, everything changed, as he broke his neck in a car accident and suffered paralysis in most of his body. Suddenly he found himself severely handicapped, unable to perform the simplest of tasks by himself. Yehoshua could have very easily chosen to wallow in self-pity for the rest of his life, but instead he has decided to constantly count his blessings and let G-d run the show. Indeed, he is one of the happiest people I know, and he has inspired countless individuals with his positive outlook.

Now I'll share the story. As you read, try to imagine yourself in Yehoshua's shoes. One morning, Yehoshua woke up early as usual with a packed schedule ahead of him. He requires an aide to help him out of bed and the aid regularly arrives at an appointed every morning. But this morning, the aide was nowhere to be seen and the minutes passed with agonizing slowness. Five minutes turned to ten, and then to fifteen. Yehoshua was totally stuck; no one was around to help him and without assistance he couldn't so much as sit up in bed. He felt the irritation rise within him -- he had so much to accomplish that day and because of something beyond his control he was unable to do a thing. But then he realized the futility of those thoughts and decided to replace them with a proactive and positive approach. It dawned on him that if the situation was totally beyond his control then G-d must want him to be right here right now -- exactly where he was, lying in bed and not out doing all the things he had planned. He was able to loosen his grasp on the reins and let Hashem choose the terms. This brought him an inner peace and a joy rooted in the knowledge that he was doing exactly what G-d wanted from him. This attitude enabled him to make it through the entire two hours that it took for someone to finally help him out of bed.

Can you imagine yourself stuck in that bed? Can you picture the deep feelings of frustration and self-pity threatening to overrun you? And now can you come to Yehoshua's powerful realization and feel the comfort and peace of mind wash over you? Finally, can you take that powerful picture back into your own life and recognize that wherever you are, whatever the circumstances, Hashem is leading you by the hand to exactly where you need to be, whether you understand it or not.

This was the test which Avraham withstood as he followed Hashem's directive away from his home and family. Did he understand why he needed to leave in order to receive G-d's blessings? No. But he understood that he was in good hands and surely Hashem would only act in his best interests.

If we can emulate Avraham's ways, then we are sure to live a life of endless happiness, peace of mind, and security -- with G-d's help.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Go To Yourself

Noah · October 19, 2018

This week’s parsha introduces us to the progenitor of our nation. Avraham is presented to us as a Jew in his natural habitat, amidst a divine test. He has been commanded to “Lech Lecha” or “go for himself.” He is instructed to leave his land, birthplace and father’s house. Unlike the command given to Noach last week, Avraham was not called to service to save the physical earth. He did not need to amass an unparalleled menagerie in a grand gesture to repopulate a barren world. Avraham’s test was not about generalities, it was specific and requested an individual sacrifice. Managing a zoo is no easy feat but abandoning everything you know because of a private monotheistic revelation is an accomplishment deserving of its own religion. Or three.

We see from Avraham that our relationship with God is most frequently developed through intimate and personal tests of moral character. This is the realm where we have opportunities to grow closer to the best version of ourselves and to God. These tests are not generic or standardized and certainly not multiple choice. Frequently they are steeped in nuance and force us to question our innermost views of morality. Rabbi Nachum learns Lech Lecha as meaning, “Go to yourself.” He teaches that we are being commanded to reconnect with our inner self, our personal morality. Only through reestablishing a dialog with our easily ignored consciousness can we truly discern if we are moving closer to God or farther away. And as the world is a reflection of the Torah it is clear is why the archetypal characters throughout Bereshit are so complex. The Ramban criticizes Avraham for leaving Canaan and holds him partially responsible for the Egyptian exile. Likewise, he laments Sarah’s oppression of Ishmael and links our ongoing tensions with our cousins to her harshness. But these critiques hardly detract from the greatness of our forefamily. To the contrary, they show us that the tests they faced were of the same nature as ours, personal, messy and complicated.

Rabbi Sacks explains that we too can rise to the test of Avraham even in an age where idol worship is decidedly passe. He tells us to look with the clarity of Avraham at what aspects of our lives are directed toward service of God and which are directed toward service of self. The latter, he explains, are the idols of our time and we must emulate our foremost patriarch in annihilating them. It’s far from simple but we must remind ourselves constantly that, “We are not what we own but what we share, not what we buy but what we give.” Any deviation from this philosophy leads to the worship of material possessions for their own sake, independent of their higher purpose to be elevated in Godly service.

While we are given the gift of an Avraham Avinu within us, hearing his loving advice and accepting his guidance is no easy task. We have buried this voice deep within us, covered it with years of ego-driven self-satisfaction and trained ourselves to ignore it at all costs. Our society at large has primed and trained us through an obsession with “rugged individualism” and personal rights to value the individual above all. Avraham teaches us that the value of the individual lies within its ability to experience God in a close and personal way. The value of an individual is the ability to work on one's self, constantly striving to become better. Without a focus on spiritual introspection and the needs of the community, individualism is idolatry.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Family Drama

Cali · November 8, 2019

In this week's Parasha we being to follow Avram and Sarai on their journey, from leaving Avraham's birthplace to the trials they faced building a family of their own. One part of this Parsha which always sparked my curiosity is the relationship between Avram, Sarai, and Hagar. How did Sarai go from encouraging Avram to have a baby with Hagar to afflicting Hagar once she became pregnant?

In the Parsha we read:

וַתֹּ֨אמֶר שָׂרַ֜י אֶל־אַבְרָ֗ם הִנֵּה־נָ֞א עֲצָרַ֤נִי יְהוָה֙ מִלֶּ֔דֶת בֹּא־נָא֙ אֶל־שִׁפְחָתִ֔י אוּלַ֥י אִבָּנֶ֖ה מִמֶּ֑נָּה וַיִּשְׁמַ֥ע אַבְרָ֖ם לְק֥וֹל שָׂרָֽי׃ וַתִּקַּ֞ח שָׂרַ֣י אֵֽשֶׁת־אַבְרָ֗ם אֶת־הָגָ֤ר הַמִּצְרִית֙ שִׁפְחָתָ֔הּ מִקֵּץ֙ עֶ֣שֶׂר שָׁנִ֔ים לְשֶׁ֥בֶת אַבְרָ֖ם בְּאֶ֣רֶץ כְּנָ֑עַן וַתִּתֵּ֥ן אֹתָ֛הּ לְאַבְרָ֥ם אִישָׁ֖הּ ל֥וֹ לְאִשָּֽׁה׃. וַיָּבֹ֥א אֶל־הָגָ֖ר וַתַּ֑הַר וַתֵּ֙רֶא֙ כִּ֣י הָרָ֔תָה וַתֵּקַ֥ל גְּבִרְתָּ֖הּ בְּעֵינֶֽיהָ׃: וַתֹּ֨אמֶר שָׂרַ֣י אֶל־אַבְרָם֮ חֲמָסִ֣י עָלֶיךָ֒ אָנֹכִ֗י נָתַ֤תִּי שִׁפְחָתִי֙ בְּחֵיקֶ֔ךָ וַתֵּ֙רֶא֙ כִּ֣י הָרָ֔תָה וָאֵקַ֖ל בְּעֵינֶ֑יהָ יִשְׁפֹּ֥ט יְהוָ֖ה בֵּינִ֥י וּבֵינֶֽיׄךָ׃: וַיֹּ֨אמֶר אַבְרָ֜ם אֶל־שָׂרַ֗י הִנֵּ֤ה שִׁפְחָתֵךְ֙ בְּיָדֵ֔ךְ עֲשִׂי־לָ֖הּ הַטּ֣וֹב בְּעֵינָ֑יִךְ וַתְּעַנֶּ֣הָ שָׂרַ֔י וַתִּבְרַ֖ח מִפָּנֶֽיהָ.׃.

As the Parsha goes, Sarai approaches Avram in distress and tells him that he should have a child with Hagar. When Hager becomes pregnant it says that she "lost esteem from her mistress" (Sarai) and felt entitled to be viewed as the mother of Avram's child and not as Sarai's maidservant.

Upset by the change in dynamic, Sarai asked Avram, how could you go to my maidservant on behalf of yourself when we are trying to build a family together? Avram agrees with Sarai and says, "Your maid is in your hands. Deal with her as you think right." From that point, Sarai began to treat Hagar harshly, afflicting her with hard labor.

In the midst of affliction, Hagar decided to leave the tent of Avram and Sarai but on her way out is approached by an angel who tells her to, "Go back to your mistress and submit to her harsh treatment. I will greatly increase your offspring, And they shall be too many to count…. For God has paid heed to your suffering."

Ramban strongly criticizes Sarai's treatment Hagar. "Our matriarch sinned through this oppression, as did Avraham by allowing this to transpire. So God heard her [Hagar's] affliction and gave her a son who would be a wild ass of a man to afflict the seed of Abraham and Sarah with all kinds of affliction." Ramban who was living in a time of conflict between Jews and Muslims, clearly saw the episode of Sarai and Hager reflected in his own historical reality.

Living in our neighborhood enables constant interaction with our cousins. It means going to the store and calling out "Shaku Maku?" in Arabic, and receiving a "Baruch Hashem" in return. It means sitting in my house listening to the call to prayer 5 times a day. And it means grieving for lives that are taken away too early in the name of God.

I don't think this conflict is easy to explain but I can't help but wonder: How would our current situation be different if the relationships among Avram, Hagar, and Sarai would have been simple? If only Sarai would have understood the implications of Hagar's pregnancy on her relationship with Avram, if only Hagar would have been more sensitive to Sarai's marital insecurity in this trying time, if only Avram would have facilitated better communication within his household, could generations of tension and hostility have been averted?

When I think of Avram and Sarai, or any of our patriarchs, they seem untouchable. But I think it is important to remember that these holy characters were also human, with human emotions and reactions. Complicated family dynamics and miscommunication are fundamental parts of the human condition. Even without overt hostility, our free will enables us to become entangled in complicated emotional webs where there is no perfect solution, where it is inevitable that someone's feelings will get hurt. It is silly for us to judge Sarai or Avram, the progenitors of monotheism, but it is no coincidence that the uniquely human relationship to God was forged in the uniquely human dysfunctional family.

No family is perfect but when we focus on clearer communication, when we emphasize empathy, and when we honestly analyze the implications of our actions, we strengthen our familial bond and get closer to peace.

Shabbat Shalom Cali

Avi · October 27, 2020

Lech Lecha, go for your self. In this week's Parsha Avram enters an eternal covenant with God, as part of this covenant Avram's people are promised great wealth. "Know that your children shall be strangers in a land not theirs, and they will enslave them and afflict them . . . and afterwards they will go out with great wealth." We as people were founded after are eygyptian exile, and exiled to babylon after the destruction of the first Beit Hamikdash, then exiled again by the greeks, today we live in a roman exile after our second Beit Hamikdash was destroyed. For nineteen centuries we have been at the whim of kings, dictators and politicians that sometimes masacre us and sometimes let us live.

Galut is more than being away from our home land, being in galut means we must fend for ourselves, spiritually and politically. In galut our identities are in jeopardy, galut is a journey and every journey is dangerous.

The prophets tell us "because of our sins we were exiled from our land." but our galut is foretold to Abraham before the sins it atones for were even committed. This must mean our galut is essential to our role as Jews in the world, and that our galut does more than just atone for our sins.

Any slave in Egypt would have forsaken gold and silver to end their servitude a day early. Instead God draws out our exodus from Egypt, causing darkness to freeze Egyptians where they stood, allowing us to take inventory of their wealth.

If a prisoner is told tomorrow he will be set free with a lot of money, he will still beg, "Let me out today I don't want any money!" The Jews in Egypt felt the same way. No amount of reparations could possibly make up for generations of servitude. While this is true, God sees to it that when Israel leaves Egypt we have wealth for generations.

The Talmud teaches us "The people of Israel were exiled amongst the nations only so that converts might be added to them." while the basic understanding of this is also true, the main idea is that we are in galut to uplift not only other nations but their laws and very infrastructure. The chassidish belief that every person, item, or phenomenon has at its core a spark of God, embellishes the value in everyday people, items and phenomenon. Jacob famously exemplifies this value when he risked his life to save jugs from the river bank. The talmud says a righteous man values their possessions more than their bodies, because they can recognize the breath of life and spiritual value in matter.

We are in Galut today, But tomorrow we could be free. The moral of this dvar is to take what we can from the galut around us. At work my boss asked me why are Jews stereotypically rich? At a loss for words I told him most stereotypes are grounded in reality and that I know many very poor and very wealthy Jews. As prisoners in this metaphor we have the privilege to not care for money and only yearn for our freedom. However thanks to Avram's covenant with God no matter what the warden tells us, we know there are more than just reparations on the way.

Stop Dreaming. Start Doing.

Yosef · October 15, 2021

Avraham achieved a large part of his greatness when he followed the command of Hashem to leave his homeland and venture to the land of Canaan, which later became known as the land of Israel. The land of Canaan was not a random place on the map which G-d just happened to choose as the holy land, rather the land was (and is) intrinsically special. In fact, we are told that the waters of the great flood which covered the entire globe did not enter the land of Canaan. It was Hashem's chosen land from the beginning of time, and it has many qualities which can aid a person in spiritual matters.

What is fascinating to note is that traveling to Canaan was not a new idea which Hashem gave Avraham in this week's Parsha. Actually, it was thought up before by someone we don't consider particularly virtuous. That person was none other than Avraham's father, the idolatrous Terach, who had his own son thrown into a furnace for breaking Terach's idols. It seems that after Avraham emerged unscathed from the furnace his father had a change of heart and decided to follow Avraham's lead and pursue the service of Hashem. To that end, the Torah states that Terach took his son Avraham and the rest of his family, left their hometown of Ohr Kasdim, and began traveling towards Canaan. Why Canaan? For no less lofty a purpose than to reap all the spiritual benefits of the land. This means that, before Avraham ever set out on his own journey to Canaan, his wicked father was inspired to make the trek first.

Terach's aspirations and convictions at that time were every bit on par with the aspirations of Avraham – and yet he fell short. The very next verse tells that, while en route to Canaan, Terach stopped in a city called Charan, where he settled and eventually died. He dreamed, he yearned, but ultimately he died the same as he had always lived, never succeeding to elevate himself.

Not so our father Avraham. In this week's Parsha, the Torah states, “Avraham set out to go to Canaan, and he arrived at Canaan.” When Avraham set his eyes on the goal he couldn't be deterred by anything, in such a way that his plans were inextricably linked to their fulfillment.

This is what set Avraham apart from Terach, this is what sets the righteous apart from the wicked, and this is what will decide the fate of each and every one of us.

We all have flashes of inspiration, we all have dreams of achievement, and we all have moments when we stare at ourselves in the mirror and resolve that we must change. But all of our good intentions and promises to become better are far from enough. We must take immediate and aggressive action to fulfill our dreams. If we get distracted, then we are at risk of ending up just like Terach, who died the same as he had always lived, without ever reaching the levels for which he aspired. But if we harness the power of our dreams by acting on them without delay, there's no telling how far we can go.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Hope, Confidence and Compassion

October 27, 2023

Lech Lecha is replete with events applicable to this horrific situation we find ourselves in. Avraham is instructed to leave his birthplace, one of the ten major trials he will face in his life, he is given the land of Israel for his offspring to inhabit, and Hagar is expelled from her home.

While the scale and magnitude of this conflict make it easy to try and draw direct historical parallels to understand what lies in store, this approach is left best to prophets. The deeper themes, however, can give us a more nuanced and empathetic understanding of the people living through these situations. While the geopolitical situation has changed greatly over the last couple thousand years, the basic psychology of people has remained remarkably constant and our parsha can offer perspectives from people who endured many of the challenges we are faced with today.

By most counts, Avraham's first test was when he was instructed to leave his home. Despite being promised that this departure would be for the best, his ability to comply and trust in his future is considered one of his fundamental achievements. As a people familiar with displacement and exile we have no trouble imagining the incredible difficulty and sacrifice involved. Across the country hundreds of thousands are now displaced, hopefully so that they can have a better future, but nonetheless they are enduring a life-altering test of their faith. For those of us still sleeping in the comfort of our beds, how can we demonstrate our solidarity and empathize properly?

The Gemara in Rosh Hashana quotes our parsha: And some say: Also, a change of one’s place of residence cancels an evil judgment, as it is written: “And the Lord said to Abram: Go you out of your county” (Genesis 12:1), and afterward it is written: “And I will make of you a great nation”

The Meiri comments: Even though someone's judgment can seem as though it is already sealed against him, He should not give up on forgiveness, prayer, and repentance. He should change his actions and make plans, such as changing a name or a place, that will awaken a change in his conduct.

For us to ensure that the overwhelming feelings of displacement being experienced now are not in vain we must direct that feeling inward and use it to rouse ourselves to be better.

When Avraham is promised his inheritance in Israel he is told, “Get up, walk about the land, through its length and its breadth, for I give it to you.”

The Gemara debates whether this can be used as proof that simply walking through the property can be a means of acquiring it but concludes it cannot. If so what was the reason that Avraham was instructed to walk through it? The Gemara answers somewhat cryptically, “So that it would be easy for his descendants to acquire.”

The Rashbam fleshes out this answer writing, “It will make them seem like inheritors and not thieves and will prevent the accuser from prosecuting us.”

In a time when it seems that we are constantly being misunderstood as thieves and prosecuted for staking our claim to Israel, the Rashbam’s advice is incredibly practical. We must demonstrate that we are of this land, not mere inhabitants of it. We establish that claim by following in the footsteps of Avraham and getting up to experience the actual land.

Finally, we have the expulsion of Hagar at the hands of Sarai. The parsha says: “She afflicted [Hagar], and [Hagar] fled from her.” Regarding this episode, the Ramban writes, “Our matriarch sinned in this affliction, as did Avraham in permitting it. God heard [Hagar’s] affliction and gave her a son who would be a wild man, afflicting the offspring of Avraham and Sarah with all manner of afflictions.”

This sentiment can easily be misconstrued to lend credence to the false narrative that our current conflict is biblical and intractable. This view misses the much larger moral message that we can learn. Affliction breeds instability and wildness. Hurting others never brings about resolution, it just contributes to cycles of conflict. We constantly have opportunities to rectify this misdeed both by refraining from unduly injuring others and by never standing by when it occurs in our midst.

At a time that is so rife with pure emotion, it is easy to be overcome. We can fall into despair and hopelessness. Avraham teaches us that even when faced with displacement and tribulations we need to cling to our belief that is a necessary step toward building a brighter future. We can feel that the criticism from the prosecutors who deny our right to live in this land is irreparable. Yet through Avraham being instructed to go out and encounter the land, we learn that through our engagement with Eretz Yisrael, we solidify our role as inheritors and silence those who disagree. Finally, we can easily allow this emotion to lead us to cruelty and the natural urge to afflict those we perceive as having afflicted us. We learn from Hagar that this inclination must be suppressed and that it only perpetuates unrest.

Hopefully, the coming weeks and months will be full of hope, confidence, and compassion for all of us, and am yisroel.

Shabbat Shalom.

Parshat

Vayeira

Noah · November 3, 2017

This week's parsha introduces us to the first Jewish Family Unit. Avraham and Sarah, who have yearned to bring another life into this world, and have been promised by God that they would have the opportunity, finally conceive. Before the birth of Yitzchak there was another heir apparent to the throne of Avraham, his nephew Lot. Lot and Avraham share a relationship that is somewhere between Father-Son and Brothers, creating our paradigm for the Uncle-Nephew relationship. But whereas Yitzchak becomes the face of our nation, Lot is relegated to a much less prestigious role. What in the relationship between Avraham and Yitzchak enabled it to become the sustaining example of Jewish Family, while Lot's progeny are barred from even entering our nation?

The Torah stresses that Avraham and Lot were very similar. Avraham tells Lot, "Let there not be a dispute between me and you, and between my shepherds and yours, for we are brothers." The Midrash explains that the phrase "for we are brothers" is coming to tell us that they looked alike and had many similarities. With Yitzchak we see a very similar description. When the Torah says that Avraham bore Yitzchak, Rashi explains that the Torah is again coming to tell us that these men looked alike and shared familial similarities. How could two men who are said to be so close to Avraham end up in such different places in the Jewish Family?

The answer to this question can teach us a lot about what family means from a Jewish perspective. Through analyzing the will of these men, their hospitality, and their attitudes toward adversity we can discover some fundamental principles that lie at the foundation of our patriarchy.

The Torah tells us that Avraham's efforts to develop a relationship with Lot required him to be "madbik," implying that Avraham was forcibly and strenuously attempting to keep Lot attached to him. While Avraham strove to have a close relationship, Lot constantly wandered, never attaching himself to Avraham with an open heart. This is the first sign that Lot will not fit into the ideal Jewish Family. The nature of a relationship capable of sustaining an eternal nation cannot be forced. This is true from both the perspective of Lot and of Avraham. Lot's inability to accept Avraham fully, is partially responsible for him losing a leading role in the Eternal Jewish Family, Avraham is also criticized in the Midrash for not recognizing that fostering a sincere relationship with Lot was futile. The relationships in a Jewish Family must be maintained through a mutual desire for closeness, not by force.

The sanctity that is present is a Jewish Family is unparalleled. This sanctity is not a result of insulating ourselves from others, but by inviting in and elevating others. Avraham welcomed all guests regardless of their backgrounds and beliefs, but the order in which he served his guests shows how thoroughly he was committed to maintaining the high spiritual standard of his home. When serving the Angels that came to check on him after his Brit Milah, the Torah tells us that Avraham preceded washing his guests feet to setting a place for them to rest. Rashi explains that the desert dwellers would worship the dust of their feet and that's why Avraham was so diligent in ensuring this dust did not enter his domain. Lot, preceded "resting" to "washing" demonstrating that while he had learned from Avraham's hospitality he hadn't managed to commit to maintain a high spiritual standard for himself. A Jewish Home must be open to everyone, as both Avraham and Lot taught us. However, it is easy to rationalize putting yourself in bad company by saying you are drawing in the riffraff. There is a fine line between elevating others and allowing ourselves to fall. The hospitality that creates sanctity elevates everyone, while hospitality without strength to maintain boundaries, threatens the foundation of the Jewish Home.

The contrast in these men's conception of hospitality extends to how they react to adversity as well. When Sedom is destroyed, Lot flees, seeking solitude on a mountain. There are no travelers for him to greet there, no one to invite over for a Shabbos Meal. Ultimately, this decision to pursue solitude on the mountain leads him to incest with his own daughters. We learn that Lot fell into this sin because his family thought the world was over and this was the only way to continue humanity. While Lot allows negativity and despondency to infect his family, Avraham decides to move for the opposite reason. Avraham combats despair by rededicating himself to helping others. For Avraham, hospitality is a necessity and because there are no longer guests, Avraham moves to where he can find them. While Lot collapses into depression and loneliness after a catastrophe, Avraham understands it as an opportunity to elevate elsewhere. Avraham doesn't allow himself to become bogged down in self-pity, he picks himself and his family up to continue a mission of good, no matter what! The Jewish Home cannot be preoccupied with depression and self-pity. These traits are self serving and prove that whatever altruism was previously demonstrated was not nearly as pure as presented. When facing adversity, the Jewish Family doubles down and continues to seek opportunities to elevate the world around them.

As a descendant of Avraham and fellow member of a Jewish Family, I must strive to internalize these lessons. There are often times when I force my religious practice to be "madbik" to me, instead of approaching mitzvot with open arms and a strong will. There are times that I convince myself I am being a light to others while in reality I am just rationalizing my descent into the dark. And, this week especially, I allowed myself to be overcome by abjection instead of taking the opportunity to elevate myself and my family through rededication to our goals. God willing, through a better understanding of the examples of my Forefathers, I will be able to enter into this Shabbos with burning desire, holy inclusiveness, and a rekindled commitment to building a Beautiful Jewish Home.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Chesed

Avi · November 15, 2019

In this week's Parsha Vayeira, The notoriously evil city of Sodom was destroyed by God. The people of Sodom were not just bad but the Talmud teaches us that they incriminated being good. The root of their ideology was that helping others or "chesed" is not only criminal, but immoral helping is hurting. Sodom worshiped the individual and valued independence as the highest virtue.

Their courts punished acts of kindness with sadistic displays that were personally themed for any Sodomites crime. When a girl gave money to the poor, they smeared her with honey so the bees could eat and sting her. When a man invited a stranger to his wedding he was forced to be naked out in front of everyone. They believed that being that giving things to people for free was the most indignant way to treat someone so they punished measure for measure.

First, a gentle rain fell on Sodom and then the rain turned to sulfur and fire, the Sodomites also died measure for measure, rain watered their fields with kindness only to have it transformed into their demise.

The Jewish outlook is that it is ideal to be reliant on yourself for most things "The one who hates gifts will live."- proverbs. one must be in such a state of poverty he can no longer afford to enhance Shabbos before he can become a beggar. While there are high standards for the individual looking inward, the standards shift when one is in a position to give. We are commanded to help and understand that there are many unfortunate situations where help is needed and in the cases of orphans and widows we must not waiver in our support.

Shabbat Shalom, Avi

The Tent of Avraham and Sarah

Yosef · November 6, 2020

Our mother Sarah saw Yishmael laughing, so she told Avraham to banish him from the house. Just like that.

How are we supposed to understand such a thing? What's wrong with laughing? And whatever it is, could it really deserve such a swift and harsh punishment?

Rashi explains with the famous Midrash that the word 'metzachek', although literally translated as laughter, here refers to the three capital prohibitions – murder, idolatry, and immorality.

If we are to take Rashi's comment literally it would certainly answer our question, but a literal understanding seems unlikely. Had Yishmael really sunk to such depravity, Avraham would not have needed Sarah's plea to banish him; he would have done it himself long before. Also, later in the Parshah, when Yishmael is about to die of thirst in the desert, the Midrash records a discussion between Hashem and the angels whether or not to save him miraculously. The angels said that a miracle could not be performed for one who's progeny would later kill the Jews with thirst, to which Hashem responded that since Yishmael was still innocent at the time he would be saved. If Yishmael had already committed such a heinous list of sins bearing the death penalty, he could hardly be called innocent.

Rabbi Avigdor Miller therefore concludes that Yishmael must not have committed the sins in actuality, but Sarah saw from his laughter that he already had the potential for such things – it was only a matter of time.

But what did Sarah see in this laugh?

Rabbi Miller explains that the house of Avraham and Sarah was the Beit Hamikdash of their days, so to speak. To the naked eye, it was a regular house in which they ate, slept, and lived. They performed all the daily functions of life in their house, but in everything they did they concentrated their thoughts on a relationship with Hashem. When they ate, they focused on the greatness and kindness of He who provided them with food. When they slept, they did so only so that they would have strength to serve Him. When they interacted with people, they strove to emulate G-d's love and benevolence and to teach of His ways. In this way, they elevated the mundane nature of a house to the loftiest heights, and thereby their house became the house of Hashem.

So holy was the tent of Avraham and Sarah, that when, generations later, the Jews set forth to build Hashem's house, they found no better model than the house of Avraham and Sarah. And so we find the midrashim linking every facet of our forefather's tent to the tent of the Almighty Himself.

Laughter is no crime, but there are some places where you don't laugh. In the Beit Hamikdash, laughter is understandably inappropriate. The holiness and sublimity of the place demands a gravity which leaves no room for laughter. The greatest of happiness, yes, but not the levity of laughter.

Such was the state of things in Avraham's tent on a daily basis. It was the holiest place on earth, the dwelling place of the Shechina itself, and laughter was therefore highly improper.

Now we can imagine that when Yishmael did laugh, everyone stopped in their tracks, unsure of what to do. One could have heard a pin drop. Those gathered were aghast at the lack of respect for everything which Avraham's tent stood for. Laughing here showed a grievous dishonor to the presence of G-d which permeated the place.

But Sarah alone saw where this was headed. If Yishmael could shake off the fear of Hashem here of all places, there was nothing stopping him from the worst of sins. It is for this reason that Yishmael was banished.

There are many lessons here, but I'd like to focus on one that is incredibly empowering. We think that holiness and greatness come from lofty spiritual pursuits and magnificent acts. We therefore tend to think that true greatness is just not for us, and we resign ourselves to mediocrity. Here we find that Avraham and Sarah achieved their exalted relationship with Hashem not through big things, but by channeling the small day-to-day grind towards Hashem. They achieved the highest spiritual hights in their tent – at home, with normal everyday functions.

This gives me a tremendous sense of power and possibility. I need not travel to the heavens to find Hashem. No. I can find him right here. In the seed of an apple and the wind rustling through the trees. As I talk to my friend and drive down the street. When I play with my child, and while cleaning the house. When I eat. When I sleep. At every turn – there I can connect. I can turn the mundane into the ultimate holiness. All I need to do is think, focus, and channel my actions towards the service of my King.

Good Shabbos, Yosef

Anger

Noah · October 22, 2021

In this week's Parsha we are treated to a masterclass in overcoming anger, taught to us by Avraham and God.

Then the LORD said, "The outrage of Sodom and Gomorrah is so great, and their sin so grave! I will go down to see whether they have acted altogether according to the outcry that has reached Me; if not, I will take note."

Hashem has heard a wretched scream, either from a certain female victim or from the country itself, emanating from Sodom and Gomorrah and is enraged. The first thing God does when teaching us how to deal with our anger is, "I will go down to see…" Or HaChaim and others question this language. What does it mean that God has to descend to see his creations? Surely in the context of the infinite creator, up and down have little meaning. Rather, he explains, this is God informing us that he does not judge a person in comparison to his infallible self but instead takes into account the inadequacies of the sinner. "God does not literally descend to earth to gain knowledge He did not have, but God lowers His profile in order to give the defendant an additional chance to downgrade his sin."

The next part of God's response is also telling. "I will go to see whether…" does not sound like it is coming from an omniscient God. Rather we are being taught another lesson about dealing with the impulses of anger. We must always give others the benefit of the doubt. Furthermore, Rashi explains that this is God giving them an opportunity for Teshuva. While God knows what has already transpired, our free will allows us to make different decisions going forward and to rectify our ways. These points are essential in dealing with anger, extending the benefit of the doubt and always remembering that people have the capacity to improve.

Now Avraham gets a turn at the blackboard:

Abraham came forward and said, "Will You sweep away the innocent along with the guilty?"

Rashi, based on Targum Onkelos, explains that the words "Will You" (הַאַ֣ף) can be translated as "Anger" rendering the meaning of the verse, "will Your anger urge you to destroy righteous with wicked?"

This understanding reveals an important tool we have when we are angry or helping someone who is. Our anger is something separate from us. It is not a fundamental part of who we are but an external force that encourages us to overreact. Furthermore, Avraham shows us the main exploit anger uses to get us to react, throwing the baby out with the bathwater! When we are blinded by anger, we quickly restrict our thinking to extremes; everything is awful, this person is worthless, and it's all your fault! This juvenile thinking leaves no room for us to appreciate our much more nuanced existence. We must instead heed Avraham's advice and adopt his mindset, realizing that even in the Sodom and Gemmora's of our lives, there may be hidden goodness that we are responsible to find and redeem.

Next, we have an appeal to justice.

Far be it from You to do such a thing, to bring death upon the innocent as well as the guilty, so that innocent and guilty fare alike. Far be it from You! Shall not the Judge of all the earth deal justly?"

Once we have calmed our anger and changed our attitude, we can rely on the concept of justice to placate our anger. We live in a society of laws and norms that are meant to protect us and protect others from us. To avoid the Hobbesian state of nature, we have agreed to a social contract of rules and guidelines that preserve our individual freedom. Part of this contract is the mediation of disputes through a court system or system of justice.
