We cannot extrajudicially react in anger (or overreact as is commonly the case) to a perceived slight or there would be no end to conflict.

"In such condition, there is no place for industry; because the fruit thereof is uncertain: and consequently no culture of the earth... no knowledge of the face of the earth; no account of time; no arts; no letters; no society; and which is worst of all, continual fear, and danger of violent death; and the life of man, solitary, poor, nasty, brutish, and short." - Leviathan

We have been given step by step instructions on how to avoid the above mentioned hell and to deal with our anger constructively in this week's parsha. Upon receiving news that inspires ire: * Get off your pedestal, we all make mistakes, if an infinite and infallible God can take our faults into account we certainly can amongst each other! * Offer the offender the benefit of the doubt * Remember that this one episode doesn't define them, they are capable of change * Acknowledge that our anger is an external force that pushes us to overreact * Consider the complexity in every situation and remember rarely are things all bad * Have faith that a system of justice exists to help you achieve recompense

With these pointers from the Parsha I hope we are better equipped to deal with anger wherever it appears!

Shabbat Shalom,

Noah

Dirt and Ashes

Yosef · November 3, 2023

When Avraham prays for the salvation of Sedom, he excuses himself to Hashem for coming with so many requests. In his apology, he humbly notes that really, he isn’t worthy of speaking to Hashem in the first place, and he eloquently expresses this sentiment with a statement of incredible humility.

Before we go into what Avraham said, I think it is worthwhile to mention just how great Avraham was, although my depiction will not even be a drop in the bucket of his full stature. Avraham lived in a world devoid – completely and totally devoid – of any knowledge of Hashem. The entire world served idolatry and to even think otherwise was something punishable by death. And yet Avraham, based on his own thoughts and musings, was able to discover the truth of God and was bold enough to act on those convictions. Possessed of incredible oratorial and inspirational power, Avraham began to teach the masses about The Omnipotent God. He traveled from town to town and tens of thousands were inspired to change their lives and follow him. Additionally, he was tremendously wealthy and was famed and beloved for his incredible generosity. And to top things off, he had just won a magnificent victory against four mighty kings, defying all odds. To put it shortly, Avraham was the most respected, wealthy, and powerful person alive; he was quite simply on top of the world.

But Avraham didn’t see it that way. He spoke to Hashem, He who discerns even the slightest insincerity, and declared, “I am but dirt and ashes.”

The Beis Halevi explains the analogy. Dirt is something that has no past, it has never been formed into anything useful and it itself is almost valueless. However, dirt does have a future, since bricks and pottery are formed of dirt. Ashes, on the other hand, have no future. You can’t build anything out of ashes, but they do have a past as they once stood in the form of a proud tree.

Avraham said to Hashem that he is not just like dirt or just like ashes, but rather the hybrid of both – one with no past and no future – no redeeming qualities whatsoever. Now, Avraham certainly knew of his greatness; he knew of his impact on the world, recognized the advantages of his wealth, and acknowledged his prowess in war. Without knowing one’s qualities, one cannot accomplish anything in the world. So, what did he mean by such a statement? He recognized with utter clarity that all that he had was nothing but handouts from Hashem. His achievements, his wealth, his wisdom, his influence, his victories – everything was just the greatness of Hashem bestowed upon him. But Avraham, by himself, without the help of Hashem, was but dirt and ashes. Avraham had no personal past and no personal future; Hashem alone was the source of his every success.

In the merit of this statement, the Jewish people were given two mitzvos. They received the mitzvah of the red heifer, by which the ashes of the calf can cleanse the impure. And they received the mitzvah of sotah, where a woman suspected of immorality can be proven innocent by drinking a mixture of water and dirt combined with the name of Hashem.

The Beis Halevi beautifully explains the precision of Avraham’s reward. Because Avraham declared himself to be like dirt, without a past, he received the dirt of sotah which ‘has a past’ as it reveals what previously happened. Because he claimed to be like ash, without a future, he merited the ash of the heifer which ‘has a future’ as it purifies going forward.

The terrifying situation in Israel, with the lives of our people in grave danger, urge us to realize, as the progeny of Avraham, that we too are but dirt and ashes. We must pursue success and victory in every way possible, but we must realize that at the end of the day it is not in our hands. Personally, we have no past and no future, and that’s a very scary predicament to be in. But thankfully, we are not supposed to do it alone, but to graciously receive the blessings of God by acknowledging his ultimate control of it all.

Good Shabbos and may we hear only good news,

Yosef

Parshat

Chayei Sarah

November 10, 2017

When one door closes another door opens... "Well if that's the case, I'm not buying the car!" "The Life of Sarah," Uncle Jonathan's bar mitzvah parsha, opens with the death of Sarah, Avraham finding a burial space for her, remarrying Hagar and finding a spouse for Isaac. All seemingly mundane things considering the families recently turbulent path. Avram had just marched his son off to be sacrificed and the Midrash explains this is the very thing that killed Sarah, like any good mother- she died worrying about her son. Why don't we call the parsha "The Death of Sarah?

Sarah spent a lifetime traveling and spreading the word of monotheism side by side with Avram, despite her early inability to bear a child. Upon her death, many long-time goals were established in ways that we still see today. With Sarah's infertility, the Jewish population was running thin, to say the least, without her endeavoring Abraham's prayer that they begat Isaac the Jewish family tree would have ended. After birthing at 91 Sarah could not have lived to see Issac be the last of her lineage; that why in this very parsha Yitzchak marries Rebbeca, who our sages say had similar virtues of Sarah. Our parsha acts as an extension of a life whose story will be everlasting. Sarah's prosperity perpetuates at Maarat Hamachpelah as her burial plot and much more. This is the first time a jew acquires property in the holy land once again making the promise Hashem made into reality.

The gap between heaven and earth was so distant in their generation that Avraham is accredited for bridging it. He was known as the lone Hebrew or "Ivri" - literally "from the other side". He made it his life's work to oppose popular theory and push monotheism. Rashi says that Avram acknowledges his accomplishment while speaking to his servant Eliezer. After Avraham assures Eliezer that he will succeed in finding a wife for Issac he says: The L-rd, G-d of the heavens, Who took me from my father's house and from the land of my birth, and Who spoke about me, and Who swore to me, saying, "To your seed will I give this land," He will send His angel before you, and you shall take a wife for my son from there. Whereas in an earlier verse he called God by "the G-d of the heaven and the G-d of the earth." Rashi writes that Avraham was telling Eliezer that when he left from his birthplace to go to Canaan, God was only of the heavens; Because his name was not on the tongues, and his Torah not in the minds of the generation god remained bound away. Avraham managed to break away from idol worship and go off on a limb and open a new door. From the moment God tells him to leave his home, he is on God's mission totally binding himself and family to his goals and G-d. Anyone who was privileged to know our uncle grew an interest in understanding uncle, in many ways he was the lone Hebrew defining jews every kiddush Hashem along the way. The man from the other side only put himself there to grow. Uncle continues to live on like Sarah, but like god himself will only be in heaven if we do not hinge our lives to his will.

This week's parsha describes Avraham as old and advanced in years. We see other people called old or advanced in years but Avraham's maturity paralleled his age. His lifestyle moved with the calendar with his excitement toward the next good day, his growth was circular. Passing points of encounter, enveloping every practice. Yes we can learn from others and yes we can get ahead of ourselves - this is why we do not endure like our fathers. Our peak experiences, the ones we mention on Yom Kippur are only glimpses into outliers, to learn from ourselves we must take a step back and seek a smooth steady familiar path opening every door along the way. The early Avram was not familiar with god he went door to door questioning the sun, moon, and earth and found nothing remained. Through a driven routine, we find ourselves in uncharted territory, learning and growing.

Even Higher

Tali · November 2, 2018

“I'm too young.” “I'm too old.” “I'm way too busy.” “I just had a baby!” “Now is definitely not the right time.” We all have plenty – very legitimate – excuses why now is not the ideal time for us to involve ourselves in spiritual growth.

In this week's Parshah, the Torah eulogizes our matriarch Sarah, by saying:

“Sarah's lifetime was one hundred years, twenty years, and seven years; the years of Sarah's life.” (Breishit, 23:1)

Why doesn't the verse simply state that Sarah's lifetime was 127 years? Why must we split each group of years into its own number? The Ramban explains that each group surpassed the group preceding it – the last twenty years found her at greater spiritual heights than the previous hundred, and the last seven found her even greater than the previous 120.

What an incredible lesson! How often do we give up on ourselves and the potential greatness that we can achieve? How often do we tell ourselves “Oh, I'm too old, what can I accomplish at this point?” How often do we assume that the years in which we could have attained greatness surely have long passed? Yet, we learn from Sarah that such an excuse is not at all valid! At the age of 120, she continued to work on herself and grow to new levels of spiritual achievement!

The Ramban further explains that the seemingly superfluous use of the phrase “the years of Sarah's life,” is written to teach us that all of the years of Sarah's life were equally good. The phrase “equally good years” obviously cannot refer to her actual spiritual level, since that level was constantly changing for the better. Rather, the Chasam Sofer explains, the phrase refers to the diligence that Sarah applied to her spiritual growth. Sarah's years were equally good in that she approached every age and stage with the same excitement and zeal for growth. Every year of her life, from the time she was a child to the time she was an old woman of 127, she climbed the rungs of her spiritual ladder, climbing higher and closer to G-d. Her life was one of consistent and unrelenting growth.

The Chasam Sofer writes that the concept of continual spiritual growth is not relegated to only great people of the past, but is a fundamental requirement of every single Jew.

From the countless, inspiring stories that I've heard from Yosef, Uncle Yonatan exemplified this attribute of consistency in growth. No matter where he was, or what challenges came his way, he steadfastly continued to grow in his service of G-d. He never used circumstantial excuses to absolve him of spiritual obligations, and went to great extents to serve G-d in the most difficult situations.

Let's learn from Uncle Yonatan to follow Sarah's example and constantly work on ourselves, taking baby steps to climb the ladder closer to G-d, no matter what situations we are in.

Good Shabbos and warmest regards,

Tali

Proper Perspectives

Yosef · November 22, 2019

When a person arrives in a new town and is looking for a kosher place to eat, it becomes readily apparent just how seriously he takes his religion. A person who is marginally observant might be satisfied with a simple sign in the window reading 'kosher'. One who is more observant would probably insist on seeing an actual certificate from the local Kashrus Agency. And a truly devout man might not even be satisfied with that, but would rather ask the local Rabbi where the highest level of Kosher food can be found.

But let's turn the subject at hand to something more worldly. After grabbing a bite to eat, our friend begins looking for a venue in which to invest ten-thousand dollars. Could we imagine him seeing an ad in the paper with the simple statement 'guaranteed returns' and promptly wiring over the money? Of course not! Money is something everyone takes seriously and you can rest assured that our friend, no matter who he is, will not part with his hard-earned cash before doing some serious research about the trustworthiness and track-record of the said investment.

Unfortunately, this clearly illustrates that our spiritual well-being is something that we take much less seriously than our material standing.

So let's try to learn a proper outlook from the very best -- our forefather Avraham.

Avraham sent his slave Eliezer to search for a suitable match for his son Yitzchak. Now, Eliezer was not an ordinary slave but a man of incredible piety and trustworthiness. So much did Avraham rely on Eliezer, that he is described as the one who governed everything in Avraham's colossal estate. Not only that but when he was sent to find a match, Avraham handed him a document of ownership for everything, intended to show the value of the estate to the prospective match. Throughout all of this, with such a huge amount of money on the line, Avraham didn't ask for any sort of security -- he trusted Eliezer implicitly.

However, later in the same conversation, when Avraham expressed his desire that the match not be from the daughters of Canaan, we find something very peculiar. This was a matter that concerned the spiritual well-being of his son Yitzchak, as the Canaanites were known to be a people severely lacking in character. And so, after casually handing all of his worldly possessions over, he suddenly grew wary that something may go wrong. Now Avraham insisted that Eliezer swear to him that he would follow his directions and not take a Canaanite girl. For spiritual matters, only an oath would suffice.

Here we see what a proper perspective of life should be. Money and all worldly matters are things of temporal value, good for only a few decades and relatively unimportant in the grand scheme of things. But Torah and mitzvot are things of eternal value, things that can put us in good standing forever! So let us try to keep things in perspective and attach at least as much importance to our Judaism as we attach to our livelihood.

Good Shabbos, Yosef

Leading Through Loss

Noah · November 13, 2020

This week’s Parsha, Chayei Sarah, marks the death of our primary matriarch. In his essay on this week’s Parsha, Rabbi Sacks ZT’L describes Avraham’s reaction to the death of his partner:

“Sarah, his constant companion throughout their journeys, has died. He is 137 years old. We see him mourn Sarah’s death, and then he moves into action. He engages in an elaborate negotiation to buy a plot of land in which to bury her. As the narrative makes clear, this is not a simple task.”

When great people face tragedy, they respond with action. In this same piece, Rabbi Sacks contrasts the differences in detail between the purchase of Ma'arat haMachpelah and Yitzchak's hunt for a wife with the Akedah. While the former is described in elaborate detail, the latter leaves much up to interpretation. His explanation of the difference is that Avraham’s purchase of land and the betrothal of his daughter were fulfillments of covenants that he had already made with God and that they carry extra significance. When Sarah dies Avraham realizes that the legacy he was promised, abundant land and children, is far from reality. Others would throw their hands up at age 127 and absolve themselves of further responsibility. Lesser men would blame God for not upholding his end of the bargain and allow themselves to turn bitter. But not Avraham.

Avraham understood that a covenant isn’t a promise so much as it is a relationship. As Rabbi Sacks so eloquently explains, “God promises, but we have to act. God promised Abraham the land, but he had to buy the first field. God promised Abraham many descendants, but Abraham had to ensure that his son was married, and to a woman who would share the life of the covenant, so that Abraham would have, as we say today, “Jewish grandchildren.”

This perspective on covenant transforms a concept that can be used to buck responsibility, into one that mandates we do all we can to make God’s will a reality in our lives. This approach is both profoundly inspiring and intensely demanding. We are required to strive for Godly results while simultaneously being confident in human means of achieving them. This is the attitude of leaders. As Rabbi Sack explains, “That is what leaders understand, and it is what made Abraham the first Jewish leader. Leaders take responsibility for creating the conditions through which God’s purposes can be fulfilled. They are not passive but active – even in old age, like Abraham in this week’s parsha.”

Rabbi Sacks was among the Jewish leaders that had deeply internalized this teaching. Until months before his death, he was still writing, speaking, and teaching Torah. He was not afraid to wade into conflict when he felt it was necessary for the community and stayed incredibly active in Jewish life until his final days. His teachings reached millions and his reputation and renown were an unparalleled kiddush hashem.

Throughout this year that has been so full of loss Avraham’s response to the death of Sarah can be a guiding light. If we use loss to inspire us to achieve and to tap into our personal covenants with God we can strive to become great leaders as Avraham and Rabbi Sacks were, each in their own time.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Avi · 2021

Although the Parsha is called the life of Sarah it opens with the passing of Sarah. "Sarah's lifetime -- the span of Sarah's life -- came to one hundred and twenty-seven years." Avraham mourns and prepares for his own death when he buys a burial plot for Sarah, Maarat Hamachpela, the cave of doubles.

Avraham sets out to find a wife for Yitzchak; he sends his servant Eliezer to Charan to find a suitable mate. He instructs Eliezer to find a girl who will draw water even for his camels. When Eliezer meets Rivka she invites him home where he arranges the marriage with Rivka's parents. When they arrive home Yitzchak is out praying in the field; they are married and Yitzchok is consoled from the loss of his mother.

Avraham also takes a new wife, Hagar. He fathered five children with Hagar but Yitzchok remained his only heir. When Avraham himself dies he is buried next to Sarah in the cave of doubles.

Rivka follows in Sarah's footsteps and is the second matriarch of the Jewish people. Although she came from pagans in the desert she is like a rose among thorns.

When faced with hard things many of us freeze in place or wallow in our sorrow. Avraham mourns but he also acts quickly, making all the arrangements for him and his son Yitzchok to be prepared for his death at the end of the Parsha. This week's Parsha is Uncle Yonaton's Parsha. Uncle prepared me and the rest of us for a world without him. By instilling a love of lifelong learning and Jewish practice in all of his children Uncle Yonaton made sure the Torah he brought to our family continues to grow here forever.

Good Shabbos! Avi

November 9, 2023

This week's Parsha Chayei Sarah opens by recounting the years of Sarah's life -- 127 years -- and her burial in the cave of Machpelah, purchased by Avraham from Ephron the Hittite for four hundred silver shekels. The Torah repeats "the years of Sarah's life," and Rashi teaches the repetition comes to show us that all her years were "equal in goodness."

Eliezer is sent to Charan to find a wife for Yitzchak, with the sign being a woman who offers to water his camels. Rivka does so, is invited back to Canaan, and marries Yitzchak, who is comforted from the loss of his mother. Avraham takes a new wife, Hagar, and fathers six more children before dying at 175, buried beside Sarah by both his sons, Yitzchak and Yishmael.

This week's Parsha is Uncle Yonaton's Parsha. Uncle prepared me and the rest of us for a world without him. By instilling a love of lifelong learning and Jewish practice in all of his children, Uncle Yonaton made sure the Torah he brought to our family continues to grow here forever.

Good Shabbos! -- Avi

Parshat

Toldot

Adam · November 17, 2017

This week's Parsha includes the famous account of Yitzchak preparing to bless his sons. He sends Esav, the firstborn by birth, to bring delicacies back from the field before he bestows the blessings upon his son. We are familiar with the saga, depicting Rivka, the sons' mother commanding Yaacov to bring her food to prepare. She then dresses him in his brother's clothes and covers his hands and neck with fur so that Yitzchak, now blind at the end of his life, would bestow the firstborn blessings upon Yaacov, the younger son.

When we read this story as a fictitious tale, it seems like a classic story of family difficulties and trickery. Two brothers competing for their father's blessings before his death.

But the Torah is not a book fables, but a historical account, in this case leaving us with many questions.

First of all, the Torah tells us of the terrible moral state of Esav leading up to this point. With the Medrash depicting Esav as a murderer, rapist, and idolator, how is it possible that Yitzchak could be blind to his son's nature? He may have been losing his eyesight, but it seems that as a father, he would have some idea of the lack of morality his son was displaying. Why was he looking to bestow the priceless blessings on his devious son?

Furthermore, didn't he know that this wasn't Esav? Was Yitzchak, the brilliant sage, so easily fooled by some lambskin and clothing? He seems to notice that something is up; three times he questions Yaacov before actually blessing him. Was he truly convinced by this cunning disguise?

And in any case, if he blessed the wrong child due to trickery, wouldn't he be able to change this after the fact? The Jewish concept of a "mistaken sale" seems applicable. One is not simply able to keep the things he steals (or even those he is inadvertently given); if Yaacov was not supposed to receive the blessings, shouldn't Yitzchak have simply blessed Esav (and possibly cursed Yaacov for taking what was not his)?

To begin to understand this story I believe we need to understand the differences between Yaacov and Esav. Yaacov is described as an Ish Tam Yosheiv Ohalim, a man of completeness and simplicity, a dweller of tents (study halls). And Esav is an Ish Yodea Tzaid, Ish Sadeh, a man who knows weapons, a man of the field. Yaacov interacted primarily with ideas, staying indoors. Esav was out in the world, working fields and learning how to hunt.

Chassidus teaches that everything in this world progresses through stages. First, there is tremendous power and light that enters the world with insane force, called Tohu and then that power is restrained and contained so that it can be effective, called Tikkun. We see this on a small scale in the way that ideas are brought into reality, someone throws out a grand idea in a boardroom that is brilliant but crazy (Tohu) and others "reel it in" and contain it (Tikkun) until it is able to be a positive force for change. In the progression of childhood learning, we see a similar pattern. When a child first learns of a new concept it is all-consuming (Tohu) and only later is he able to contain it and utilize it for good (Tikkun). Most crazy ideas in the world are founded on something positive, they are just properly contained to be a usable force for good yet. Even communism, we can see is based on a beautiful idea of unity and oneness. But as long as it is in a state of Tohu, or uncontained potential, it is a destructive force, a light so bright that it burns or destroys. It needs to be restrained in the containers of Tikkun in order to be constructive.

Yaacov is referred to as a Neshama from the world of Tikkun and Esav a Neshama of Tohu.

The reason Yitzchak wanted to bless Esav, was not because he was blissfully unaware of what his child was doing. Chassidus teaches that he was very aware of what Esav was capable of. And it was this very awareness that drove him to want to bless Esav. Yaacov knew that what one Esav could accomplish in this world, ten Yaacov's could not! And he desired to bless him that he be able to utilize his brother's talents of Tikkun to contain the light and turn it into a positive force in the world. He wanted his sons to together join forces and bring the world to its ultimate perfection.

But Rivka knew, in her superior vision of the spiritual realm, that this was not to be. And she intervened in order to save both the future of the Jewish people and the rest of the world.

The sages say that on a deeper level, Yitzchak did know what was going on and the fact that he was able to give the blessings indicated that they were truly meant for Yaacov. This is why he wasn't able to "take it back" as Esav requested. They had reached their true destination and there the blessings remained.

May we all merit to see our potential as the beautiful, awesome light that it is and be able to find the proper containers for that light so that we can utilize it to help ourselves, those around us and ultimately the world to reach true Emes a knowledge of Hashem with the coming of Moshiach!

Love, Adam

Truth Above All

Yosef · November 9, 2018

Rivkah waited many years to have children. Yet when she finally conceived, she was distraught about some very peculiar behavior on her fetus' behalf. When she would walk by a study hall where Torah was learned the baby would excitedly kick and jump, as if pulled by the holiness of the place outside. Yet when she would pass by a house of idolatry the same thing would happen, demonstrating a deep desire for the evil found nearby. Not knowing what to make of such behavior, she went to a prophet to request an explanation. The answer she received was that this was not a single pregnancy but rather it was twins. One would be a great tzadik and one would be wicked. This explained the inconsistency – the righteous son was excited by Torah whereas the wicked son liked the hype of idolatry. This explanation calmed her and she was able to continue through the pregnancy, eventually giving birth to Yaakov and Esav.

What kind of consolation is that? Why was she suddenly so happy - wasn't there some very bad news here about the wicked son she was carrying?

In good Jewish style, let's answer that question with another. Yitro, the father-in-law of Moshe, had several name including the name Putiel. This name was given to him by virtue of the fact that, prior to discovering the truth of Hashem, he had fattened calves for idolatrous sacrifice. This is a hard explanation to swallow, because we have a rule of thumb that one may not remind a righteous person of his sinful past. Why, then, does the Torah refer to him with this seemingly disdainful name?

The answer is that Yitro did not only serve idols, but he fattened up the calves in order to serve them in the best way possible. Yitro was someone who gave his whole heart and soul to his beliefs. Yes, at the beginning his beliefs were misguided, but he left no stone unturned in his quest for the truth. Because of his complete dedication to the truth, he was inclined to fatten the calves for sacrifice – to serve those idols like no one ever did before. As he climbed to the top of each religion he eventually found its holes and that same dedication to the truth caused him to drop it all and search for truth elsewhere. It is this unbelievable character trait for which the Torah named him Putiel, because this was ultimately the reason he was able to finally discover the truth of Hashem.

In light of this explanation, we can understand why Rivkah was comforted with this seemingly ill report. Originally, she believed that she had only one baby and it was this same baby who seemed so drawn to Torah today and to evil tomorrow. This prospect disturbed her greatly because the incongruence demonstrated a total lack of dedication to the truth. Someone who can act one way today and the opposite way tomorrow is clearly unbeholden to any belief system. This apathy was something Rivkah could not bear because one who does not care for the truth is beyond help. Nothing she could do or say would ever make an impression on such a child. When she was told that this was in fact a double pregnancy and there were two different children with different leanings, this calmed her nerves. Now she understood that she would have a wicked son, but at least he was loyal. If he stood for his value system, however crooked it may be, there was hope of showing him the proper way and bringing him back to the truth.

In our society, apathy reigns supreme. In years gone past in was not at all uncommon to convince somebody of the truth of Judaism and watch them change their entire lives in order to follow that truth. Uncle Yonatan was a case in point. Kiruv was largely an intellectual field, which aimed to show the truth of Torah through logical reasoning. Now, such arguments are almost futile – you can convince someone from today to tomorrow that this is the truth but the answer is simply, “Who cares?” What hope can there be for a generation that gives no value and no loyalty to whatever the truth may be?

Let us examine our own lives and strive to see to it that our actions and lifestyles are indeed congruent with the truths that we perceive. Let us dedicate ourselves to the pursuit of truth and thereby we too will find the correct way in every facet of our lives.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Avi · November 18, 2020

In this weeks Parsha Toldos or Generations, Yitzchak and Rivka conceive after twenty barren years. Rivka's pregnancy was difficult because two nations struggled inside her. She gave birth to Esav and then Yaakov. Esav was favored by Issac for his hunting abilities while Rivka loved Yakov who was a dweller in the tents of learning. When Issac got old and on his deathbed he told Rivka to bring Esav, he wished to bless him before he died. Rivka brought Yakov to Issac and the old blind Issac blessed the second born with the first born blessing. While Rivka's deception was not that simple, it was deception nonetheless.

Rivka's deception teaches us when it is okay to pull a fast one, and circumstances where there may be a responsibility to withhold the truth. The rabbis instruct us to distance ourselves from lying because Just as liars sought out only the favor of humans, ignoring the all-knowing, God will not find favor in Liars. The Torah however only holds a man accountable for a lie that leads to a death. "Distance yourself from words of falsehood; do not kill an innocent or righteous man."

The rabbis are lenient on some liars and exaggerates. A man may lie to maintain his humility and his modesty, and the humility and modesty of others. For example he may downplay how good of a host someone is as to not overwhelm them with guests. He may tell his host his meal was delicious if it was sub-par and he may also tell someone that they are glowing when really they are just looking good. He may actively lie to obtain a debt owed. The Talmud relates A story of several rabbis that were traveling from one city to another. A rabbi who approached them thought that they had come to greet him. In such a case, the Talmud concludes, it is not necessary to correct the mistake. If someone has a terminal illness and simply knowing of it would bring their demise sooner there are those who believe it is important to not tell them. Lying to maintain or regain peace is a mitzvah, the high priest would mediate spousal disputes simply by lying and telling both the husband and wife that they each were sorry and asking for forgiveness.

Now that we understand the spectrum of lies it is needless to say we lie everyday, the rabbis remind us that even white lies should even be avoided so we do not come to say one thing and believe something totally different in our hearts. There are bad lies there are good but there is no lying to God.

Keep Climbing

Yosef · November 5, 2021

Sometime last year, Elon Musk, the billionaire CEO of Tesla Motors, received a notice that his net worth had just jumped, establishing him as the wealthiest man on earth. Musk responded with a tweet that read, 'How interesting – well, back to work.' Despite my nausea with the fact that such a person is still not satisfied with his wealth, I think there is something for us to learn from this highly successful businessman. Although he is wasting his energies amassing things he will never even enjoy, it is the insatiable drive that has driven his success which we can use to fuel our everlasting spiritual endeavors.

When Eisav was born, everyone together called him Eisav (עשו), which connotes something complete and fully done – a finished product. The reason for this unanimous naming was because Eisav emerged from the womb in a mature state with hair like a full-grown man.

Yaakov, too, was named after the state of his birth. He was born tugging at the heel of his older twin in an effort to win himself the status of the firstborn, and he was thereby called Yaakov (יעקב) – a form of the Hebrew word for heel (עקב).

This is the simple understanding of their names, but there seems to be greater depth here. We know that a name has tremendous bearing on the entire essence of a person and his destiny in the world. It would serve us well to understand the deeper meaning of these names and how they are connected with the respective destinies of Yaakov and Eisav.

Rabbi Simcha Maimon explains that Eisav and Yaakov were not so very far from each other at birth. Eisav in his own way shared equally the potential to become one of the forefathers. His natural talents and internal strengths were suited no less for greatness than his brother Yaakov, although the battles he would need to overcome were different than the battles of Yaakov.

What then was the deciding factor which catapulted Yaakov to the highest levels man could ever reach, whereas Eisav sunk so low as to having earned the title 'Eisav the Wicked'?

It all lies in the name.

As mentioned, Eisav came into the world with everything going for him. The son of Yitzchak. The grandson of Avraham. One possessing almost super-human strength and intelligence. One gifted with a soul of unspeakable greatness. But Eisav viewed himself as depicted in his name – complete and perfect, lacking nothing and with no need to accomplish more. He wanted to ride the wave of his innate greatness without putting in the effort to accomplish more. In his book, he had it made. Eisav, a finished product.

Not so Yaakov. Yaakov found himself gifted with all the same greatness and God-given gifts, but never was he satisfied. With his first breath, Yaakov was already striving for more, clawing at his brother's heel in an effort to secure the spiritual status of the firstborn. Yaakov never stopped, never rested on his laurels, and never thought himself to be a finished product. From his formative years as the quintessential yeshiva bochur, never leaving the tent of Torah, to his superhuman efforts in winning Rochel's hand in marriage, to his eventual battle with Eisav's protecting angel – Yaakov never stopped fighting, striving, and accomplishing.

This is the secret which defined the destiny of the two brothers.

Life has been compared to a treadmill. If you try to stand still, you can rest assured that you're falling back. The idea that we can just sit tight and enjoy the levels which we have been gifted, or even those that we have previously earned, is nothing but a fantasy. When Eisav tried to coast on the gifts of his birthright, he ultimately lost it all, falling even lower than one not possessed of those gifts could have ever fallen. Yaakov, on the other hand, started in the clouds but never stopped reaching for the stars. Ultimately, he rose to indescribable heights – to the point that his picture is depicted on the heavenly throne.

Sometimes we see two people who, in their youth, were more or less on equal footing and headed more or less in the same direction. Yet if you meet them years down the road you sometimes see a tremendous gap between them. One has far outdone his peer. This happens in many arenas, be it in spiritual growth or in corporate success. Very often, the differences between them are just like the differences between Yaakov and Eisav. One of them looked at his past accomplishments, patted himself on the back, and tried to ride the wave, whereas the other looked only forward. The one who thought to take easy street is ultimately left empty-handed, while his friend strives ever higher, never content with past achievements.

So let's take the example of those who know how to achieve success. Not just the Musk's of the world, who have shown what mettle it takes to make it but waste the effort on material pursuits, but the Yaakov Avinu's who have shown us what true accomplishment is and the key to getting there.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Achieving Immortality

Noah · November 17, 2023

And Esau said, “I am going to die, so of what use is my birthright to me?” וַיֹּ֣אמֶר עֵשָׂ֔ו הִנֵּ֛ה אָנֹכִ֥י הוֹלֵ֖ךְ לָמ֑וּת וְלָמָּה־זֶּ֥ה לִ֖י בְּכֹרָֽה׃

This moment when Esau agrees to sell Yaakov the birthright sets the stage for the most severe familial split in the Torah. What was Esau’s reasoning?

The posuk above provides an insight into his decision-making process. Esau’s anticipation of his own death is apparently the factor that made him acquiesce to Yaakov’s offer.

The Gemara in Sotah makes reference to Esau’s attitude in a very different context. While Pharaoh's daughter walked along the riverside to retrieve Moshe, the Torah tells us, “her maidens walked along.” The Gemara says that the word “walking” (holech) in regard to Pharaoh’s maidservants alludes to impending death just as Esau said, “I am going (holech) to die.”

ונערותיה הולכות וגו' א"ר יוחנן אין הליכה זו אלא לשון מיתה וכן הוא אומר (בראשית כה, לב) הנה אנכי הולך למות The verse continues: “And her maidens walked along [holekhot] by the riverside” (Exodus 2:5). Rabbi Yoḥanan says: This walking is nothing other than the terminology of going toward death, and similarly it states: “Behold, I am going [holekh] to die” (Genesis 25:32). שמות ב, ה: ותרא את התיבה בתוך הסוף כיון דחזו דקא בעו לאצולי למשה אמרו לה גבירתנו מנהגו של עולם מלך בשר ודם גוזר גזירה אם כל העולם כולו אין מקיימין אותה בניו ובני ביתו מקיימין אותה ואת עוברת על גזירת אביך בא גבריאל וחבטן בקרקע The verse continues: “And she saw the ark among the willows” (Exodus 2:5). Once her maidens saw that the daughter of Pharaoh was intending to save Moses, they said to her: Our mistress, the custom of the world is that when a king of flesh and blood decrees a decree, even if all the world does not fulfill it, at least his children and members of his household fulfill it, and yet you are violating the decree of your father. After the maidens tried to convince her not to save Moses, the angel Gabriel came and beat them to the ground and they died.

The Gemara is drawing a connection between the death delivered to Pharaoh's-daughter’s naysaying-maidservants and Esau’s mindset. What links these events? Esau would continue to live for years after he claimed, “I am going to die,” while the maidservants didn’t make it through the afternoon. What links these events and why was Esau so certain that he would die?

Rashi explains: אָמַר עֵשָׂו: מַה טִּיבָהּ שֶׁל עֲבוֹדָה זוֹ? אָמַר לוֹ: כַּמָּה אַזְהָרוֹת וָעֳנָשִׁין וּמִיתוֹת תְּלוּיִין בָּהּ כְּאוֹתָהּ שֶׁשָּׁנִינוּ, אֵלּוּ הֵן: שְׁתוּיֵי יַיִן וּפְרוּעֵי רֹאשׁ; אָמַר: אֲנִי הוֹלֵךְ לָמוּת עַל יָדָהּ, אִם כֵּן מַה חֵפֶץ לִי בָהּ שֶׁבְּמִיתָה Esau said, “What is the nature of this Service?” Jacob replied, “Many prohibitions and punishments and many acts involving even the punishment of death are associated with it — just as we read in the Mishna, (Sanhedrin 22b): The following priests are liable to death: those who carry out their duties after having drunk too much wine and those who officiate long-haired. He said: If I am going to die through it, why should I desire it?

Even if the birthright and the accompanying temple service were valuable in Esau’s eyes, he was intimidated by the risk to his own life. He was too afraid to commit to something important because he couldn’t imagine giving himself over entirely to the cause. Contrast this with Pharoh’s daughter who likewise was warned about the riskiness of her actions. Her maidservants took Esau’s approach and deserted what was right in favor of the safe route.

What is the result of risking life itself for our greater values and morals? We see that Yaakov, who was willing to shoulder the responsibility of the temple service and Bas Pharoh who put her life on the line for Moshe are both granted eternal life.

The Gemara in Taanis says about Yaakov Avinu: א"ר יוחנן יעקב אבינו לא מת Rabbi Yochanan said, “Yaakov our father never died”

And in Derech Eretz Zuta among the 9 people who, “entered gan eden alive” we find Basya Bas Pharoh: תשעה נכנסו בחייהם בגן עדן. ואלו הן: [...]ובתיה בת פרעה

When we elevate moral conviction above our material existence we can achieve immortality.

Shabbat Shalom Noah

Parshat

Vayeitzei

Yosef · November 24, 2017

Did you ever hear of the great ladder which stands right here on earth but reaches into the highest heavens?

In last week's Parsha, we read about Yaakov taking the blessings from his father. Now, Yaakov's brother Esav, who feels that the blessings rightfully belong to him, vows to kill Yaakov. Yaakov follows the advice of his mother Rivkah to flee to her brother Lavan's house. However, despite being Rivkah's brother, Lavan is hardly cut from the same cloth. Rivkah is the paradigm of sanctity while Lavan is one of the most depraved men in history; a swindler and idolater of the highest order. His house is a place of great tribulation for Yaakov, as it defies every ethic he holds dear.

As Yaakov sets out on this most perilous of journeys, he has a prophetic dream. In the dream, he sees a great ladder, rooted on earth, yet rising all the way to the heavens, with angels ascending and descending upon it.

What is the lesson of this prophecy, and what message was it meant to convey to Yaakov? Rabbi Shlomo Wolbe explains that this dream was meant to teach Yaakov, as well as all of his future descendants, that besides for doing all of the mitzvos, there is another task with which we are charged. That is the task of always climbing, always growing, and never but never resting on our laurels. A Jew can never look at himself and say he's satisfied with his spiritual level -- that there's no more need to grow. Rather, we must always continue climbing the great ladder of life in an eternal ascent heavenwards.

One may ask, isn't this is a lesson vital to be learned at the very beginning of life, as one starts his journey through the world? Why was Yaakov told this now, when he was already sixty years old?

The answer is that Yaakov was well aware of this concept long before his dream, but as he set off towards the spiritually depraved house of Lavan, this lesson took on new meaning. There was ample room to think that in the house of his father, Yitzchok, where there was such an abundance of godliness, it was necessary to constantly absorb all the growth that was so available to him. But here, as he entered into a spiritual wasteland, to the hands of one bent on destroying everything Yaakov stood for, here it would be sufficient to maintain the level where he stood.

It is for this reason that specifically here came the message that even under such duress, there can be no such thing as staying put. Even here, Yaakov was charged with continuing to steadily climb that ladder. Even against the most fearful tests, one may face in life it is imperative to ever strive for greatness. And even here the ladder still reaches the highest heavens, if one will only continue to climb.

But the lesson goes further. Not only can one achieve greatness despite being in a difficult situation but actually it is only through difficulty that one can attain true growth. Specifically in the house of Lavan was Yaakov able to reach his full and awesome potential. It is only through standing up to these great tests that Yaakov achieved such spiritual greatness as to have his image engraved on God's Throne of Glory.

There's a beautiful story that I always remember when thinking of this concept of growth through hardship:

Amir, an Israeli teenager who had recently become a baal teshuva, avidly attended Rabbi Shalom Shwadron's nightly class. Over time he became quite close with the Rabbi, and there was almost nothing in the world that could make him miss the class. It, therefore, came as quite a surprise when suddenly he missed the class for a few days in a row without any call to the Rabbi. Concerned, Rabbi Shwadron went to Amir's house one afternoon to inquire after him. Amir greeted the Rabbi sheepishly and fumbled out an explanation for his absence. He explained to the Rabbi that he was an avid soccer fan, and this week was the championship, being held in Israel. He said that he just couldn't bring himself to miss out on one of the games, but he assured the Rabbi that he would be back next week with all of his previous enthusiasm.

Rabbi Shwadron, known for his wit, asked the Amir if he could please explain to him what soccer is, so he could better understand its great draw. Amir excitedly told the Rabbi how there are two teams on a field, each one with a goal, and one ball which they each try to kick into the other team's goal. Hearing that the goal was quite large, the Rabbi said that it sounded far too easy to score. Amir told the Rabbi that it wasn't so simple because there was a goalie whose job it was to block any attempt to get the ball inside. The Rabbi laughed and told the boy confidently that he could easily score a hundred points. He'd outsmart the other team and go to the field when nobody was around; that way there would be no goalie and he'd easily win the game. Amir tried to hide his amusement and patiently explained that this would miss the whole point -- you can only score when the goalie is blocking the goal.

The Rabbi smiled and told Amir to listen what he himself was saying. In life, we have many things that we must accomplish. Sometimes we wish that we could achieve all of them through peace and serenity, living on easy street with it all coming our way. But that's just not the way things are. Next week, when the championship is over, the Rabbi explained, it will be easy to come to class. But if you wait until it's easy, you haven't gotten yourself anywhere at all. If you want to grow and achieve greatness, then it is specifically when it's difficult that you have to show your mettle, step up to the test, and fight tooth and nail to reach greatness. After all, you can only score when the goalie is blocking the goal.

Uncle Yonatan personified this great lesson. Throughout his life, he faced countless challenges which would have broken a lesser man ten times over. Yet Uncle knew the secret. He knew that in the face of pain and suffering, in the face of trial and tribulation, the only way to go is up. He knew how to utilize every test to strive higher and higher still. He taught us the power one man can wield and the incredible effect he can have on his whole world. I pray that we be able to learn his lesson and bring him pride, as we continue the path he set us on -- growing through our challenges and never ceasing to reach for the stars.

Good Shabbos, Yosef

Jacob's Ladder

November 16, 2018

This weeks Parsha “Vayeitzei” means “And He Left.” Yaakov leaves his hometown of Be’er Sheva to go to his uncle Lavan. On his way to Charan he has a famous vision which remains in the imaginations of all Jews. Alone and fleeing his birthplace Yaakov puts his head on a stone for the night. As he dreams a ladder connecting heaven and earth floated angels up and down to God's heavenly throne and back to earth. The basic perspective is a connection between heaven and earth. Earth, practicality, and physicality. Heaven, spirituality and holiness. Although heaven and earth are far, we have an opportunity at all times to connect the two. The zohar tells us that the ladder in yaakov's dream represents prayer.

Following this interpretation, the angels represent the words we say in prayer, we say them with purpose and they deliver our longing and dedication to Hashem. The angels descending the ladder bring God’s blessings to the whole round world. Our prayers resound through all divides. Prayer has an order, and steps to it, like rungs on a ladder each small upward motion, amounts to great heights. We start our morning service thanking God for returning our souls and not depriving us of life. By the end of Amidah we ask for the rebuilding of the temple and add our own supplication for personal help may it be financial, interpersonal, or really anything. By the end of prayers at the height of our service, we have approached the Holy Throne. When jacob awoke he saw this famous exchange of angels and knew what he had to do. Yaakov resolved to give ten percent of his money to charity. Yaakov said to god “of whatever you give me I will give you a tenth”. That simply Yaakov intertwined his financial fate with that of his religious practice. Since yaakov's commitment we have been donating a tenth of our wealth and in return we are reimbursed. I hope we all remember to say “thank you, and please” but kindly don't forget to say “please and thank you”. When we want God's help to get us something we start by recognizing what we have already received. “Modeh Ani” the prayer upon rising means - “I'm thankful” after we thank God for breathing life into our souls we can make greater pleas. The word for thank “modeh” also translates to “I admit” and “I Surrender” Yaakovs maariv service says please and thank you. The prayer Maariv AravIm means blessed are you who night's the night, or to be fair darkens the evenings. A sunset is as magical as sunrise in a blast of color we remember and foresee our time, watching the sun on the horizon, it seems perfectly timed for our viewing. The difference is when night falls we don't want to know if you're thankful, acknowledging, or throwing your hands up in surrender. We have had a whole day of that! The hebrew word erev is close to the english word eve, as in evening. The hebrew root erev means to mix together. God spreads light out over the darkness in the morning. Over the course of the day he mixes the light and the dark together in the evening we return to pitch black. A bracha for brighter tomorrows!

The Right Derech

Avi · December 6, 2019

In Parsha Vayeitzei, Yaakov leaves Be'er Sheeba to work for his Uncle Lavan as a shepherd. On his journey he sleeps with his head on a rock and dreams of a ladder connecting heaven and earth, he designates the place with an alter. When he gets to Charan to work for Lavan he falls in love with Rachel, Lavan offers his younger daughter to Yaakov in exchange for seven years of Yaakov"s servitude. At the end of seven years, Lavan switches the brides and Yaakov is swindled into marrying Leah. At the end of they're Sheva Brachos, a week later, Yaakov signs up for another seven years this time to marry his love Rachel.

Leah gives birth to Reuben, Simeon, Levi, Judah, Issachar, and Zebulun, also a daughter, Dinah. Rachel being baren offers her handmaid Bilha to Yaakov, Bilha gave birth to Dan and Naftali. Then Leah followed suit also offering her handmaid to Yaakov. Her name was Zilpah and she gave birth to Gad and Asher. Rachel finally baring children gives birth to Yosef.

Four wives, 13 kids, and 14 years later Yakov is ready to leave. However, Lavan offers him a deal, six more years of work in exchange for sheep. Yaakov continues to work for Lavan and when six years came he gathered his family and some sheep and snuck out in the night fearing pursuit. In Lavan's vengeance, he had a dream where God told him not to pursue Yaakov. A monument was made to mark their truce.

The commentators teach us that even though the forefathers lived before the giving of the Torah, they accepted the 613 mitzvot preemptively. The forefathers and other tzaddikim were also on such a personal level with their spirituality sometimes their derech crossed the boundaries allowed by the Sinai-Torah.

In Noach's dark time people were very corrupt, however, Noach did not just build an ark and do everything God commanded him, he also observed his own Noahide Laws which are later found in the Torah.

In Yaakov's dark time of being tricked by Lavan, Yaakov could not regard the law of not marrying two sisters, he had promised Rachel his love and keeping a promise is more important than observing laws that have not been commanded yet.

Living before the giving of the Torah, social contracts and communal morals were weak, the individual virtue is what guided people. Today we have the Torah and many other laws and social constructs that dictate our behavior, we all strive to stand out as someone with unique virtue but that is usually not our slice of life. Today we daven with a minyan and make our mark by contributing to groups of people that share our vision, Baruch Hashem we do not have to invent religion.

Shabbat Shalom, Avi

The Dual Identity of Yaakov Avinu

Yosef · November 27, 2020

The Midrash records a puzzling conversation between Yaakov and Leah, immediately following their marriage. Yaakov questions Leah's integrity, asking how she could have deceived him by saying that she was Rachel, to which Leah responds that he had also lied when he told his father that he was Esav.

At face value, this would seem like a petty argument between two children – a tit for a tat. It is obvious that such great people could never have engaged in such an immature squabble, and it behooves us to understand the deeper intent here.

Let us begin with another question. We know that already in the womb Yaakov was attracted to the study halls and Esav to the houses of idolatry, but this appears to run contrary to the basic Jewish premise of free will. How could it be that still before birth Yaakov was naturally righteous and Esav was naturally evil?

I heard a beautiful explanation from my Rosh Kollel, Rabbi Shmuel Fasman shlit”a. He explained that Yaakov is described as a 'yoshev ohalim', one who dwells in the tents of Torah, whereas Esav is deemed the 'ish sadeh', the man of the field. These descriptions bring out the calling of each brother and his potential to perform the will of Hashem – each in his respective domain. Yaakov, as the dweller of the tent, was charged with the service of aseh tov (doing good) by proactively pursuing Torah and mitzvos in their natural setting. Esav, however, was to serve Hashem in the field, representing the physicality of this world. There he was charged with the service of sur mei'ra (avoiding evil) by engaging and triumphing over the lusts and desires of this world.

Each brother was equally equipped and expected to reach the highest levels of righteousness, albeit from two opposing realms and through different approaches. This is the explanation of Yaakov and Esav's fetal attractions, each towards their own domain of service. Esav's urge to engage the house of idolatry was not a display of any innate evil, but simply a yearning to face his calling.

But on the fateful day when Esav came back from that proverbial 'field', he is described as ayef, worn out. This means that his reservoirs of strength had been depleted and he would no longer be able to fulfill his destiny as man of the field; he was already overcome by the very physicality which he was meant to defeat. When Yaakov saw that Esav could no longer play the part which was meant for him, he put a bid on Esav's firstborn right. In doing so, Yaakov effectively stepped into the shoes which Esav was meant to fill and acquired the additional role of being the man of the field.

At that point, Yaakov became personally responsible for the dual role of dwelling in the tent of Torah as well as conquering the physical world. This two-fold calling is apparent throughout the life of Yaakov, as he switched seamlessly between the two arenas, in quest of perfecting both. For example, Yaakov first spent fourteen years of uninterrupted Torah learning, the paradigm of a tent-dweller, and then immediately was thrust into a twenty-year sojourn in the idolatrous house of Lavan, where he engaged the 'field' of evil and handily triumphed over it.

We can now begin to understand Leah's position in the story. Our sages say that the older sister Leah had really been destined for the older brother Esav while the younger Rachel was meant for the younger Yaakov. This means that Leah's calling was aligned with that of the man of the field; it was her potential to vanquish the evil within the physical domain. But when Esav sold away his role, Yaakov additionally became the man of the field, thus redesignating Leah to him as the quintessential helpmate in this particular mission.

Thus, when Leah's integrity was questioned, she pointed to the defining point which had aligned her destiny together with Yaakov, when Yaakov had clothed himself as Esav. If we examine this facade with a deeper understanding, we can see that Yaakov wasn't wearing a mere Purim costume, but rather he had clothed himself in field-clothes as an expression of his newly acquired mission as the man of the field. Leah explained that since Yaakov had taken upon himself the part of Esav, whereby he was entitled to call himself Esav, she was henceforth destined for Yaakov. In this light, she had the right to call herself Rachel, the one originally designated for Yaakov, since Leah now shared that designation.

On a practical note, as the children of Yaakov we are all endowed with this ability to straddle both ends of the spectrum. The Jew is to live in the tents of Torah, meaning to be fully focused on the spiritual service of Hashem in Torah study, prayer, and introspection. But at the same time he is able and expected to engage the physical world, overcome it's tests, and ultimately prevail in elevating it to the service of his Creator.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Perseverance and Appreciation

Noah · November 12, 2021

ADAPTED FROM AN AUDIO SHIUR FROM A RENOWNED TALMUD CHACHAM

In this week's parsha we have the famous incident of Yaakov and the ladder.

Reb Yerucham Olshin says that the ladder is a parable to our individual obligation of continual personal growth, a separate obligation aside from our standard obligation to the mitzvot.

Yet this is seemingly a strange lesson to be teaching to a 77-year-old Yaakov. Yaakov definitely understood from his time growing up in Yitzchak's house and learning with Shem V'ever that you always need to be growing. Why is this lesson applicable at this point in Yaakov's life?

He answers that Yaakov was now confronted with the unique challenge of Lavan who wanted to uproot everything! In such a dire and difficult circumstance there is room for a mistake to be made. One could think that in such a situation it is enough just to maintain their current level of spiritual achievement and not necessarily continue to focus on growth. This is alluded to in the posuk:

וְשַׁבְתִּ֥י בְשָׁל֖וֹם אֶל־בֵּ֣ית אָבִ֑י וְהָיָ֧ה יְהֹוָ֛ה לִ֖י לֵאלֹהִֽים and if I return safe to my father's house—the LORD shall be my God.

On which Rashi comments:

IN PEACE, perfectly free (שלם) from sin, not having learned evil from Laban's ways

This seems to indicate that Yaakov was content to leave his association with Lavan just having not descended from his previous level.

That's why now this lesson is so relevant. Even in the bleakest and hardest situations, we are still obligated to strive for growth. And in fact, these are the circumstances where the most growth is possible!

The Medrash says that Hashem tested the Forefathers:

נסיון אחר נסיון, וגידולין אחר גידולין, בשביל לנסותן בעולם, בשביל לגדלן בעולם Test after test, opportunity for growth after opportunity for growth, in order to make signs out of them and to make them great.

Reb Yerucham Levovivtz says this is where we clearly see that it was through the trials and tribulations that our forefathers were formed.

Ohr Gedalyahu writes that when Yaakov was going to Lavan the Torah writes;

וַיִּפְגַּ֨ע בַּמָּק֜וֹם וַיָּ֤לֶן שָׁם֙ כִּי־בָ֣א הַשֶּׁ֔מֶשׁ וַיִּקַּח֙ מֵאַבְנֵ֣י הַמָּק֔וֹם וַיָּ֖שֶׂם מְרַֽאֲשֹׁתָ֑יו וַיִּשְׁכַּ֖ב בַּמָּק֥וֹם הַהֽוּא׃ He came upon a certain place and stopped there for the night, for the sun had set. Taking one of the stones of that place, he put it under his head and lay down in that place.

"וַיִּפְגַּ֨ע" implies surprise. On his way down to Lavan seeing the angels made a significant impression on him but after his time in Lavan's house the Torah writes:

וְיַעֲקֹ֖ב הָלַ֣ךְ לְדַרְכּ֑וֹ וַיִּפְגְּעוּ־ב֖וֹ מַלְאֲכֵ֥י אֱלֹהִֽים׃ Jacob went on his way, and angels of God encountered him.

This verse uses the same word as above (וַיִּפְגְּעוּ) again implying a surprise or seeing something of significance. But after the arduous stay with Lavan, it wasn't Yaavok impressed by the angels, the angels were impressed by him.

When it is time for Yaakov to return to Cnaan, he informs Rachel and Leah and they reply:

תַּ֤עַן רָחֵל֙ וְלֵאָ֔ה וַתֹּאמַ֖רְנָה ל֑וֹ הַע֥וֹד לָ֛נוּ חֵ֥לֶק וְנַחֲלָ֖ה בְּבֵ֥ית אָבִֽינוּ׃ Then Rachel and Leah answered him, saying, "Have we still a share in the inheritance of our father's house? הֲל֧וֹא נׇכְרִיּ֛וֹת נֶחְשַׁ֥בְנוּ ל֖וֹ כִּ֣י מְכָרָ֑נוּ וַיֹּ֥אכַל גַּם־אָכ֖וֹל אֶת־כַּסְפֵּֽנוּ׃ Surely, he regards us as outsiders, now that he has sold us and has used up our purchase price.

Reb Meir Simcha asks what Rachel and Leah mean when they say that their father has sold them. He married them off the same way as every father has for generations! What is their complaint? He explains that Yaakov really married Rachel and Leah with his years of work for Lavan. We ask in the Gemara Kiddushin, when someone labors for another does the credit for his labor accumulate daily or only once it is completed? This answer determines whether or not the promise of labor is a valid dowry for marriage.

If the credit is only owed at the end of the service, at the time he completes the work, then it is not a loan at all but a single lump sum that can be used like a ring or other object of value.

If the credit constantly accumulates, by the time the labor is done the laborer will have essentially given a loan to the contractor for every day's work since the commission of the project. And because a loan is not a valid method of betrothal, contracted work cannot be used for marriage. We concluded that the credit constantly accumulates. If so, how could Yaakov marry?

Reb Meir Simcha answers that there is a fundamental difference with a Jew. A Jew cannot be a permanent slave and females can only be temporarily enslaved to someone that is a suitable marriage partner at the end of the servitude.

So Rachel and Leah's complaint was that they weren't married like Jews should be! They were just sold as slaves!

At the end of the parsha Yaakov is told by Hashem to leave: וַיֹּ֤אמֶר יְהֹוָה֙ אֶֽל־יַעֲקֹ֔ב שׁ֛וּב אֶל־אֶ֥רֶץ אֲבוֹתֶ֖יךָ וּלְמוֹלַדְתֶּ֑ךָ וְאֶֽהְיֶ֖ה עִמָּֽךְ׃

Then the LORD said to Jacob, "Return to the land of your fathers where you were born, and I will be with you."

When Yaakov goes to inform his wives, however, he preempts telling them about God's command with summarizes the hardships they have to endure with Lavan. Rav Elya Lopian says from here can learn an important lesson about our perspective toward our service of God. The people who say that they are going to sacrifice all the pleasures in the world and give up on the good life have totally missed the point! The ultimate satisfaction is living a spiritually informed and meaningful life and those chasing meaningless pleasures are not achieving happiness in the least. That's what Yaakov is teaching us. Yes, Hashem gave us a command and we will definitely follow. But we can do it without feeling resentful or like it is a great sacrifice. We can look at all of the positive changes that will result from this command and move into this next phase with a full heart.

The Shelah comments on the posuk:

וְדוֹרְשֵׁי יי לא יַחְסְרוּ כָל טוֹב Those who seek Hashem won't lack anything good.

The verse doesn't say that those who seek Hashem will have a Cadillac. It says that those who seek Hashem will not be missing anything.

This is analogous to the man who walks into his friend's house, looks into his medicine drawer, and upon seeing it is empty becomes distraught. His incredulous friend has to explain to him that due to the family's consistently good health they don't need medications! Likewise, the tzaddikim are known to live in sparse conditions with just the essentials. This is not a sign of great need but a sign of so much contentment there is no need. These people don't need a Caddilac, they already are not lacking any good.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Parshat

Vayishlach

Noah · December 1, 2017

Each blade of grass has an angel that tells it to grow. - Zohar

This week's parsha begins with Yaakov sending angels to his brother. On this opening verse, Rashi comments that the angels were, "literally angels." This is a strange observation for Rashi to make. What is he pointing out to us that isn't immediately understood from the verse itself?

To answer this question we first have to understand what angels are from a Jewish perspective. Everything, from plants to animals to humans has a spiritual force that causes it to grow and express its full potential. A tree does not grow independent of the spiritual reality that exists around it, rather it grows because there exists within the spiritual reality a force that commands it to sprout, develop and eventually bear fruit. Additionally, angels are the guardians of the created world that we exist in. They represent the forces that we see, in very practical ways, around us.

Our forefathers throughout Bereshit facilitate conversation with God almost exclusively through angels, while Moshe does not use angels as his primary method of interaction with God. The difference in these communication styles can be attributed to the distinct roles that these characters played in our development as a people. Our forefathers were the seeds of spirituality struggling to germinate in a physical world. They strove to put out strong eternal roots that could eventually sustain a Holy People in a mundane world. Our forefathers were altering nature itself through their actions in order to carve out a place for the future Jewish People. They were responsible for building a physical vessel that would eventually be able to receive the Torah, our direct link to God, from Moshe.

Moshe's purpose was very different. He had the impossible task of bringing the Torah to the Jewish people. The Midrash famously relates the angel's concern with God's decision to "defile" the Torah by putting it in the hand of flesh and blood humans capable of error. Moshe responded in classic Jewish fashion by asking them a question, "The Torah says, 'Do not worship idols,' do you live among people who worship idols?" It is clear from this exchange that Moshe is showing that only a human being can elevate creation by infusing it with spirituality. Moshe would have never arrived at the point to argue with angels if his ancestors had not worked with them to make room in creation for spirituality to take root. While Avraham Yitzchak and Yaakov modified the physical world to accommodate spirituality through angels, Moshe could actualize this potential as a human.

With this understanding, Rashi makes a bit more sense. Yaakov didn't send out couriers, messengers or emissaries that were anything short of angels. These angels were responsible for showing Esau that things had changed, the physical world was fundamentally different now as the seed of spirituality had taken root and a holy nation had room to grow.

If this was the case and Yaakov's message to his brother was clear, we still must ask why Yaakov himself had to follow. Why wasn't sending the entourage of angels enough to diffuse the situation?

The answer to this lies in the nature of the relationship between Yaakov and Esau. Yaakov prays to God for deliverance "from the hand of my brother, from the hand of Esau." The battle that was being waged here was on two levels. Yaakov had to confront Esau both as an adversary who hated him but also as a brother who was close and dear. While the angels could demonstrate that the room for spirituality had been carved out, they could not demonstrate that this void had been filled. Esau was not ignorant of the existence of a spiritual realm. Our tradition maintains that he was spiritually powerful and knowledgeable. By being informed that a space between these two realms had been made for a special nation to inhabit he was not deterred. He still thought that he and his children could be that people. In the capacity of a physical adversary Esau was stopped by Yaakov's angel, but as a brother, the eldest brother with incredible potential, he remained committed to stopping Yaakov from filling the space of a chosen nation with his progeny. This conflict with Esau as a brother could only be resolved once Yaakov, a human endowed with the ability to bring holiness into the physical creation, showed that he had already created the family that would be the chosen people.

In my life I often blur the boundaries between what I can do and what requires angelic assistance. While the entities on high make room for development and even demand it, only a human can actually fill this potential. Our rich tradition and heritage provide us ample opportunity for spiritual fulfillment and growth but too often we expect divine intervention to not just provide the opportunity, but to also take full advantage of it. I am blessed with a family, a community and with Shabbos, and I pray that these opportunities for development only grow and deepen. But this week I am not praying that I will be a good father, husband, son, community member, and Jew. I am confronting Esau myself and showing him that I am here to fill the potential that was so graciously given to me.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

A Good Name

Keira and Amalya · November 23, 2018

At the beginning of this week's Parsha, Vayishlach, Jacob sends angels to Esau in hope of reconciliation, but the messengers report Esau is coming with 400 armed men. Jacob encounters the angel, the spirit of Esau, and wrestles with him and dislocates his hip. Jacob defeats the angel who renames him Israel which means "he who prevails over the divine".

The Torah does not fully change Jacob's name to Israel, but both Jacob and Israel are now used to refer to Jacob. As the father of the 12 tribes, Jacob contained within him the entire Jewish nation, therefore his ancestry is vital to the Jewish nation. It is said, “In the quest to create a dwelling place for G-d in this world, a Jew can operate in two ways: as Jacob or as Israel”. The word Jacob represents a Jew’s service while hindered by the world. Israel refers to a jew who serves G-d in a way that allows G-dliness to be revealed for all to see. Jacob started with the name Jacob because the name represents the way a Jew begins service to G-d. Only after he triumphed over Esau’s angel and returned to the land of Israel did he get the elevated status of Israel. The Sages say that naming a baby is a statement of her character, her specialness, and her path in life. For at the beginning of life we give a name, and at the end of life a "good name" is all we take with us. May we all strive to live up to our names.

Good Shabbos,

Keira and Amalya

The Miracle of Nature

Yosef · December 13, 2019

After a 34-year stint away from home, Yaakov finally decides to return to the land of Canaan. He remains fearful of a confrontation with his brother Esav, who has vowed to kill him. And of course, as soon as word gets out that Yaakov is coming home, it is none other than Esav, surrounded by an army, who goes to 'greet' him. In an effort to appease his brother, Yaakov sends a number of lavish and opulent gifts ahead, hoping that Esav will take the bribe and call off his murderous plans.

This all sounds like a very sound and logical way to avoid a conflict, but the Torah's wording throws in an element of the supernatural. Instead of saying that he sent the gifts with 'shluchim' or messengers, it says that he sent them with 'malachim' – literally translated as angels. And indeed, according to one opinion in the Talmud, these were no ordinary men but actual angels which Yaakov sent to be his emissaries.

This needs an explanation. We have a general rule to avoid the miraculous except when it is absolutely necessary. When a tzadik has a miracle performed on his behalf without a true need, it costs him dearly from his heavenly dividends. Why would Yaakov have tapped into the miraculous, at such a premium, when he could have sent ordinary men?

The answer, says Rabbi Moshe Feinstein, lies in a Talmudic story about Rebbi Chanina ben Dosa, which Uncle Yonatan used to constantly recount:

One Friday afternoon, he (Rebbi Chanina ben Dosa) found his daughter upset. He asked her, “Why are you sad?” She said, “Because I mixed up a jug of oil with a jug of vinegar and I poured the vinegar into the Shabbos candles.” He responded, “What's the matter? He who told oil to light will tell the vinegar to light.” And the candles burned the entire Shabbos until they were able to make havdala with that flame.

To Rebbi Chanina, burning was no natural and necessary quality of oil. He perceived that the only thing allowing the oil to light was Hashem's decree that it do so. It didn't have to be that way, it wasn't natural, it was simply a miracle which Hashem performs on a regular basis. With such a perception, he was no less astounded by this daily miracle than by an 'unnatural' miracle. Both are no more and no less than the will of Hashem.

Because Rebbi Chanina perceived Hashem's involvement in every 'fact' of 'nature' so clearly, an open miracle was no bigger an event. This, says Rabbi Feinstein, was Yaakov's perspective as well. He saw the hand of G-d in every facet of life and thereby felt no less fascination at the ability to send men than the ability to send angels. So why not? Because of his exalted level of belief in G-d, it cost no more to send angels than to send men.

With Chanukah just around the corner, we can use this to answer one of the most well-known questions about Chanukah. We know that the flask of oil which was found was enough to burn for only one day, and because it miraculously lit for eight days we have eight days of Chanukah. The question is that since there was naturally enough oil for one day it comes out that there were only seven days of miracles. Why do we celebrate eight days for a seven-day miracle?

According to what we have learned, we can answer very simply that the fact that the oil burnt for one day is really no more miraculous than the other seven. It is for this reason that our sages declared eight days of celebration, in appreciation of the miraculous reality of nature.

This is a lesson for all of us to realize that Hashem's will is the only thing powering the world and all of the 'natural' miracles which we witness all around us. Nothing has to be, nothing is by chance, and nothing but nothing is beyond His reach.

We're in good hands.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

2021

In this week's Parsha Yaakov sends messengers to his brother Esav ahead of his return to Israel. Though sent to make peace, the messengers return with news that Esav is approaching with an army of 400 men. Yaakov prepares three ways: for war, in prayer, and with a gift of hundreds of livestock.

That night, Yaakov moves his family across the river but stays behind, where he wrestles until dawn with a man our sages identify as Esav's angel. Though injured, Yaakov overcomes the angel and receives a blessing -- and a new name, Yisrael, "he who prevails over the Divine."

The next morning Esav meets Yaakov and embraces him; he tries to refuse the gift but Yaakov insists. Esav invites Yaakov to Seir, but Yaakov politely declines. Passing through Shechem, Yaakov's daughter Dinah is taken and violated by the local prince; her brothers Shimon and Levi respond with force against the city.

Yaakov is troubled by his sons' violence and does not condone it. He learns of his mother's death, and as he rushes home his wife Rachel gives birth to Binyamin -- but dies shortly after, buried in Beit Lechem. When Yaakov finally arrives home, his father Yitzchak passes away at 180. The Parsha ends with a list of Esav's lineage.

Good Shabbos!

Parshat

Vayeishev

Say What You Mean

Noah · November 30, 2018

This week's parsha, Vayeshev, introduces us to Yosef. We are told that he is Yaakov's progeny and quickly are acquainted with the precarious family politics he finds himself amidst. The Torah tells us, "וַיִּרְא֣וּ אֶחָ֗יו כִּֽי־אֹת֞וֹ אָהַ֤ב אֲבִיהֶם֙ מִכָּל־אֶחָ֔יו וַיִּשְׂנְא֖וּ אֹת֑וֹ וְלֹ֥א יָֽכְל֖וּ דַּבְּר֥וֹ לְשָׁלֹֽם" or, "And his brothers saw that their father loved him more than all his brothers, so they hated him, and they could not speak with him peacefully." Rashi is bothered by this verse. He says, "From what is stated to their discredit, we may learn something to their credit, that they did not say one thing with their mouth and think differently in their heart." Rashi seems to be extolling the virtue of authenticity even when this leads to hatred and resentment.

On the other hand, the Kli Yakar learns a similar lesson from the exact opposite perspective. Bothered by the redundancy of the bolded word in the posuk, "וְיִשְׂרָאֵ֗ל אָהַ֤ב אֶת־יוֹסֵף֙ מִכָּל־בָּנָ֔יו כִּֽי־בֶן־זְקֻנִ֥ים ה֖וּא ל֑וֹ וְעָ֥שָׂה ל֖וֹ כְּתֹ֥נֶת פַּסִּֽים" or, "And Israel loved Joseph more than all his sons, because he was a son of his old age to him, and he made him a fine woolen coat." The extra, "to him" begs to be explained. The Kli Yakar first explains what behavior is characteristic of a "son of old age." He describes a maturity and thirst for the previous generations wisdom. Separate from the other children, this gem cleaves to his parents and behaves like a man among children. The Kli Yakar explains that Yosef did behave in this manner but only to Yaakov.
