This is what the extra word is coming to teach us. Yosef's behavior was not always as refined and sophisticated as it was when Yaakov was watching. This duplicity is regarded as a critique of Yosef's character, notwithstanding the grim sequence of events that would be triggered by his sibling's outsized reaction.

The commentators seem to be establishing legitimacy for the abusive brothers through claims of authenticity while heaping criticism onto the victim. What can we learn from this emphasis on the authentic even when it runs counter to our moral code?

To answer this question, we must understand how deeply our convictions must run. Judaism is not a religion about the cosmetic. Our rituals and practices are designed to not just elevate what we do, but who we are. When we are insincere and assert sets of ideas and beliefs because they are easy or politically expedient, we undermine the core of our religion. This is true to such an extent that even when mistakes are made through sincere intentions we are willing to extend praise. And, when chicanery leads to a pit, we are willing to offer critique. How fortunate we are to have a system of beliefs that encourages us to be true to ourselves in the pursuit of god and self-development. This coming week we should all merit to continue on our paths of sincere authentic connection, even if it is a struggle.

Shabbat Shalom

Noah

P.S. The posuk says, "וַיִּ֨קָּחֻ֔הוּ וַיַּשְׁלִ֥כוּ אֹת֖וֹ הַבֹּ֑רָה וְהַבּ֣וֹר רֵ֔ק אֵ֥ין בּ֖וֹ מָֽיִם" meaning, "And they took him and cast him into the pit; now the pit was empty there was no water in it." Rashi explains, "Since it says: "now the pit was empty," do I not know that there was no water in it? For what purpose did the Torah write, "there was no water in it"? [To inform us that] there was no water in it, but there were snakes and scorpions in it."

How did Rashi know that there were snakes in the pit? I heard in the name of the Vilna Gaon that it is hinted to by the very next word and its cantillation mark. The next verse begins with the word, וַיֵּֽשְׁבוּ֘ with a squiggly line over the last syllable. While this word means, "and they sat down" it can be broken down into two separate words, וַיֵּֽשְׁ בוּ֘ meaning "There was in it." If you are wondering what was in the pit simply look at the squiggly cantillation mark and there is your snake. Shout out to Yehoshua Krasnow who has been following Zichron Yonaton and who taught me this dvar torah.

Festival

Chanukah

Yosef · December 15, 2017

During the Greek occupation of Israel, they enacted countless decrees targeted at uprooting the Jewish religion. The last straw was when they stopped the Jews from their service in the Beit Hamikdash, the holy temple. Then, a handful of Talmudic scholars, men of the book and not of the sword, decided to wage war against the might and majesty of the Greek empire. In a stunning miracle, these few Rabbi's managed to defeat the Greeks and to regain possession of the temple. To their dismay, they found that all of the pure oil used for lighting the menorah had been defiled, except for one small jug which would only suffice for a single day. Since the process of making new oil would take eight days, they were left with no way to restore the temple service. Still, they lit what oil they had, fully expecting it to go out after a day. Miraculously, that small jug burned for eight full days until they were able to replenish their stock. In thanksgiving for this great miracle, the holiday of Chanukah was established, when every Jewish home lights the menorah and relives the miracles of yesteryear.

The question we must ask ourselves is that the temple was filled with miracles every day of its existence for close to eight-hundred years. There, a heavenly fire descended each day upon the altar, the showbread remained fresh and piping hot for the entire week that it sat on the table, and the entire Jewish nation found enough room to prostrate themselves in the temple courtyard, to list but a few. In fact, there was even a daily miracle quite akin to that of Chanukah -- every day the western candle of the menorah, which had the same amount of oil as the rest of the candles, would be lit first and would continue burning long after the others. For all of these miracles, we don't find that any holiday was established in commemoration. Our sources tell us that the reason these are not celebrated is that we only celebrate miracles on which the existence of the Jewish people hinged. The miracles of Passover created our nation, those of Sukkot ensured our survival in the desert, and those of Shavuot defined us as the nation of the Torah. All of the aforementioned miracles, great as they may have been, were not necessary for our survival and we, therefore, do not make any special remembrance. Why then was a Chanukah created in memory of the oil which burned for seven days, couldn't we have very well survived if the temple service would have waited another week?

An answer to this question lies in the historical setting of the story of Chanukah. This was at the end of the Second Temple Era, right after prophecy had ceased to exist. Until then G-d's presence in the world was obvious for all to see -- prophets communicated His will directly. Now, the era of prophecy had just come to an end and the world was plunged into darkness, into a time when G-d hides his face and cannot be seen by the untrained eye. Understandably, this was a very dark time for the Jews, as they did not know how to proceed in their relationship with G-d.

It was at this time that G-d performed the miracle of Chanukah and proclaimed to the Jewish people that even in the darkest times, He was still there with them, lighting the darkness to show them the way.

This message was absolutely essential for our nation to survive and thrive in this new era of exile, as spirituality became veiled and hidden. Now it would only be possible to carry on with the firm knowledge that even when we cannot feel our loving Father's embrace, He is nevertheless watching over us and guiding us through the darkness.

An American businessman was once vacationing in Israel. He entered the Sbarro pizza shop in Jerusalem to grab a quick bite to eat, anxiously checking his watch so he shouldn't miss his bus. The man in front of him, a total stranger, noticed that he was in a rush and insisted that he take his place in line. The businessman thanked him and gladly stepped ahead, relieved that he would catch his bus after all. He exited the pizzeria with his pizza in hand and just as he emerged, the whole store went up in flames -- the result of a terrorist attack. Meanwhile, the man who had so graciously switched places with him was still inside and was severely injured by the bombing. He accompanied his unlikely savior to the hospital, sitting by his bedside and praying his heart out that this kind man be saved. It was a long healing process, and the businessman soon needed to return home. As he departed, he told his savior that he owed him his life and that he should let him know if there was any way that he could help. A while later, the businessman received a phone call from the son of his savior, informing him that they were coming to New York for an operation. They requested that he be there during the procedure. The businessman readily took off work and accompanied them to the hospital, glad to be able to somehow help out the man who had taken his place in the whole ordeal. During the operation, the news came in that the World Trade Center, where he worked, had been destroyed in the 9/11 attack.

This story really portrays how G-d is there with us even in the darkest of times. Even in the midst of tragedy, when the whole world is up in flames, there too G-d is orchestrating everything with precise attention to detail. We cannot know G-d's reasons for allowing tragedy to strike, but we can know beyond a shadow of a doubt that he has our best interest in mind and that he's holding our hand throughout it all.

So this year, as we light the Chanukah candles, let's take with us this message, that even when our lives are shrouded in darkness, even when things seem bleak and hopeless, even then God is lighting our path and leading us exactly where we need to go.

Good Shabbos and Happy Chanukah, Yosef

Illumination and Education

Adam · December 7, 2018

Chanukah! A time for light! A time for family! Latkes, dreidels, and fire! Every pyros second favorite holiday (after lag baomer)!

But what is it really about?

One jug of oil that lasted for eight days? The Chashmonaim’s defeat of the Assyrian Greeks?

Uncle Yonatan was the first to teach me that the great sages of the time actually didn't establish the holiday until the year after the miracles. Strange that they had to wait a full year to give the Jewish people the most celebrated holiday even amongst the most secular Jews today.

What does Chanukah mean anyway?

Actually, the word Chanukah derives from the word chinuch, to train. It's the same word we use when referring to teaching children mitzvos. Only in this case, it is referring to “teaching” or “training” the Beis Hamikdash, the Holy Temple.

It’s a funny word to use really. To teach would be lilmod, from the root lomed, to learn. But chinuch refers to something else. To training something or someone into its full utilization of potential. If you ”taught” a child math, that would be lilmod, to teach. But when you teach a child mitzvos, that's chinuch, to train the child into his or her full utilization of potential.

This is why chinuch is something we daven, or pray, constantly to be able to give to our children. Teaching a subject is not nearly as difficult as being mechanech, the verb of chinuch.

In order to be mechanech, we must continually demonstrate how to connect to Hashem, we must take into consideration the age and level and even the unique attributes of the one we are training, and we must daven that Hashem bless our work and allow us to reach into the depths of our child and help him reach his full potential. A very difficult job indeed.

The sages waited a year because of an important idea in Jewish thought, the “endless spring of time”. Just like a spring circles constantly back to the same longitudinal point, but every time at a different latitudinal layer, the Jewish calendar circles around and around but each instance is capable of taking us up to a new level.

The sages waited to see if they could feel the energy of the previous year's events or not. If they could, it would become a holiday for the ages. If not, we could commemorate what was, but it wouldn't become the holiday Chanukah that we have today.

So, embedded in this time of year are unique spiritual energies that every one of us can tap into. And our great Rabbis gave us hints of what we can uniquely access at this time. One of those hints is in the name of the holiday.

Chanukah is a time that we can be mechanech, we can train into potential-utilization.

It is a wonderful thing that Jews around the world, bring their children, even from a very young age to light the candles and experience the mitzvah year after year.

But there is another layer we must address. What of our own potential? Can we use this time to be mechanech ourselves?

Actually, our sages say that after our parents finish their job, so to speak, it falls on us to be mechanech ourselves. Once we fly the nest, the responsibility of our own training falls squarely on our own shoulders.

This time is a beautiful time to train ourselves to connect. And Shabbos Chanukah, is a beautiful opportunity to connect to Hashem (our true purpose here in this world). Using a hammer to hold open a door, would be teaching the hammer to do something new. But using a hammer to bang in nails, is chinuch. When we deal with our physical needs, which we surely must do, that is teaching ourselves. But when we train ourselves into our true purpose of connecting to the infinite, that is chinuch.

May we see the beautiful light of Chanukah, and use it to be mechanech those around us at this time of year. But even more so, may we merit to be mechanech ourselves into the greatest purpose we have! Loving connection with Hashem!

A Happy Chanukah and a Great Shabbos! With love, Adam

Channuka

Shira · December 27, 2019

Happy Chanukah to all! What a beautiful time of year! The festival of lights has been illuminating every Jewish home for millennia, and as the days pass and the number of candles increase, we feel more and more empowered by the Menorah and what it represents. We can learn a lot about what Chanukah is here to teach us by examining the different laws in conjunction with lighting the Menorah. We light the candles just after the sun has set. Ideally, we place the Menorah no higher than 40 inches off the ground.

The fight against the Babylonians seemed impossible to win. It started off with just five brothers against an army of thousands. Both once they had one victory, many more join the cause and they conquered their enemies and held on to the Holy Temple. Chanukah always comes out in the winter months.

The date that we beginning celebrating is the day the war was over. The day of Chanukah literally means "They rested on the 25th day (of the month)". The fight was over, but now it was time to rebuild. with the Holy Temple in such disarray, it seems like an impossible task. But that tiny jug of oil lasted for 8 days instead of just one and encouraged the Jewish people that Miracles can happen. It also takes place during the reading of the Torah Portion of Vayigash, during which the family of Yaakov goes down to Egypt, marking the beginning of the long and painful Exile.

It seems to be the most bleak and dour time of year. Yet, there is a light. The light is the promise that we won't be in exile forever and that there will be salvation. All it takes is one small light and darkness vanishes. From one light, it doesn't take any extra effort to create a second light and it doesn't decrease or diminish from the first one. In fact, even if one explicitly states that no one may light their candle off of his, it is not considered stealing or a sin to do so and you have not wronged him. That is the power of fire. It is the power of hope, happiness, and hugs. When you share it, when you give it, you are both stronger. When you have the tiniest flame, you have the potential to light up the whole world. Uncle Yonatan was known for being a ray of sunshine. He walked into the room and greeted everyone by name and with a smile and a hug. The sun lights up the whole world. But sometimes, it's dark outside; the sun is not ready to rise. And that is when you light a candle. Uncle Yonatan was also that candle, that beacon of light that assured us that there was good in the world, good people, kind souls, honest and thoughtful, who are there just to provide light where there is none. Although we miss him terribly, just remembering what a special person he was, brings light to our hearts and hope to every soul. May his memory be a blessing.

Shabbat Shalom, Shira

Avi · December 10, 2020

Other than the fact that Chanukah paraphernalia can be bought at Hobby Lobby, there is something else that makes Chanukah feel very different from other holidays. On Chanukah we can go to work, on the shalosh regalim and high holidays we stay home and have Yom Tov. Keeping Yom Tov is prescribed by the Torah to help us safeguard miracles and pass them on to future generations. There is no Yom Tov prescribed for Chanukah or Purim at the same time the Rabbis tell us that the light of Chanukah and celebration of Purim will overshadow all other holidays in the messianic era.

Neither Chanukah or Purim are mentioned in the Torah, they were both universally accepted by Jewish communities long after the Torah. On holidays like Yom Kippur, just like Shabbos a divine energy enters our presence and through observing Yom Tov and Shabbos we gain something we could not have on any other day. On Chanukah we light candles to remember that the Greeks defiled our oil and forbid our observance, and despite their elephants and archers we fought for our place and won.

Being a Maccabee and waging war with the Greeks was nothing short of suicidal, these Jews acted beyond logic and reason This is why no Yom Tov is observed, the motivation of the Macabee is fully beyond our comprehension, even ceasing work won't help us understand.

Simply by the fact that Chanukah is observed on weekdays one may think that somehow the miracles it commemorates are somehow less significant. In Fact the opposite is true: the light of Chanukah is bright enough to penetrate even the mundanity of work. The Light of Chanukah is unavoidable, even at your local King Soopers and Wal-Mart.

Shabbat Chanukah

Shira Freedman · December 6, 2021

As we approach the final days of Chanukah, one might feel compelled to ponder what it is we have gained from this festive week. It's been quite an ascension, steadily increasing the number of lights as we have worked to compound illumination in our homes.

One of the reasons we light the menorah is because of the Torah commandment, Pirsumei Nisa, publicizing the miracle. By igniting the chanukah lights in our windows, doorways, and in some communities, outside, we bring attention to the glorious salvation Hashem granted us in those days at this time. People may inquire about the custom of lighting the menorah and we eagerly educate them regarding our victory in war and the jug of oil that lasted for eight days.

Acknowledging that the opportunity to spread the word of the miracle is a key reason for lighting, one may ask a question.

What about an individual who lives in a remote location, or who is only able to light long after anyone is around to witness? Is he still obligated to light the Chanukah candles?

We are told that he still meant to kindle the Menorah, for the one he is publicizing the miracle to is himself.

But if he is lighting the menorah, surely he is already aware of the miracle, so it seems superfluous for him to do the action.

This question clues us in to an important component of Judaism not just in regard to lighting the Menorah, but every commandment and custom.

Hashem doesn't want lip service. He doesn't need our light. He is the one who created light. There is nothing we can do for Hashem that He can't do for Himself.

What Hashem wants is the one thing that we alone can offer: our hearts.

Our obligation to bring light into the world is not a physical flame that is required. It is the light of our souls and the fire that we feel when we choose to do something, even when it's hard. It is the responsibility to focus our attention on our own growth and constant improvement.

We light the Menorah to inspire ourselves and to remind ourselves that all it takes is one little spark to banish darkness.

May we all merit to see the Light of the Truth in our times, and may we be so fortunate as to be the ones to light it.

Have a wonderful Shabbos!

Shira Freedman

Parshat

Miketz

The Present-Day Famine

Yosef · December 18, 2020

The Pharaoh had been up for nights, tossing and turning with dreams of wheat and cows. He knew these dreams must have a meaning, but he just couldn't find an interpreter that would assuage his worries. Finally, Yosef was recommended, taken out of jail, and brought before the king. He provided a clear explanation, addressing every point and even was divinely informed of the pieces Pharaoh had changed or left out. He didn't even make do with a dry bones explanation of the dreams themselves, which foretold of seven years of abundance followed by seven years of terrible famine, but even outlined a plan of action. He advised that someone be appointed to take a store for the hard years while the land was still plentiful, thus guaranteeing an economic stimulus package to last the people of Egypt through the famine. Pharaoh, understandably amazed at the young man's wisdom, immediately appointed Yosef himself to carry out the plan, explaining the promotion by virtue of the fact that 'there is surely no one as wise and understanding as you.'

The question that begs to be asked is that this position doesn't seem to be one that requires the most wise and understanding person in the land. Nowhere in the job description was there any expectation to advise, set laws, or otherwise make sensitive decisions. It was a simple, albeit critical, management position which required command and execution, but seemingly not so much wisdom. Why was Yosef's great wisdom so critical to his role?

The answer is that to properly fulfill the task at hand, the appointed party would need to perceive in vivid clarity throughout the seemingly endless years of plenty that there would come a time when not a grain would be found. He would have to skimp and scrape not to waste a morsel even when food was abundant beyond anyone's wildest dreams. This ability to see seven years down the road as if it were right in front of him, to act in a way incongruent with the present because of knowledge of the future, and not to be lulled by today's affluence into forgetting about tomorrow's poverty, are things possessed only of the wise.

In our lives as well, there are figurative times of plenty and times of famine; times of tremendous opportunity for growth and accomplishment followed by times when opportunity is scarce.

For example, youth is the years of plenty, when one is full of vigor and has little responsibility on his shoulders. In one's youth, he is free to pursue all of his personal goals and he has the strength and vitality to attain them. Later, hopefully, a person is laden with the responsibilities of a family and the need to make a living; things which, although certainly a worthy obligation to carry, make personal achievement something much more elusive.

But don't worry if youth has passed you by, there are many more times of plenty if one opens his eyes, some big and some small. Retirement is certainly an opportunity, when one is freed of the time constraints of work but is still of able body and mind. Even old age can be a time of plenty, when one is unencumbered by many physical pursuits and able to turn inward and achieve greatness of heart and soul. And there are many others, too many for this author to think of or to enumerate, but each person can surely find them in there own lives.

In truth, though, with all the ups and downs in our lives, the overarching time of plenty encompasses our entire sojourn in this world – as long as we are still outside of the grave. This world is our only chance to accomplish, to perfect ourselves, to perform mitzvos and study the Torah, and to earn the immense and eternal reward of the World to Come. When this life ends, there will be a 'famine'; a time when we will enjoy fully every mitzvah which we have amassed but in which we will be completely and utterly unable to accomplish anything more.

In fact, when we enter a graveyard, we hide our tzitzis in order not to cause pain to the deceased. If they would see the simple strings hanging from our garment, with which we can so easily earn untold reward, they would be eaten up with jealousy for the opportunities which we are afforded every day that we are counted among the living. Even the great Vilna Gaon, while lying on his death bed after a life of tremendous accomplishment, held his tzitzis longingly and lamented the fact that he would soon pass from the world in which the opportunity for eternal reward could be had for just a few dollars.

So let us not be so short-sighted and obtuse as to be fooled by our times of plenty into ignoring the inevitable famine which is sure to follow. Let us, like Yosef, see in vivid clarity how the achievements and accomplishments which are today so readily available, will one day be beyond our grasp. And let us be spurred on by this perception not to squander a single day or a single chance, but to spend every moment of our oh-so-short lives attaining greatness, amassing true wealth, and ultimately meriting eternity.

Good Shabbos, Yosef

December 13, 2023

Parsha Miketz tells the story of Yosef's rise from imprisonment to kingship. As a young man Yosef dreamt of himself as a celestial body with his siblings bowing to him; jealousy led his brothers to sell him into slavery. In Egypt, his skill interpreting dreams -- first for fellow prisoners, then for Pharaoh himself -- lands him second to the Egyptian throne.

The Rambam's Eight Chapters notes that in dreams the imagination allows for images impossible in waking life -- a boat floating in the sky, an elephant through the eye of a needle. Yet in English we also use "dreams" to speak of our hopes for the future. How could there be a greater contradiction? We do not simply stumble into our life's path; it takes real interpretation to refine our dreams and direct them toward something tangible.

A famine strikes Canaan, and Yaakov sends his sons to Egypt for grain, not recognizing that the powerful Egyptian official before them is Yosef. He accuses them of spying, takes Shimon hostage, and later plants a silver goblet in Binyamin's sack to test his brothers' loyalty.

In this trying time, many of us carry contradictions in our own dreams -- we want peace but at what cost; we love Israel and we hate war. I pray that someone will make the right interpretations and plans to steer us in the right direction, that those in power will seek counsel from those beneath them, and that we remember all of Israel is responsible for one another. May we only have good dreams.

Parshat

Vayigash

Rebuke and Refocus

Yosef · December 14, 2018

After months of drama, accusations, and blackmail, Yosef finally reveals himself to his brothers. “I am Yosef. Is my father still alive?” With this sudden realization, the brothers recoil as if bit by a snake, unable to say a word.

On this the Medrash comments, “Woe is to us for the day of judgment, woe is to us for the day of rebuke. Yosef was from the youngest of the brothers, yet they could answer when he rebuked them. How much more so when Hashem will judge each person according to who he is, that we will be unable to answer.”

The Beis Halevi asks a famous question. The entire preceding discussion between Yosef and the brothers centered around their father's well-being. The brothers were begging him to spare Binyamin so as not to cause distress to their father Yaakov. Where, then, was there room to turn around in the middle of the conversation and ask if Yaakov was still alive?

The answer is that this was not really a question, but rather an accusation in question format. With this short sentence, Yosef was showing them the incongruence of their entire life story. Here they were, begging and pleading that Yaakov could not bear to be separated from his precious son Binyamin. They went on and on about how unthinkable it was to cause pain to their elderly father. To that Yosef responded, “I am Yosef.” I am the beloved son of Yaakov that you yourselves sold into slavery. It is me whose death you staged, devastating our father for the last twenty-two years. “Is my father still alive?” How is it that he could possibly survive such intense grief after the story you've spun of his inability to be separated from Binyamin?

With these short but stinging words, Yosef effectively disproved all that they had done for the last twenty-two years – and from their very own actions. He showed the glaring contradiction between their words and their actions. They themselves had disregarded the feelings of Yaakov in the face of their own interests, yet when it came to the viceroy's interests suddenly this was unthinkable.

This was the criticism to which the brothers had no reply. They may have had myriad answers for the sale of Yosef, but when faced with their own opposite reasoning they had no way to dispute their guilt.

With this, the Beis Halevi goes on to explain the words of the Medrash. The Medrash says that Hashem will judge each person “according to who he is”. What it the implication of these seemingly superfluous words? Also, the Medrash lists two things which we must worry about. The “day of judgment” and “the day of rebuke”. What are the meaning of these two separate concerns?

He explains that judgment refers to the actual sins a person committed, whereas rebuke refers to how our own actions disprove our defenses. For example, one who does not give enough charity may have many ways to explain and justify his actions. Money is tight, he has a large family to feed, and he just can't afford to give anything more to the poor. These answers, while in a vacuum, may serve to partly vindicate him. That is “judgment”. But then there is the “day of rebuke”. The Judge turns to him and asks, if you really couldn't afford to give anymore, then how is it that shortly after you sent that pauper away empty-handed you splurged on a not-so-kosher and extravagant form of entertainment or the like. To this, the man is silenced. He himself has disproved the purity of his intentions.

This is what it means that we will be judged “according to who we are”, meaning with our own actions setting the bar for unacceptable defenses.

So let us reexamine our actions and our reasoning, ensuring that we are honest with ourselves. Let us be aware of the standards we ourselves are setting every day, so that we not be haunted by those very standards when we later cross them.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Avi · January 3, 2020

In this week's Parsha, Yoseph the King of Egypt, says he will be keeping Benyamin as a slave for stealing the royal goblet. Fearing for his younger brother's well-being Yehuda offers himself instead, to serve his brother's sentence. Yosef seeing Yehudahs kindness, Yosef reveals his true identity to his brothers. This is the first time they are reunited after they threw him in the pit. The brothers are obviously stunned and impressed by Yosef's position and power. His brothers bring the news to the extended family. The Jewish family of seventy moved to Egypt where Yosef had bought them the city of Goshen. Thanks to Yosef's foresight, the stores in Goshen supplied his family and all of Egypt with grain during the years of famine.

Yosef rose from the pit, his experiences at home of being exalted by his parents and resented and cast off by his siblings showed him the fragility of power and favoritism, public image and jealousy. After being thrust into the world of the slave trade and sitting in prison Yosef became homesick. He sought favoritism from the pharaoh and used that leverage to save Egypt from famine. The once spoiled son turned around and provided for his whole family. Yosef is called "the great provider" in psalms not because he can get what everyone needs, but because he does the best with what he has.

Yosef's unique strength is extracting the best possible outcomes from the worst scenarios. When famine strikes it is easy for anyone to say it is time for tax reform, it takes Yosef with the foresight of a "great provider" to know when there is extra and to implement a plan. Yosef's name means to increase, this foresight literally increases our deficits and leads us to rising success.

Good Shabbos! Avi

Never Give Up

Yosef · December 25, 2020

For twenty-two years, Yaakov mourned the loss of his beloved son Yosef, whom he assumed must have been devoured by a wild animal. Though so many years had passed, Yaakov simply could not be comforted or consoled – the wound was ever fresh and painful as the day Yosef had gone missing.

What is less well known is the spiritual repercussions that Yosef's disappearance had on Yaakov. He had been accustomed to frequent prophecy and the ecstasy of such closeness to the Almighty, but for those same twenty-two years Yaakov was altogether barred from divine revelation.

One reason for such is that a prerequisite to prophecy is joy; one who is deeply saddened is simply unable to muster up the joy required for the experience.

We can assume that being so distanced from Hashem was a personal tragedy of the highest proportion and was supremely agonizing for Yaakov, who breathed only for the will of his Creator. What's more, Yaakov had been previously told that if none of his children would die in his lifetime he would merit the world-to-come; conversely, if he were to lose a child he assumed he would lose his portion in the afterlife. This means that Yaakov thought for all those years that Hashem wanted nothing to do with him and that he would have no part in the next world. Effectively, Yaakov had lost all his dreams and aspirations and the entire purpose of his life.

On the topic of prophecy, we must know that one cannot become a prophet until they have scaled the highest heights and achieved the most lofty levels of perfection in mind and character. To make the cut, men of antiquity would spend a lifetime in preparation and achievement. If one faltered in his spiritual level, it could take months to regain the baseline requirements necessary to be visited by the Almighty. Perfection was essential at all times.

And yet, after twenty-two years of torment and the belief that he was unwanted by G-d, when Yaakov finally met up with Yosef, he immediately prophesied! As soon as the barrier of sadness had been lifted Yaakov was right there waiting, fully poised and perfected for divine revelation without missing a beat. Throughout all those terrible years, Yaakov maintained every ounce of personal achievement without giving up a single inch to the despair which would have destroyed a lesser man.

How did Yaakov accomplish such a superhuman act? What is the secret that empowered him to stand strong despite it all?

I think the answer may be a perception which can give all of us strength. Yaakov knew that when Hashem 'turns his back' it never means that He wants us to bug off and give up. When Hashem hides his face, it is because he wants us to redouble our efforts to connect. He wants us to rise up and overcome all obstacles in search of His countenance. He wants only to fortify our bond, replacing our run of the mill relationship with one that can go through fire and water unscathed and undiminished. If Hashem is hiding, He's waiting longingly for us to find him.

All of us go through times in our lives when things seem bleak, when we feel forsaken and forlorn; perhaps we even feel that G-d is uninterested in our relationship. At those times, we must remember the courage and fortitude of our father Yaakov. We must garner strength from his message that even when all seems lost we can hold our ground and continue to grow. And we can rest assured that Hashem is right there waiting to be found, waiting to be reunited with us, and waiting to shine His countenance upon us in a way we never could have imagined.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Light in the Darkness

Yosef · December 10, 2021

From all of the forefathers, Yaakov alone experienced prophecy in the form of מראות הלילה, a vision of the night. The Meshech Chochmah (1846-1923) explains that Yaakov experienced such to send a message regarding the Egyptian exile which he was about to enter, as well as all of the subsequent exiles of the Jewish people. The message was that even in the darkness of night and even in the most bitter exile, Hashem's presence would still be with Yaakov and his progeny.

Yaakov was the paradigm for a Jew in exile; always fighting off the darkness of night. He was the one who faught and overcame the angel of Eisav in the dead of night. He was the one who instituted Maariv, the prayers of night. Likewise, the four overarching trials of Yaakov's life -- the stories of Eisav, Lavan, Dina, and Yosef -- parallel the four exiles of the Jewish people.

As a nation, we just celebrated the holiday of Chanukah, commemorating the miracles which accompanied us even into Greek exile. We light the menorah in the darkness of night -- testimony that the shechinah, the Heavenly Presence, is with us right here in exile. It's also no happenstance that Chanukah always falls out on the winter solstice, the darkest day of the year, or that the Menorah (when lit outside) is supposed to be lit lower than ten handbreadths to the ground. The idea is all one -- we may be in the darkest, lowest, most unholy of places, but the light of Hashem and His Torah is with us even here.

The Meshech Chochmah further tells us that while Yaakov's nighttime prophecy signaled that Hashem will accompany us into the exile, there are some strings attached. He explains that generally prophecy was limited to the land of Israel and only one who first experienced it in the Holy Land was able to retain the ability in exile. Yaakov was only able to have a revelation in the dark of night because he had experienced such during the brightness of day.

It follows that in order for the Jewish people to have the shechinah with us in exile we must be able to claim that we have it with us from previous times. But, after thousands of years of exile, how are we supposed to make such a claim?

The answer is that as long as we act and view ourselves as scions of our fathers, we are tied to all the greatness of our people. When we follow in their footsteps, then they live on through us and we are able to claim membership in a nation that lived with G-d while yet in the Holy Land. As such, we have access to the divine presence grandfathered in and we are able to take it with us even into the deepest darkest places. But if we do not hold sacred the link to our people; if we stray from the paths of our fathers, then we are no more than our small and pitiful selves. If we cut ourselves off from the timelessness of our people, then we cannot hope to be joined by Hashem. Such a relationship can only begin in the Holy Land of antiquity.

We've already put away our Menorahs for the year, and now is the time to make sure that the deeper light of the menorah, the light of Hashem, will stay with us throughout the night. So let us cling to the Torah and cling to the ways of our glorious and ancient people. Only as we stand atop our predecessors' shoulders can we hope for that light to find a place in our lives. And very soon, may our flickering candlelight give way to the light of dawn with the coming of Moshiach.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

An Unlikely Hero

December 22, 2023

Rabbi Sacks points out that the narrative spanning Bereshit 37 to 50 stands as the Torah's lengthiest unbroken story, with Yosef at its core. Despite Yosef's apparent destiny for greatness, it is another brother, Yehudah, who ultimately shapes the fate of the Jewish people. The Jewish people, known by various names, find their identity through Yehudah, who becomes the forebear of Israel's greatest king, David, and the lineage of the Moshiach.

Yehudah's significance is underscored by a pivotal moment in the narrative when he suggests selling Yosef into slavery, displaying a callousness that contrasts with the nobility of Reuben. However, over time, Yehudah undergoes a profound transformation. In a later episode, he willingly offers himself as a slave to spare Binyomin from the fate he once proposed for Yosef. This dramatic shift in character highlights Yehudah's newfound concern and courage, illustrating his capacity for change.

The turning point in Yehudah's life is traced back to the Tamar story in Bereshit 38. Recognizing his mistreatment of Tamar, Yehudah undergoes a significant change, marked by the acknowledgment of his guilt. This episode becomes the genesis of teshuvah, the ability to recognize wrongdoing, feel remorse and change. Yehudah emerges as an exemplar of teshuvah, earning him the name "he who acknowledged his sin."

The significance of Judah's transformation is encapsulated in the saying that "in the place where penitents stand, even the perfectly righteous cannot stand." This challenges the prevailing notion that Yosef, consistently regarded as righteous, holds a superior position. Instead, Yehudah, as the first penitent in the Torah, becomes the progenitor of Israel's kings, underscoring the profound power of penitence and the potential for personal growth and change.

May we all be able to follow in the steps of Yehudah and humbly acknowledge our mistakes so that we can all achieve greatness.

Shabbat Shalom

Parshat

Vayechi

Noah · December 29, 2017

הִנֵּה מַה טוֹב וּמַה נָּעִים שֶׁבֶת אָחִים גַּם יַחַד

"Behold, how good and how pleasant it is for brethren to dwell together in unity!"

This week's parsha is the conclusion of the book of Bereshit. We wrap up the section of the Torah that deals with the Jewish Family and move on to the book of our aspiring nationhood, Shemot. In this week's parsha Yaakov prepares for his impending death by blessing his progeny and revealing what their roles in the Jewish Country will be. Yaakov then dies and is escorted back to Israel to be laid to rest with his forefathers in Ma'arat HaMachpelah. The parsha proceeds to recount the death of Yosef and presents his final request to be have his remains taken out from Egypt and brought to Israel as well. Before he dies he reminds his family of the promise that God has made to them saying, "G-d will surely remember you, and bring you up out of this land to the land of which He swore to Avraham, Yitchok and Yaakov."

When Yaakov begins blessing the future generations he does not start with his children. Instead, he blesses Efraim and Menashe, his grandchildren from Yosef, first. Only after completing their blessing does he move on to his children. Not only are Efraim and Menashe out of order generationally, but they are given the status of full blown tribes, effectively doubling the inheritance of Yosef and bestowing upon him the firstborn birthright.

In a further breach of procedure, Yaakov puts his right hand on the head of the younger son Efraim and his left hand on the head of Menashe. Surely Yaakov realized the terrible heartache and pain that was caused by preferential treatment in the previous generation. He was estranged from his beloved son Yosef for years because of the jealousy that that his favor aroused in his other sons. Furthermore, Yaakov experienced first hand the strife and conflict caused by the inequality between him and his own brother Esau.

With these two actions Yaakov seems to be upsetting the order of both his children and his grandchildren. How could Yaakov, with his incredible wisdom and foresight, risk agitating another generation with the same partisanship that had wreaked havoc in prior generations?

Yaakov's actions provide deep insight into the how a family can rise above its struggles to become great. The hardest challenges we face in life are often multigenerational struggles that we have seen play out before us. Like the color of eyes or hair these obstacles are passed down hereditarily. We are all too familiar with the consequences and fallout that result from succumbing to these pressures but a genetic chink our armor frequently prevents us from fully escaping the pressure. We see this clearly in our ancestral bloodline. From Yitzchak and Yishmael to Yosef and the Tribes the challenge of preferential treatment is a dominating force in our forefamily's history.

Yaakov surely understood this. In fact, he understood the magnitude of this problem so clearly that he realized if left unrectified, we could never sustain nationhood. This explains why he chose to bless Efraim and Menashe first. The relationship between grandchildren and grandparents is uniquely human. While most animals have parental relationships the concept of a multigenerational family is reserved for people. Ephraim and Menashe represented the perpetuation of Yaakov's heritage beyond a single generation, a continuation that is the bedrock of establishing a People. Additionally, these children would grow up in Egypt, away from the 'jewish natural habitat' that is Israel. This would expose them to the forces of a foreign, corrupt, and abusive culture with no easy way to insulate themselves. Yaakov needed to know that there was peace between them. If there was, they could cleave to each other and survive the coming diaspora. Peace would be our sustaining force through Egypt. Peace would keep us connected without a homeland. However, if they did not have the ability to dwell together in unity, the Jewish People would surely be lost and the blessing for each tribe to finds its place within our nation would be irrelevant.

Yaakov also knew that the solution was not to turn a blind-eye to the problem, to avoid confronting it directly. In the moment before he blessed his grandchildren he could have chosen ignore his premonition that the younger would exceed the older. He could have blessed the children as Yosef presented them, the older on the right and the younger on the left. Even further, Yaakov could have not blessed them at all and avoided the contentious decision to bestow the double inheritance on Yosef entirely. Yaakov understood that the Jewish Nation would emerge from these two generations. The first generation, Yaakov's children, lost their first battle to sibling rivalry in a pretty catastrophic way. Unless this vulnerability was entirely repaired on their part, it could be passed down to the generations without end. By giving Yosef the recognition that had originally caused him to be sold, Yaakov was creating an opportunity for his other sons to directly face this challenge once again. This time, however, they were grown men, with families, who had the benefits of a much broader perspective. And this time, they passed the test and Yosef was awarded the birthright without conflict.

The final test to determine if the "genetic" struggle had finally been overcome was to observe the grandchildren. If this flaw was completely rectified within the previous generation, the next would have an immunity to the conflict that can only come from a spiritual immune system that had developed resistance. Efraim and Menashe proved, through their peaceful acceptance of their respective blessings, that finally this particular challenge had been conquered.

Through hardship and heartbreak, slavery and servitude, may peace among brothers continue to bind us.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Survival and Growth

December 21, 2018

Parsha Vayechi focuses on the preparations Yaakov makes for his death after living in Egypt for 17 years. He famously asks Yosef to bury him in the Holy Land even though he will die in Egypt. Yosef’s sons Ephraim and Menashe are elevated to the level of their uncles by performing this task for their grandfather. As Yaakov is on his deathbed he blesses his children and divvies out roles for their future as the nation of Israel. Yaakov also tries to relate secrets about the end of time but cannot because of divine intervention. His funeral procession includes all his family along with pharaohs ministers and other high ranking citizens. He is buried with his predecessors in the cave of doubles. Yosef dies in Egypt also leaving instructions for his bones to be carried to the Holy Land. His bones are not brought to Israel until the exodus. Yosef's dying words fill us with the hope of redemption “G-d will surely remember you, and bring you up out of this land to the land of which He swore to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob.”

The Baal Haturim comments that Yakkovs best years were his final 17 in Egypt. 17 has the numerical value of the Hebrew word tov, or good. These good years were made possible by Yehudah because before Yaakov made the journey to Egypt he sent Yehudah ahead to begin establishing a yeshiva in Egypt. Both Yehuda and Yosef are kingly, each in their own way. Yehuda means to give thanks- from the root hodaah while Yosef means to increase. Yosef was the king of Egypt, but Yehudah was the king of his brothers. Even as a boy Yosef, learned everything from Yehudah. Yehuda”s strength was in the primacy of action, acting consistently through hard times. Yosef's strength is in self-growth and attaining more and more knowledge as he got older. Both the attributes of Yehudah and Yosef are integral parts of Jewish life the difference between them is the emphasis. Yehudah would be the first to jump into action and Yosef would amass more skill before extending himself.

Yaakov chose Yehudah to establish the yeshivah because, through exile, turmoil and hardship Yehuda's deep devotion to Torah would not sway. Through Years of hardship and centuries of suffering a Jew's heart will sway. His seated submission to God will still exist. This is why the best approach to Jewish education is Yehuda’s route, first, you nurture survival and then you encourage growth.

Winning Before the Fight

Yosef · January 10, 2020

When Yaakov Avinu passed away, Yosef and the brothers escorted him on his last journey to be buried in the land of Canaan. There, in the middle of the funeral, an unwanted guest popped in. It was Esav, threatening to disrupt the entire burial and insisting that the final plot left in Maarat Hamachpeilah was rightfully his and not Yaakov's. A debate ensued, with Yaakov's children stating that Esav had sold his plot years before to Yaakov. Esav insisted that they prove it with the sale document, which was all the way back in Egypt. Naftali, known for his alacrity, was elected to run back and get the document, but in the mean time Yaakov's body was in a state of dishonor; unburied for so long. Everyone was upset about the situation, but they felt they had no choice but to wait for Naftali to procure the document and prove their case. Everyone but Chushim, the deaf son of Don. Chushim watched what was going on but, due to his deafness, couldn't understand what they were waiting for. He asked and was told simply that Esav was holding things up until Naftali would return. Chushim wondered how it could be that they would leave his grandfather in a state of dishonor for such nonsense and decided to take matters into his own hands. Without any questions, he promptly picked up a stick and knocked Esav over the head, enabling the funeral procession to continue.1Gemara Sotah 13a

Gemara Sotah 13a

What was it that enabled Chushim to act with such confidence and cool even when his uncles, some of history's greatest men, were paralyzed into inaction? What gave him the clarity to realize that there was no need to dance to Esav's beat and thereby defile the body of Yaakov?

The answer is that Chushim was deaf. Yosef and the brothers were caught up in a debate with Esav, with each party trying to prove the point. Just by becoming embroiled in the debate with Esav, they already saw a little bit of his side of the story, and they were therefore unable to step out of the argument and perceive the reality of the situation. But not Chushim. Chushim couldn't hear any of Esav's arguments, he never entered the conversation, and so he was able to remain unmoved and to set things straight.

It is well-known that Esav was not just a historical figure, but rather an embodiment of the Yetzer Hora (Evil Inclination) himself, who lives to this day in the heart of every person. The story of Chushim is likewise not just a piece of history, but marching orders for all of us in our battle against that same Yetzer Hora. If we want to win the battle against this most cunning and sweet-talking enemy, the best course of action is to never enter the conversation. When our Yetzer Hora speaks up and tries to entice us to do something we know is wrong, let us know that just by entering the debate we have already lost half the war. When we start considering why not to do it, when we begin compiling a case for the side of good – that's already the beginning of the end. If we want to win the battle we have to end it abruptly and forcefully, never allowing the Yetzer Hora an audience with whom to engage.

To illustrate, I'd like to share just one example that I think we all can relate to. You decide at night that you must wake up early the next morning to do some act of good. You set an alarm (or three) and climb into bed, ready to take on the world the coming day. The next thing you know (and it can't possibly have been more than an hour or two) your alarm is ringing – time to face the test and get yourself out of bed. But somebody (who doesn't seem to ever sleep) is there in your ear, telling you that this is a bad idea. After all, you're exhausted, you barely slept last night, and besides, you're in the middle of a great dream.

At this point one of two things can happen. The first: You're a good person and you truly want to do the right thing, so you're not going to go down without a fight. You answer back, with all the authority you can muster, that tired or not I have work to get done and...... Usually you don't finish the sentence before you've dozed back off and, ultimately, lost the fight.

That's the almost inevitable result of conversing, even arguing, with the Yetzer Hora.

The second scenario is for the student of Chushim: You hear the Yetzer Hora trying to engage you in conversation, so you end it before it starts by bolting upright and hitting him over the head with your pillow. No conversation, no debate, and no room to fall in.

May we all learn from Chushim when to make ourselves deaf and how to knock the Yetzer Hora out of our lives once and for all.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

December 17, 2021

In this week's Parsha Veyechi, two of our most beloved patriarchs die in Egypt. Vayechi means and he lived, the Parsha teaches us the Halacha of proper Jewish burial and the laws regarding one's personal obligations before passing away.

As Yakov laid on his death bed he blessed his children and grandchildren making arrangements for all his progeny. He assigned each Tribe a different livelihood and role in settling the holy land. Although Yakov wished to share the details of the final redemption he was prevented from doing so.

After his death, a funeral procession including Egyptian elites and politicians accompanied Yakov's family to Hebron where Yakov was buried in the cave of doubles among his family.

Yosef also died in this Parsha at the age of 110, Yosef instructed his children not to bury him in Egypt but to bury him in the holy land. Before he died Yosef reminded his children of God's covenant and told them to have hope through their impending descent into slavery. Despite his wishes, Yosef's bones were only carried out of Egypt once Israel was freed from their bondage.

We learn from Yaakov's death that it is better to be buried in Israel, and proper to be buried next to one's family on land personally owned. We learn that making arrangements for burial is itself an omen for long life. The preparation will lead us to time better spent.

Yosef's burial teaches us that one's bones can be moved with the help of an expert Rabbi. It is important for Jews to be buried in Jewish plots near their family and for the wicked to be buried away from the group.

Burial practices are deeply primal, and even ants bury their dead. Ants inside of an enclosure will carry dead ants and parts of dead ants to the farthest corner of the enclosure and bury them together in a mass grave, science suggests this is to prevent diseases carried in the bodies of dead ants. Another interesting ceremony I heard about this week is practiced by the indigenous people in remote Papa New Guinea. In this tribe eating the bodies of dead loved ones is commonplace. A western bird researcher was astounded when on an expedition, his indigenous trail guide had to leave after receiving the bad news that his young nephew had died. It was time for the family to eat him. When asked about the practice the bird researcher said just as you wouldn't miss out on your loved one's funeral you wouldn't miss out on eating your loved one if you were from this tribe in Papa New Guinea. The researcher notes that there is a lack of big game in that region and that in a place where people starve for protein there is a good chance this practice serves a practical purpose as well.

Across the board, the ceremonies and traditions before and after death are not for the dead they are for the living. They are to facilitate the safety and well-being of those near and affected by the death.

Your Kindness is Greater than Life

Yosef · December 29, 2023

The enormity of the kindness of Hashem experienced with the birth of a healthy baby, is in itself quite overwhelming. But when that kindness comes against the backdrop of so many other gifts -- the gifts of health, a new home, the opportunity to be teaching Torah, and above all the privilege to still be learning in Kollel, to name but a few -- it is simply too much to bear. It leaves me feeling grossly inadequate, humbled, and undeserving. Perhaps more than anything, however, is the question and concern of what will be left in the next world. After receiving so much in this world, will any reward still await us in the next? Rav Yisroel Salanter said that, by enjoying a small delicacy, one is at risk of consuming his entire share in the world to come. If that is true of a silly delicacy, how much more so is the risk when enjoying blessings of such magnitude.

In truth, Dovid Hamelech already voiced this question in Tehillim. מה אשיב לה' כל תגמולוהי עלי -- Dovid asks what he can possibly do to repay or somehow absolve himself of the weighty debt incurred as the recipient of the kindness of Hashem. Thankfully, Dovid answers the question, כוס ישועות אשא ובשם ה' אקרא, that he will proclaim the greatness of Hashem and bring honor to his name. How, though, is this the answer?

The Malbim refers us to an earlier chapter of Tehillim (פרק ס"ג), where Dovid states כי טוב חסדך מחיים שפתי ישבחונך -- Your kindness is greater than life, so my lips will praise you. How can Hashem's kindness be greater than life; aren't all the blessings we enjoy only additional enhancements to life? The Malbim explains that life is not an end unto itself, but a means by which to come to the main goal of life, which is to praise Hashem (see Ramban, end of Bo). This means that Hashem's kindliness, which is the opportunity to thank and praise Him, is the ultimate purpose of life, while life itself is no more than a prerequisite. Hence, Hashem's gifts are truly better than life, since they bring about the ultimate purpose that my lips will praise you.

We named our daughter Chana, after the great Chana and her seven sons who gave their lives to bring honor to Hashem during the period of Greek oppression preceding Channukah. Incidentally, the Shla"h writes that Channukah (חנוכה) is the letters of Chana's name (חנה) plus כ"ו which is the numerical value of the name of Hashem. As if to say that the honor brought to Hahem's name through Chana and her sons' sacrifice, were part and parcel of the miracles of Channukah and the sanctification which came through them.

But how is it that young children, confined to solitude and without outside influence, should have the courage and conviction to give their lives instead of bowing to an idol? The answer is because they were raised with this ideal, that life is not an end unto itself but just a vehicle by which to thank Hashem and bring honor to his name. With such an outlook, instilled in the children by their illustrious mother Chana, it was obvious that a life of disgrace to Hashem wasn't even life and wasn't worth living for.

This is our blessing to our daughter Chana, and our hopes for all of us. That she should be the recipient of endless kindness from Hashem, but that she should perceive all that goodness not through a selfish lens, but as a means to thank Hashem and give honor to his name. And, as such, she should live a long and fruitful life of Kiddush Hashem.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Sefer Shemot

The Book of Exodus

Parshat

Shemot

January 5, 2017

A group of women met together to learn, and came across a verse in Malachei (3:3) referring to G-d: "He will sit as a refiner and purifier of silver."

This verse puzzled the women, and they wondered how this statement applied to the character and nature of Hashem.

One of the women offered to find out more about the process of refining silver.

The following week, the woman called up a silversmith and made an appointment to watch him while at work. She didn't mention anything about the reason for her interest, beyond her curiosity about the process of refining silver.

As she watched the silversmith work, he held a piece of silver over the fire and let it heat up. He explained that in refining silver one needed to hold the silver in the middle of the fire, where the flames are the hottest, as to burn away all of the impurities.

She asked the silversmith if it was true that he had to sit in front of the fire the entire time the silver was being refined. The man answered that yes, not only did he have to sit there holding the silver, but he also had to keep his eyes on it the entire time it was in the fire. If the silver was left even a moment too long in the flames, it would be destroyed.

The woman was silent for a moment. Then she asked the silversmith,

"But how do you know when the silver is fully refined?"

He smiled at her and answered,

"Oh, that's easy -- when I see my image in it."

This week we start the book of Shemot, Exodus, and with it the beginning of the Jews' difficult exile in Egypt. Egypt is often referred to as the "iron furnace," because the hardships that we endured in Egypt, refined us and allowed us to become a pure and holy nation.

Yet, just as the silversmith never casts his gaze away from the precious piece of silver in his hands, G-d lovingly held onto His people throughout their hard exile, never taking His compassionate eyes away of their pain. Indeed, the book of Shemot, which describes the Jews' sojourn into Egypt and the various afflictions that they encountered there, is nearly overflowing with clear demonstrations of Hashem's loving kindness.

In the beginning of the Parsha we learn about an incredible phenomenon. Pharaoh becomes king and sets out to get rid of the Jews who are inhabiting his land. Yet, at the same time, we find that the Jews start multiplying at an unprecedented rate, miraculously having six babies at one time! In fact, Rashi tells us just as the Egyptians set out to afflict and get rid of the Jews, so Hashem set out to bless and increase them. Despite the Egyptians' evil intents and the intense fire of oppression, G-d openly displayed His ever-present love for His people, showering them with blessing in the hardest of times.

Another mind-boggling portrayal of G-d's guiding hand, which never releases its loving hold of the Jewish nation, takes place in Pharaoh's palace. Pharaoh's astrologers tell him that the Jews will have a savior who will help free them from their bondage. Pharaoh thus sets out to have every Jewish baby boy thrown into the Nile so that their savior will never be able to rescue them. However, despite the Egyptians' great efforts to kill out all of the Jewish boys, the Jewish savior himself, Moshe, ends up being raised in Pharaoh's own house! This unbelievable twist of events is such a beautiful demonstration of G-d lovingly manipulating events for the best of His people, thus showing them that the difficulties that they were encountering were likewise for their ultimate best.

Later on, as the afflictions in Egypt get worse, the Jews are literally crying out from anguish, and the verse tells us that Hashem hears their painful cries. Rashi explains that G-d did not close His eyes to their sorrow, but rather took their cries to heart. Much like the silver refiner, Hashem did not take His eyes off of His precious people for even a moment, carefully and lovingly watching them to ensure that the painful oppression that they had to endure should not destroy them.

Perhaps, with this understanding of the Jews' exile in Egypt, we can appreciate why G-d chose to appear to Moshe in a burning bush. G-d was demonstrating to Moshe, and to the entire Jewish nation, that not only was He watching over them, but He was actually with His people in the burning fire, and feeling their pain.

Here we are, centuries later, but sadly, still in Exile. It is imperative for us to constantly remember that even when the going gets rough, Hashem is with us, lovingly guiding us through every step of the way.

If today you are feeling the heat of the fire:

Remember that you are in Hashem's hand,

He has His eye on you,

And will keep holding you and watching you

Until He sees His image in you.

Good Shabbos!

Appreciate the Outsider

Noah BenEzra · December 28, 2018

This week's Parsha introduces us to a new Egyptian Pharaoh who we are informed, “didn’t know Yosef.” How is it possible that the king of Egypt didn’t know about the chief “central banker” that saved them from economic collapse. Asserting that the Pharoh did not know Yosef is as unbelievable as asserting that the current president doesn’t know Ben Bernanke. How are we to understand the massive change that enabled Yosef and his descendants to go from the progeny of a respected technocrat to the slaves upon which the Egyptian empire would be built? What changed so drastically that all of the good that Yosef did was forgotten?

One answer that is commonly provided to this question is that the new king knew who Yosef was. The name was more than a little familiar. The knowledge of Yosef that was suddenly lacking was hakares-hatov, or appreciation. While Yosef the character was still renown throughout the land, the new king no longer felt a sense of appreciation toward him or his descendants. While the previous attitude was based on the axiom that Yosef was working for the betterment of Egyptian society as a whole, a new attitude, fueled by a combination of Yosef’s families success and hateful speculation arose. Suddenly appreciation was replaced by fear. What if they outnumber us? What if they join with our enemies? These hypothetical fears took the place where appreciation used to reside. These fears worked to undermine all the benefits brought to Egypt with Yaakov and his sons. Fear resulted in years of enslavement, untold misery and the eventual massacre of our baby boys.

The book of Shemos is the transition point of our story as a family to our story as a nation. No longer are we an insulated group of 70 souls. We are numerous and many, with smaller families, clans and division within our ranks. Despite this, we are to be molded into a unified nation, forged in the fiery destruction of the greatest empire the world had ever seen. Each time the Egyptian nation falters and crumbles the future Jewish nation is watching and taking notes. We have the opportunity to learn how to be a successful nation-state by learning from the failures of Egypt. And what is the first lesson we are provided on our outset? Appreciation instead of fear.

A nation cannot thrive and prosper built upon a foundation of distrust and fear. We cannot achieve stability and tranquility unless we open our eyes to the kindness and good being done all around us. Acknowledging that we have needs that can only be filled by others, outsiders, the stranger in our land, and sincerely appreciating them, fixes faults and strengthens society. But when we are afraid, constantly worried and distrusting, we become like the Egyptians and are capable of committing unbelievable atrocities. An inability to see the good done by individuals enables stereotyping and hate. Only by opening our minds and hearts to the positivity that exists all around us can we stave off the inevitable fear of the unknown.

Shabbat Shalom,

Noah BenEzra

But How?

January 17, 2020

In this week's Parsha we are introduced to the protagonist of the Exodus saga, Moshe Rabbenu. His miraculous entrance into the world is described in Sotah 13a.

ואומרת עתידה אמי שתלד בן שמושיע את ישראל וכיון שנולד משה נתמלא כל הבית כולה אור עמד אביה ונשקה על ראשה אמר לה בתי נתקיימה נבואתיך וכיון שהטילוהו ליאור עמד אביה וטפחה על ראשה אמר לה בתי היכן נבואתיך והיינו דכתיב (שמות ב, ד) ותתצב אחותו מרחוק לדעה מה יעשה לו לידע מה יהא בסוף נבואתה And as a child, Miriam would say: In the future, my mother will give birth to a son who will save the Jewish people. And once Moses was born, the entire house was filled with light. Her father arose and kissed her on her head. He said to her: My daughter, your prophecy has been fulfilled. And once they put him into the river, her father arose and hit her on her head. He said to her: My daughter, where is your prophecy? And this is as it is written: "And his sister stood afar off, to know what would be done to him" (Exodus 2:4), i.e., to know what will be the ultimate resolution of her prophecy.

This story begs many questions. Where did Amram's faith in the prophecy go? He just experienced a miraculous birth! Furthermore, it was known the whole time that there was a decree against the Jewish males. Why his drastic change in demeanor from a kiss to a potch?

The Beer Mayim Chaim explains that Amram's frustration wasn't rooted in a lack of belief that the salvation would come, rather he wanted to know how it would arise. He wanted to know the exact way his son would be saved and eventually rescue the Jewish people. His initial positive reaction was caused by the confirmation of the prophecy while his second more aggressive reaction was from the frustration of now knowing how the prophecy would be fulfilled.

This is fleshed out even more generally by the Kedushas Levi. He explains that when get bogged down in the physical details of a situation we lose the heavenly perspective necessary to recognize gods plan. This is alluded to by Miriam's reaction, "she stood far off." She distanced herself from the immediate situation to regain the perspective from which she could still see her prophecy come true.

Each child is a miracle. We all know the feeling of being in a house filled with light emanating from the bassinet in the corner. But so quickly, this experience of endless potential is bounded by the how. Sure your a perfect creature capable of anything but how will you get there? How will you learn to sit up and walk and read and socialize and assimilate into Egyptian royalty without losing sight of your people? I believe you will, I really do! But HOW?

And we have to take a lesson from Miriam and step back and view the situation from a distance. We have to remember the overwhelming light that the child brought into the world with them and that original promise and potential. From this perspective, we can escape how and again realize that this is part of God's plan.

Shabbat Shalom

Avi · January 7, 2021

In this week's Parsha "Shemot" or "Names" Moshe was born. As the children of Israel multiplied in Egypt Pharaoh saw Jews as a threat so he enslaved the Jews and ordered his midwives to drown Hebrew babies in the Nile.

When Moshe was born to Yochevded and Amram the parents tried to conceal their baby, the midrash tells us that a brilliant light radiated from Moshe which made him especially hard to conceal. As loving parents they cast baby Moshe into the Nile safe in a waterproof basket. Moshe's older sister Miriam watched her baby float down the Nile toward the Pharaoh's palace.

Pharaoh's daughter saw the baby floating in the basket and raised Moshe in the palace as an Eygyptian.

As Moshe grew up he witnessed the slavery of the Jews and it weighed on him heavily. As a young man Moshe left the palace, when he saw an Eygyptian taskmaster beating a Jewish slave, Moshe killed the taskmaster. The only witnesses were Jewish Slaves. However this was not the action that made Moshe the leader of the Jewish People. The next day, when Moshe saw two slaves fighting he admonished them, they did not take kindly to Moshe's interference and revealed Moshe's deed of the day before. Because Moshe killed a taskmaster he had to flee to Midian.

There he met his father in law and wife, he became their shepherd.

It is at the base of Mount Sinai where Moshe encountered a burning bush, in this bush God revealed himself to Moshe and told him to free his people so they may serve him. Moshe being nervous to approach the Pharaoh asked that his brother Aaron be the spokesperson, in addition to this God taught Moshe magic to impress the eygyptian sorcerers.

When Moshe performed his wonders and Aaron demanded their freedom, Pharaoh only increased the workload for the Jews, causing Moshe to call out to God and ask " Why have you done evil to these people?" God promised Moshe the redemption was close at hand.

Parshat

Vaera

Sticking to Our Route

Yosef · January 4, 2019

For as long as Ehud could remember, he'd been riding the buses in Jerusalem. And for almost as long, he'd been entranced watching the bus drivers effortlessly handling the flow of passengers, the sharp turns, and the steep hills. And so, as Ehud grew older, he dreamed of one day getting his license and joining Egged [the largest Israeli bus company]. Finally the day arrived, Ehud received his license and soon after signed a contract with Egged -- in just a few days his dreams would finally be realized. He could barely sleep at night as he imagined driving the busy Kotel route, with a bus packed literally to the rafters at all hours of the day. He dreamed of the endless flow of passengers, raking in the bus fares too fast to keep track of. He envisioned capably keeping track of both entrances while opening the bottom compartment for anyone in need. Yes, he would be the greatest bus driver that ever lived!

His first day came and he was up early, dressed in his uniform and ready to conquer the world. But as he received his bus route, his heart dropped. Instead of the big number 3 assigning his duties to the Kotel, there was some route he'd never even heard of servicing the outskirts of Jerusalem. Not wanting to make a bad impression, he swallowed hard, thanked the boss, and hurried off to his bus. That day was the longest day of Ehud's life. No throngs of clamoring passengers, no traffic to navigate, no cash flow, and no fun. Ehud knew that he hadn't even earned enough for the company to pay for his gas, and he felt less than worthless.

Ehud cried himself to sleep every night that week, but over the weekend he developed a grand solution. On Monday, he reported as usual and received his expected depressing orders. He got into the driver's seat and started toward his route, but halfway there he changed course and adjusted the sign on his bus. It now read "Number 3 -- Kotel". As he arrived at the first stop on the new route, he was overjoyed. At the stop waited tens of people, all pushing their way to get a seat on his bus. He deftly took everyone's payment and provided the necessary change and tickets, while simultaneously navigating the traffic to the next stop. He loved the noise, the money flying in, the excitement -- every bit at grand as he had dreamed! For all eight hours of his shift, the smile never left his face. At the end of the shift he put all the money into a huge and sagging bag and proudly marched into the boss's office, placing it with a satisfying thud on the desk. Beaming, he told his boss how he had manned the Kotel route, making a veritable fortune for the company while providing unparalleled customer service.

As Ehud neared the end of his speech, he noticed his boss's face turning beet red. "You WHAT?!" bellowed the boss. "I was trying to contact you all day! Dozens of people were unable to get to their jobs, students missed school, and I've never received so many complaints as I did today! You're route may be quiet, but its an essential part of the system and is necessary for the city to function. By switching your role for the role of another you ruined the greater picture that we must always keep in mind!"

Sometimes the Torah lists Moshe before Aharon, and sometimes Aharon comes before Moshe. According to Rashi, this teaches us that they were equal. The Kli Yakar further points out that in a verse regarding prophecy Aharon is listed first, whereas in a verse about advocating to Pharaoh Moshe is first. This is very puzzling because we know Moshe was the greatest prophet to ever live, whereas Aharon did almost all the talking with Pharaoh. How then can we say that they were equals?

The answer is that each of them had something that they were expected to do, and they each fulfilled their duties to their utmost. Yes, Moshe's place was to be the greatest prophet to ever live while Aharon was supposed to take a back seat. Yes, Aharon was to be the main spokesman to Pharaoh while Moshe was more subdued. But, like the bus driver, each role was of vital importance to the Master Plan and therefore they were considered equals.

Each of us has our own, very specific role which we have to play in the world. To that end we are set up with our place, our family, our capabilities, and and our limitations. Our physical qualities, our intelligence, our inner leanings, and our financial standing are all custom made for us to be able to fill that role to perfection. If we will accomplish that which we are supposed to then we are a shining success, equal to Moshe himself. If not, no matter what glory we pursue in its stead, we are ultimately a failure, leaving a gaping hole in the Master Plan.

Sometimes we look around and see others who are stronger, taller, smarter, or wealthier than us. We see their shiny position in life and wish we could switch places. Let us remember Ehud's dreadful mistake and how absurd it was to think he'd be more successful driving the route assigned to another. Let us realize that so it is with our route in life -- it may seem quieter and less glamorous than our friend's, but it is just as vital and important to the Master Planner. That is the definition of success.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Shira and Adam · January 24, 2020

This week's Parsha represents the first seven of the ten plagues. And among them, my favorite. Not because it demonstrates the great power of the one above, although it obviously does that. Not for how well the punishment matches the crime, although it does that as well. But this plague I love most for what it teaches us about ourselves. I'm talking about frogs. The slimy, green, hopping creatures that literally blanketed all of Egypt. Indoors and outdoors, in the outhouses, even in the ovens that they baked in. They were literally everywhere.

The funny thing is, the midrash tells us it didn't begin this way. It all started with just one frog in Pharaoh's palace. One slimy green pest that belonged outside. And Pharaoh took a staff and hit it with all his might to kill it once and for all… And it multiplied. So he hit it again. And again. And again. With greater anger each time, he struck the frogs thereby enlarging his problem.

And I listen to the story and think, "What an imbecile! Stop hitting it!"

It's easy to criticize and ridicule the Egyptians for approaching their problem with anger. I once heard a line, no one in history has ever calmed down because someone told them to calm down. When the fierce grip of rage overtakes someone, it is almost impossible to not respond in kind. It becomes a problem that escalates until it is completely beyond repair.

The only solution is to take a step back and think logically. Count to ten before taking any action.

Our sages teach us that someone who gives in to anger, it is as if they have committed idolatry. It is worship is the ego, belief that we should be the ones in charge and loss of control over our inclinations.

The Egyptians were notorious idol-worshipers, bowing down in submission to everything from the Royal King to the ordinary housecats.
