It is no surprise to me that a nation who was so ensconced in idolatry would not stop when it came to worshipping themselves. I am so much more mature and mindful than they were, so I am a completely different story. Right?

Until I think of all the problems I've hit over and over, only making them worse. When we're angry, we react in ways that sane people think insane. Even when we ourselves look back, we can see the insanity of what we've done, but in the moment it's very hard to hold back. So the frogs teach us about human nature. When we feel ourselves becoming upset, before we sabotage our own goals, we can stop. And respond rather than react.

I love this Parsha because it reminds me to look for the frogs in my life. And to stop, take a deep breath and slowly stop multiplying them. Good shabbos! Shira and Adam

Hidden Salvation

Yosef · January 15, 2021

The weekly reading of the Torah has a profound effect on the world, and therefore we are very careful never to finish a reading with something negative. In fact, the two longest aliya's are the ones containing the Torah's curses – we go on and on until we find something positive to close with.

Why then, on Monday and Thursday of Parshat Va'eira do we finish the second reading with the seemingly negative phrase 'and the people did not listen to Moshe because of shortness of spirit and hard labor'? Here the Torah describes the hopelessness of the Jewish people, who were so downtrodden and broken that they could not accept the message of salvation. Does this not violate the principle of always finding a good ending?

The answer, says Rabbi E. B. Finkel, is that the sorry state of the Jews and the unbearable oppression which they were subjected to were indeed the catalyst for their early redemption. Viewed from this lens, the verse is ultimately a good thing, not worse than the painful procedure performed to save the life of one who is ill.

This brings us to the very poignant picture painted by Rabbi Shalom Scwhadron. He describes how, from the tortures of Egypt, the Jews screamed out to Hashem for salvation. We can't even imagine what a cry that was, as they poured out their heart to their loving Father to save the from the most terrible conditions known to man. What feeling, what concentration, and what trust must have been contained in that prayer! And yet, they immediately found themselves facing yet another decree, as Pharaoh ruthlessly determined that they would no longer be given the straw with which to make bricks, but would have to gather it themselves. This on top of their already insurmountable workload. Can we imagine how they must have felt? Here they truly had prayed their hearts out to Hashem, and not only were they not answered, but they were stricken once again. Could one possibly feel more forsaken? Surely Hashem must have totally abandoned them, uninterested in his relationship with the Jewish people!

But in truth, explains Rabbi Schwadron, they had been heard, they had been answered, and this very trial was the key to the salvation for which they had begged. There was a certain amount of suffering which the Jewish people needed to be subjected to in order to form them into the nation they would one day become. There was no way around it – it simply had to be. This was set at four-hundred years of Egyptian bondage. But when Hashem heard their prayers and saw that they could not possibly retain their identity for such a long time; He decided to end the sentence early. But what would be with the suffering they needed in order to achieve their identity? It couldn't be skipped, so it would be concentrated, effectively delivering four-hundred years of pain in the span of two-hundred and ten years. A terribly difficult period in our history, but one which we could survive and from which we would emerge still stronger and prepared to accept the Torah.

This means that the intense suffering which they endured after crying out to Hashem was none other than the answer to their prayers. While they may have felt forsaken, they were actually already experiencing the answer to their problems.

The same is true for us. The divine master-plan is far far beyond the understanding of our mortal minds, and we don't know the reasons for most of the things that happen to us. Life is no cup of tea, and it is often fraught with heartache and challenges. Indeed, sometimes we are cast into utter darkness with no ray of light, and hopefully our reaction is to cry out to our loving Father for help. Even then, we often don't hear Him answer, and we may even feel that we are worse off than we started. At those times, let us remember how our fathers in Egypt must have felt the same way, and let us understand that, just as then, what we perceive as more misfortune may really be paving the way for the grandest of salvations.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

The Torah of Appreciation

Yosef · December 31, 2021

Moshe came onto the scene in last week's Parshah, and from here on out he is the central figure for the rest of the Torah. Yet we find three places in our Parshah where Moshe takes a background role and gives the spotlight over to his brother Aharon. These are the plagues of blood, frogs, and lice, which were all brought about by Aharon. The reason given for this switch in roles is that Moshe could not be the one to bring a plague upon the Nile or the dirt of Egypt. Why? Because Moshe had been saved in the Nile as a baby and protected by the dirt years later when he killed an Egyptian and covered the evidence with soil. Since he had benefited from these things, his appreciation would not allow him to damage them with the plagues.

This needs to be understood. It is obvious that because the Nile and the dirt are inanimate objects, there is no need or implication of 'returning the favor'. They need not and cannot be repaid and they feel no lack of gratitude either way. What's more, they didn't act in any way to benefit Moshe but were merely the vehicles through which people protected him. Where is the need for gratitude here?

The answer is that gratitude is not only something we express in order to 'make it up' to our benefactor -- it is an internal trait that should give us a personal need to show appreciation. Moshe was not concerned for the well-being of the Nile, but it was unthinkable to him to act callously toward something which had benefited him. Had Moshe smote the Nile, it would have been a personal breach in character, even though the Nile would have felt nothing.

In a similar vein, I was taught not to throw away a used garment in the garbage amongst all the dirty diapers and banana peels, but to first wrap it in a bag. For after benefiting so much from that shirt, how could you so disdain it to treat it like the rest of your refuse?

I believe that this is a beautiful portrayal of how the Torah's ideals, even in things which are part of the basic human conscience, are on a plane above human theory. Appreciation is a universal ideal, and any decent person will tell you that it is morally incumbent upon the recipient of goodness to thank and try to repay his benefactor. But here we are learning that this is not enough. Here we are learning a much more delicate and refined form of thanksgiving. The Torah is teaching us that we cannot be internally complete if we can bring ourselves to disdain or hurt our source of benefit -- even if it makes no difference to the person or item in question. It is obvious to me that one who acts according to such an ideal will have achieved a greatness of character far beyond the general public.

On this note, am I reminded of how Rabbi Yaakov Bender shlit"a used to constantly reiterate that the American catch-phrase 'There is no way I can possibly thank you' is no excuse not to try. Rabbi Bender was telling us that even when we find ourselves completely inadequate to repay a favor, we still must do everything in our power to show appreciation. Will it aid our benefactor? Perhaps not. But for ourselves we must become people of thanks; people who yearn and pine and wish to repay every ounce of goodness which we receive.

While we may be used to thinking that appreciation is relegated to the realm of interpersonal relationships, we are learning now that its scope extends even to our personal lives. The same follows that it is of paramount importance in our relationship with Hashem. In fact, the Chovot Hal'vavot bases his entire approach to mitzvah observance on the trait of appreciation. He explains how one is supposed to observe the endless amount of goodness which Hashem bestows upon him -- his every breath, his body, light, sound, family, friends, a house, clothes, food, and the list goes on. As one begins to think of the magnitude of the debt which he 'owes' to G-d, it should spur him on to serve Hashem with every ounce of his being, as an act of thanksgiving. Here too, man's service is to One who lacks nothing and to Whom he cannot possibly benefit -- so what good is his appreciation? The answer is as stated; it is true that we cannot possibly 'make it up' to Hashem, but we are expected to become people consumed by thanks, even when it will not help the one who we are thanking.

So let's make it our business to become people of thanks. Let's keep our eyes out to realize every good thing which we enjoy, from Hashem, from people, and even from inanimate objects. As we learn the habit of recognizing the good, let's strive to not let any goodness go by without a show of appreciation. It may be a thank you, it may be a gift or a card, it may be a favor in return, or it may be just a feeling of respect and indebtedness. Whatever it is, it will help us grow to become thankful people. Without a doubt, this will improve our relationships with man and G-d alike and will help us lead happy and fulfilled lives as we truly appreciate all the goodness bestowed upon us.

Good Shabbos, Yosef

Parshat

Bo

Yosef · January 19, 2017

Each Passover we eat only matzoh in commemoration of the unleavened bread that the Jews ate as they left Egypt. But did you ever wonder why it had to be that way? Couldn't they have waited another few minutes for the bread to rise? Why did G-d deem it necessary to take them out in such a mad rush?

Rabbi Simcha Z. Ziv answers that this was to teach the Jewish nation an invaluable lesson. Now, as they left the Egyptian bondage en route to a life of freedom, they were prone to a grave mistake in what the true meaning of freedom is. One might think that freedom means living it up, capitalizing on every pleasure available to man, and fulfilling every desire which tickles your fancy. This is the driving force behind all of Western society. Everyone is chasing after money, comfort, and pleasure, in the belief that through having more stuff they'll somehow fill the void which eats away at their hearts, day and night. "If only I had that house, that car, or that gadget, then I'd finally be happy."

Here, at the outset of their free lives, G-d taught the Jews that this is not what it's all about. The only way to achieve true happiness is by breaking our wants and desires, by learning to find joy in the important things in life and not being dependent on the fulfillment of our whims. This was the message of the unleavened bread, to show for all time that happiness and freedom is something attained not by succumbing to our impulses but by rising above them and learning to be happy with our lot, or in this case our matzoh. As our sages tell us, "Who is truly wealthy? One who is happy with his share."

To illustrate, Rabbi Yeruchom Levovitz explains that true happiness comes from menuchat hanefesh, peace of mind. When we are in a state of inner contentment and tranquility it gives us a sense of great joy. The mistake we all make is in the belief that the way to achieve such inner peace is through menuchat haguf, or physical comfort. Most people assume that if we will be physically content, sleeping on the finest linen money can buy, eating the most luscious meats, and drinking the finest wines, that will give us the inner peace and happiness which we truly crave.

In truth, however, the opposite is true. Rabbi Levovitz turns our attention to the training of a soldier. The objective of his training is to teach absolute equanimity and inner peace, to the point that when in the midst of battle, as all hell breaks loose around him, he will be able to keep his composure and make calm calculated decisions. According to the mistaken formula mentioned above, one might expect training for such an event to include the fulfillment of every wish the soldier has. He should be wined and dined, treated to plenty of hours of restful sleep, the occasional sauna, and perhaps some Beethoven playing softly in the background. We know of course that the opposite is the case - boot camp is tough as nails. The soldiers are awakened roughly in the wee hours of the morning after just a few hours of sleep on a wooden plank. They are fed tasteless mush and forced to work tirelessly all day in poor conditions. They are denied all the pleasures they are used to at home and taught to make do with nothing but the bare minimum. And this all in order to teach them peace of mind?

Indeed. As long as one is wrapped up and dependent upon the materialistic things in life he can never achieve inner peace. If he needs his beauty rest, then inevitably when his baby keeps him up at night he will be off-kilter for the day ahead. If he craves wealth or honor then he can rest assured that he'll never have enough. When our happiness depends on physical crutches, then we will always remain a cripple. We will be subject to all the winds blowing around us which can easily knock us off our precarious perch atop all of our stuff.

Through internalizing this timeless message of the Torah, we can learn to be happier, calmer, and more fulfilled people. We can achieve true freedom of the spirit, free from the unattainable pursuit of stuff that has decimated our society. We can live a life of unadulterated and uninterrupted joy that needs no outside fuel and is completely self-sufficient.

Have a terrific Shabbos,

Yosef

The Temptation to Not Be Moved

January 11, 2019

(Based on the works of Rashi, The Rambam, and the Ramchal)

Last week’s Parsha concluded with the Plague of Hail, which demolished almost every field left in the Land of Egypt. I say almost because there was one crop left unharmed. The wheat crop, which bent instead of breaking at the impact of the hail, was left intact.

The Egyptians were strangely triumphant about the survival of their amber waves of grains. True, Egypt was largely in ruins, but the wheat not having succumbed to the previous plague gave them a sliver of hope.

Perhaps the G-d of the Jews was not quite so powerful after all? If He had intended on wiping out their last form of nourishment, He failed. (Remember, this is after every fish was drowned by blood, all the livestock had perished by plague, and all other forms of produce had been wiped out through fiery hail.)

To any sane person, the question seems ludacris. How could one possibly doubt the power of G-d, who was clearly intent on reducing the Egyption dynasty to ruins and leading His Chosen People to redemption?

But such is the power of disbelief and dismissal.

Every miracle performed by G-d, either directly or through a messenger, is only to communicate something. Once it has been witnessed it imposes obligation upon those that acknowledge it. In this case G-d was demonstrating His existence and power over and over again.

The Egyptians were avid idol worshipers, as well as scientists, astrologers, and even magicians. With the arrival of each plague, they attempted to explain it away.

When all the water in the Nile had turned to blood, Pharoah laughed at Moshe and said, “So you’re a magician! Anyone can turn water to blood!”

He then announced that he could prove it. Pharaoh summoned his cabinet of magicians and instructed them to turn water into blood. There was just one problem. All the water the Egyptians possessed was already blood. Slightly embarrassed, but left with no other option, he asked Moshe’s brother Aaron if he could BUY a cup of water from him.

That’s right. Pharaoh had to pay a Jew for a cup of water to turn into blood, only to prove that turning the Nile River red was not a miracle but an elaborate magic trick.

The irony was completely lost on him.

During the following plague, the infestation of frogs, Pharaoh demands to talk to Moshe. He pleads that the plague commence, as the frogs (and alligators, according to many sources) were ravaging Egypt.

(It’s interesting to note that Pharaoh once again demanded that his sorcerers duplicate this feat. They delivered, filling the Royal Chambers with, you guessed it, MORE FROGS!)

But once Moshe is in his chambers, Pharaoh can’t help himself not to test the authenticity of the plague’s source. Moshe offers that he can end the plague at any time; Pharaoh could choose the exact minute. Instead of the obvious answer: “This second!”, Pharaoh speculates a moment and says “Tomorrow.” The king of a country that was becoming the laughingstock of the inhabited world was willing to endure the suffering for another day, just to test if Moshe could actually do it.

The night before the invasion of the locust, the Torah mentions that an Eastern Wind blew all night. The Egyptians had an explanation too easily handed to them. Why even mention it?

It’s quite simple actually: to not rob them of their free will. Yes, it was highly unlikely that the wind caused the (prewarned) plague, but it did give the obstinate a way to explain the occurrence.

Let’s skip to the last Plague now, The Death of the Firstborn. G-d tells Moshe to warn Pharaoh that at midnight, a loud scream would rise from every home as the Oldest Child would fall dead.

When Moshe transmits the message he changes the wording slightly. Instead of announcing the event would take place AT midnight, he tells Pharaoh that it would happen “Around midnight.”

Why the change in verbiage?

Moshe knew that the Egyptians were very advanced in astronomy, and they would be waiting, calculating. If the timing didn’t match up perfectly, Pharaoh would proclaim that the Plague was not caused by G-d.

The wail was heard at EXACTLY midnight.

There were no loopholes left. No way to explain this one. So indeed, Pharaoh ran around the streets in his night robes, searching for Moshe, insisting that the Jews leave Egypt at once.

But here’s the thing about the impact of miracles: it doesn’t last very long. If Pharaoh had just allowed the Jews to freedom, he might have had a chance to rebuild and restore what was left of a once flourishing country.

As we know, he just couldn’t let it go. Three days later he was strapping his own horse to his wagon to chase down the Jews, and we all know how that ended. Pharaoh followed them directly into the Sea of Reeds where he met his gruesome end as the walls of water came crashing down, drowning him and his army, sealing their fate as a footnote in history.

What can we learn from Pharaoh’s behavior, (aside from not murdering, enslaving, and torturing people, obviously)?

There were so many miracles, so many chances to just BELIEVE that there is a G-d, Something greater than himself. He chose to ignore, explain, and defy. It was right in front of him, but he could not bring himself to admit it.

We see miracles every day. Some are as simple and expected as opening our eyes in the morning, and some are dramatic and life-altering, like missing a plane that crashes into a skyscraper.

How do we react? Do we, in fact, alter our lives? Or do we say “That’s amazing, that’s incredible, unbelievable!” and then go back to routine completely unchanged.

Only forty days after leaving Egypt, the Jews were presented with the Torah. Why so fast? What was the rush?

G-d knows how fast the effects of a miracle wear off. He knew that if we didn’t act on it, we didn’t commit to keeping the commandments immediately, our awe would also fade.

When something happens that gives us a wake-up call, we need to take something upon ourselves, resolve to change our lives in a tangible way. Otherwise, the jubilation dies down and we are the same as we were before.

This Sunday, Adam returned from a trip to Israel, where he took college students to explore their Heritage. At the end of the trip, the students were truly moved and inspired. But inspiration fades, so what did Adam do? He asked each person to make a commitment, something tangible so they would truly gain from the experience.

This is a trait I’ve witnessed in our Dear Uncle Yonatan Z’L (Of Blessed Memory). Many people see things that have the potential to change them, but they remain complacent, satisfied with who they are.

Uncle Yonatan was not one of those people. He was always working on himself, wanting to change and better himself. He saw signs and used them as directions. He brought an entire family back to their roots, simply by ploughing ahead, no matter what obstacles he faced.

The measure of a man is not what he is, but how hard and how far he worked to get there. Uncle Yonatan was constantly examining himself, reaching for greatness, aiming for higher and higher moral ground.

I can safely say that when he left this world, he was the closest thing we knew to an Angel. We feel him looking down at us, and he inspires us to look upward, to become as great as he always knew we could be.

Avi · January 31, 2020

In this weeks parsha "Bo" the Jewish people are given the first mitzvah, to establish a lunar calendar. As God inflicts the final three plagues on the Egyptians B'nai Yisroel flees to the desert, for the first time the Enslaved nation kept their own time. Referring to the spring month of Pesach, the Torah says "This month (Nisson) shall be for you the first of the months." Of course most of us think of Rosh Hashanah as the new year.

Rabbi Moshe Feinstein commented that the month of Tishrei is the month of the creation of the world. The month of Nisson is the month of the exodus from Egypt. Both months are lessons in understanding God's power. On Rosh Hashanah we celebrate the creation of the whole universe on Pesach we celebrate divine intervention and recognize that God enslaved us and freed us.

This posuk is coming to teach us that recognizing divine intervention is even more important than the recognition of creation. This is why the calendar starts when we become free. Remembering that the world began in spring time will imbue all of our days with a rejuvenating sense of exodus, again and for the very first time. The calender is cyclical, and the earth is a Globe with two poles. Greek philosophers challenged the Talmudic sage Rabbi Joshua to identify the exact center of the world. Rabbi Joshua held up his finger and said, , "It is right here. You can take ropes and measure it, if you wish." as Jews we measure both from Pesach to Pesach and Rosh Hashana to Rosh Hashanah. The new year in Nisson is the first of the months, the new year in Tishrei is the head of our year.

Shabbat Shalom, Avi

The Time to be Free

Noah · January 22, 2021

Egypt lays in ruins still smoldering. The Israelites are teetering on the brink of freedom and nationhood. Moshe has already warned Pharaoh about the impending plague. Right before the Israelites receive their exodus instructions there is an unexpected addition.

הַחֹ֧דֶשׁ הַזֶּ֛ה לָכֶ֖ם רֹ֣אשׁ חֳדָשִׁ֑ים רִאשׁ֥וֹן הוּא֙ לָכֶ֔ם לְחָדְשֵׁ֖י הַשָּׁנָֽה׃

This month shall mark for you the beginning of the months; it shall be the first of the months of the year for you.

This unassuming verse receives the distinction of containing the first mitzvah in the Torah, to sanctify the new moon. In the midst of our fundamental national drama, why is our first commandment as a nation so tangential to the story we are telling? Instead, let us start with the korban Pesach, or the instruction to memorialize this saga.

The Seforno explains that slaves in Egypt had no control over their time and, consequently, over their lives. They were worked to exhaustion, and always subject to having still more demands made on their time. The central benefit of achieving freedom and independence would be the control they could exercise over time. Only once they had achieved this measure of autonomy could they then decide what to devote their time to.

The Netziv takes a slightly different approach. He explains that Rosh Chodesh came first because it differentiated the Israelites from the rest of the world. While all life had been oriented around a solar calendar, the first commandment would be to break that mold. To have a new national calendar that could contain unique holidays and symbolism, it had to be governed by a different celestial body.

This whole crisis has made time act funny. Since the very first lockdown people have been mourning the loss of circadian rhythm. Confusing a Tuesday with a Thursday has gone from a concerning sign of detachment to a common occurrence. This daily struggle has been compounded by the loss of large familial celebrations. Days have blurred into weeks and weeks into seasons without our unique temporal landmarks guiding the way around the year.

That's why this first, fundamental mitzvah is more relevant for me today than ever. To come close to God, to better myself, to achieve anything, I need to reclaim autonomy over my time. I can set better boundaries in the "working from home" arrangement that has morphed into "living at work." I can keep to a healthy routine despite having nowhere to go and no one to see. And, I can instill celebratory occasions with extra meaning despite the distance. As we see from this week's parsha, these changes are the foundation on which our national identity is built.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Moshe's Resistance

January 7, 2022

In this week's parsha we have the dramatic final meeting between Moshe and Pharaoh. Amidst the plague of darkness, Pharaoh calls Moshe and Aaron seemingly to acquiesce to their demand for a spiritual retreat. Pharaoh finally agrees to allow women and children to attend, only demanding that the livestock remain. Moshe presses Pharaoh further telling him that not only would the Jews be taking their cattle but that, "[Pharaoh] You yourself must provide us with sacrifices and burnt offerings to offer up to the LORD our God." This is too much for Pharaoh to relent. "Pharaoh said to him, "Be gone from me! Take care not to see me again, for the moment you look upon my face you shall die."" Moshe confirms that this will be their last meeting and warns Pharaoh about the ultimate plague still to come and the devastation that will be wrought on Egypt. Finally, Moshe reveals how the exodus will finally begin; "Then all these courtiers of yours [Pharaoh] shall come down to me and bow low to me, saying, 'Depart, you and all the people who follow you!' After that I will depart." And he left Pharaoh's presence in hot anger."

This is one of the most contentious interactions in the entire Torah. Here are two leaders of opposing factions who are fiercely committed to achieving opposite ends. Moshe grew up in Pharaoh's home and sacrificed everything to become the leader of a ragtag nation of slaves. Pharaoh has already sacrificed his nation's infrastructure, livestock, and sanity through his continued obstinance despite horrific plagues. Each has put everything on the line and only now at this apex, do we see that this elicits an emotional reaction from Moshe; hot anger.

In this moment of confrontation, Moshe is setting an eternal example of how we deal with evil authority. We are a people passionate about pursuing justice and this means having respect for authority while also being able to confront it when it runs counter to our sense of morality. Moshe's actions form the archetype for all of Jewish resistance and as such, it is no surprise it is the subject of much debate.

Rashi comments on Moshe's final words to Pharaoh based on a Gemara in Menachos as follows:

He showed respect to the king, for as a matter of fact ultimately Pharaoh himself came down to him at night, (Exodus 12:31) "And he said, Arise, go out from the midst of my people"; but Moses did not at the outset say to him, "And thou shalt come down to me and shalt prostrate thyself", out of respect for the king, (Exodus Rabbah 7:3)

Rashi points out that even in this time of great emotion, Moshe kept a level head and continued to respect the authority of the king. He deliberately chose less provocative language to accord respect to the throne.

This interpretation is significantly different than that of Reish Lakish as expressed in Zevachim:

And is it true that for every burning anger that is stated in the Torah, its effect is also stated? But isn't it written with regard to Moses: "And he went out from Pharaoh in hot anger" (Exodus 11:8)? And Moses did not say anything to Pharaoh. Reish Lakish said: Moses slapped him and left.

The Gemara is trying to defend the assertion that whenever the Torah describes an emotional state of "hot anger" there are explicitly mentioned consequences. Our episode seems to contradict this rule as there is no explicit consequence to Moshe's anger. However, according to Reish Lakish Moshe did not simply walk away from the interaction outwardly unchanged by his emotional state. He slapped Pharaoh!

This is a very different picture than what is painted by the Gemara Rashi quotes. Ben Yehoyada finds a third route, a middle ground between these extremes.

He explains that Reish Lakish could not mean a literal slap in the face as this would contradict the principles of respect for authority we have already established. Rather, Moshe would regularly leave his meeting with Pharaoh in a deferential way, without ever turning his head away from the thone. At the end of this meeting though, Moshe slightly turned away from Pharaoh while exiting, never fully facing his back to him but turning enough that it was a noticeable slight.

These three opinions of Moshe's resistance represent a wide range of reactions that are justifiable in the face of bad authority. Sometimes like the Gemara quoted by Rashi, we need to remain humble and respectfully communicate our demands. Other times, we are forced to take it a step further and violate a social norm to make clear that we cannot fully recognize the authority of a despot. And sometimes, given the opportunity, we have to slap a tyrant.

Shabbat Shalom

Parshat

Beshalach

The Nature of Doubt

January 18, 2019

After Israel's darkest exile Pharoh finally lets his slaves go. As Jews rush toward the border nobody is sure of their future. Will Pharoh change his mind and dispatch his army to catch up with the Jews at the Red Sea? Will the sea in fact split? How will we survive in the desert after our escape? Can we go to war? Can we just sell ourselves back into bondage? All this uncertainty is met by the most miraculous display of walls of water and columns of fire Egyptians and chariots drowning and Israel bursting into songs of praise. Women and men sing and dance with Miriam and her tambourine. We are led into the desert where manna falls from heaven and quail fly by slow and, around dinner time. We are clearly God's chosen people until Amalek cuts into our path where we are weak and all goes into question. Moses himself renames the spot on the path calling it "challenge and strife" as if to say "is God amongst us or not?"

This is not only the question Pharoh wanted to know before letting us go but it is also a question a quarter or more of the Jewish population wanted to know. For our sake God answered doubt with miracles; for Pharoh, he answered with plagues, and for us wonders and signs. At each moment of doubt, Pharaoh's heart grew colder, I can only assume that the quarter of jewish doubters also had icy hearts. There were those who said, "Let us throw ourselves into the sea." A second group said, "Let us return to Egypt." A third faction argued, "Let us wage war upon the Egyptians." Finally, a fourth camp advocated, "Let us pray to G-d."

When fear and doubt are rampant chaos ensues. When fear and doubt are used for political leverage this is exile and slavery. the consequences are long and drawn out and every day becomes a battle to remember being free and the promise of living a good life. However in the barracks of a slave camp hope is hard to find. Miriam and the women of israel are attributed with sustaining hope in the community. When moses was born his parents put him and his basket in the Nile hoping he may live a better life than their own. Miriam his older sister could not hold back her fears she had doubt and watched and followed the baby until he was in the right hands. Instead of accepting her tormented reality she saw it through. When women have a fear they don't turn around until they see a resolution. As a result they go through infinitely more suffering than those who hope for the best, this is why when resolution does come at the splitting of the sea she and the women sing a more elaborate praise to god because though she was afraid miriam stayed tuned with her hope for a new generation.

In the aftermath of our escape from bondage, we are met with fear in the desert. What is there to eat? God gives us manna but we are skeptical the term "mon" has a few translations, some say the word was adopted from Egyptian. "Mon" means "what?" no really that's what it means, so it is no wonder why god wanted us to eat our doubts before leading us to Sinai. It is also no wonder why some of us wanted Egyptian slave food, we cleave to our doubts as a source of freedom in times of inescapable dominance.

At this point jews have just witnessed the greatest, most out right miracle short of the giving of the Torah, likewise, the world had witnessed what God did for us. This gave us reassurance in our displacement but this confidence was not enough to scare amalek. This is the nature of doubt, some doubt comes logically with no motive, other doubt comes with the sole motive of clouding our rational mind. Amaleks doubt in our relationship with the creator is definitely not logical because nations stood witness to the signs and wonders of exile. Their doubt could not be coming from personal motives because we were a brand new slave nation with no political past. Amaleks desire to wage war is nothing more than a cynical shrug of skepticism, that shook us to our core. "To what is the incident (of Amalek) comparable? To a boiling tub of water which no creature was able to enter. Along came one evildoer and jumped into it. Although he was burned, he cooled it for the others. So, too, when Israel came out of Egypt, and G-d split the sea before them and drowned the Egyptians within it, the fear of them fell upon all the nations. But when Amalek came and challenged them, although he received his due from them, he cooled the awe of the nations of the world for them." We are at war for generations with Amalek because what they represent is the archenemy of Jewish thought. Nobody is safe from baseless doubt when faced with fear without a bond to their creator.

Moses's response to each jews uncertainty at the shore is befitting. To those who wanted to jump into the ocean in a leap of faith moshe pointed out that even if it worked it would only work for them, these men are tzadikim their trust absolute yet entirely impractical unless everyone levels up. They live in a spiritual atlantis.

The stockholm Jew thinks it is great to be a chosen people but we are a minority if we want representation we need pharaohs political regard. It will be hard having two masters we will give it a try.

The warrior Jew does not isolate or hide from his wretched reality. He knows his path is not only just, but "chosen". He's got a holy chip on his shoulder and battles sinners, jew -haters, un- jewish-jews and non-fighting jews alike. He will knock some sense into the world that spat him out.

The spiritualist when thrown into an ocean of doubt takes a moment to pray. They are not quick to try to defy nature, yet they believe there is a place for jews beyond the constraints of exile, he does not violently tread water in an attempt to rise out of the ocean victorious. He takes a pause and reminds himself life in the future gets better, we prefer the law and order the torah puts forth and fighting anything ends up damaging one's self. So the spiritualist prays and when the order comes down from heaven we all go in unison. This is a miracle less because of the wonders and more because of our survival, when one's very existence goes into question our trust in prayers is challenged, if we seize the opportunity and name our situation and desired outcome no matter what comes into play seems predestined and miraculous. Maybe this is why we were attacked by amalek, by God allowing the amalekites to attack our caravan he made us reaffirm the ground we are standing on and often that is all it takes to witness a miracle. Good Shabbos!

Yosef · February 7, 2020

As the Israelites marched triumphantly out of Egypt, still giddy with freedom, they found themselves between a rock and a hard place. Behind them, they were pursued by the might and majesty of the Egyptian army, while in front of them loomed the impenetrable Sea of Reeds. Chaos ruled.

Sensing the impossibility of the situation, our leader Moshe took the only sensible course of action, engaging in fervent prayer to the Creator. Just what you would expect him to do. But Hashem didn't approve of this decision, and responded with some very cryptic words. "Why are you crying out to me? Speak to the Children of Israel and tell them to travel."

The Ohr Hachaim formally asks the questions that must bother all of us. Firstly, what should a Jew do in a time of need but cry out to Hashem? And second, where exactly should they travel with the sea just as wet as ever?

The Ohr Hachaim answers with a life-altering lesson. He says that to merit a miracle, one must be vindicated by the divine trait of mercy. If there is an accusation against him which does not allow a merciful judgment, prayer alone cannot make one deserving of the miraculous.

At that time, there was a great heavenly accusation being leveled at the Jewish people. The Jews had worshiped idols in Egypt just like the Egyptians, so why were they deserving of victory at the enemy's expense. Because of this charge, prayer was of no use – they simply did not deserve to be saved.

What then is one's recourse? They must take the next step themselves, throwing their lot in with G-d. When they act out of absolute faith in Hashem, by relying only on Him, that faith itself can increase their merits and make them deserving of salvation.

This was Hashem's message to Moshe. There is no room for prayer to improve your situation; therefore, you must tell the people to march headlong into the water, relying on Me to save them. And that is exactly what happened. Nachshon the son of Aminadav heroically took charge and walked confidently into the water until it reached his nose – when Hashem saw the faith which the Jews placed in Him, he split the sea and smote their enemies.

Each of us face challenges in life which seem insurmountable. We find ourselves with enemies on all sides and no route of escape. Hopeless. Sometimes we even cry out to G-d, but to no avail. At times like these, we need to learn this lesson, and take a step in the right direction, even if it seems like it's into a brick wall. We have to trust in Hashem to fill in where we fall short, and in this way we can merit our own splitting of the sea.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Avi · January 28, 2021

In this week's Parsha the Jews were sent out of Egypt in an epic display of open miracles. God splits the sea and guides the emancipated nation into the desert, lighting their way with a pillar of fire. In this week's parsha God does not pull strings behind a curtain, instead God lights up the sky with wonders beyond comprehension. If He had split the sea for us. Dayenu! If He had led us through on dry land. Dayenu! If He had drowned our oppressors. Dayenu! If He had provided for our needs in the wilderness for 40 years. Dayenu! If He had fed us manna. Dayenu! - it would have been enough!

The generation of Jews that left Egypt did not only experience the miraculous, they were sustained by heavenly bread. As fugitive slaves in a scorched landscape they saw water flow from rocks and collected manna that fell from the sky. Today we commemorate the miracle of manna by having two loaves of bread on Shabbos.

The Halachos of a double portion come from what we observed in the desert with manna. Manna would fall in the morning onto a layer of dew that protected it, hence we cover the bread with a cloth, to resemble the protective layer of dew.

In order to count as a double portion the loaves must be complete, however if it is missing less than 1/48th of its mass it may still be considered complete. If a loaf is broken it may be propped with a stick to appear complete, or may be re-baked rendering both sides of the previously "broken" loaf complete.

God gave a double portion of mana on Shabbos so we wouldn't have to walk to collect it. The gift of Shabbos makes our miracles multiply and allows us to be close with God in ways that slaves cannot. Shabbos is what makes Jews free. Good Shabbos!

2022

Bnai Yisrael runs free from Mitzrayim, but Pharaoh, changing his mind, gives chase. At the sea, Moshe lifts his staff and the waters split; Israel crosses on dry land, protected on all sides, while the sea closes behind them to drown Pharaoh's army. Bnai Yisrael sings the Song of the Sea, which we still recite each morning.

But gratitude fades quickly in the desert -- the people grow hungry and thirsty and complain to Moshe. G-d sweetens the bitter waters of Marah, draws water from a rock, and sends quail and manna to nourish them, instructing that a double portion be gathered on Fridays since none will fall on Shabbat. At the Parsha's end, Amalek attacks, and thanks to Moshe's prayer and Yehoshua's army, Israel prevails.

This Shabbos is called Shabbat Shira, for Miriam's Song of the Sea. Miriam's name means "bitter" -- she was born into exile, watching her infant brother float down the Nile. Her unique experience as a woman in exile gave her song the power to move an entire nation into song and dance. Our tradition counts ten notable songs of redemption across Tanach; the midrash says a tenth, "new song," will one day mark the final redemption -- not merely a new melody, but an entirely new musical language we cannot yet imagine.

Each and every Jew alive today is the result of a miracle, and throughout life's challenges we learn which coping mechanisms carry us through. But if all we do is cope and forget to move forward, we remain in Egypt.

Parshat

Yitro

The Princess and the Pauper

Yosef · January 24, 2019

There's a widespread misconception that you cannot tell someone how to feel. You can tell them not to kill, but you can't tell them not to hate. You can tell them to act kindly, but you can't tell them to love. You can tell them not to steal, but you can't tell them not to covet. This school of thought claims that our feelings are automatic impulses which are out of our control. Yet the Torah teaches us otherwise. We are commanded on many occasions to control and direct our inner feelings themselves. It is not enough to refrain from hurting another; we must remove the hate from our hearts. It is not enough to act kindly; we must love our brother as ourselves. And it is not enough to refrain from stealing; we may not even desire that which belongs to our fellowman.

How can this be expected from a person? The answer is that feelings come from our thought processes and our actions. By thinking positive thoughts about another we can indeed direct our feelings toward them. Likewise, by actions of kindness to another, we can plant love in our hearts.

What about desiring another's possessions? One of the Ten Commandments states, “You shall not desire your fellow's wife, nor his slave, his maidservant, his ox, his donkey, nor anything that belongs to your fellow.” Jealousy is one of the most deep-seated feelings in the human psyche. A one-year-old infant will already cry when his mother holds another child. For children, one of the most frequent complaints is that a sibling or friend has more than themselves. And isn't the idea of “keeping up with the Joneses” the single greatest cause of unhappiness in the adult world? How are we expected to rid ourselves of this most natural of feelings?

The Ibn Ezra offers a parable. If a simple peasant sees a beautiful princess, he will not even desire to marry her because he knows that she is totally and completely outside of his reach. He knows that never in a million years can he achieve such a notion and thereby his heart does not even desire it. We must realize, says the Ibn Ezra, that one can only have that which is granted to him by heaven. Anything which was given to your friend and not to you is completely and totally out of your realm. If we will realize this, then we will completely cease to desire our friends' possessions. We will fulfill the commandment of the Torah and simultaneously enable ourselves to live happier, more fulfilled lives.

Good Shabbos, Yosef

Perfection

Noah · February 14, 2020

The events of this week's parsha cement Moshe as the archetype for human achievement. While the revelation at Har Sinai was supposed to be for all of Bnei Yisrael to experience together, after the first commandment it was clear that only Moshe had achieved the spiritual perfection necessary to comprehend and deliver the message without dying. His relationship with God was fundamentally different from that of anyone and his level of prophecy exceeded that of all prophets that came before or after. Moshe is closest we get to a perfect person and yet at the outset of this episode we see a lackluster family reunion.

וַיָּבֹ֞א יִתְר֨וֹ חֹתֵ֥ן מֹשֶׁ֛ה וּבָנָ֥יו וְאִשְׁתּ֖וֹ אֶל־מֹשֶׁ֑ה אֶל־הַמִּדְבָּ֗ר אֲשֶׁר־ה֛וּא חֹנֶ֥ה שָׁ֖ם הַ֥ר הָאֱלֹהִֽים׃

Jethro, Moses' father-in-law, brought Moses' sons and wife to him in the wilderness, where he was encamped at the mountain of God.

וַיֹּ֙אמֶר֙ אֶל־מֹשֶׁ֔ה אֲנִ֛י חֹתֶנְךָ֥ יִתְר֖וֹ בָּ֣א אֵלֶ֑יךָ וְאִ֨שְׁתְּךָ֔ וּשְׁנֵ֥י בָנֶ֖יהָ עִמָּֽהּ׃

He sent word to Moses, "I, your father-in-law Jethro, am coming to you, with your wife and her two sons."

In these two posukim we see a stark contrast in the presentation of Moshe's relationship with his children. In the first posuk, the Torah tells us that Eliezer and Gershom are Moshe's sons. In the second, Yitro pointedly refers to them as only the sons of Tziporah, Moshe's wife.

The Sefer Chibba Yezteria picks up on this inconsistency and resolves in it a way that seems to challenge the notion of Moshe as a perfect person:

ואשתך ושני בניה עמה. בניה ולא בניו, כיון שלא סייע לגדלם לכן נקראו על שמה.

They are referred to as her children and not his because he didn't help raise them.

This explanation can be combined with the Ibn Ezra and other Rishonim's position that Yitro's arrival chronologically occurred after the Torah was given. According to these commentators, while the entire nation watched Moshe ascend to the greatest spiritual height, his immediate family was nowhere to be found.

In light of their absence, the following passage from Bamidbar Rabba is not very surprising.

"Moses said, "See it is the time for me to claim my needs. If daughters inherit, it is [also] proper for my sons to inherit my glory." The Holy One, blessed be He, said to him (in Prov. 27:18), "He who tends a fig tree will enjoy its fruit.' Your sons sat with their own concerns and were not involved with Torah study. Whereas Joshua served you much and showed you much honor. And he got up early to your house of meeting and left late. He arranged the benches and spread out the mats. Since he served you with all of his ability, he is worthy to serve Israel, so as not to lose his compensation." (Numb. 27:18) "Take Joshua bin Nun," in order to fulfill what is stated (in Prov. 27:18), "He who tends a fig tree will enjoy its fruit."

Moshe wanted his honor to be passed to his progeny. He wanted Gershom and Eliezer to become central leaders of the Jewish people, following in his footsteps. But they couldn't. They weren't raised by Moshe, they didn't have the opportunity to arrange the benches in Moshe's beis medrash or accord him honor. They missed the central revelation of Judaism and their father's greatest achievement. And still, Moshe is the example of the full realization of human potential.

To resolve this we have to accept that there is no such thing as human perfection. Being human requires making decisions and every decision we make has unforeseen consequences and tradeoffs. Every moment of our time invested somewhere comes with the opportunity-cost that it could have been spent somewhere else. Moshe's single focus on spiritual development enabled him to reach the highest heights but came at the expense of family life.

I find this perspective inspiring. Through humanizing Moshe we can see the tremendous personal sacrifice he was willing to make on behalf of his people. He sacrificed his role as a father and as the first king in a birthright monarchy. He endured years of separation from his wife all while shouldering the burden of a stiff-necked people. And all of this was done while being a person. While experiencing desires and emotions and frustration and longing Moshe still managed to commit himself fully to his goal of actualizing his spiritual potential. And although there are tradeoffs and compromises Moshe proved the sky's the limit.

Shabbat Shalom,

Noah

What is Life Without Torah?

Yosef · February 5, 2021

Yisro was a powerful and wealthy priest in Midyan, living in the lap of luxury. And yet, when he heard about the miracles of the Exodus, he was moved to leave behind everything and venture into the desert to join with Hashem's people and become a Jew. Our sages were amazed by the dedication and sacrifice displayed by this heretofore unaffiliated idolater, so they set out to discover just what Yisro had heard which had such a profound impact on the course of his life.

Rashi, in one explanation, explains that it was the war with Amalek which really got Yisro going. At face value, this is a reference to the miraculous salvation which Hashem had performed for the Jewish people, giving over the mighty army of Amalek to their hands. Such an understanding would simply render that it was Hashem's special protection of the Jewish people which Yisro also sought to enjoy.

But under further speculation, it seems unlikely that this is Rashi's intention. Firstly, if the victory was the defining point here, Rashi should have called it the 'victory over Amalek', whereas the wording 'the war of Amalek' would seem to point to something about the war itself as opposed to the ultimate result of the war. Second, if the much more stunning and miraculous display of wonders which had brought Egypt to its knees had not yet inspired Yisro, it would be rather strange that the much smaller-scale victory over Amalek would suddenly light the spark.

Rabbi Yaakov Neiman, answers these questions with a striking change of perspective. It was not excitement for the Jewish victory which drove Yisro, but disgust for the actions of Amalek which made him long for something greater.

To understand, let's try to picture the times in which this battle took place. Egypt was the greatest nation of antiquity; the wealthiest, the most powerful, and the most advanced. You can be sure that the goings-on in Egypt were very well circulated across the globe. Suddenly, the front pages of every worldwide newspaper were filled with the unbelievable destruction taking place right in the home of this world power. They told of the water supply turning to blood, of the frogs and lice which gave them no rest, of wild beasts and pestilence. Next came news of boils and fire-filled hail, of locusts the likes of which no one could dream, and the week of absolute darkness. Finally, they told of the death of every firstborn and the civil war which it brought upon the already shattered nation. There were even some pictures of Pharaoh, pajama-clad, begging Moshe to leave in the middle of the night, which immediately went viral.

And in case somebody didn't read the news or had something against social media, they couldn't possibly have missed the splitting of the sea when ALL bodies of water in the entire world miraculously split. With your bathwater and drinking water splitting before your eyes, you couldn't help but ask around and try to figure out what on earth was going on over here. And every person one bumped into was talking about it, at bars and at bus stops, at social gatherings and when passing a neighbor in the street, there was just one topic of conversation -- the miracles and retribution taking place in Egypt. All around the world, people spoke about the greatness of Hashem, about His special care and love for the Jews, and about the gift of the Torah which He was about to give his people. The Jews were universally revered, celebrated, and feared.

It was at this incredible juncture in history, when Amalek reared its ugly head, refused to be moved by the miracles of which they knew, and did the unthinkable act of waging war against the fledgling Jewish nation.

This apathy and refusal to learn the lessons staring Amalek in the face is what brought Yisro to the decision that he could no longer remain a member of the nations of the world. As he witnessed just how blind man can be without the Torah to guide his life, he knew that he must join the nation of G-d and have the Torah for himself -- no matter what it would cost him. He realized that man, when left to his own self-concocted value system, can see the undeniable truth before his eyes and remain unchanged and unyielding. He understood that, when driven by our own distorted view of right and wrong, one can literally march to his grave in the name of the greatest perversions, while convinced of his own righteousness. And he knew that the only way perceives the world through a lens of truth and clarity, is by subscribing to the unchanging morals and ideals of Hashem himself, as communicated through the Torah.

We have lived through, and continue (please G-d) to live through, unprecedented times. Since the Exodus, there have not been many events in history that have caused such a disruption in our way of life, or demanded such introspection and thought as the novel Coronavirus. Like the Exodus, it has captivated and terrified the world in a way no one would have thought possible. No one has been spared of its devastation, and no one can possibly have missed the clarion call imploring us not to turn a blind eye, but to change.

And yet, we see the world around us striving mightily to put this whole story behind us, forget about it, and continue onward to the same abyss toward which it has been hurdling for decades. The world wants nothing of teshuva and self-improvement; it can view life as we know it halted and destroyed, but remain unmoved and unchanged.

The hopelessness of such a worldview and culture, which will follow its desires to the ends of the world unwilling to heed the call to change, must shake us to our core. It must light within us deep unrest with the society around us, and ignite a burning drive to rededicate ourselves to the Torah and its path of truth and self-perfection.

Good Shabbos, Yosef

Strangers in a Strange Land

Yosef · January 21, 2022

Yeedle was a peddler who lived with his family in a tiny shtetl in Poland. Once a year, Yeedle would travel to the other end of the country by horse and buggy to attend the grand trade show for two weeks. It was a long and arduous journey both ways and the stay at the show was no picnic, but Yeedle knew that his investment for those two weeks was the way that he could provide for his family for the entire year to come. During the show, as you can imagine, Yeedle barely ate or slept. He was singularly focused on the work at hand, and couldn't afford to waste a minute of this precious opportunity.

One year however, Yeedle ran into an old childhood friend whom he hadn't seen for years. For old times' sake, he gave his old friend a few minutes of his time, but after a short while he excused himself to return to his business ventures. His friend, however, wasn't so keen on cutting their conversation short, nudged Yeedle to come with him to the local tavern for some well-deserved rest and good times.

Do you think Yeedle took the bait? Of course not.

Yeedle understood full well that the entire year ahead was dependent on what he could produce in these two weeks. Sure some relaxation sounded nice, but it was a pleasure he simply could not afford. Instead of being lured by his friend's invitation, Yeedle was incensed -- feeling like his friend was trying to ruin his livelihood. Without a second thought, Yeedle turned and marched out the door, bidding his friend a terse farewell.

Moshe had two sons, Gershom and Eliezer, who were both named after events in Moshe's life. "The first was named Gershom, because he said 'I was a stranger (ger) in a strange land', and the name of the other was Eliezer, 'because the G-d of my father was my helper (ezri) and he saved me from the sword of Pharaoh.'"

The Chofetz Chaim asked two questions on these verses. First of all, Moshe named them out of order: chronologically his salvation from Pharaoh preceded his sojourn in Midyan? And second, why is the fact that he was a stranger in a strange land an event worth naming his child after?

The Chofetz Chaim explains that Moshe was concerned that his stay in the idolatrous land of Midyan, far away from any traces of Judaism would have an effect on him, and he might come to learn from their ways with the passing of time. To this end, he immediately named his firstborn son Gershom, as a personal reminder that this was not his place. Every time Moshe spoke the name of his son, he reiterated to himself that his stay in this place was only temporal and he would soon return to his people. Moreover, it was a message to himself about this world at large, which is also only a short journey until we will return our soul to its maker. In this way, Moshe was able to keep a constant vigil and never lose track of his ideals and his goals. Since this name was a safeguard for Moshe's very identity, it had to proceed the name commemorating his salvation from Pharaoh, even though it was out of order.

The Chofetz Chaim now draws our attention to the story with which we started. He explains that it is a parable to our life in this world. The true home of our soul is in the highest heavens, where it was hewn from beneath the heavenly throne itself. But in the heavens, the soul cannot achieve and it cannot earn the mitzvos which are the provisions necessary to sustain it for eternity. To this end, each soul is sent on a short business trip, as it were, to this world. It is a hard and strenuous journey, very far from its spiritual comfort zone, but the soul makes the trek because it knows that this is its only opportunity to provide for itself for all time. The soul arrives in this world with a singular drive to fulfill its mission before the time comes to return home, but along the way it encounters many a 'friend' who try to distract it with the cheap pleasures of this world. If the soul listens to these urges and allows itself to be deterred from its mission, the results can be catastrophic -- it only has one chance to earn a living for eternity. Just like our friend Yeedle, we need to keep the goal clearly before our eyes in order to withstand the temptations which are constantly pulling at us. Like Moshe, we need to find ways to constantly reiterate to ourselves the fact that we do not belong here, but are just passing through for a short time.

Practically speaking, though, not everyone has the opportunity to name their son Gershom, so what can we do to keep the message before our eyes? I think everyone has to find their own reminders, but the main thing is to find every excuse we can to think about the World to Come. If we hear of a death r"l, we should take a moment to remind ourselves that we won't live forever. When we see over-the-top physical indulgence, we should quietly condemn to ourselves the lack of priorities we are witnessing. When we do a mitzvah, we should consider the fact that these will be the only things which will be ours for eternity. If we are tempted by a misdeed, we should realize the everlasting damage of such actions. In this way, we can hope to properly utilize the immense opportunity of life and be able to return 'home' heavily laden with mitzvos to last an eternity.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Parshat

Mishpatim

Lobes and Liberty

Noah · February 1, 2019

This week's Parsha, Mishpatim, is the foundation of Jewish Justice. From verses in the parsha, we learn about Jewish civil and criminal law. Of all of the ways that the Torah teaches us how to pursue justice, the law governing a Jewish slave stands apart. If a Jew is caught stealing and cannot repay his debt or is too poor to sustain himself he can be sold as a slave. If after his 6 years of servitude, with freedom on the horizon, he chooses to remain with his master and family his ear is pinned to the doorpost and bored. While other punishments seem deeply linked to the crime, an eye for an eye, in the case of a Jewish slave, it seems to be liberty for a lobe. The boring of a hole in the body of a trusted domestic servant seems awfully corporal coming from a tradition that is want to interpret physical discipline as monetary. Additionally, we don't find this punishment for any other crime. While other physical punishments are consistent across a variety of cases like lashings and capital sentences, boring a hole in the ear on a doorpost is entirely unique to the Jewish slave that recommits to bondage. Why?

The first place to look for clarity is Rashi. But he begins his explanation of this with a seemingly insignificant clarification. On the verse, "...and his master shall bore his ear..." Rashi explains, "The right ear, for if you would think it is referring to the left ear, we have already learned (in the case of a leper) that when "ear" is mentioned it is referring to the right one." Only after this explanation does Rashi ask, "Why was the ear chosen to be bored? Rabbi Yochanan ben Zakkai said, 'The ear that heard "You shall not steal" and then stole should be bored.' And if one sold himself into servitude, the ear that heard, 'For the children of Israel are slaves to Me' and acquired himself a master should be bored." Rashi continues, Rabbi Shimon used to interpret this verse in a beautiful way. Why were the doorposts singled out from all the fixtures in the house? God said, the door and doorposts were witnesses in Egypt when I passed over doorposts and I said, "For the Children of Israel are slaves to Me, they are My slaves," but they are not slaves to slaves yet this one went and acquired a master, his ear should be bored for everyone to see. Immediately, Rashi's explanation is confounding. If, as he says, this procedure is a punishment for stealing or selling himself, why is it only imposed at the end of his servitude, once he has presumably served his time? Why does this only apply to slaves who "renew their contracts?"

When he committed his original sin, it was presumed to be out of desperation. What person who values freedom risks it to steal more than he can repay? Who values self-determination and decides to enter servitude voluntarily when they have other viable options? For 6 years we allow this desperate person to pull themselves back together in the closed confines of a set schedule and affluent family. He is given a chance to show that the crime he committed is not a part of him, he is merely a temporary victim of circumstance. At the end of this period, he is given the ultimate opportunity for a fresh start. For this person, the 6 years of servitude proved to be both rehabilitation and discipline. If he rejects this chance to go on, it reveals something about his original crime and his mindset. If a person does not value self-determination, then slavery is more comfortable than freedom. His original crime cannot be assumed to have been committed under extreme duress because the consequence was not even severe enough for him to reject it when offered. For him, there has to be a more severe consequence, a permanent reminder that this person has sacrificed the ultimate attribute that can be dedicated to the service of God. His freedom.

With this perspective, we can understand Rashi much better. The right side is considered to be dominant over the left. This means that a punishment affecting the right side is considered to be more severe than one affecting the left.* Rashi has such a strong preconception that the punishment for a crime of desperation must be merciful that he has to explain specifically why the right ear is chosen. Once Rashi establishes that this punishment is meant as a specifically harsh one from the case of a leper, he struggles to understand why. He brings the first two explanations but as we wondered, they don't seem specifically related to his decision to stay enslaved. Only once Rabbi Shimon delivers the final "beautiful" exposition does Rashi's commentary finally crystallize. The decision to stay enslaved shows that this person was not acting out of extreme desperation when they stole or sold themselves. Furthermore, their time in servitude was not dedicated to reclaiming independence but becoming more immersed in and defined by the circumstances of their slavery. For this reason, the Torah tells us to make a defining mark to separate these people who reject the incredible gift of true freedom given to us through the Exodus. While we were redeemed to dedicate ourselves to service of God, they dedicated themselves to service of another.

It is so easy to slip into the slavery mindset. While I occasionally start the week feeling in control I rarely feel so empowered by Thursday. I become a slave to my routine instead of using my week to serve God. But, as the Torah shows me, desperate situations happen. Sometimes I need 6 days of slavery to recommit to my loftier ideals. But if when Shabbos arrives, instead of being freed from the servitude of the week I choose to remain bound to my material concerns, I have chosen slavery over freedom. We should all merit to have the confidence, strength, and commitment to choose freedom in our lives!

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

*This sentence was edited in later editions to correct an error. Thanks to Yosef for his commitment to reading understanding and engaging with the Dvar weekly!

Avi · February 19, 2020

In Parsha Mishpatim Bnai Yisroel are commanded many laws that remain applicable to our daily lives. Laws of damages, rentals, loans, and the actual laws of how to appoint judges are all in this Parsha. We learn that if a man has an ox and it gores he is liable to pay the damages, but only half. This is because an ox is not a yellow ferrari, it is a "tam" - simple low risk animal. If the animal however gores three times it gets a new status as a "muad"- warned animal. Under this new status the owner is liable to pay for the damages in full.

The Rabbis explain that we each have an ox and an owner inside of us, the ox is not necessarily destructive but he is selfish. If his selfishness becomes destructive we assume that is in error, and the exception not the rule. If the ox stays on his path un supervised he will make the same mistakes into selfish habits, grazing and goring. His reputation will suffer and his owner will pay in full for his negligence.

The Mishnah tells us further of cases where "warned" oxen regained their status as low risk oxen! When the ox transfers owners and is sold he is given a new tag "innocent ox." The obvious next question is, how could a sale change an ox? The Rabbis teach that a single bad habit is impossible to overcome in isolation. A new environment and fresher hay and grain may do the trick.

Along with these most mundane rules of ox keeping we are also commanded in this Parsha of not mixing meat and milk the Chagim that take us through the calendar year and our daily prayers. Reminding us that rules and laws govern every aspect of our existence and without them we do not have freedom, we have nothing. Good shabbos!

Societal Sympathy

Noah · January 28, 2022

Mishpatim is densely packed with mitzvot addressing interpersonal obligations, financial responsibilities, and respect for authority. Together these comprise a large section of our moral fabric and are the backbone of a just society. It is curious then that the first commandment presented in this Parsha is the process of acquiring slaves. What about this process is representative of our judicial system and why is it our introduction to building an ethical society.

The Or HaChaim touches on an answer in his remarks on the first verse of the Parsha. He says that this mitzvah is not necessarily easy for the purchaser. He may feel entitled to keep the slave longer than the maximum six years set out in the Torah. But this six-year limit is still a benefit to him, as he may be in a similar situation someday. Every slave ended up where they are through financial desperation. And money has a way of rolling around and faced with desperate times the purchaser would act no different than the slave he bought. That is why these mitzvot are, "set in front of us" (תָּשִׂ֖ים לִפְנֵיהֶֽם) because they are for our future benefit and it is incumbent on all of us to feel as though the kindness in our systems touch us directly.

This lesson is played out over and over again in the Parsha. The extreme importance of empathy and trust in each other is an inescapable theme throughout all of these laws. We are instructed, "You shall not wrong a stranger or oppress him, for you were strangers in the land of Egypt." which asks us to look to our past to find compassion in the present. And immediately afterward we are commanded, "You shall not ill-treat any widow or orphan. If you do mistreat them, I will heed their outcry as soon as they cry out to Me, and My anger shall blaze forth and I will put you to the sword, and your own wives shall become widows and your children orphans." Here we are directed to imagine a painful future to use as a source of sympathy.

Later we are warned again: You shall not oppress a stranger, for you know the soul of the stranger, having yourselves been strangers in the land of Egypt. And Rashi succinctly expresses this same sentiment in his comment on the words, "The soul of a stranger." He writes, "You know how hard it is for him when he's oppressed."

In light of the incredible focus on empathy throughout the Parsha, it is more evident how slavery is a perfectly representative process for our ethical system. We had just been freed! Slavery was the national trauma that was still resonating deeply enough to evoke our sincerest sensitivities. We needed to connect to this experience so that we could truly build a society based on compassion and mutual respect and only through amending what it means to be a slave could we start this endeavor.

Shabbat Shalom Noah

Logic and Equality

Noah · February 17, 2023

This week we read Parshat Mishpatim and its expansive list of laws covering a broad range of civil topics, including personal injury, property damage, theft, and various types of social obligations. The list is introduced simply: "And these are the laws that you must set before them."

Rashi comments on this, "God said to Moses: It should not enter your mind to say, "I shall teach them a section of the Torah or a single Halacha twice or three times until it will become current in their mouths exactly according to its wording (i.e. until they know the text verbatim), but I shall not take the trouble to make them understand the reason of each thing and its significance"; therefore Scripture says, אשר תשים לפניהם, "which thou shalt set before them" like a table fully laid before a person with everything ready for eating."

Rashi is understanding that this phrase is emphasizing the importance of learning the underlying reason behind these mitzvot. While we have other categories of Mitzvot that have very abstract reasons or meanings beyond comprehension, these civil laws must be taught with their reasons. Rashi explains in Eruvin that this is meant to avoid a situation where a teacher can say to a student, "This is what I heard from my teacher, go and understand it for yourself."

The importance of transmitting a logical understanding of Mitzvot may seem strange when we have the Parah Adumah (Red Heifer) and other Chukim where we seem to abandon logic in favor of maintaining a tradition.

The Gemara learns another lesson from this first line as well, "The school of Rabbi Elazar taught: The verse states with regard to the civil laws: "And these are the civil laws that you shall set before them" (Exodus 21:1). The reference to "them" in the verse is referring to all those who stood at the revelation, both men and women. The verse thereby equates a woman with a man with regard to all civil laws in the Torah."

This lesson of equality seems to run counter to other hierarchical systems that the Torah establishes elsewhere. Kohanim have different laws that govern them, women are generally exempt from time-bound mitzvot. Despite the importance of equality stressed in our Parsha we still have legal stratification built into our society.

To understand the tension between each of these values we have to understand what the point of these laws is. Conflicts between blind faith and reasoned understanding are not unique to the Jewish experience and the struggle to balance equality and individuality has been a primary force in shaping civilization forever. The Mishpatim in our Parsha form the bedrock of justice on which our particular Jewish society can be built. Our unique traditions and abstract religious ideals can only survive when they are protected by a logical framework. Our priests and social hierarchies must be predicated on a justice system that acknowledges fundamental equality.

This lesson can further be seen by the placement of these laws before Moshe even ascended to Mount Sinai to receive the Torah. These prerequisites were necessary for us to be able to enter a contractual relationship with our creator.

In a time when it can feel like logical governance and commitment to equality are out of vogue we have to remind ourselves that these values support the rich beautiful traditions that define our lives as Jews.

Shabbat Shalom Noah

Parshat

Terumah

Our Mishkan

February 8, 2019

Trumah focuses on the architecture of the Mishkan and the contributions that made it Gods temporary dwelling on earth. Thirteen contributions came from each household; gold, silver, copper, blue, purple, and red dyed wool flax, goat hair, animal skins, wood, olive oil, spices, and gems. God said to Moses "They shall make for Me a Sanctuary, and I shall dwell amidst them." the Mishkan was beautiful and every facet played a key role in building a home for Hashem. This week's Parsha is about designating living spaces. God is everywhere around us but only home in the places, we uplift for the divine.

A home is a place that greets you as you enter, it is a place you frequent and have established. A place devoted to your needs that allows the space for your privacy. This is the environment the Mishkan created for God and B'nai Yisrael. The Mishkan appealed to all our senses, the Mishkan's furnishings or "vessels," are seen as representations of the various organs and faculties of man: the Ark (containing the Torah) corresponds to the mind, intellect and speech; the Menorah, to the eyes and the sense of sight; the Table that held the "showbread," to the sense of taste; the Inner Altar on which the Ketoret (incense) was burned, to the sense of smell; and the Outer Altar on which the animal and meal offerings were brought represent the digestive system and other "functional" organs. The rituals in the Mishkan existed to excite all of our senses, in the desert in a tent this was necessary to dignify a holy place. Our senses are our only way to spirituality. Today synagogues and schools play the role of Gods designated places in our world, we are expected to keep a high level of decorum when visiting and wear the right dress code. We all come as individuals but while we are there we are indivisible.

When sharing space like this there are many rules, it becomes an obligation for a community of a minyans size to own a synagogue. It should be the tallest building in the town! Today that is unreasonable so one should be careful not to buy a home taller than the local synagogue. Contributions for the building and weekly herring should come from community according to households financial needs. The building should look like a holy temple, it should be highly ornamented with 12 windows that face Jerusalem. There must be an entryway so people can wash and compose themselves before walking into the back of the sanctuary. I division of men and women is appropriate in these places and the Chazan's bimah should not be directly in front of the ark for modesty's sake. The Torah is kept in the ark which faces the community, it has a curtain and doors like in the courtyard of the Beit Hamikdash. No art is hung in the sanctuary. These are a few of the facets of modern shuls but the Mishkan had many more! Good Shabbos!

Bringing in Adar

Adam · February 28, 2020

"When the month of Adar enters, we increase our happiness!" -The Sages of Blessed Memory

So Adar started this past Wednesday. The month in which arguably the happiest Holiday occurs, Purim. The holiday of dressing up, gift-giving, Tzedakah, festive meals, and singing and dancing until you're on the floor; what could be more happy?

But it's not enough to be happy on Purim, we are meant to be happy now, two weeks prior. Many point out that the wording increase implies we must always carry happiness but now is the time of abundance. The time to feel increasingly happy. The question is, how are we supposed to do this? What is meant to be happening that we should suddenly look at a calendar and start yodeling with joy?

The Sfas Emes explains that a cursory reading of the words of the sages will misguide us. One would assume the "entering" we refer to is the Jewish people entering the time-space of Adar. The Sfas Emes says we're actually talking about the month of Adar entering into us. It's not when we turn the calendar, but when internally, we bring Adar into us!

"Great! ...But how do I do that? What does it mean to bring Adar inside? How do I cause Adar to enter me and increase my happiness?"

The secret lies in a key juxtaposition of two encounters in the megillah.

The first is when Haman offers to buy the Jewish people for extermination at the price of 10,000 silver bars. This is an exorbitant sum of money from arguably the richest man alive at the time other than King Achashverosh himself.

The second is a discussion hiding in the Megillah. On his way to get Mordechai to parade him around the kingdom, Haman found Mordechai learning with thousands of children.
