He asked what Mordechai was learning and Mordechai responded, about the Mincha offering. The half-shekel that every Jew would give. At that moment, Haman realizes that the Jews half-shekel offering is going to win out against his 10,000 silver bars.

My Rebbe, Reb Moshe Weinberger says this is the secret to allowing Adar into our beings. Adar is all about the half-shekels. The way of Amalek and Haman, his descendant, was to create coldness by always looking toward the grandiose. 10,000 and it's all worth nothing unless every one of the Jewish people is exterminated.

But we fight with our half-shekel! Always focusing on the little things. When we bring the warmth of every little part of our day- every good morning, every smile, every bracha we make thinking about the words and relationship we have with the one above, every moment of remaining calm with our children, every time we stop and focus on our spouse with our full attention- all of the small things, we bring Adar into us. And when we live in the warmth of Yiddishkeit, we immediately become happy. When we allow Adar to enter us, we melt the coldness of Amalek with the warmth of a Judaism that sings to the one above!

And the beautiful thing is, it's always available. At every moment, there is a half-shekel to find. When we focus on each moment and what we can accomplish at that moment, we cause Adar to enter into us and become happier, more joyous, and more unified!

May we continue to grow together, one meaningful little step after another, until we all return home together, quickly in our days!

Love, Adam

Avi · February 18, 2021

Terumah means lifted apart, this week in Meme's Parsha we learn about all the ways the Torah makes our lives more beautiful. God said to Moshe, "Make me a sanctuary and I will rest there"

The Parsha enumerates the materials and dimensions for the Mishkan: gold, silver and copper; blue-, purple- and red-dyed wool; flax, goat hair, animal skins, wood, olive oil, spices and gems were to be donated by each household based on their financial means.

Today we build and design our schools and sanctuaries in the image of the mishkan and holy temple. It is not practical today to take on such a feat of architecture however many synagogues are beautiful nonetheless, with balconies and stages some even have stained glass. More often of course synagogues are in a basement, or the sixth floor of an apartment complex. Some busses on the east coast are really like the mishkan, portable little sanctuaries.

The responsibility of having a library and place to pray falls on the community as a whole, if a community cannot afford to own a place they must rent a place. Conversely, If they have the means to own they must buy. The synagogue should be as beautiful as possible without having art that may appear to be idol worship, curtains and architectural embellishments are encouraged yet pictures of even a Rabbi may be inappropriate on some walls.

Ideally the synagogue would be the tallest building in the city, because today that is unreasonable some are careful not to buy a house taller than the synagogue they belong to. Beauty and aesthetics work hand in hand to create a synagogue. When designing a room we choose things for comfort and purpose. When designing a room for God Beauty is number one priority. Building a synagogue from scratch gives us the opportunity to permanently construct the halls, curtains, and barriers that direct our lives as Jews. Good Shabbos!

February 2022

This week's Parsha is Terumah, "offering." Bnai Yisrael is commanded to contribute fine materials -- gold, silver, copper, dyed wools, flax, animal hides, oil, spices, and gems -- to build the Mishkan, a dwelling place for Hashem. On Mount Sinai, Moshe was instructed on how to build it so it could be disassembled, carried, and reassembled with ease, so the Jewish people could carry it with them anywhere in the desert.

The two tablets, kept safe in the ark behind a woven curtain in the innermost chamber, represent our unique bond and communal experience at Sinai. In the outer chamber sat a seven-branched menorah and a table of showbread. Every structural detail -- the wood posts, the walls, the multi-layered roof, the sixty surrounding posts and linen tapestries -- was designed with intention. Not all design is aesthetic; the Mishkan teaches that the best projects are those built to involve an entire community and to meet that community where it is.

Design is not just what we see, but how we use and interact with things. We will never see the Mishkan with our own eyes, but by learning the principles behind its construction, we can see our own lives improve. Good Shabbos!

One Heart

Yosef · February 24, 2023

The Mishkan was, so to speak, a house in this world for Hashem's presence. It was the holiest place in the world, the place most central to the service of G-d, and one of the greatest undertakings ever attempted by man. It was built with every vestige of wisdom and craftsmanship possessed by the Jewish people, as well as a huge amount of wealth, lovingly donated by the nation.

It would stand to reason that such great expense and effort were required because nothing less could possibly be fitting to 'house' the King of kings. Where else would the Shechina, the Divine Presence, reside if not in the most fantastic palace man can dream of?

But further examination reveals that the reality is not so. There is, in fact, another 'house' for the Shechina that is possibly even greater than the Mishkan.

Hashem commands, "Speak to the Jews that they should take a tithe for me; from each person whose heart moves him [to donate] you shall take my tithe... And you shall make for me a Mishkan and I will dwell in them."

The Alshich famously notes that the verse does not say that Hashem will dwell in it, meaning the Mishkan, but rather that he will dwell in them, meaning within the Jewish people. This means that the ultimate abode for the Shechina is not in the holy Mishkan, but rather in the hearts of the Jewish people!

However, this begs the question that if ultimately the 'home' for Hashem is not in the Mishkan but rather in our hearts, what do we need the Mishkan for in the first place?

Another question. The mitzvos in the Torah are not optional. Hashem doesn't say that whoever wants should keep the Shabbos, eat kosher, and don tefillin. Mitzvos by definition are commandments which we are to keep to whether or not we are in the mood. Why then, in this mitzvah of taking a tithe for the Mishkan, does the Torah specify that it should be taken only from those whose hearts move them to donate?

The Vilna Gaon offers a beautiful understanding, which changes our whole perspective of what the Mishkan was.

He explains that the Shechina was to dwell in the collective heart of the entire Jewish people, joined together as one. No single individual could merit housing the Shechina, but as one great collective Jewish heart, that heart could merit being a 'home' for Hashem. The only problem was how to achieve that unity of so many individual hearts, each with their own thoughts and feelings.

This was the role of the Mishkan, which served as a place where all the hearts of the Jewish people could come together as one, in one unified effort to bring Hashem's presence into this world. And so each person donated their most prized possessions, the things they loved and cherished most, with hearts brimming with love and devotion. They were not just giving money, they were giving a piece of their hearts. And, as each and every Jew came bearing their heart of hearts, they became unified as the ultimate abode for Hashem's presence.

So yes, the Shechina did dwell in the Mishkan, which is why it had to be such an impressive edifice, but the real beauty of the Mishkan was not the gold and the gemstones, but the heart of a nation expressed and contained within them. And of course, it goes without saying that such a tithe could only be brought by those driven from within – gold can be taxed, but hearts can only be gifted.

If we wish for the coming of Moshiach and the rebuilding of the Beit Hamikdash, then we must strive to lay the foundation for such a 'house' for Hashem, by joining and uniting our hearts with the hearts of each of our brothers.

Since this is much easier said than done, I'd like to conclude with one practical and fresh idea that has been on my mind. Perhaps all of our troubles, grievances, and arguments with our fellowman stem from a lack of thought and understanding. After all, if I am always convinced of the righteousness of my feelings and actions, then it would be likely that the person across from me feels the same way about his position. Most functional people don't do things that they feel are wrong, so if they are doing something, they probably have a reason for it. Just that I am so consumed with my own view that I am blind to any other vantage point.

What, though, would happen if we would spend some time putting serious thought towards understanding the people around us? Maybe it could be a quiet five minutes sitting on the couch, maybe just some contemplation while driving to work, or perhaps even some time thinking with a pen. Whatever it is, some dedicated thought trying to understand our friends is bound to have wonderful effects. What challenges does this person face? Which background does he come from? What does his family, financial, and social situation look like? In which areas does he excel? And then, how does the object of our disagreement fit into that bigger picture?

After such an exercise, I think we may just have a chance of seeing his side of the story; where he is justified or even correct in his actions. This regimen would take a decent amount of work, but I think it could make a drastic change for the better in our world. Through heightened understanding, we could all lead happier, kinder, more peaceful lives. And we can work towards achieving that unified Jewish heart which can indeed be the dwelling place for the Shechina.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Parshat

Terumah-Tetzaveh

34 – The Leper's Honor

Yosef · 2018

In the olden days, one who spoke badly about his fellowman was struck with leprosy. The leper was so sullied by his impurity that he was banished from the city and branded with this unmistakable mark of shame, his evil ways revealed for all to see. Only after his leprosy was healed was he allowed to rejoin civilization after an arduous purification process. Because he slandered his friend behind his back, as if no one would know, therefore he was punished with this public disgrace. The lesson so powerfully imparted is that every word we say is heard and recorded from on high.

And yet, despite the embarrassment heaped upon this man, it is exactly calculated -- beyond what's deserved we find an incredible sensitivity to his feelings. Tosafot tell us that each of the gates of the Beit Hamikdash were sanctified with the great holiness of the inner chamber (the azarah); a holiness so great that only one who was completely pure was permitted to enter. There was one exception to this rule -- the gate through which a leper was required to anoint his hands during his purification process. This gate was left on a lower level than all the others, allowing the impure to stand beneath the gate. Tosafot goes on to explain that this was not actually necessary. Just like they stood under the gate and stuck their hands into the inner chamber to be anointed, so too they could have stood outside the gate and extended their hands inside. Why, then was this gate left without the higher level of sanctity. The answer, says Tosafot, is for just one thing. That the leper shouldn't have to stand in the rain or the sun but should be able to find shelter beneath the arch of the gate.

This is mind-boggling. One can only imagine the effect every square inch of that great holiness had on the world. It was from that place that the entire universe drew sanctity and Godliness; surely every extra bit was precious beyond words. What's more, the process of anointing was quite brief, and he would only have had to wait a minute or so. Beyond that, who are we talking about? A sinner, one who badmouthed his fellow and payed no heed to the other's pain or discomfort. And for this petty concern and this lowly man's sake we're willing to forgo all of that Godliness in the entire universe?!

The answer is yes. Because this man has a history of disregarding an other's feelings, that is precisely why he needs this courtesy. As he comes to again join society, we enable him to learn the incredible sensitivity the Torah has towards every human being. We tell him that yes you are imperfect, and yes its just a minute in the heat anyway, but even so it is worth sacrificing so much just to care for you. Seeing such sacrifice for the honor and comfort of a single person, we hope that he can learn the lesson and begin again with renewed respect for others and right his previous wrongs. Furthermore, with this show of respect towards the leper, we aim to give him a true sense of self-respect, thereby removing his need to put down others in order to lift himself up.

So let's try to emulate the Torah's respect for our fellow, realizing that there is nothing more precious than another human being. Let's work on realizing our own self-worth and the respect the Torah accords each one of us, so that we need not knock other's to raise our own stature. And let's try to guard our tongues, taking great care not to ever hurt another with our careless or hateful words.

Have a terrific Shabbos, Yosef

Parshat

Tetzaveh

To Fill a Void

Yosef · February 15, 2019

A "filler" usually refers to something that doesn't have much of its own worth; it is just to make something else seem more full. It might refer to the rice or potatoes which are supposed to make the meal go further in place of the meat or fish that is the true centerpiece. It can describe the extra paragraph you stick into your essay just to meet the required number of words. Or the green leaves florists put into an arrangement of flowers to make it appear bigger. All these things lack personal worth or interest and are thus no more than fillers for the main part.

It's surprising, then, that the magnificent gems which appeared in the choshen, the High Priest's breastplate, were called avnei milu'im, or "filler stones". These gems included twelve of the world's most precious stones; diamond, sapphire, ruby, pearl and the like. Additionally, the gems were, rendering them more valuable than all of the gold and silver that was put into the tabernacle. If these stones were the most precious items of all, how is it that they are referred to as no more than simple "filler stones", as if they had no personal value?

The answer, says Rabbi Shmuel Berenbaum, contains a remarkable lesson in what the Torah considers true value. According to the Torah's benchmark, the true stature of a man is not according to his personal accomplishments and greatness, but rather by the degree to which he fills another's void. This is surely true with materialistic acquisitions; if one has attained great wealth but does not bestow kindliness upon others according to his means, then his wealth is essentially a waste. But even in spiritual attainments, the rule holds true. If someone has achieved great and lofty spiritual heights but does not share his wellsprings with others, this cannot be called true greatness. Only insomuch as one positively affects his fellowman can he be truly great.

It is for this reason that these most precious of gems are ranked not by their own incredible worth but by that which they fill the choshen, bringing it to its state of fulfillment.*

Of course, in order to make a true and lasting impression on another one must begin with internal growth and perfection. The goal should be to fill up one's own cup with knowledge and greatness of spirit, so much so that his cup overflows and naturally nourishes those around him. To spill out from one's cup without ever having it filled will only leave the giver as well as the receiver devoid of fulfillment. Just as the gems started with tremendous self-worth, so too we must look to help others from a place of self-perfection and achievement.

I'd like to close with a phenomenal thought from Rabbi Ephraim Shapiro of North Miami Beach. Throughout the Torah, the right side is always considered the more prestigious. It represents mercy as opposed to judgment and is almost universally favored by the Torah. We wash our right hand first, hang the mezuzah on the right side of the door, and even put our right shoe on first, to name just a few instances. Why, then, is the heart placed on the left side of our chest. Shouldn't this most central and important organ, both in a physical sense as well as a spiritual one, be accorded a place of honor on the right side?

The answer is that your heart was not created for you but for your fellow man! Yes, your heart is placed on your left, but when you face your friend it is on his right! It is for him that you are given the capacity to feel and to love, and it is therefore fittingly placed on your friend's right side.

Uncle Yonatan fully embodied this lesson. He determinedly strove for mind-boggling personal achievement but lived for anyone but himself. All of his many accomplishments were universally shared with each and every one of us who merited to be in his circle of influence. He was a giant among men, but with every level attained, he saw to it to pull us all up with him.

Let us constantly remember that we aren't here for ourselves but for others. May our ambition in life never be centered upon ourselves but on those whose lives we may touch and change. And may we forever remember Uncle Yonatan's remarkable ability to change the lives of so many and strive to emulate him always.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

*Heard from R' Yaakov Bender shlit'a

Hiding, But Never Far Away

Yosef · March 6, 2020

The Gemara states, "Where is Esther (אסתר) hinted to in the Torah? Because it says 'And I (G-d) will surely hide (אסתיר) my face on that day.'"

This is very puzzling. The deeper sources tell us that G-d hiding his face from us is the worst of curses. With the shine of Hashem's countenance comes all blessing and only when he hides can evil exist. Why then are Esther and the Purim miracles which came through her mentioned here of all places? Wouldn't it be more fitting to mention her in a place of heavenly good-will and blessing?

In good Jewish form, instead of answering let's ask another question. We find a very peculiar thing in the climax of the Megillah, during Esther's second party with Achashveirosh and Haman. Queen Esther finally feels ready to beseech the King to spare the Jewish people, and she opens with an impassioned and eloquent speech. "If the King pleases, let him give me my life as my request and my people as my desire. Because we have been sold, myself and my nation, to be annihilated, killed, and destroyed." The speech of a lifetime. And from then on out, the rest is history. Achashveirosh, infuriated, inquires as to the identity of he who would want to harm his precious Queen, Esther incriminates Haman who is promptly hung, and the Jews are freed of their arch-nemesis. Just as you would expect things to unfold, in perfect succession.

But for some reason, right between Esther's request and the King's response, the Megillah has a break. In the scroll, this break is an elongated blank space and in the books, it's noted with a letter "ס". Either way, such breaks are usually reserved to separate different topics, kind of like a new paragraph. So what is it doing here smack in the middle of their conversation?!

The answer encapsulates the primary lesson of the entire Megillah. What looks to us like the simple and expected way of the world, as innocent cause and effect, is none other than the hand of G-d. To the naked eye, it appears that a beautiful and beloved Queen succeeded to sway the heart of her adoring King through the power of persuasion. But in reality, the two are separate and unlinked. The cause did not have to achieve the effect, and Esther was truthfully unequipped to bring salvation. The only One running the show was, and always is, G-d himself.

This is why Esther is hinted at in the curse of Hashem hiding his face from us. Because Hashem may be hiding and we may not see his actions openly in the world, but truthfully it is Him running the entire show from behind the scenes. The miracles of Purim, unlike those of the Exodus or even of Channukah, didn't contain any outright breaches of the 'laws of nature', but anyone following the story would have to be insane to blame the uncanny chain of events on mere happenstance.

And so it is in our everyday lives. Yes, Hashem may be hiding, and no, He's not splitting any seas for us, but it is Him and Him alone who is lining everything up. Hiding, but never far away.

Good Shabbos and Happy Purim,

Yosef

Purity of Heart – Feeling for a Brother

Yosef · February 11, 2022

The Urim Vetumim were two of the most precious artifacts in the tabernacle. These two precious stones bore the names of each of the tribes and served to bring their memory before the Almighty. Additionally, when properly placed inside the Choshen (the breastplate of the high priest), these two stones possessed the remarkable ability to communicate the prophetic word of G-d, as well as the divine answers to any questions which were posed before them. The Kohen Gadol (high priest) would stand facing the Ark, while the questioner would stand behind him and voice the question. Then, the Urim Vetumim's letters would be illuminated before the Kohen Gadol in response to the question. Among other things, this was how the Jews would decide which battles should be fought and could know what the outcome of the battle would be.

Now that we understand the powerful and lofty nature of these stones, we can appreciate the Medrash which goes looking for an explanation as to why Aharon merited to wear the Urim Vetumim. Such a lofty adornment requires special permission to wear, and the Medrash finds something special about Aharon for which he earned the honors.

The heart that rejoiced at the greatness of his brother should wear the Urim Vetumim. As it states, 'And they shall be upon the heart of Aharon'.

When Moshe first encountered prophecy, at the burning bush, he tried to refuse the position as the redeemer of the Jewish nation, arguing that it would be a slight to his older brother Aharon's honor. Hashem responded that Aharon would feel no ill, to the contrary 'he will see you and rejoice in his heart'. In this episode, Aharon showed how pure his heart was; not only did he not feel jealous of his younger brother's position over him, he went so far as to rejoice at his brother's success. In this merit, Aharon was chosen as the proper bearer of the Urim Vetumim.

This is a beautiful story, but why specifically were the Urim Vetumim tied to this merit of rejoicing over his brother's success.

I believe the answer lies in the end of that very same verse. The verse, in it's entirety, reads: 'And you shall place the Urim Vetumim inside of the breastplate, and they shall be upon the heart of Aharon when he comes before Hashem, and Aharon will carry the ''mishpat'' of Israel upon his heart before Hashem, constantly.'

Chizkuni explains that the word 'mishpat' here means the needs of the Jewish people. He thus explains that Aharon was charged with forever bearing and mentioning before Hashem the things which the Jews needed, as communicated to him by the Urim Vetumim.

It would seem that only one who can fully rejoice at the good fortune of his brother is capable of truly understanding and feeling the needs and the pain of his fellowman. Most people run to participate in the wedding of even a casual acquaintance, but they are far more hesitant to attend a house of mourning, particularly in the event of a tragedy. Is this because it is easier to connect to the joy of our fellow than it is to connect to his pain? I think the opposite is true. Just that it is not so difficult to feign joy -- one can dance and sing and smile even if he doesn't feel it in his heart. But sharing in one's pain is something that cannot be just on the surface; if our heart isn't there people will feel it in a second.

When Aharon learned of Moshe's authority, he didn't merely smile and clap for the cameras. He rejoiced in his heart! Down to his very core, Aharon felt nothing but the happiness of his brother. No trace of jealousy or competition; just pure and unadulterated joy. Only such a pure and selfless heart can take on the task of carrying the needs of his people on a constant basis. Only the heart of Aharon could connect to the pain of his entire nation as if it were his own pain.

Here in Los Angeles, we have suffered two terrible tragedies in the last few weeks alone. First, a 16-month-old baby suddenly didn't wake up, and, shortly after, a young mother was killed in a car accident. After hearing such news, every person is shaken up, we feel badly, but then we move on just too quickly. It hurts us, but do we feel as if it is our pain? Do we carry the searing, unbearable wounds of our people upon our hearts?

If not, then we need to question the purity of our hearts and how much we really care about our brethren -- both in times of pain and in times of happiness.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

CORRECTION (sent two days later): I mistakenly wrote in this week's dvar that the Urim Vetumim were stones engraved with the names of the twelve tribes. This is incorrect. In truth, there is some question as to the exact nature of the Urim Vetumim, but the consensus among most commentators is that they were the written name of Hashem, possibly the ineffable seventy-two letter name. My apologies for the oversight.

2023

Parsha Tetzaveh and the holiday of Purim may seem to have little in common, but both share a theme of hiddenness and revelation. In Tetzaveh, Moshe relays G-d's commandments regarding the priestly garments -- yet Moshe's own name is not mentioned in the parsha, the only one in the Torah from which it is absent. During the sin of the golden calf, Moshe had told G-d, "If You destroy our nation, erase my name from Your book" -- and here, G-d addresses him only as "you," referring to Moshe's full and complete essence rather than his name.

Similarly, in the Purim story, G-d's name does not appear even once in the Megillah, and the events unfold as a chain of apparent coincidences -- yet as the story unfolds, we see G-d's hand at work behind the scenes, orchestrating the salvation of the Jewish people.

In both, hiddenness gives way to revelation: the priestly garments, anointed with oil at the parsha's end, come to represent the revelation of G-d's presence; the hidden hand of Providence in Esther's story is ultimately revealed through the people's miraculous salvation.

As we celebrate Purim and reflect on Tetzaveh, we can take comfort that even when G-d's presence feels hidden, He is still there, guiding and directing our lives -- and we can strive, like Moshe and the heroes of Purim, to reveal that presence through our own actions.

Festival

Purim

February 23, 2018

The Malbim begins his commentary on Megilas Esther by describing two different archetypes of leaders. He introduces us to the democratically elected leader that is chosen in a process that reflects the will of the people. On the other hand, he presents the totalitarian and autocratic ruler, who conquers other lands and imposes his reign. The Malbim then describes some key attributes that define these types of rulers.

The democratic leader is chosen by the people, and has well defined and known powers. These rulers are required to publicly affirm their commitment to protecting and upholding the values and ideals of the country.

A democratic leader works in service of his country. He is responsible for the safety of his constituency, both physically in his capacity as a military general and economically through fair taxes. He is a first and foremost a public servant.

The democratic leader views the treasury as belonging to the country itself. They don't see the countries wealth as being connected to them at all, rather it belongs to the public. Democratic leaders do not use a countries resources for private gain.

A democratic leader is only able to make decisions after consulting with and acquiring the approval of his advisors. He is not an autocrat.

The democratic leader is bound by the customs of his country. He must respect precedent.

A democratic leader cannot change the capital of the country. Because he derives his authority from the office itself, he must sit in the same throne as all of his predecessors.

The Malbim then goes on to explain that Achashverosh, the king that can easily be dismissed as a bumbling fool, really was undermining the fabric of democracy that had been keeping the Jews safe in diaspora. By dismissing democratic norms and consolidating power around himself he was directly assisting in a plot to exterminate an entire creed within his own kingdom.

The Malbim's word take on an eerie tone in today's political environment. We are experiencing a degree of international turmoil unparalleled since WW2. Across the globe we are seeing budding democratic societies wither into kleptocracies and oligarchies. And amidst these crises, countries that were dependable pillars of democracy are starting to crumble.

In America we have seen an astounding departure from democratic norms and practices. We have a president that for the first time has no experience in the military or as a public servant. For the first time in history, the leader of the united states has refused to divest from his business interests and receives profits from visiting dignitaries, effectively leveraging his political position for personal wealth. Additionally, he has spent 12 times the taxpayer money on personal travel and business than the previous administration. Raw, unedited, unvetted, and often misspelled proclamations on Twitter have become government policy with no oversight or approval from advisors and staff. This president defended defunding the state department on national T.V. by saying, "The one that matters is me, I'm the only one that matters, because when it comes to it, that is what the policy is going to be." The current administration has intentionally defied all precedent, leaving long standing international agreements and failing to reaffirm commitments to allies. And finally, in what may be the most visible representation of the digression from tradition, Mar-a-lago has become the "Winter White House."

Meanwhile in Israel things don't look much better. The Prime Minister has been recommended for indictment on charges of bribery by the police. Along with this recommendation, has been accused of illegally undermining the free press, a cornerstone of any democratic society. While arrests relating to this case are being made, the Prime Minister denies any wrongdoing and works to discredit the efforts of his accusers. The political situation looks grim.

But the Jews aren't being persecuted now! We enjoy religious liberty in our communities. We have many of our own in high seats of power and authority. Sure, anti-semitism is far from extinct but we don't seem to be the immediate target. This is especially true in Israel where we are in control of our destiny to an even larger extent. Why should we worry?

The Gemara asks, "Why did Esther invite Haman to the party?" Reb Nechamya answered, "So that the Jews wouldn't say, 'We have a sister in the palace' and forget to pray." We cannot let complacency set in. We had a Queen and only avoided extinction by a hair's breadth, can we really rely on the Kushners and Netanyahus to save us?

No! We absolutely cannot. We must cry bitter tears, don sackcloth and weep for our democratic institutions. We must direct our cries toward both inward in the pursuit of Teshuva and to God, demanding salvation. And throughout this whole exercise of dedicating ourselves to our values and convictions, we must trust that this is all a part of the divine plan.

Shabbat Shalom

(From last week)

Ask to Your Heart's Content

Tali · March 15, 2019

One day, on his way home from school, Sammy sees the coolest looking bike he's ever seen. One look is all it takes for him to know that he's just got to have this bike. He runs home, bursts into his father's study and says, "Dad, I just saw the coolest, fastest, latest model bike in the store on my way home from school; can you buy it for me?"

His father turns to him and says, "Sammy, you already have a bike, you don't need a new one. And, as we both know, you do not deserve anything new right now. You have been slacking off both at school and at home. I'm sorry, but no. You simply do not deserve a new bike."

A couple of hours later, Sammy leaves the house to go visit a friend. As he steps into the street, a car comes flying at him and knocks him to the ground. Paramedics rush him to the emergency room and hook him up to many machines. After a few minutes, the grim-faced doctor goes out to the waiting room to tell Sammy's parents that it's over; there's nothing more than they can do. Sammy's heart-broken parents walk towards their son's room, where they see a different doctor frantically standing over him and shouting, "We have a pulse!" Sure enough, the doctors bring him back to life and slowly nurse him back to complete health.

Weeks later, when Sammy finally returns home from the hospital, what's waiting for him?

Not just the brand new bike that he had asked for, but every single toy and gadget that a little boy can dream of.

What changed from before, that now his parents bought him the bike that they had previously not agreed to? Did he suddenly deserve that which he did not deserve hours before?

We can all understand how on the day when Sammy's parents thought that they had lost their child, all of the appreciation and love for him came to the fore.

Rabbi Shimshon Pinkus explains that this is exactly what happened on Purim.

On Purim, there was a decree that all of the Jews would be killed and destroyed forever. For the only time in history, Hashem had signed and sealed the decree to destroy the entire Jewish nation. It was as if G-d was about to lose His precious only child forever.

And then, the Jews repented and the decree was miraculously changed – it was as if we came back to life! This near-death experience of G-d's precious nation brought about an incredible abundance of love for His people.

Imagine for a moment, if exactly one year after Sammy's accident, Sammy would walk up to his father and say, "Dad, you know my bike is already a year old, can you get me a new one?" Sammy's father would probably say something along the lines of, "Son, I love you very much, but nice try."

Yet, as we know, the love that Hashem has for us, His children, is exponentially greater than the love that any human father can possibly possess. In his infinite love and kindness, Hashem demonstrates the same love to us each year, as He did on the original Purim. This shower of love miraculously opens up every single year in the same quality, quantity, and depth as it did on that very first Purim.

Throughout the year, we ask Hashem for many things, most of which we do not deserve. But on Purim, when Hashem is so overjoyed that His People are alive, He will grant us whatever we ask for, even if we do not deserve it. On Purim, we are told to give charity to whoever stretches out a hand. Whereas throughout the year, we are supposed to check to see if those who request money from us are legitimate, on Purim we give without any considerations. We are taught that Hashem also behaves this way on Purim. He gives freely, without calculating whether or not we deserve what we are asking for.

Let us take advantage of this unbelievable, once-a-year opportunity! There is no limit to what we can accomplish with our prayers on Purim. If we would all spend the whole day of Purim praying, we could change the world! We could bring the redemption! We could accomplish anything and everything! LET US! Let us tap into the incredible power that we are given on the day of Purim, when G-d's love for us is at its peak, to pray for ourselves and for the entire Jewish nation.

Good Shabbos and Happy Purim!

May all of our prayers be answered.

Tali

Happy Purim One and All!

Shira · February 26, 2021

Here is a little thought for you to enjoy.

When the evil Haman was trying to convince King Achashverosh to grant him permission to kill the Jewish People he made the following argument: "There is a nation that is scattered and separated."

One may wonder what the difference is between the two adjectives. Are they not one and the same?

I recently heard an interesting explanation. Scattered is referring to their physical location. They literally live all over the kingdom. But separated is describing the overall emotional state. Although they are all belonging to the same people and religion, they feel separated from one another. They don't see each other as equals or as having things in common with them. They may be next-door neighbors, pray in the same synagogue, and send their children to the same schools, but they are aloof and judge one another and hold themselves apart.

King Achashverosh hands over his signet ring to Haman and says that he may do with it as he pleases.

Sure enough, letters go out to every corner of the kingdom informing them of a special day that would approach when they would be allowed to kill all of the Jews. The Jews began fasting and crying and praying and through the righteous Queen Esther, they received salvation.

The day that they were going to be killed became a day of celebration instead. Jews gave baskets of food one to the other. No matter how they had felt about their neighbors and fellow Jews before the terrible decree, they now felt very united and as one.

We may live on opposite sides of the globe but still feel very close to one another and accepting of them in our hearts. On the other hand, we may have people in our own homes who we judge and look down upon. There is no greater pain than feeling separate or other than. We may not always agree with others, but we should never feel separate in our hearts.

This year, may we merit to feel close and as one with our fellow humans so we may merit to have salvation once again.

Have a wonderful Purim.

-Shira

Purim, the holiday of assurance

Adam · March 17, 2022

Nesivos Shalom asks an important question that we may have missed as we read the Megillah year after year. It's easy to get caught up in the success of the underdog and the beautiful way that every move the "bad guys" make is suddenly flipped to create their own destruction. How satisfying it is! But I'm out excitement, we must slow down and ask, how on earth could Mordechai endanger the entire Jewish nation by not bowing down to the viceroy of the king? This wasn't his own skin he was risking; every man, woman, and child would be at risk for playing with the ego of a tyrant! Does the Torah not have anything to say in such a case?! Aren't we meant to exercise caution in such scenarios?!

To understand we have to open our hearts. Mordechai was a prophet. He knew full well that the Jewish people were already written and sealed for destruction in the heavenly tribunal. He knew that the case was closed; there was no longer room to argue or submit any more evidence to the court. The Jewish people had been sealed for destruction.

AND Mordechai also knew that the power of true, real, deep-seated trust in the almighty was exactly the ticket out. As a 2-year-old jumping from a high place toward the back of a parent absorbed in other things, with certainty far outside the natural order that he will safely land in his mother or father's arms, Mordechai knew we would be saved. But he knew we would be saved for the sheer fact that we placed our complete trust in the almighty. So if he would bow, submitting to the natural order of things, we wouldn't stand a chance. But if Mordechai placed his utter and complete trust in the infinite Source of all existence, the Jewish people would be saved with miracle after miracle.

The Slonimer Rebbe, author of the Nesivos Shalom points out as well that the fast is called the fast of Esther not of Mordechai. His trust was so strong that fasting was not necessary. He submitted to all that Esther asked but knew that none of that would yield results. Only complete trust in the Source would save us. Mordechai even said as much to Esther, "And if not, expansiveness and salvation will arise for the Jewish people from another place and you and your father's house will be lost."

Any time we reach a pillar in time of a certain character trait that underlies the holiday, there is a unique opportunity for us to tap into and gain further perfection within that specific area. As we look around a world that is increasingly terrifying from a lower perspective, may we all merit to tap into the deepest sense of trust in our Creator, and the Sustainer of entire worlds. May we have the wisdom to know when it is time for us to act and also to know that our actions do not truly yield the results we seek. May we reach the level of Mordechai and Esther and through our trust and connection to the united shared consciousness with the Oneness of all, may we merit the full and complete redemption of the entire Jewish people and the entire world, soon in our days!

--Adam

Parshat

Ki Tisa

Ki Tisa (census/coronavirus)

March 13, 2020

The Eternal One spoke to Moses, saying: "When you take a census of the Israelite men according to their army enrollment, each shall pay the Eternal a ransom for himself on being enrolled, that no plague may come upon them through their being enrolled." - Exodus 30:11-12

What is the connection between being counted and being spared from plague?

What is the connection between the counting people and plague

Rashi says it is the evil eye:

"For numbers (i. e. things that have been numbered) are subject to the influence of the "evil eye", and therefore if you count them by their polls pestilence may befall them, as we find happened, in the days of David (II Samuel 24:10 and 15).

Explain a bit What they mean is, ...

These sources make it clear that purposeless enumeration of people purley for pride is a prescription for plague.

This connection is incredibly timely. We are amidst a wave of unprecedented pestilence that happens to coincide with the beginning of the American census. Now seems to be an opportune time to reflect on the importance of ensuring the enumeration of people is a constructive endeavor and to embrace humility.

What would be destructive?

What would be constructive?

Coverage of the Coronavirus has inundated us with statistics, mortality rates, death counts, the total number of the infected. These counts are the polar opposite of the unnecessary and self-gratifying censuses we have been warned against. Lets be sure to use these numbers to do good!

Labor of Love

Yosef · March 5, 2021

After the Jews perpetrated the unthinkable sin of the Golden Calf, Hashem was ready to destroy them entirely. Our leader Moshe stood up to defend his people and beg for their forgiveness. But among his heartfelt and eloquent pleas, we find one line that would seem counterproductive. He says, “Why Hashem should Your wrath burn upon Your people, whom you have taken out of Egypt with great strength and a mighty hand?” We would expect Moshe to recount the good deeds of the people, in order to arouse divine mercy upon them, but enumerating all the miracles which Hashem performed on their behalf would seem only to strengthen the case against them. After all, Hashem did for them, this is how they repay Him?!

Rabbi E. B. Finkel answers with a parable to the parents of a child suffering from cancer. They fight mightily on his behalf, taking him from specialist to specialist, in and out of hospitals, and across the country, to find him the best care. All that time, they put their lives on hold, barely holding down their jobs, barely finding time to eat, and certainly at the expense of any unnecessary pleasures they would have wanted. They can't sleep at night and are consumed with worry for their precious child. Such pain do they suffer on his behalf!

But when the child recovers, what happens to the relationship between that child and his parents? Is it worn thin and uncomfortable because of all the trouble he brought them? Of course not. Their love for this child knows no bounds, and their bond is stronger than that of any of their other children. Why is this? Because the more we give to someone, the more we come to love them. With every sleepless night, every ounce of worry, and every penny they spent on him, they came to love him more and more.

When Moshe came to arouse the mercy of Hashem, he chose to mention all that Hashem had 'invested' in their relationship, reminding Him of the great love which He acquired through these miracles.

This is indeed true of any relationship, parent to child, child to parent, spouse to spouse, and friend to friend. The more we give, the more we love. A relationship founded on the quest to get from another is doomed to failure, and only one rooted in giving can breed true love.

Notably, this is true in our relationship with G-d as well. If we want to achieve a true love of Hashem and find the beauty of a true connection with Him, we need to invest in that relationship. The more we give of ourselves in the service of Hashem, the more we are going to be fueled with burning love and desire for His closeness. With every mitzvah and every word of Torah, the bond becomes stronger. Especially when those mitzvos aren't easy to come by.

But, unlike human relationships, when it comes to Torah and mitzvos this outcome isn't guaranteed. Unfortunately, without proper thought, it is possible to perform mitzvos and learn Torah all day without once thinking about Hashem. If we want our mitvos to build a relationship, we need to think, with every mitzvah that we do, about Whom we are doing it for. If we act merely by rote, or even if we are motivated because it is just the right thing to do, we have not connected with Him. We have not invested in the relationship, because we don't even perceive for Whom we are doing the mitzvah.

So let's try to pause before each mitzvah and think for half a second that we are about to do Hashem's will. And if we find the mitzvah difficult, all the more should we focus on doing it as a labor of love and as an act of dedication towards our loving Father. This is a small exercise from which we can reap vast rewards. Imagine if we could live life with a constant feeling of love and connection to Hashem – what a difference it would make in our entire existence, and how joyous a life we could lead. And now let's turn that image into reality.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

The Power of Man

Yosef · February 18, 2022

Imagine the most magnificent building you have ever seen. Picture the ornate engravings which adorn every molding, the masonry and woodwork which take your breath away, the wondrous tapestries which hang from the walls. In this incredible edifice you find the finest artifacts, crafted from solid gold, more grand than any museum showcase. As you marvel at the artistry and precision, you try to calculate how many of the most skilled and practiced artisans it must have taken to create such a masterpiece; surely the work of the world's most elite experts in each of many fields. You ask your tour-guide who created this and where each of them studied their crafts, and he points to the simple construction team standing outside, people who obviously spend all their days doing coarse labor.

Unbelievable, right?

But in fact, this is exactly what took place with the building of the tabernacle. It was crafted of gold and silver, the finest cedar wood, silk and fur. Its artifacts, including the ark, the menorah, and the altar, would leave the most seasoned art critic in awe. This was one of the most breathtaking pieces of art ever created -- but did you ever wonder who possessed the skills for such an undertaking? For generations the entire Jewish people had been brick layers; hard laborers not unlike the construction team mentioned above. Besides for lacking any experience in the field, you can imagine their coarse hardened hands, hardly the tools for such finery. So how did they do it?

We can answer with the following anecdote. In the circus, there is usually a herd of elephants which are central to the show. How are these gigantic and powerful creatures contained when not under the trainers watch? The answer is fascinating -- they are tied to a simple wooden stake stuck into the ground. They are far stronger than the stake and could easily rip it out, but they never try and therefore it is plenty to hold them captive. The reason they don't try is because they have been tied to that stake every night since they were babies. They tried to uproot it when they were little and at that stage they learned that it was beyond their capability. And so, inhibited by their own underestimate of their strength, they remain captive to the stake for their entire lives. In fact, when Barnum and Bailey's once suffered a fire, the elephants panicked and broke their bonds. After seeing once that they could break loose, they could no longer be contained by their simple post in the ground and the circus was forced to set them free.

We find a similar thing with people. There are countless stories of people lifting huge loads in the face of emergency, doing things which are way beyond normal human capacity. It would seem that we don't really lack the physical strength for such things, but that we usually perform far below our true potential. We are used to selling ourselves short and thereby most of us never tap into our true inner power, much like the elephant who is hoodwinked into believing that the stake is stronger than he.

The greatness of the human spirit is nearly limitless. Indeed it is hewed from under the heavenly throne. A small piece of Godliness, as it were. We can do so much and go so far if only we'd believe in ourselves and give it everything we've got.

True, the Jews were unpracticed and ill prepared for such a daunting task as building the tabernacle. True, their hands were far from the hands of an artist. But they saw the need to build an earthly abode for G-d's presence and they dared to believe that they could do it. As Moshe told them of the work at hand, they came running. They didn't know how they would do it, but they would pour their heart and soul into the work and they were sure they'd be met with success. If we only put our heart to it, there is nothing we cannot do.

Uncle Yonatan was a living example of this greatness of spirit. He did so much and went so far. He touched so many and grew so much. He fell but never let it keep him down. His power was limitless, unbound by all the limitations we impose upon ourselves. He refused to shackle himself and he was therefore capable of the impossible. So let's look to Uncle, break all of our bonds, and strive for no less then absolute greatness.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Perspectives on Shabbos

Noah · March 10, 2023

At the beginning of this week's parsha, we reiterate the commandment to keep Shabbos before the description of the building of the tabernacle. While previously we had read the divine blueprints, we will now be recounting the actual construction of the building. In both of these places, the tabernacle is juxtaposed with the commandment of keeping Shabbos. This relationship is the source of many laws, in fact, the 39 principles forms of prohibited labor on Shabbos come from the actions necessary to construct the Mishkan. These two commandments are fundamentally related, yet they appear in opposite order in each iteration. When originally presented in command form, Shabbos appears after the Mishkan. In our parsha, we receive the commandment to keep Shabbos again while the description of the Mishkan's construction doesn't start until next week's parsha. Why does the Torah change the order?

The Gemara in Shabbos (69b), raises the following question: what happens if you are far away from civilization and you forget what day it is. How do you observe the Sabbath? The Talmud offers two answers:

R. Huna said: if one is traveling on a road or in the wilderness and does not know when it is the Sabbath, he must count six days [from the day he realizes he has forgotten] and observe one. R. Hiyya b. Rav said: he must observe one, and then count six [week] days. On what do they differ? One master holds that it is like the world's creation. The other holds that it is like [the case of] Adam.

The difference in opinion between these Rabbis is rooted in the change of order mentioned above. There are two perspectives from which Shabbos can be viewed. R. Hiyya's opinion corresponds to the first instance of the Miskan and Shabbos. This is the divine perspective, from which Shabbos is the culmination of a week of creation. For this reason, R. Hiyya prescribes waiting 6 days before observing Shabbos and the Torah mentions Shabbos only after the commands regarding construction. The second perspective, that of R. Huna and the one reflected in this week's parsha and the next's, is from the human viewpoint. As Sixth Day creations, for us, Shabbos is the first day of the week. It precedes our labor and toil.

This dichotomy reveals a difference between Man's creative power and God's. Man works with no foreknowledge of the result of his actions. He is vulnerable to mistakes, unequipped to handle unintended consequences, and prone to distraction. God does suffer from these deficiencies. For this reason, Man needed to start with Shabbos. We couldn't be trusted to engage in the imperfect art of creative work and emerge capable of realizing the original idyllic utopia. We had to experience it first so we would have an untarnished reference toward which to aspire.

On a larger scale, every Shabbos is still that first Shabbos in Gan Eden. Our weekly celebration is intended to create a reference point that can guide us in doing the hard and complicated work of creating the ultimate utopia speedily in our days.

Shabbat Shalom Noah

Parshat

Pekudei

Divine Trust

Yosef · March 8, 2019

Every morning a Jew awakes with the declaration of Modeh Ani on his lips:

"I give thanks before you, O Living and Eternal King, for mercifully returning my soul to me. How great is your trustfulness."

With this, we acknowledge that our soul was entrusted to Hashem as we slept and that it is He who has returned it to us. Furthermore, we state that we did not deserve another day – it was granted to us just because of His abundant mercy. This means that although we did not live the previous day correctly, He has still given us another chance to achieve and become meritorious.

In light of this statement, that we did not deserve to have our souls returned but rather received them as a charity, the final sentence is perplexing. "How great is Your trustfulness." Simply understood, this is a reference to the fact that Hashem has returned what is ours and is thereby described as a trustworthy watchman, as it were. But what does this have to do with trust? We didn't deserve it back anyway, so this is more a display of His kindliness than His trustfulness.

I believe the answer is that we are not referring to the degree that we can trust Him, but to the degree that He places trust in us. We realize that yesterday, and the day before, and the day before that, we failed in our mission. We don't deserve to wake up in the morning and yet everyone reading this did wake up. He gives us a second chance after second chance for years. Long after we have given up hope in ourselves, He keeps on trusting. He trusts that today we'll be different. Today we'll turn our lives around. Today we'll finally begin to place proper ideals before our short-term wants and desires. Today will finally be a day deserving of another.

As soon as we wake up, before we even get out of bed, we take note of Hashem's great trust in us, and we are empowered to regain trust in ourselves as well. With the Almighty's own vote of confidence, it dawns on us that perhaps we do have it in us. And, hopefully, we resolve not to let Him down, but to really attain quantum change. After all, G-d believes in us. In Parshat Pekudei, the Torah gives an accounting of each and every material used to build the Tabernacle. Nothing went to waste. Not one ounce of metal, not one thread of fabric, and not one single stone. Each and every piece was fully utilized to fulfill the will of Hashem.

Rabbi Moshe Feinstein [1] says that this is to teach us that we too should take account of everything given to us and make sure that whatever we have is properly used. We each have been given so many resources with which to achieve in this world. From our talents and material possessions to our family situation and our intellect, everything was measured out to us with precision. They are exactly what we need to reach our personal form of perfection, and every little bit must be utilized.

But above all, the most prized possession entrusted to us is life itself. Every day that we are granted is a goldmine of endless potential and possibility. So let us realize the great trust that Hashem puts in us with every morning that we awake. Let us capitalize on that trust and believe in ourselves. Let us use every day to its fullest so that we will be able to make an accounting of each of our days. And when the time comes to take a final account may we be able to say that this was a life worth living.

Have a wonderful Shabbos,

Yosef

[1] Quoted in Sefer Kol Ram.

March 2022

Pekudei ("amounts of") records a full public accounting of every metal donated to the Mishkan project -- not just to give credit where due, but as a mark of transparency, so that no Jew could suspect fraud or corruption in the handling of communal resources. In Judaism we are obligated to act beyond reproach -- the Kohen Gadol's garments have no pockets, and he must speak continuously in the Holy of Holies so no one could suspect him of hiding coins in his mouth. Our commandment to live beyond reproach touches not just our division of resources, but our spirituality: it is best to avoid anything that may even look forbidden. Moshe's transparent accounting exemplifies the standard of transparency a Jew should strive for in all things.

Parshat

Vayakhel-Pekudei

The Creative Mindset

Avi · March 1, 2019

Betzalel and Oholiab are regarded in this week's Parsha for building the Mishkan. Their complementary skill sets had the capacity to carve out divine architecture. Both are chosen based on the attributes and merits of their ancestry and lineage but for very different reasons. It was no secret that building the Mishkan was a wonderful honor, every Jew contributed enough that they had to stop accepting donations. Who were Betzalel and Oholiab?

Betzalel means in the shadow of God, this is not only because his intuition was aligned with the will of God but his lineage rendered him a cousin of moshes. He belonged to the aristocrats. His great grandmother was Miriam and in appreciation of her childbearing despite slavery in Egypt God makes her great-grandson an architect. Betzalels grandfather also has merits, when Bnai Yisroel gathered gold for idol worship in the scene of the golden calf Miriam's son Hur protested and refused to participate. Betzalel is an architect because both Miriam and Hur know when to be part of the construction, when most mothers would think to not bring more generations into slavery Miriam pursued and when b'nai yisrael lost faith Hur did not build a calf. They are clearly not in it for honor and have good discretion.

Moshe struggles to receive instructions from God and relaying them to Betzalel but this is no problem because Betzalels intuition is strong. There is less in the Midrash about Oholiab but it is important to know his skills as a craftsman were comparable to Betzalels skills. Their trades were complementary and equally miraculous. They both had no experience working with all these materials.

Oholiab was from the tribe of Dan, and regarded as a lower class because they descended from Jacobs handmaid Bilhah. This itself is a sort of merit- while Moses is getting criticized for choosing one of his cousins Oholiab a descendant of Dan evens the scales. Oholiab means ohel-av a tent for my father, his father Achisamachs name translates to my brother is close. He and his lineage continue artisanship and participate in the construction of the first temple.

Betzalel represents the right side, kindness, and benevolence while Oholiab, the left, is associated with severity, and strictness. In building a house for Hashem when details may be hard to access it is good to keep an open mind. Moses received the instructions from God in pieces. Betzalel made sense of moshes communications and breathed life into his ideas. Oholiab was able to construct and tell others what to construct. Nobody built it alone.

The 18 prayers in Amidah correspond to the 18 times we are "commanded" in this parsha and the next. If you count the words you find 19! The Talmud clarifies that the first mention doesn't count because Oholiab had not entered the scene yet. This week we should all appreciate our work and consider our surroundings, our commandments are opportunities and there are golden tickets everywhere!

Shabbat Shalom

Avi

Avi · March 19, 2020

In this weeks double Parsha Moshe instructs Bnai' Yisrael to donate for the creation of the Mishkan. Bnai yisrael feeling a rejuvenated dedication after recovering from the sin of the calf donate many of their most precious items to the building of the Mishkan. Moshe accepted jewelry, and died wool and fine tapestries, the Torah describes how women brought more to donate, however when women finally wished to donate their makeup mirrors Moshe insisted that it was immodest for use in a sanctuary.

Moshe saw the mirror as a danger, a lustful temptation at worst and an external waste of time at best. The midrash explains that it was not until God spoke to Moses and explained why mirrors are not only appropriate but holier than all the other gifts. While enslaved in Egypt the women were exhausted and the mirrors helped them make themselves attractive to their equally exhausted husbands.

What the women had done by beautifying themselves in a time when it was hard to want to procreate, sanctified those mirrors. Now using those mirrors in the Mishkan Bnai Yisrael would see the light of God in the desert. While Moshe did not need a Mishkan or mirrors in his personal service, Bnai yisroel benefits greatly from external benefits and physical beautification of mitzvot. God has to remind Moshe that the Mishkan is external and so is all of creation.

Building Campaign

Noah · March 12, 2021

Vayakhel dives into the donations made to the mishkan. Each segment of Israelite society is represented, contributing materials and labor to the house for Hashem. The Torah goes into great detail about this process, with each nuance carrying messages about the attitudes and procedures that make for a great fundraising campaign.

Moshe first gathers, “the entire assembly of Israel.” The Or Hachaim points out that this should be obvious. Every time Moshe relayed a commandment from Hashem he would gather the people, why is this assemblage specifically mentioned here? He answers that these, “words included orphans, minors, women, etc. Moses actually used two expressions of amplification, i.e. את כל. The reason Moses used only two amplifying statements here, and not a third one representing the wealthy is simple. There was no reason why the wealthy person would not come even if not especially invited seeing he would be happy to be part of the community.”

The invitation to the first fundraising dinner wasn't sent to the big machers in the community, as any wealthy and involved community member would come out of a sense of communal responsibility. Explicitly opening up the event to the segments of society that were dispossessed of material wealth set a tone of inclusion.

Next Moshe begins his entreaty, ‘Take from among you gifts to the LORD; everyone whose heart so moves him shall bring them—gifts of the LORD: gold, silver, and copper”

קְח֨וּ מֵֽאִתְּכֶ֤ם תְּרוּמָה֙ לַֽיהוָ֔ה כֹּ֚ל נְדִ֣יב לִבּ֔וֹ יְבִיאֶ֕הָ אֵ֖ת תְּרוּמַ֣ת יְהוָ֑ה זָהָ֥ב וָכֶ֖סֶף וּנְחֹֽשֶׁת׃

The Chasam Sofer points out a difference in the phrases “gifts to the Lord” and “gifts of the Lord.” He explains the slight discrepancy is meant to point out what the true gift is. The first gift is not referring to any tangible object but rather the willingness and generosity of spirit. This is the only gift we can give to Hashem. The gold and silver were created and given to man by god, these are already gifts of Hashem. This bifurcated message highlighting the value of generosity while also reminding people that their wealth is not solely a product of their achievement, fosters a sense of responsibility without diminishing the greatness of giving.

Furthermore, the choice of words, “take from among you” are famously expounded to be taken literally, as one who gives from their heart is considered as though they sacrifice themselves on the altar.

וי"א מאתכם ממש כי הנותן מנדבת לבו דומה כאילו הקריב את גופו (כלי יקר)

Moshe here is recognizing the sacrifice that comes with giving. This further tempers the implication mentioned above that our material wealth is not our true gift to God. Sure, I didn’t earn everything through sweat and determination alone but that doesn’t make giving it up any easier. Moshe’s recognition of this sentiment makes his appeal even more sincere and resonant.

Moshe now goes on to list the different recognition levels. Gold donor status, Silver donor status etc.. No surprises to anyone who has seen the wall of plaques adorning every Jewish institution. The Or Hachaim notices a detail in the list of membership levels that drastically changes this tiered system we are so familiar with.

“The paragraph begins by listing contributions for the construction of the Tabernacle, listing thirteen different materials and joining each one to the other with the conjunctive letter ו to show that each of these materials was of equal importance in the construction of the Tabernacle and was equally indispensable. All of these materials could be subsumed under the heading תרומת השם. The Torah considered someone who had contributed copper as on the same level as someone who had contributed gold, for instance. According to Menachot 110 the Torah describes the person who offered a bird, as a total offering as having offered a ריח ניחוח, no less so than a person who offered a four-legged mammal as an עולה, a total offering. The evaluation is based on the principle אחד המרבה ואחד ההמעיט, that the quantity of a contribution does not determine how G'd evaluates it but the intent does. As long as the intent is to please Heaven that is all that matters.”

While we might look at the top row of big plaques and be impressed by a surely sizable donation, the Torah doesn’t measure value this way. Moshe again acknowledges mortal perspective by listing the items in level of material value but he counters this with a clear message that the true measure of a contribution is the intent.

The work of fundraising is often the trickiest part of community building. Drastically different perspectives on and access to wealth make it a fraught subject. In our parsha, Moshe lays the groundwork for a successful campaign. His invitation encourages inclusivity. His messaging stresses the responsibility to give without detracting from the goodness of giving or the sacrifice that comes with it. Moshe even acknowledges value from a material perspective while reinforcing that intent is the true gift. These principles are a roadmap for financing any dwelling place for hashem and form the basis for our perspective on giving.

Shabbat Shalom Noah

Work

Noah · February 25, 2022

Through Vaykira I feel it can be difficult to connect with the parsha. The stories disappear and are replaced with minutiae about the mishkan. It can be difficult to understand the deeper meaning behind these details and to even connect to the broader themes. Why does the Torah go on at such length to describe these processes and repeat them many times?

The Ramban answers this question in this week's parsha by referencing the Midrash's conversation about the story of Eliezer, "Rabbi Acha said: The ordinary conversation of the patriarchs' servants is more pleasing to God than even the Torah of their children, for the chapter of Eliezer is repeated in the Torah whereas many important principles of the Law are derived only from slight indications given in the Text."

Using this same principle, the Ramban points out how valuable to Hashem our work must be, as the work of the Mishkan is repeated five times. Additionally, he extols the wisdom and intuition of the craftsmen who were not given exact blueprints by Moshe but relied upon to make aesthetic and structural decisions based on their own acumen.

The Ramban's commendation praising the virtue of labor is not limited to the physical. He explains that the verses are repeated due to Hashem's deep appreciation for those who toil intellectually. They provide more opportunities for them to earn merit for their study.

This lesson is closely mirrored in pirkei avot, where the value of our physical efforts and torah study are spelled out explicitly:

"Rabban Gamaliel the son of Rabbi Judah Hanasi said: excellent is the study of the Torah when combined with a worldly occupation, for toil in them both keeps sin out of one's mind; But [study of the] Torah which is not combined with a worldly occupation, in the end comes to be neglected and becomes the cause of sin. And all who labor with the community, should labor with them for the sake Heaven, for the merit of their forefathers sustains them (the community), and their (the forefather's) righteousness endures for ever; And as for you, [God in such case says] I credit you with a rich reward, as if you [yourselves] had [actually] accomplished [it all]."

Here we can see the unique union between these two concepts. Having ambition in our labor and Torah study keeps us occupied and gives us ways of giving back to the community. It is the bedrock of productive life and comes with the ultimate reward of knowing that we helped accomplish it all.

This lesson from the Mishkan and its repetitive details seems far from trivial in this perspective. It is an exhaustive reminder of the definition of a productive and accomplished life. The emphasis on labor is a reminder to occupy ourselves with learning new skills and abilities and to use them to give back. And the repetition is a reminder that Hashem values our Torah study immensely and gives us ample opportunities to engage with it. These lessons can help imbue a once difficult set of parshiot with a new perspective and remind us to use these weeks to calibrate our sense of accomplishment.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Today is the Day

March 17, 2023

In light of recent events that have transpired in our hometown of Los Angeles, I feel compelled to break from our usual forum and discuss something not necessarily related to the Parsha. Over the past few weeks, we have been struck by blow after devastating blow. First a young mother in her forties, Mrs. Sharon Shenker a"h, succumbed to cancer. Then last week, Mr. Chaim Elefant a"h, a father in his thirties, was tragically killed by a heart attack while aboard an airplane. And, just this past Shabbos, Dr. Julian Mirman a"h, was hit and killed by a car while walking home on Friday night. And there are yet others among us still battling fiercely for their lives (may they have a speedy and complete recovery).

We are reeling – shocked, battered, and bruised. Hashem is shaking us, begging us to wake up, but often we grope in the darkness, unsure where to begin. What is wanted from us? How can we ensure that these tragedies should not go to waste, but should leave us changed for the better?

Of course, every individual must search to find his own personal message; the tailor-made call for self-improvement that is unique to oneself. But there is a general message which must hit each and every one of us sharply between the eyes:

Life is short. Life is precious. And we dare not let life pass us by.

Every morning, a Jew awakes with the declaration of Modeh Ani on his lips: "I give thanks before you, Living and Eternal King, for mercifully returning my soul to me. How great is your trustfulness."

With this, we acknowledge that our soul was entrusted to Hashem as we slept, and that it is He who has returned it to us. Furthermore, we state that we did not deserve another day – it was granted to us just because of His abundant mercy. This means that although we did not live the previous day correctly, He has still given us another chance to achieve and become meritorious.

In light of this statement, that we did not deserve to have our souls returned but rather received them as a charity, the final sentence is perplexing. "How great is Your trustfulness." Simply understood, this is a reference to the the fact that Hashem has returned what is ours and is thereby described as a trustworthy watchman, as it were. But what does this have to do with trust? We didn't deserve it back anyway, so isn't this more a display of His kindliness rather than His trustfulness?

I believe the answer is that we are not referring to the degree that we can trust Him, but to the degree that He places trust in us. We realize that yesterday, and the day before, and the day before that, we failed in our mission. We don't deserve to wake up in the morning and yet everyone reading this did wake up. He gives us second chance after second chance for years. Long after we have given up hope in ourselves, He keeps on trusting. He trusts that today we'll be different. Today we'll turn our lives around. Today we'll finally begin to place proper ideals before our short-term wants and desires. Today will finally be a day deserving of another.

As soon as we wake up, before we even get out of bed, we take note of Hashem's great trust in us, and we are empowered to regain trust in ourselves as well. With the Almighty's own vote of confidence, it dawns on us that perhaps we do have it in us. And, hopefully, we resolve not to let Him down, but to really attain quantum change - today. After all, Hashem believes in us.

My friends, we have lost so much. The best among us have been taken away. But – today – we are alive. We have been trusted with one more day.

So let us realize the limitless potential in each day of life. Let us remember the trust Hashem places in us. And let us make this the day that we start living up to His trust.

We have a long way to go, but we must start today. Life is just too short to wait for tomorrow.

Good Shabbos and may we only hear good news.

Sefer Vayikra

The Book of Leviticus

Parshat

Vayikra

2018

Shimon HaTzaddik was from the remnants of the Great Assembly. He used to say:

On three things the world stands.

On Torah,

On service,

And on acts of human kindness.

This week's Parsha is our first formal introduction to one of the foundational pillars of Judaism, ritual sacrifice. Parsha Vayikrah details the laws and procedures for the Olah (ascending offering), Mincha (meal offering), Shelamim (peace offering), Chatat (sin offering) and the Asham (guilt offering). From slaughter to consumption, each of these offerings has a set of unique procedures and laws that dictate every step of the ritual. These instructions feel incredibly foreign from a modern perspective. Animal sacrifice is an aspect of Judaism that has not been practiced in almost 2000 years. Our relationship with cattle and livestock has been revolutionized since then and it is very hard to understand the significance of these laws. Furthermore, after the destruction of the Temple and the end of the sacrificial era, prayer was instituted in its place. Understanding why God would want us to sacrifice animals to him is a hard enough task, but equating this bloody and messy ritual with our serene, calm and peaceful prayers is nearly impossible.

To understand the purpose of the sacrifices and how prayer can help achieve the same purpose we need only look to the second verse of this week's parsha.

Speak to the children of Israel and say to them: when one of you offers a sacrifice to the Lord, the sacrifice must be taken from the animal (behemah), cattle (bakar) and flock (tzon). (Lev. 1:2)

The First Chabad Rebbe took issue with the grammatical structure of the beginning of this verse. In Hebrew the word order of the sentence is strange and unexpected. We would expect to read: adam mikem ki yakriv, "when one of you offers a sacrifice". Instead what it says is adam ki yakriv mikem, "when one offers a sacrifice of you." From hear the Rebbe teaches that the true sacrifice that is requested from God is not that of the animal, but rather the real sacrifice is "mikem" or "of you." God is asking us to offer a part of ourselves to him, to fully give over a facet of our existence.

But what can we dedicate to God through prayer that is the equivalent of slaughtering an animal on the altar? How can it be that prayer and animal sacrifice can both fulfill the same level of self sacrifice? Rabbi Sacks explains that the answer to this question is alluded to at the end of the verse: "the sacrifice must be taken from the cattle, sheep or goats." Each of these permitted sacrifices parallel the internal sacrifice that is at their heart.

Behemah or the class of domesticated animals represents our animal instincts themselves. As these animals are domesticated, this cannot not refer to the predatory instincts of animals. Rather this refers to the constant struggle for basic survival. These animals lives are solely dedicated to the quest for food and shelter. There is no time to appreciate, ponder, or search for meaning. To sacrifice the Behema to God is to elevate these pursuits to a higher plane. This enriches the fulfillment of our basic needs and allows us to harness our animalistic desires to achieve spiritual connection.

The word Bakar or cattle shares a root with the word Boker or dawn. Just as the first rays of sunlight break through the darkness of night, stampeding cattle break through barriers. Cattle do not respect boundaries. To sacrifice the bakar within ourselves to God, is to learn to recognize and respect boundaries. Whether they be between holy and profane, pure and impure, permitted and forbidden these boundaries are what give definition to our lives and keep us on the right track.

Finally tzon, flocks, represents the herd instinct, groupthink, peer pressure, mob mentality, and the sheeple everywhere! Tzon represent our overwhelming desire to follow the crowd. What sets the Jewish people apart has been our refusal to desert our customs and faith. This commitment to our identity even in the face of extreme persecution has kept our religion alive and strong and is clearly a pillar on which our existence depends.
