With this in mind, we are able to understand the correlation between animal sacrifice and prayer. While we might not walk away from Shul with our shirts covered in blood without the company of our beloved goat we still have the opportunity to reach inside ourselves and give over our precious animalism to the service of God. We reach out to God with even our most mundane and basic requests for sustenance and health. Three times daily we have the opportunity to stand before our creator and exclaim, "Holy! Holy! Holy!" a proclamation of our belief that God and his service are elevated and seperate. And while organized religion has become a four letter word, there are Minyanim everywhere of Jews eager to embrace the unique identity and continue serving God with the commitment and conviction of their ancestors. Prayer is our method of developing a relationship with God and as is true with any relationship, it takes sacrifice.

Shabbat Shalom

March 27, 2020

There is a lesson that we learn in the Torah: Greater is one who is commanded and does then one who is not commanded but does anyway".

This is referring to doing good deeds; something that we are explicitly told we must do is a greater accomplishment than an even harder thing that we do voluntarily.

I used to think this statement was contradictory. Isn't it much greater to put ourselves out there and extend ourselves to something that we don't even have to do?

However, I found a couple of answers that clarified the matter for me.

Firstly, when we are commanded to do something, it suddenly creates the resistance in us to not want to do it, otherwise known as the evil inclination. Thus, doing the thing we are commanded to do becomes that much harder.

Secondly, when we do something voluntarily, we get a good feeling, an impression of righteousness about ourselves that detracts from the purity of the good deed.

Thirdly, when we get caught up in voluntary acts of heroism, we may neglect the core commandment that was actually necessary for us to focus on.

I find that these principles are eerily applicable in our times.

Firstly, many people were happy to stay home all day until they were told that they had to.

Secondly, some people want so badly to be the person that saves the day that they are going from hospital to nursing home trying to cheer people up and thereby spreading the gems.

And finally, (most ironic of all), some people are so busy preparing for self-isolation that they keep running to the store and buying toilet paper and hand sanitizer, but they never actually get to the part where they just stay home and don't leave the house.

Let's all try to be like the person who is given a commandment and then does what he is told. We are being implored to stay in our homes, so let's just do that.

Together (by ourselves) we can stop the spread! 4!

Avi · March 18, 2021

Parsha Vayikra is all about sacrifice, Moshe gathers the people and tells them the laws of ritual sacrifice. There are different sacrifices that accomplish different things, some are brought daily out of gratitude and others are brought by those who have sinned by accident or purposefully. One thing sacrifice does for everybody is bring them closer to God.

Sacrifice is largely lost to us in our place in time, not only are we far away from the temple service that once bound us as Jews, but many times we do not need to part with valuable things to reach our goals. Sacrifice was well understood by our ancestors who may have been nomadic and had to decide what to carry with them. Sacrifice was surely known to the farmer who worked to yield one mono-crop instead of hunting and foraging for self sufficiency. The sacrifice of industrialization has been the loss of earth's natural habitats which will result in more displacement and extinctions.

As Humans we have sacrificed our individuality for the benefit of the group time and time again. Living in a globalized world has made our lives more spectacular than our ancestors could imagine. There is a fine line between sacrifice and destruction, God does not accept any burnt meat as offering. by taking an animal to the altar we kill the animal inside of us reaffirming our humanity.

Closer to the Light

Yosef · March 11, 2022

This week, we begin the Book of Vayikra, which begins with Hashem calling out to Moshe from the tabernacle. One who looks inside the Sefer Torah, however, will find something peculiar about the word Vayikra, or ויקרא. Here the first four letters are written normally, but the Alef at the end of the word is written in a much smaller font. The Baal Haturim famously states that this was a result of Moshe's profound humility. He explains that to be called by Hashem is something reserved for people of tremendous levels – that Hashem himself should call a human to a meeting is something incredible. Therefore, Moshe did not want to write such an accolade about himself and wished to instead write Vayikar (ויקר) which would sound like Hashem, so to speak, just 'happened' upon Moshe, without a predesignated meeting. This is a much lower level of prophecy which even the wicked Bilaam was able to attain. Hashem insisted that the word be written in its entirety, and Moshe obeyed, but he requested the right to leave it in small letters so as not to call attention to his greatness.

This is the famous explanation of the Baal Haturim. However, when I looked up the Baal Haturim's actual words, I was perplexed to find a very strange inconsistency. His words read: 'Because Moshe was great and humble he only wanted to write Vayikar etc.'

The question that begs to be asked is why he stuck in a mention of Moshe's greatness. Yes, of course Moshe was great, but what does it have to do with the small Alef, which seems to be exclusively an expression of humility?

We can answer with an anecdote from Rav Avrohom Yeshaya Karelitz, the Chazon Ish. When he first moved to the city of Bnei Brak in 1933, the local community was still in it's fledgling state. In a (very successful) effort to expand the Jewish neighborhood, the Chazon Ish chose a house some way away from the other houses, hoping that people would eventually move to bridge the gap. Many people flocked to the sage's house at all hours of the day and night, and soon someone decided that a single street light had to be erected at the halfway point to allow for safety.

When the Chazon Ish found out about the light, he commented that we can learn a great lesson from it. When one is standing far away from the light, his shadow looks very large, while the closer he gets the smaller the shadow. When he stands directly under the light, his shadow disappears entirely. So too, the further one is from Hashem, the greater he is in his own eyes, but as he draws closer and closer to Hashem, he realizes how small man really is.

This, said the Chazon Ish is the reason that Moshe was both the greatest man that ever lived as well as the most humble. Moshe was the greatest prophet ever, and the person who achieved greater proximity to Hashem than anyone else. Because he stood right under the light, he was the epitome of humility.

Now we can return to the words of the Baal Haturim and understand that Moshe's greatness and his humility were not two disparate qualities, but two sides of the same coin. His humility was born of his greatness and his closeness to Hashem.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Contemplating the Consummate Connotations of the Complex and Compelling Concept of Korbanot

March 24, 2023

Sacrifices are often seen as an outdated and meaningless practice by many. However, the Torah teaches us that offering animals and burning them on an altar is actually a beautiful and profound way to connect with God. Let's set aside our preconceived notions and try to understand this important concept that has been a central part of our ancestors' heritage.

Imagine a close relationship that is very important to you, whether it's with a parent, spouse, or child. Such a relationship requires that you constantly keep the other person in mind to some degree. The things you do for them should not be done as a burden or obligation, but rather as acts of service that come from your own desire to connect with them and strengthen the relationship.

In Judaism, our most important relationship is supposed to be with God. This means that we should dedicate our minds, emotions, excitement, and life's work to serving Him. When we offer a sacrifice, we use our hard-earned money to connect with God. We tell Him, "I would sacrifice myself to you, but I know that you want me to live and that's how I can serve you best, so instead, I offer you my sheep." When the animal is slaughtered, we imagine that we are being slaughtered as well, but happily so, because we are deeply in love with God. Even though the animal may not be able to express it, its inner soul is delighted to be used as an instrument to help us get closer to God. This is just a small glimpse of the meaning behind sacrifices, which the book of Leviticus, which we begin reading this week, explores.

Wishing you a peaceful and meaningful Shabbat.

Parshat

Tzav

The Clothing of Reb Zusha

Noah · March 23, 2018

We are now in the second parsha of the book of Vayikra, Parshas Tzav. We find Moshe telling over all of the laws of the first offerings in the temple.

Moshe also places the garments on the Kohanim, the priests. He dresses Aaron, the first Kohen Gadol, or High Priest in the eight garments to be worn by every successive High Priest. Four are the same as a regular Priest and the other four are special garments only to be worn by the Kohen Gadol.

Yosef shared with me a very powerful thought that Rashi, our foremost commentator mentions in last week's Parsha regarding these garments. Rashi says that if a regular Kohen wears the extra garments, his service is invalid. And if the High Priest lacks the extra garments specific to his position, his offerings is likewise invalid! This stands true, however, even if the Kohen Gadol is performing an offering that any Kohen is allowed to perform, not only when he carries out an assignment specific to his role.

We find here a very potent thought that is counter to much of what we find in today's society's lifeview but that gives us a real idea of the Torah's understanding of an individual's purpose in life.

A Kohen Gadol is actually just a regular Kohen that has been anointed to take on the role of Leading Kohen for the Jewish people. A Kohen Gadol can do any regular Kohanic service in the temple whenever he likes but Rashi tells us that in order to perform even these tasks not specific to his position, he must wear the garments assigned to him.

We see that a person has specific tasks and roles to play in the world and that he cannot ever compare himself to the yardstick of another to measure his success even when he is carrying out the mundane tasks that fall upon him.

We find a similar idea that Uncle Yonatan taught me regarding Pesach. When the Jews left Egypt, we imagine the splitting of the sea as it's demonstrated in "The Ten Commandments" with one giant path that the Jews walk on through the sea. But in fact, the Medrash tells us that when the Jews left, there were twelve paths and every individual left in the special path for his tribe! Imagine the miracle!

Every person in this world has a purpose and tasks specific to him! Be that person a Jew or a non-Jew! From the tribe of Judah, or that of Shimon! And walking down the path of others, or comparing ourselves to others, only hinders our ability to perceive our unique talents and roles we are meant to play in shaping the world!

If you are meant to be the Kohen Gadol, the clothing of a regular Kohen does not suffice! And if you are meant to be a "regular" Kohen, all the garments you add to your costume only serve to further you from your ultimate purpose!

Interestingly, for those searching for their purpose, Rav Tzadok Hacohen in his magnum opus, Tzidkas Hatzadik shares that all one must do is look at what challenges him most in this world. To find that which is most difficult for him and work to overcome it!

Every person in the world is here to fight his or her demons and to become the greatest YOU that you can be! As Reb Zusha famously said, "When I get upstairs, they're not going to ask me why I wasn't more like Avraham our forefather or Moshe our esteemed teacher, they're going to ask me why I wasn't more like Zusha!"

Good Shabbos!

Avi · March 31, 2023

In this week's Parsha Tzav, Moshe continues to instruct Aaron and his sons on the priestly duties surrounding the rituals of animal sacrifice. The fire on the altar must remain burning constantly to consume various, offerings. Jews brought peace offerings as expressions of gratitude, these were eaten by those who brought them and a portion went to the priests. Sin offerings were an admission of guilt, a way to purge and reconcile the unintentional misdeed. Mostly these offerings were eaten not by the person who brought them but by the priests. Guilt offerings were made by those who were uncertain if they had misstepped or breached trust. Unlike a sin offering a guilt offering is not an admission of guilt and no punitive measures are carried out. Guilt offerings were also eaten by the priests. Meal offerings were made with dough as a symbol of man's labor and devotion to God. A portion would be burnt and the rest would be eaten by the priests.

Sacrifice was and is a central part of Judaism, even without a holy temple or tri-annual pilgrimage to Jerusalem sacrifice is something we can all understand. We make sacrifices interpersonally, we make sacrifices for the well beings of others and we may even sacrifice for ideas or movements that are important to us. In the temple, offerings would be totally consumed, and other offerings would be partially consumed and eaten by priests. "And the remainder of the meal offering shall be for Aaron and his sons; it is a holy thing from the offerings of the Lord made by fire"

When we give something up whether it be out of gratitude or guilt devotion or submission the first person we benefit is ourselves. Sacrifice has the power to heighten our awareness and help us take a new perspective. When we sacrifice our time money and attention to others we will think about those who gave us their love and attention to make us the people we are today. Rabbi Jonathan Sacks write "When we perform an act of kindness, we light a spark of the divine in ourselves and in others. And when we do it again, and again, and again, we create a flame that burns bright and never goes out."

Good Shabbos! -Avi

Festival

Pesach

The Time of our Freedom

Yosef · March 30, 2018

Pesach is almost upon us and we're all running around like chickens without heads, frantically trying to get everything done in time. As we work so hard (and spend so much money) to celebrate Passover properly, it behooves us to tap into its power and take all that we can out of it.

I'd like to share a quick thought from Rabbi Dessler (1892-1953), of which Uncle Yonatan was very fond. Rabbi Dessler says that, in a spiritual sense, time does not flow as a line but rather as a circle, going around each year. He explains that each chag (holiday) has the same power as the original event had, so many years ago. Hence, Rosh Hashana is intrinsically a time of renewal, Shavuot a time of connection to Torah, Pesach a time of freedom, and so on. During these times we are able to tap into their incredible forces and experience similar events in our own lives.

Hence Pesach, the time of our freedom, is a time when we can be set free from whatever shackles may bind us. If we are in bondage to bad habits and poor decisions of the past, we can break free and turn over a new leaf. If we are stuck in a rut in our personal or professional lives, then we can move on this Pesach.

This is an intrinsic element which lies within these days, and we need only open our eyes to see its impact on our world. That said, it is in our hands to decide how much we will allow ourselves to be changed by it. The more we connect to the lessons of Pesach and to the true meaning of freedom, the more we will be able to take out of the chag. This means learning the lesson that true freedom is not the ability to do anything we wish but rather the ability to rein in our desires and take control of our lives.

So this Pesach, as we sit like royalty at our seder tables, let us understand the power of the chag. Let us see it's repercussions in the world around us. And let us become free people, free of our own limitations and able to choose our own direction in life.

Chag Sameach,

Yosef

Transformation of a Nation

April 13, 2018

I was asked by a friend at the local office Minyan, "If I told you that I know a Doctor who came over to me with a two by four and whacked me across the head, but immediately offered to stitch it back up perfectly, and was successful, would you be willing to schedule an appointment?"

Being a new Oleh, with limited Hebrew and little exposure to the Israeli healthcare system, I asked the only relevant question, "Does he speak English?"

All jokes aside, my friend was pointing out the absurdity that we spend a week appreciating our redemption from a situation that was seemingly avoidable. Why should we say Hallel and exalt God when he was the one that put us in this mess, rather Yaakov was forced down to Egypt by famine to begin with? We did not end up in Egypt on our own.

We say in the Haggadah,

וַיֵּרֶד מִצְרַיְמָה – אָנוּס עַל פִּי הַדִּבּוּר

"And he went down to Egypt" - helpless on account of the word

Rebbeinu Avrham Chadida explains that this exposition is meant to deny any claims that Yaakov's descent to Egypt was willing.

Many other Rishonim explain that this drasha is based on God's promise to Avraham, during the Brit bein HaBetarim (Covenant of the Pieces)

"וַיֹּאמֶר לְאַבְרָם, יָדֹעַ תֵּדַע כִּי-גֵר יִהְיֶה זַרְעֲךָ בְּאֶרֶץ לֹא לָהֶם, וַעֲבָדוּם, וְעִנּוּ אֹתָם--אַרְבַּע מֵאוֹת שָׁנָה."

"And He said unto Abram: 'Know of a surety that thy seed shall be a stranger in a land that is not theirs, and shall serve them; and they shall afflict them four hundred years"

Others prove that our forefathers were unwilling to make this trip from God's reassurance to Yaakov Avinu,

"וַיֹּאמֶר, אָנֹכִי הָאֵל אֱלֹהֵי אָבִיךָ; אַל-תִּירָא מֵרְדָה מִצְרַיְמָה, כִּי-לְגוֹי גָּדוֹל אֲשִׂימְךָ שָׁם."

"And He said: 'I am God, the God of thy father; fear not to go down into Egypt; for I will there make of thee a great nation."

The Spanish kabbalist Rebeinu Yosef Chiquitilla explains that God would not have told Yaakov to not worry unless he was indeed very worried about the upcoming descent. He explains that Yaakov was afraid that his progeny would never merit redemption and that the strict forces of justice that govern our universe would make it impossible for Bnei Yisrael to deserve to be retrieved. It was only with God's promise that he would change the laws that govern the universe, from the laws of physics all the way to the laws regarding judgment and merit, that Yaakov finally acquiesced to descend.

These commentaries seem to enforce my friend's question. God clearly put Klal Yisroel in subjugation against their will. If this is the case, how is redemption praiseworthy? It seems like the least God could do for us after enslaving his chosen people.

To ask this question is to ignore the fundamental transformation that takes place during the process of subjugation and redemption. This is a process that often described as transitioning from individuals to a people, but I think it has to be much more than that. The Hagaddah says shortly after describing our descent to Egypt:

"וַיְהִי שָׁם לְגוֹי. מְלַמֵד שֶׁהָיוּ יִשְׂרָאֵל מְצֻיָּנִים שָׁם."

"And he became there a nation" - [this] teaches that Israel [became] distinguishable there.

The Ran in Nedarim (:כִ) "Says that Bnei Yisrael were extraordinary in Egypt and distinctive from the surrounding population like the Malachei HaSaares. They conducted their lives independently and separately from the Egyptians."

Before the redemption, we were clearly already a people. We already had a national identity that we were committed to in the face of oppression.

It is known that Jews thrive when we have a contrasting society to work against. This contrast is what shapes who we are and what our values are. But at this point, our identity is still defined by our hosts. Sure, we are deliberately different but this holds no independent value.

Despite having already achieved distinction we still were required to endure years of backbreaking labor and constant suffering. There must be more we needed to gain from the process than just being different from the Egyptians.

The Gemarah in Sotah (.ה) explains:

א"ר יוסף לעולם ילמד אדם מדעת קונו שהרי הקב"ה הניח כל הרים וגבעות והשרה שכינתו על הר סיני (והניח כל אילנות טובות והשרה שכינתו בסנה)

Rav Yosef says: A person should always learn proper behavior from the wisdom of his Creator, as the Holy One, Blessed be He, disregarded all of the mountains and hills and rested His Divine Presence on the lowly Mount Sinai. And similarly, when appearing to Moses, He disregarded all of the beautiful trees and rested His Divine Presence on the bush.

From the beginning of our redemption, Moshe receiving his message at the burning bush, to the ultimate culmination of our journey at Har Sinai, Hashem has been communicating an incredibly strong message about humility.

The Gemarah further states:

אמר רב חסדא ואיתימא מר עוקבא כל אדם שיש בו גסות הרוח אמר הקב"ה אין אני והוא יכולין לדור בעולם

Rav Ḥisda says, and some say that Mar Ukva says: Concerning any person who has arrogance within him, the Holy One, Blessed be He, said: He and I cannot dwell together in the world.

In order for us to be truly redeemed from Egypt to become a nation that would be defined solely by our relationship to the creator of the universe, we had to create space for God. The only way we can vacate this area to allow Hashem to become the backbone of our identity is to make ourselves lower.

We need to develop an identity that is free from the shackles of subjugation. Throughout the explanation of the work that Bnei Yisrael was forced to do you see a resounding theme of making them lose themselves above all else.

The Gemara Sotah explains that the city of Ramses is an allusion to the word, "mitroses" which means to collapse. The cities built by the Bnei Yisrael would immediately sink into the earth below them requiring them to start from scratch and never allowing them the satisfaction of seeing the fruits of their labor.

Furthermore the Gemara states:

"את כל עבודתם אשר עבדו בהם בפרך. אמר רבי שמואל בר נחמני אמר רבי יונתן שהיו מחליפין מלאכת אנשים לנשים ומלאכת נשים לאנשים."

"The verse concludes: "In all their service, wherein they made them serve with rigor." Rabbi Shmuel bar Naḥmani says that Rabbi Yonatan says: The meaning of befarekh is that the Egyptians would exchange the responsibilities of men and women, giving men's work to women and women's work to men, requiring everyone to do work to which they were unaccustomed."

Even if it would result in a lack of productivity or no productivity at all we were expected to keep working. No attention was given to individual skills or achievements. No preferences were honored and people were not allowed to excel in a craft. Our individual identity was stripped away.

And at this same time the haggadah relates that our taskmasters forced us to work under pressure:

וְאֶת לַחָצֵנוּ. זֶו הַדְּחַק, כְּמָה שֶּׁנֶּאֱמַר: וְגַם־רָאִיתִי אֶת־הַלַּחַץ אֲשֶׁר מִצְרַיִם לֹחֲצִים אֹתָם

"And our duress" - this [refers to] the pressure, as it is stated; "And I also saw the duress that the Egyptians are applying on them."

The hard work we did destroyed our sense of self. We no longer had time to ourselves, choices in our lives, or any independent pursuits. We were worked down to the point all we could do was moan out for Hashem.

And only at this point of total degradation,

וַיּוֹצִאֵנוּ ה' מִמִצְרַיִם בְּיָד חֲזָקָה, וּבִזְרֹעַ נְטוּיָה, וּבְמֹרָא גָּדֹל, וּבְאֹתוֹת וּבְמֹפְתִים.

"And the Lord took us out of Egypt with a strong hand and with an outstretched forearm and with great awe and with signs and with wonders."

וַיּוֹצִאֵנוּ ה' מִמִּצְרַיִם. לֹא עַל־יְדֵי מַלְאָךְ, וְלֹא עַל־יְדֵי שָׂרָף, וְלֹא עַל־יְדֵי שָׁלִיחַ, אֶלָּא הַקָּדוֹשׁ בָּרוּךְ הוּא בִּכְבוֹדוֹ וּבְעַצְמוֹ

Once we were relinquished of any form of ego, there finally was the room necessary for God to retrieve us from the depths and to change the entire order of the universe to create an eternal bond between creator and nation.

At this moment we were finally able to forge a bond with G-d that would serve as the foundation of our national identity. And we see that from Kriyas Shema to Shabbos, so much of what we do and how we believe as a Jews refer back to this pivotal moment.

With this, I have answered my friend's question. We are grateful for the opportunity to be separated from all exterior connections and distractions to allow our relationship with God to reclaim its rightful place as our central motivation and concern. After a year of allowing our feelings and personalities to become inflated and push godly service from our lives, we are given the holiday of Pesach. It comes with many fine laws and restrictions regarding chametz, to the extent that we feel overwhelmed and incapable. But with bleach and perseverance, teamwork and tinfoil, we break ourselves down bit by bit. The house will be a mess after the Seder and it can feel like this whole exercise is fruitless labor enforced with unnecessary pressure. But this was really our opportunity to scrub the schmutz from our souls and to lose ourselves in the service of God. After weeks of cleaning, at our wit's end, we sat together with our loved ones in our pristine homes with deflated egos and reaffirmed our love and appreciation to God.

And from this point, and this point only, we can begin counting the Omer. Each night we acknowledge a corresponding pair of emotional attributes that, combined, represent the entire human psyche. The period of Sefiras HaOmer is our chance a to rebuild the qualities that we symbolic incinerated with our bagels. But this time, instead of using our talents and passion for the service of self, we have an opportunity to dedicate them to our creator. Like the offering of the Shtei Halechem that was given on Shavuot, we should merit that what was chametz in our personalities just weeks ago is now a becoming offering to God.

The Depth of Laughter (Shabbos HaGadol)

Yosef · April 12, 2019

This Shabbos, the Shabbos before Pesach, is called Shabbos Hagadol, or the Great Shabbos. The reason for this prominent name is because of the 'Great' miracle which occurred then, as the Jews in Egypt set aside sheep for their Pesach offerings. They tied the sheep to the posts of their beds, and when the Egyptians asked them the purpose of this practice they revealed their plans to slaughter these animals in the service of Hashem. The Egyptians' teeth chattered with outrage upon hearing of the plans to slaughter their gods, but they were unable to do a thing about it. In commemoration of this 'Great' miracle, this Shabbos is forever called Shabbos Hagadol.

But the questions begs to be asked -- what is so special about this miracle? Yes, it was certainly miraculous, but we're talking about a time in history when miracles were happening every day. The ten plagues, the splitting of the sea, the countless miracles in the desert, to name just a few. What singles out this miracle to be called 'Great' when observed in the context of so many wonders? Rabbi Mattisyahu Salomon explains with a very deep and piercing thought. The Gemara states that there is a difference between an incomplete tzadik (righteous man) and a complete tzadik. The incomplete tzadik is not permitted to incite an evil man for whom the times are 'laughing', meaning one who is enjoying good fortune. Conversely, a complete tzadik is permitted to incite and openly defy such a wicked man.

Rabbi Salomon explains that the underlying reason why an incomplete tzadik may not defy this wicked man is because his good fortune shows that Hashem has granted him favor for the time being. Because, for whatever reason, this wicked man is enjoying the good favor of Hashem, the tzadik must also follow suit and may not openly defy him. The times 'laughing' for him, connotes that G-d himself is laughing, so to speak.

He goes on to explain that there are two types of laughter that are not wholehearted. The first is laughter of flattery, by which one laughs to trick another with a false sign of approval. The second is laughter of mockery, where one laughs in another's face in order to highlight his disdain. Sometimes, these two forms of laughter combine into a single devastating laugh -- one of outward flattery, all the while coupled with inner mockery.

The divine 'laughter', the good fortune, which the evil sometimes enjoy is this double form of laughter. Still, it takes a great deal of clarity to perceive it that way, and we'll take it step by step.

The most basic understanding one must have when observing the good fortune of the wicked is that this cannot be a 'laughter' of true favor. Hashem is running the world and there is no way that the wicked receive any good because of true favor on G-d's part.

The next level of understanding is to perceive the element of flattery. This is the perception level of the incomplete tzadik, who realizes that this cannot be true favor but rather sees it as a temporary show of approval. Since he perceives the temporary favor which heaven is granting this wicked man, he is obliged to follow suit and may not treat him with contempt.

Only the complete tzadik reaches the depth of perception that this laughter is really a double-edged sword -- one of outward approval laced with inner mockery. The complete tzadik sees right through the success of the wicked to the ultimate demise which this very success will bring upon him. He perceives that the wicked man's success in this world is only a scheme to use up any merits he may have in order to destroy him eternally in the World to Come. Because the complete tzadik perceives this so clearly, there is no reason for him to refrain from defying a wicked man for whom the times are 'laughing'. He sees no hint of favor from on high -- just a laugh of contempt and mockery, as the evildoer revels in the digging of his very own grave. As such, he is permitted to follow suit and defy the wicked just the same.

This, says Rabbi Salomon, is the greatness of Shabbos Hagadol. As the Jews staked out the Egyptian gods for the slaughter and openly advertised their intentions, they were a prime example of one who incites an evildoer for whom the times are 'laughing'. The Egyptians were still on top, still the masters, and were still enjoying a divine 'laughter'. Yet here we were commanded to openly defy and incite them in the most provocative way. Here we were charged with rising above the fickle outlook of this world and viewing the universe through the lens of the the World to Come. We came to the perception of a complete tzadik, and were thereby permitted and indeed required to mirror the true feelings of G-d by openly defying the wicked.

Because of the lofty and elevated level we reached through this miracle, it is singled out as 'Great' and commemorated for all time.

On this Shabbos Hagadol, let us strive to attain the clarity we reached then. Let us strive to view the world not from the shallow vantage point of this world, but from an understanding that everything has a purpose in the eternal plan of the World to Come. In this way we can begin to find the hand of G-d in every facet of our lives and constantly perceive His guiding light.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

COVID-19's Lasting Impact

Yosef · April 8, 2020

My friend just told me a personal vignette that taught me an important lesson from the new reality that we're all learning to deal with. He had a flight scheduled for Monday, and, when asked about his plans, he replied that 'with the help of G-d' he would fly Monday. Not too surprisingly, he was told just a little while later that his flight had been canceled and he would have to wait until Tuesday. He related to me that even though he had said 'with the help of G-d', he really had meant that he was going, and G-d's involvement had really been more of a formality.

I was struck by the fact that in these times it is so much easier for all of us to realize how little we are in control. Who today makes firm plans more than a day in advance? Who really believes that they will certainly be able to do anything come tomorrow? We can't really assume that we will have work tomorrow, that we will be able to travel tomorrow, or that we will even be able to leave our houses. In fact, we can't even plan to get simple items like toilet paper or noodles at our supermarkets. And unfortunately, we are beginning to learn that there's no guarantee on life itself either.

There seems to be a very powerful message bombarding us on all sides, which I'd like to bring out with a lesson from the matzo we are about to eat.

Matzo would certainly seem to be the central focus of Pesach, but what is it that is so important about these flat pieces of flour and water?

Nachmanides explains that matzo reminds us of two points. On the one hand, it is called a poor man's bread, reminiscent of our destitute state in Egypt when we ate only hard bread akin to matzo. But at the same time, it symbolizes the matzo which we ate upon the exodus from Egypt, which reminds of Hashem's abrupt salvation which did not even allow us the time to let our bread rise.

Rabbi Mattisyahu Salomon explains that these are not two disparate ideas, but rather each one builds on the other to bring out an incredible realization. The 'poor man's bread' shows us how, when left to our own devices, we had absolutely nothing; just dire poverty, slave labor, and untold torment at the hands of our masters. We ourselves are absolutely powerless to achieve any kind of success, to acquire any wealth, or to execute any action simple though it may be.

And yet, the moment that we merited the help of Hashem we were suddenly and abruptly on top of the world. Immediately, before we even had eighteen minutes for our bread to rise, we went from the depths of slavery to the ecstasy of freedom. We marched out with a high hand as Pharaoh, so long our tormentor, came begging in his pajamas for us to get out.

This is the message for all of us to this very day. With all of our modern-day affluence, our technological advances, and our medical and scientific knowledge, it is very easy for us to imagine that we are in control. If I would have told you two months ago that there would come a day when you wouldn't be able to get your hands on simple household staples, you would have told me I'm crazy. And the same if I would have suggested that every department store, mall, restaurant, etc., could suddenly close down. Or that 6.6 million Americans could suddenly lose their jobs. And that barely scratches the surface of the upheaval which has transpired in such a small amount of times across the entire globe.

And with all of this upheaval we are forced to raise up our hands in submission and admit that, indeed, we are not in control.

Coronavirus is a very painful, even calamitous reality which has certainly shaken us all up. One day, however, it will come to an end, the world will move on, and I imagine the people of the world will do their very best to forget that it ever happened. But isn't that a tragic waste? Should all of the lives lost, all of the businesses destroyed, and all of the pain suffered be for naught?

Let us not allow such suffering to go to waste, but rather take the message of coronavirus to heart and allow it to affect an everlasting change in our worldview. Let us come to the firm realization that we are entirely dependent on the grace of G-d, and that on our own we are incapable of even the smallest success.

And, as we eat our matzos on the Seder Night and throughout the days of Pesach, let us remember the double identity of the matzos. That is, the poor man's bread when left on our own, and the bread of immediate salvation when we merit Hashem's help.

And, as we work to achieve greater awareness of human frailty and divine mastery, let us also merit immediate salvation, both from COVID-19 and from our long and bitter exile.

Chag Sameach,

Yosef

Yosef · March 26, 2021

Moshe was just a small child during the holocaust, and yet he miraculously survived the inferno until the camps were liberated. There he stood, destitute, robbed of his family, robbed of his friends, robbed of any sense of security. Completely at the mercy of his liberators, but alive. We fast-forward a few decades to find Moshe in America, where he has become a wealthy and powerful businessman. A true rags to riches story. From time to time, Moshe avails himself to interviewers, sharing with them the stories and the horrors of the camps. He can remember each and every facet with vivid detail, and as he speaks, the listener is able to perceive the experience to its smallest details. It seems as though nothing has been forgotten. But as much as he can relate the tortures of Auschwitz, he understandably only conducts such meetings from the comfort of his executive office. He sits in an oversized and luxurious leather armchair, with an impressive mahogany desk in front of him and a butler waiting on him hand and foot. He speaks only from a position of power.

Moshe shares his story generously, but there is one place he isn't willing to go. He will not allow himself to recount, or even remember, that feeling of helplessness and dependency which he felt standing there upon liberation. He simply refuses to see himself as once having been so unable to care for himself.

On Pesach, we are charged with the uneasy task of viewing ourselves as if we personally left Egypt. But what does that mean? Must we dress up, pick up a walking stick, and throw our matzos over our shoulder in order to fulfill the mitzvah?

Rabbi Uren Reich explains that it is this very feeling of dependency which we are asked to envision and experience. As the Jews left Egypt, they understood with utmost clarity that they were totally dependent on Hashem. They felt with every fiber of their being the hardships with which they were faced before G-d's salvation, and they knew without a doubt that they had nothing without Him. On Pesach, each of us needs to work on achieving this feeling of dependency. We need to realize that on our own we have nothing; that our intelligence, strength, charisma, and hard work are not the catalysts for whatever success we may enjoy. It is only the will of our Creator which decides what one will achieve.

Last year, we were afforded the opportunity to experience human frailty at its finest. We were shown up-close and personal how limited we truly are and how, but for the grace of G-d, we are unable to do as much as leave our houses. Last Pesach was one filled with pain for so many – those who were sick, those who lost loved ones, those who spent the seder all alone.

But this Pesach is different. No, Coronavirus is not yet in our rearview mirrors, but the severity is certainly beginning to wane. Many are breathing a sigh of relief after receiving the vaccine, many of our businesses, synagogues, and other destinations are beginning to go back to normal, and most of us will be able to spend Pesach in the presence of family.

But let us not slip back into the facade which was so strikingly shattered last year. Let us not be fooled once again into thinking that we are in any position of power. But let us see ourselves as if we left Egypt, and let us truly feel the dependency of one liberated towards his savior.

Chag Kasher V'sameach, Yosef

Celebrating Redemption

April 2, 2021

This Shabbat coincides with the enigmatic chag, “Shevii Shel Pesach.” Celebrated on the last day of Pesach, this one-day holiday has very little mentioned about it apart from the general Pesach festival. The last day of Sukkot ends in Shemini Atzeret, a chag with its own unique identity. In davening, it is called by a separate name, Shemini Chag Haatzeret, and its offerings deviate from the pattern established by the beginning of Sukkot. Shevii Shel Pesach has no such distinctions. Its details are the shortest of any other holiday, simply;

וּבַיּוֹם֙ הַשְּׁבִיעִ֔י מִקְרָא־קֹ֖דֶשׁ יִהְיֶ֣ה לָכֶ֑ם כָּל־מְלֶ֥אכֶת עֲבֹדָ֖ה לֹ֥א תַעֲשֽׂוּ׃

And the seventh day shall be a sacred occasion for you: you shall not work at your occupations.

Additionally, it is the only day on which work is forbidden that doesn’t have a full Hallel. What is the significance of this finale to Pesach?

To understand this holiday we will start with the event it is meant to commemorate. This defining moment is the splitting of the sea, kriyat yam suf. This is mentioned by Rashi, quoting the midrash who says;

“On the night of the seventh day they went down into the sea and on the following morning they (the Israelites) sang the Song of Praise and this was the seventh day of Passover. And that is why we read “The Song” as the Scriptural lesson on the seventh day of the Festival”

Yet this explanation for the celebration does not appear in the chumash, and the Torah mentions that Pesach will be a seven-day holiday well before the splitting of the sea. In fact, the Mechilta that is quoted by Rashi does not explicitly say that kriyat yam suf happened on Shevii Shel Pesach, but only gives the numbers of days needed to travel which Rashi uses to prove the date of the event. Where the seder has an aspect of pirsumei nisa, publicizing the miracles of the exodus, on Shevii Shel Pesach we seem to be doing the opposite.

The reason that we don’t say a full Hallel is the key that can begin to unlock the nature of this holiday.

“In principle, we should really recite Hallel every day of Pesach, just like Sukkot, but since on the seventh day of Pesach, i.e. Yom Tov acharon, the mitzrim were drowned in the Yam Suf, it is inappropriate to recite full Hallel on that day. Moreover, since we do not recite full Hallel on Yom Tov acharon of Pesach, we also refrain from reciting it on chol ha-mo'ed Pesach, since otherwise, chol ha-mo'ed will appear more significant than Yom Tov.

This explanation immediately infuses the chag with a sense of deference. While on the Seder night we drink four cups leaning to symbolize each way we were redeemed, on shevii shel pesach we have a much more toned-down experience. Our raucous renditions of dayenu would be disrespectful now that our enemies' bodies lay scattered before us by the thousands.

Despite this transition in tone and attitude, Shevii Shel Pesach still is not regarded as an independent holiday but entirely a part of “chag hamatzot.” This too is greatly informative about the nature of the holiday. An appreciative yet somber assessment of victory is essential to freedom. When we lose touch with the humanity of our enemies we don’t comprehend the collateral damage that our salvation entailed. This reflection is not separate from the redemption process but a fundamental part of it.

Exodus and redemption rarely have no costs. We know about the multitudes of Israelites who didn’t make it out of Egypt and the complete annihilation of Egyptian society. Taking an honest and sober assessment of the collateral effects of our salvation is essential in coming to a full appreciation of the miracles that were necessary for us to persevere. Shevii Shel Pesach is an opportunity to conclude our exodus and establish our national identity on empathy and restraint, not schadenfreude and jingoism.

The Broken Portraits

Yosef · April 14, 2022

The custom of breaking the middle matzo at the Seder is one that has many explanations, but I'd like to share a particularly beautiful explanation that I heard not long ago from Rav Uren Reich.

There were once two friends who grew up together in one of the shtetl's of Europe. From childhood and until their perspective marriages, the two were inseparable. They were closer than brothers, they understood each other like nobody else, and through thick and thin they were there for each other. But the day finally came that they both became engaged to girls from far away towns. As was the custom in those days, they would both live in the town of their in-laws, with a great distance between them. Essentially, they understood that they would have no contact for years on end, and they were greatly saddened by the prospect.

In an effort to seal their friendship to defy time and space, they resolved to hire an artist to paint a portrait of each friend. In this way, they would always be remembered. But instead of each friend taking the picture of his fellow, they decided to cut each portrait in half, each of them retaining half of their own picture and half of their friend's. This would symbolize that each of them was indivisible from their friend. Reuven without Shimon wasn't Reuven, and Shimon without Reuven wasn't Shimon. Neither could be complete without each other.

This is what we are doing by breaking the middle matzo. One half represents Hashem, and one half represents the Jewish people. In this way, we show that our relationship is no ordinary friendship of two disparate parties. No, ours is a union so deep that we cannot be divided. Without Hashem we are not a people, and, so to speak, Hashem without the Jews is also incomplete.

Yes, there are exiles, holocausts, and terribly bitter times. There are times when we are broken and cracked, aching for the embrace of our our Beloved. But even in such times, we are not just the broken parts of ourselves, but a broken half of one united whole with Hashem.

Have a happy and kosher Pesach,

Yosef

Pesach (2022, Four Factions at the Sea)

April 21, 2022

As Bnai Yisroel runs out of Egypt they meet the sea, under pursuit by Pharoh and his chariots the tide is high and Moshe says to the people of Israel "Fear not; stand by and see the salvation of G-d which He will show you today. For as you have seen Egypt this day, you shall not see them again, forever. God shall fight for you, and you shall be silent." God told Moshe G-d said to Moses: "Why do you cry out to Me? Speak to the children of Israel, that they should go forward."

But the children of Israel were divided. We all face challenges differently at this moment at the banks of the sea there were four factions. One group felt despair and was ready to hurl themselves into the sea. Another group felt defeated and was ready to turn back to Egypt. Another group felt rage and was ready to go to war, and the final group felt physically helpless and took refuge in prayer.

Moshe rejected all these ideas and in the phrase quoted above, he seeks to comfort each faction while motivating them to move forward. "Fear not; stand by and see the salvation of G-d which He will show you today." this is the answer for those who were ready to drown in despair. For those who wanted to turn back and submit to the aggressor, Moshe said "you have seen Egypt this day, you shall not see them again, forever" for those emboldened to fight Moshe insists "God shall fight for you" and to those praying Moshe said, "you shall be silent."

What then is a Jew to do when caught between an aggressor and tumultuous waters? These factions still exist among us, like the Jews who felt despair religious Jews plunge into the depths of the Torah, isolating themselves in the laws mythologies, and customs that bring us closer to god. Like a chassid in a fur coat. A man once asked a chassid in a fur coat why don't you light a fire that can keep a few people warm? Why invest in a coat that will only warm yourself? The chassid answered without stuttering. It is simply not possible to warm everyone in the world and such an undertaking would result in nothing but frustration so I keep myself warm with fur.

Those who want to return to Egypt are also engaging in escapism but rather than escaping the aggressor they deny the good God did for them in the execution of the plagues. Assimilation may be a route to having a better relationship with the aggressor but it is sure to end the heritage that Moshe worked so hard to maintain

A third response is to fight, this Jew will not escape, this Jew will not submit but this Jew is hopelessly outnumbered by the pharaoh and his chariots.

The praying Jew may feel despair but they are engrossed in a world beyond the material one they may feel like submitting but their trust in prayer makes them more resilient to such a temptation they will not fight because God's ways are good and peaceful but to the other factions they appear to be doing nothing and people doubt them.

God tells Moshe to tell the children of Israel to go forward. And as Bnai Yisroel wades in the water, despair, uncertainty, rage and helplessness are all present but just as the water reached our nostrils the water rose to let us pass. Each and every jew living is a result of a miracle and throughout our lives' challenges, we learn about what coping mechanisms help us through. However, if all we do is cope and forget to take steps forward we will remain in Egypt.

Parshat

Shemini

Connecting to the Divine

Noah · March 29, 2019

This week's Parsha, Shemini, is the culmination of seven preceding parshiot instructing the Jews on the construction and service of the Mishkan. Now, on the eighth day, all is finally ready. The Mishkan is standing in all of its glory, Aaron and his sons have been consecrated and the time to begin the service has finally arrived. "Moses and Aaron went into the Tent of Meeting and when they came out, they blessed the people. God's glory was then revealed to all the people."

Just at the pinnacle of this narrative, something horrifying takes place.

"Aaron's sons, Nadav and Avihu, took their censers, put fire into them and added incense; and they offered unauthorized fire before God, which He had not instructed them to offer. Fire came forth from before God, and it consumed them so that they died before God. Moses then said to Aaron: "This is what God spoke of when he said: Among those who approach Me I will show myself holy; in the sight of all the people I will be honored."

Celebration is immediately replaced with mourning, elation with depression.

On the most basic level, Nadav and Avihu were guilty of offering a fire that was not commanded. Why doesn't the Torah view this as an act of tremendous initiative? How does their fiery enthusiasm warrant a death sentence while other religious acts of passion are extolled?

Understanding the nature of holiness is essential in understands it's dynamic with human will. In order to create the world, God had to hollow out a part of creation in which the divine will would be concealed. Like a parent who holds themselves back, allowing a child to independently learn and make mistakes, God holds back his presence. And as a parent never abandons his child entirely, God has allowed for times and places where his presence is revealed. We have shabbos, shemitta, yovel, and the chagim set aside as times of special connection to God's presence. We have Israel, Jerusalem, and the Makom HaMikdash as places in which God's presence is more revealed. These places are holy because they are where we encounter God's presence through the concealment. This is achieved through elimination of our will, "just as God makes space for man by an act of self-limitation, so man makes space for God by an act of self-limitation." This is not because God does not value our will and initiative, this whole process is only necessary because God wants us to become partners in creation with our will and initiative.

However, we must be willing to let go of our will entirely if we want to experience God in the holy realm. Our initiative is the most powerful tool in fixing the world around us but in the world of the divine, it is an affront to the very system that enables it to exist.

To conflate the will of God with our own is to make the mistake that inspires war and conflict under the guise of religious dedication. We must approach God in a state of Bitul, where our desires and priorities are silent in the overwhelming presence of God.

Shabbat Shalom,

Noah

*Based On Rabbi Sacks "Fire: Holy and Unholy"

The Lord Giveth

Noah · April 17, 2020

This week's Parsha contains the tragic deaths of Nadav and Avihu and their father, Aaron's, reaction to hearing of the tragedy. Rashi explains:

And Aaron was silent — He received a reward for his silence. And what was the reward he received? That the subsequent Divine address was made to him alone and not to Moses also — for to him alone was spoken the section dealing with those who are intoxicated by wine.

In light of Rashi's praise for Aaron's silence, the Chasam Sofer contrasts Aaron's response to tragedy with that of Iyov and Rebbi Meir. Iyov, does not remain silent in the face of a tragedy like Aaron and instead proclaimed the famous phrase, "the Lord giveth, and the Lord taketh away" followed by a frequently forgotten benediction, "May the Lord's name be blessed." The Chasam Sofer explains that speaking was not a mistake on Iyov's part, in fact the next posuk makes this clear, "For all that, Iyov did not sin nor did he cast reproach on God." To understand the difference in mourning, the Chasam Sofer points us to the Midrash about Rebbi Meir.

'A valiant woman, who can find - They said, "There was a story about Rabbi Meir who was sitting and expounding in the study hall on Shabbat afternoon when two of his sons died. What did his mother do? She placed both of them on the bed and spread a sheet over them. At the end of Shabbat, Rabbi Meir came home from the study hall. He said to her, 'Where are my two sons?' She said [back], 'They went to the study hall.' He said to her, 'I scanned the study hall and I did not see them.' They gave him the cup of Havdalah and he separated [the days of the week with the closure of Shabbat]. He repeated and said, 'Where are my two sons?' She said, 'They went elsewhere and they are coming now.' She placed food in front of him and he ate and blessed. After he blessed, she said to him, 'I have a question to ask you.' He said to her, 'Say your question.' She said to him, 'Rabbi, before today, a man came and deposited something with me, and now he is coming to take it. Should we return it to him or not?' He said, 'My daughter, one who has a deposit with him must return it to its owner.' She said to him, 'Were it not for your consent, I would not have given it to him.' What did she do? She grabbed his hand, brought him up to that room, had him approach the bed and took off the sheet from upon them. When he saw both of them dead and laying upon the bed, he began to cry and say, 'My sons, my sons, my teachers, my teachers - my sons in the way of the world, my teachers in that they would enlighten my eyes with their Torah.' At that time, she said to Rabbi Meir, 'Rabbi, is this not what I told you - do I not need to return the deposit to its Owner?' He said, '"The Lord has given and the Lord has taken; may the name of the Lord be blessed"' (Job 1:21)." Rabbi Chanina said, "With this thing, she consoled him and his mind became composed - that is is why it states, 'A valiant woman, who can find.'

The Chasam Sofer goes on to explain that a God-fearing mourner must adopt the attitude of a borrower that was lent a great treasure. He must appreciate the time he had with it, especially upon its return. He explains further, "Until the time of its return, the borrower doesn't appreciate the good and the good itself is barely noticeable. Only in the absence of a treasure can we perceive its true worth." This was the sentiment expressed by Iyov and Rebbi Meir when they cried out, "The Lord giveth and the Lord taketh away, may the name of the Lord be blessed." May God's name be blessed in appreciation of the original giving that I only can truly appreciate now that it is gone. This response to tragedy is the highest level as it prioritizes appreciation over grief. That's why Iyov's words were regarded with the same infallibility as Aaron's silence. But why isn't Aaron censured for not offering praise in this personal moment of bereavement as Iyov and Rebbi Meir could? The Chasam Sofer explains that Aaron wanted to. He wanted to cry out in love and appreciation for the time he had with his beloved children. But Aaron knew that if he allowed himself to express appreciation for what he was given, it would inevitably lead him to resent that it was ever taken away. That's why Aaron's silence meant so much.

Everyone's lives have been turned upside down and people are losing loved ones all around us. It is very hard to know what to say and how to react in these times. I think the Chasam Sofer's message is perfectly applicable in this era of personal and communal suffering. While there has been so much taken away from us, jobs, hobbies, family gatherings, Shabbos meals, minyanim we have been given an opportunity to realize how lucky we were when they were a part of our lives. When we open our mouths to talk about this crisis it shouldn't be with expressions of complaint and reproach to God. Instead, we should address the situation with appreciation and recognition of the good that still permeates every aspect of our lives. And if we find it impossible to do this, to see the silver lining, to use absence to realize presence, to bless God on the giving even at the time of the taking, then we should follow in the way of Aaron HaKohen and remain silent.

Shabbat Shalom,

Noah

Avi · April 6, 2021

This week's Parsha is called Shemini which means the eighth. For the past seven days Moshe Aaron and the Jewish people inaugurated the Mishkan. On the eighth day Moshe and Aaron started their priestly duties, officiating kohanim with sacrifices that were consumed by a fiery pillar. Nadav and Avihu brought a "strange fire that God had not commanded" and were killed by their foreign offering.

Also in this Parsha we were taught the laws of Kashrus. Land animals must have split hooves and chew their cud, Fish must have scales and fins. We were given a list of non Kosher birds and another list of four locusts which are Kosher.

Following the laws of Kashrus we were taught the laws of ritual impurity, learning that if one comes in contact with certain corpses they are impure until they dunk in a Mikvah. The body of water has the ability to restore purity.

Nadav and Avihu death was not God punishing them for their unnecessary sacrifice rather their death was God granting their wish. They wanted a level of transcendence beyond what was feasible as earthly beings so their souls rose and rested next to God. While we all wish to be closer to God, Nadav and Avihu teach us that transcendence is not a worthy pursuit unless it is used to bring others up and spread the wisdom around the world. Transcendence is not an end it is a means to change and uplift those around us.

Kashrus and ritual impurity are very personal and physical subjects. What we eat, touch and where we go is between us and God. keeping Kosher and being aware of impurity fundamentally changes an individual's behavior, causing them to avoid certain foods and certain places. While what we eat and where we go seem like physical pursuits practicing these things does give us a transcendent consciousness.

This Parsha teaches us how to manage ecstasy. Instead of floating up to shemaim like Nadav and Avihu there are things we can do with our bodies to help us get closer to God. Judaism does not condone suicidal pursuits of spirituality however it does recognize that death is our final transcendance.

Parshat

Tazria-Metzora

Smelling Our Own Breath

April 5, 2019

This week's Parsha Tazria focuses on spiritual purity and impurity. The laws of conceiving and giving birth, the laws of Brit Milah and the laws of Tzarrat- a visible affliction of one's skin or belongings. A splotch on one's shell. When we have been deemed impure by a Kohen we must spend time in quarantine, we are allowed back into the community once the splotches have faded. This week's Parsha is heavy on Halacha and there is not much narrative, but back in Bereshit in Parsha Noach, we see the importance of clean speech personified by the Torahs narrative. When God tells Noach to gather animals He differentiates between the kosher "pure" animals and non-kosher animals. Instead of using the terminology found in this week's Parsha "tahor - pure" and "tamei -defiled" the torah records the words tahor twice. Referring to animals that are pure "tahorah" and those that are "einei tahorah - not pure" the Torah spends eight extra letters to teach us the importance of what passes through our lips.

However in this week's Parsha as we deal with the laws of our own spirituality we are stringent and allow the shameful word tamei to be read aloud. This is because as the Mishnah teaches us "all afflictions one sees, except his own." This means that we can't smell our own breath, but are quick to offer mints to those breathing on us. This is not a negative attribute of our existence but rather a great credit to our discernibility because after all we "see all afflictions" many pride themselves on providing sound judgment to others. The Rabbis and life coaches among us have an enormous responsibility.

When a man helps another man with his life and relationships it is like asking a horse to fix a merry go round, they think they can help, but mostly they will get freaked out. This is why when observing things as foreign as a pig we must be careful not to objectify it as disgusting but rather recognize our lack of understanding. When observing yourself, this is the time to be stringent. Our innermost faculties are between us and God and if we have our harsh judgment pointed outward we lose the opportunity to use our innate human discernibility for the ultimate good.

As Jews, it is our constant practice to catch a whiff of our own deepest breath, through constant refinement and spiritual practice it becomes possible for us to help others see the woods from the tree. It is part of our ethical code to hold ourselves to the highest standard and others to the baseline drawn out for us in the Torah and mitzvot we practice. In this manner, we use our highest discernibility for ourselves and when judging others we take a removed stance. When judgment must be made our ethics must be unwavering and not based on our highest personal standards

Quarantine – Now and Then

Yosef · April 24, 2020

In olden times, when the Jewish people as a whole were on an exceedingly high level and G-d's presence was not so concealed, there was a miraculous illness called tzara'as [loosely translated as leprosy]. This illness was not a product of medical stimuli but rather was brought directly by the hand of Hashem upon one who spoke ill of his fellowman. Likewise, the healing of such an ailment could not be achieved through medical means, but only through repentance and a Torah-prescribed purification process. The focal point of the leper's purification process was his exile outside of the camp -- during the entire period of leprosy, he was not allowed contact with any other people. Quarantined.

In explanation of this punishment, the sages explain that since he separated people through his venomous words, by sowing hate and bad feelings, he is therefore forced to remain separate himself. Only once he feels the pain of being alone, of being shunned and unwanted, is he able to see the folly of his ways, correct his path, and return to society.

The correlation between tzara'as and coronavirus is uncanny in two regards. The first is the degree to which the symptoms of corona seem to be arbitrary from a medical standpoint. Yes, the virus has a medical term and can be viewed under a microscope, but the way it affects different people is so varied and unexpected that it's hard to deny the hand of Hashem. Why is it that when two people with similar statistics both contract the disease one is on the brink of death and the other isn't even sure he's sick?

The second similarity is the prescribed way to combat the disease -- quarantine. Just as the leper was forced to isolate himself, so must we all be isolated to keep the virus at bay.

In light of these speculations, Rabbi Chaim Kanievsky, largely regarded as the greatest Torah sage of our generation, has suggested that we all take a lesson from the metzora to our own situation. We must redouble our efforts to bring people together and not to break them apart. To use our words to build and not to destroy. And in the merit of our social caring and connection, may we quickly see an end to social distancing.

And if I may add one more thought along these lines. It strikes me as a funny that the need for social distancing and confinement to our houses has in some ways made us closer. On Tuesday, I effortlessly attended a wedding in Cincinnati from my living room in Los Angeles. Also in attendance were scores of people from New York, New Jersey, Michigan, Pennsylvania, Maryland, Illinois, Georgia, and even Israel. Separated by thousands of miles and each locked in our houses -- yet together through the power of the internet.

We all know the destructive power of the internet to defame, damage, or slander people in front of millions with the touch of a button. But when used properly, look how the same technology has the ability to bridge gaps and span countries. Let us learn from the situation to harness all that power only to draw close and never to set apart, and may we all be truly reunited, in person and in the Holy Land with the coming of Mashiach speedily in our days.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Whose House?

Yosef · April 16, 2021

One of the many pleasures of life as an orthodox Jew is the bounty of chessed, or loving-kindness, which goes on amongst our ranks. If you take out the local phone book in Lakewood, New Jersey, or any other large Jewish community, you will find hundreds (no exaggeration) of 'Gemachs'. These are mostly small, often private, organizations looking to meet some need or other for zero profit and even at a loss. There are stroller gemachs and gown gemachs, GPS gemachs and chair gemachs, and some you would never think of like the chicken soup gemach. Not to mention the large scale chessed organizations, such as RCCS holding every cancer patient's hand, Bonei Olam helping with infertility, and Tomchei Shabbos providing food and other necessities to anyone that needs, to list just a few. The chessed of the Jewish people is unbelievable and unrivaled anywhere in the world. If you ever need a boost of Jewish pride, I invite you to open up such a phone book and be wowed by the greatness of all the 'ordinary' members of our people.

Another facet of this nation-wide dedication to chessed is the mitzvah of 'hachnossos orchim', or welcoming guests. When visiting any orthodox Jewish community across the globe, you need just to make a phone call or two and you will find plenty of hosts who are overjoyed to welcome complete strangers in their homes. Many have set aside special guest suites just for such an opportunity, while others will happily rearrange their own family's sleeping quarters in order to make room for a guest. In the chassidic neighoborhood of New Square, New York, there is actually a community-wide rule that one must have a guest room in their house. This means that large families with only a few bedrooms have made the mitzvah of hachnossos orchim such a priority as to put their guests comfort before their own.

In this weeks Parshah, we learn about the miraculous plague of 'tzaraas', which is loosely translated as leprosy but in reality was a divine sickness unrelated to the medical phenomenon of leprosy. Such ailments were not only exhibited by people, but actually came upon clothes and houses as well.

When introducing us to tzaraas of the house, the Torah uses peculiar wording. 'When you will come to the land of Canaan which I am giving you as a stronghold, and I will place an affliction of tzaraas in the house of your stronghold. And the one whose house is his will come to the priest etc.'

The Kli Yakar asks why the Torah first states that is is a stronghold which Hashem has given, and then immediately afterwards refers to it as none other than the man's own possession. Also, the wording in the last verse, which refers to him as the 'one whose house is his' is quite strange.

He explains according to the Talmud (Erchin 16a) which learns from this last verse that the cause of tzaraas in a house can be linked to stinginess. 'The one whose house is his' means that he has set aside his house for himself alone; he doesn't benefit others with his home. With this, the Kli Yakar explains the previous verse which is coming to highlight the folly of one who is stingy. It says that when Hashem will take you into the land, and He will provide you with a house to live in. Make no mistake, says the Torah, your house and all of the bounty which you enjoy is not thanks to you. Don't think these are your just deserts, which might entitle you to hoard things for yourself -- this is the blessing of G-d.

But if one doesn't learn this message, but rather views his home and his possessions as his alone, he's in for a surprise. Hashem says that He will bring tzaraas upon 'your stronghold', meaning the home which he views as his own. If he really thinks this is his, and he uses such a thought process to justify his stinginess, then he'll have to learn the lesson the hard way. If he doesn't shape up, he stands to lose the house entirely in face of the tzaraas.

Today, we are not privy to such an overt show of divine intervention, but the lesson of tzaraas is a timeless as ever. The people of the Torah do not own things for their personal purposes alone, and they do not view such things as their personal lot. The Torah nation lives for others and any blessing which they enjoy is viewed as an opportunity to share it with others. May we truly live up to the gold standard of our people, find pride and pleasure in sharing our homes and our hearts with others, and merit the continued benevolence of He who has given us everything.

Tazria

Avi · March 28, 2022

In this week's Parsha Tazria Bnai Yisroel is taught about ritual purity and how it pertains to their innermost lives. We learn that a woman who gives birth must dunk in a mikva and bring offerings to the Bais Hamikdash, doing these things restores her ritual purity. We are also taught about performing Brit Mila on male babies. This Parshas main topic is tzaraas, often mistranslated as leprosy tzarras is a spiritual ailment that left spots of discoloration on homes' clothes and on people's skin. Many protocols are laid out for the diagnosis and eradication of tzaraas. Someone who finds tzaaras must be examined by a priest who determines by size and color weather and when someone may rejoin the community. Tzarras that won't go away must be destroyed, people suffering from tzaraas have to live in quarantine.

Tzarras comes when someone is speaking ill of their neighbor, it doesn't matter if what they said is true it can still be loshon hara eventually leading them to a measure for measure punishment of isolation. Loshon Hara spreads like a virus and can make someone feel like a pariah in biblical times spots of discoloration would flip that narrative on its head. Leading to the estrangement of the aggressor instead of the victim.

In last week's Parsha we learned about kashrus and how the food we eat must be kosher and pure. Just like the things that we put in our mouths are pure anything that comes out of our mouths should follow suit. Rashi finds some difficulty in this and asks why are the laws of purity regarding human life mentioned after the laws of kashrut and how purity affects animals. Why are humans legislated about after the animals? This is a deep question about human superiority over animals and can teach us about attributes that are enate and what has to be taught and learned. This is a fundamental question of human nature that has bothered rabbis and philosophers alike; Are we better than animals?

Rashi comments that laws regarding humans are mentioned after laws regarding animals because humans were created after animals. Many reasons are given for why God created animals before us. The reasons given shed light on what human nature really is. The first reason is to humble humans, God wants us to be reminded that even gnats got here first and are Godly creations just like ourselves. The next reason given for our postponed creation is that so we shouldn't be able to take false credit for creating any part of the natural world. Humans have created a lot and changed the natural landscape but only a heretic would believe that a human played a role in creating natural order. The final reason given for our being created last is that God wanted us to enter the world like we would enter a ball. Caterers all dressed up, tablecloths laid out, and a fine buffet. God had us enter the world in fulfillment of a precept. This may be something humans appreciate more than any other animal because of our unique ability to plan something larger than us in the distant future and see it come to fruition. The Godly act of conception is where this Parsha gets its name. Tazria means childbirth but it also means conception, when something is conceived it means it was worked for labored over thought out, and planned.

We are legislated after animals because unlike animals we sin and not only do we sin but we will always have the capacity for sin. Without diligent efforts, many of are unlearned animal behaviors lead us down a path of sin. However, when we study Torah and learn about the lives we wish to live we become the narrator in a way that surpasses the free choice held by any other animal on this planet. Good shabbos!

Shabbos - The Paid Vacation

Yosef · April 8, 2022

This Shabbos, the Shabbos immediately preceding Pesach, is called Shabbos HaGadol, or the great Shabbos, because of the miracle that took place on it. On the tenth of the month of Nissan, which happened to fall out on Shabbos, the Jews all tied sheep to their bedposts with the express intent of slaughtering them for the pascal offering four days later. They were commanded to take the sheep openly before the eyes of the Egyptians, and this despite the fact that the Egyptians worshiped sheep. When the Egyptians would ask what the Jews were doing with their precious deity, the Jews unabashedly and proudly responded that they intended to slaughter them for Hashem. In any natural setting, the response for such a blasphemous and audacious statement would have been immediate death, especially when perpetrated by a lowly slave whose life was valued at only a few dollars. Even so, the Jewish people courageously heeded the command of Hashem, and, with perfect trust, put their lives in the hands of G-d. Miraculously, the Egyptians were completely tongue-tied and hand-strung, unable even to tell the Jews off and certainly unable to raise a finger against them.

Much has been said to explain why this miracle, more than all the myriad miracles of the exodus was singled out to be commemorated [see Zichron Yonatan #85], but we'd like to focus on another point. All the holidays of the Jewish calendar are set by the day of the month, independent of the day of the week. Why then was Shabbos Hagadol alone decided to be commemorated on the day of Shabbos, instead of on the tenth of Nissan?

The Meshech Chochmah (Devarim 10:20) answers with another question about Shabbos in general. Shabbos commemorates the creation of the world in its entirety. This, then, is something that should be uniform to all the nations of the world, and not specifically to the Jewish people. Yet we find that, while a non-Jew is welcome to perform any of the mitzvot, he is absolutely forbidden to keep Shabbos. This needs a serious explanation – what is the Jewish monopoly on Shabbos, which celebrates not a national event, but a global one?

He explains that Shabbos is not just about refraining from the thirty-nine categories of forbidden work – it is about resting and rejoicing in abstinence. Here in America, we are all used to the concept of a weekend, and generally speaking, even if one isn't keeping Shabbos, they probably still aren't going to work. But, back in the day, people worked every day of the week, often bringing home just enough for the day's expenses. Under such circumstances, it was quite the challenge to put away one's hammer one out of seven days. Even if one would have the fortitude to take the plunge, it would be very hard to enjoy and rejoice in the holy day, instead of biting one's nails with worry about his livelihood.

What then is the key to achieving the true aura of Shabbos, and the happiness that it is meant to contain? Trust in Hashem. The only one who truly believes that G-d is the One who provides sustenance is able to put his worries in the hands of his Creator and rejoice as his store is shuttered.

The Meshech Chochmah likens one who trusts in Hashem to a servant of the king. He lives in the palace and eats from the royal kitchen – with all his needs met, he has nothing to worry about and no personal interest in a day's work. Such a servant is the happiest man when he gets the day off because it costs him nothing and he knows he'll eat just the same either way. Not so your average working man for whom vacation carries the price tag of a day's wages.

The one who trusts in Hashem is like the servant. He knows that all his needs are met by the Almighty and he only works because Hashem decreed that he must. When Hashem gives him 'the day off' with the onset of Shabbos, he knows that the vacation carries no price tag and is able to rest with a light heart in the knowledge that he will lack nothing thereby. Only such a person is truly able to connect to the power of Shabbos.

In this vein, we can understand why Shabbos HaGadol is specifically set for Shabbos and not merely for the calendar date of the event in history. When the Jewish people dragged their masters deity through the streets to the slaughter, they achieved a paramount level of trust in Hashem, and from here they continued to capitalize and build on their achievement. They soon plunged headfirst into the sea even before it split and followed Hashem into the desert with no source of sustenance, but it all began with the act of Shabbos HaGadol.

It was this level of trust which ultimately primed the Jewish people and made them capable of receiving the gift of Shabbos. The trust which they attained on Shabbos HaGadol set them apart from all the nations of the world, making them and them alone the proper recipients of the Shabbos. Because this Shabbos miracle was the event that earned them the reward of Shabbos for all time, it was befitting for the commemoration of the event to be reserved for Shabbos every single year.

This Shabbos HaGadol, let us proudly and unabashedly denounce all of the fickle things in which we trust, realizing that Hashem alone is the One who provides. And as we rest on Shabbos, let us realize that it's a paid vacation, since our needs are met from the treasury of the King.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Parshat

Acharei Mot-Kedoshim

Room for Improvement
