Avi · April 27, 2018

This week' double Parsha Achrei & Kedoshim summarize the Yom Kippur procedure. In wake of the death of Nadav and Avihu, Achrei opens with warning the Cohen Gadol when entering the Holy Of Holies. As part of the Yom Kippur service, we are told to draw lots to determine which of two lambs is to be sacrificed and which is to be sent to the edge of the desert.

Later on, in the parsha, it mentions that no animal sacrifices be made outside the Beit Hamikdash. The prohibition of consuming blood and the laws of sexual morality are also mentioned.

The parsha Kedoshim begins with ‘you shall be holy, for I, the L-rd your G-d, am holy.” Following is a list of core traits, monogamy, charity, equality in law, Shabbat, honesty in business, respect and awe for parents, and sanctity of life. These traits separate and uplift the Jewish nation. Kedoshim leaves us off with the famous quote “love your fellow as yourself’. In other words “the whole Torah on one foot.” Or as Rabbi Akiva put it the “cardinal principle”

Rabbi Akiva himself remains a pillar in this thought, however, his students were not able to exemplify loving one's fellow as themselves. Rabbi Akiva's students were diligent and exacting, each with their unique thoughts and talents. However many were killed by the plague for “they did not conduct themselves with respect for each other.” How could Rabbi Akiva's instruction lead them so far astray?

As individuals, we are encouraged to make note of our accomplishments and amass self-worth, while constantly looking for ways to refine ourselves and grow. Growth is supposed to be a proactive process driven by desire and willingness. Often our personal change is only a reaction to having repeated a behavior until it can no longer be sustained. The change that results from this process didn't stem from a true change in the will. True growth is achieved only through constant refining of the will and proactive decisions to change your own behavior.

Rabbi Akiva's students were like brothers engrossed in the same topics and living side by side. Our sages have said that “just as every person’s face differs from the faces of his fellows, so to every person’s mind differs from the minds of his fellows.” This would have been of minor concern if Rabbi Akiva's students didn't love each other so much. Each individual loved their fellow to the level they loved themselves leaving no room for nuance, and no room for opinion. Students swallowed each other whole correcting and refining each other, from a third party these students did not look like friends at all.

Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai lived past the plague that wiped out 24,000 of R Akiva's students. When the Roman empire put a price on his head he and his son Elazar went to a cave. For twelve years they studied Torah, and when they emerged the sight of mundane tasks at hand sent fury and fire onto day laborers. When a voice called from heaven “Have you come out to destroy My world? Return to your cave!” Rabbi Shimon returned for his thirteenth year of cave dwelling in which he prepared himself to invest in the endeavors of others. Now when he reintegrated, his gaze gave blessing and healing.

Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai is revered for being the one for whom Torah was his sole vocation. In his thirteenth year, he attached himself to an eternity that is Torah and learned to advocate for the same devotion in others. In putting more emphasis on practice and learning Rabbi Shimon Bar Yochai only led by example. In contrast to the 24,000 students who held their fellow to their own standard leaving no room for growth in their community.

This week, on the 33rd of a forty-nine day period counting the days between Pesach and Shavuot, we will mourn the loss of 24,000 students of R Akiva. We will also celebrate the life of R’ Shimon bar Yochai who delicately balanced the ability to recognize the virtues of others and encourage growth by leading as an example. A true mark of a tzaddik is one whose death inspires their largest movement. This is certainly true of R Shimon bar Yochai.

Shabbat Shalom,

Avi

Tragedy of Tragedies

Yosef · May 10, 2019

Imagine the world's most promising medical student. His genius has already gained him fame and recognition in every area of the medical field. From one side of the world to the other, his name is uttered with awe by all of the world's top doctors. While still a student, he has published research opening the door to an eventual cure to cancer, and the eyes of all humanity rest on him to find the answer. He graduates college and decides to take a well-earned trip touring Europe before embarking on his dazzling career. In an effort to 'get away', he takes no means of communication and no one hears from him for over a year. Eventually, one of his most optimistic professors attends an event in England and bumps into his star pupil. Not in the Oxford library, but in a burger joint... flipping burgers. He barely recognizes his student, hair grown long and wearing faded jeans and a t-shirt. "What happened to you," gasps the professor, disbelieving. "I'm happy here," comes the apathetic response, "who needs all that work with medical research."

Tragedy of tragedies! What vast potential, what glamorous dreams, all wasted for nothing other than laziness.

The parsha begins, "Speak to the entire assembly of Israel and tell them, 'You shall be holy for I, Hashem your God, am holy.'" Here we are charged with achieving no less than kedusha, loosely translated as holiness. In the classic work, Mesilat Yesharim, we find kedusha as the very top rung on the ladder of spiritual greatness. There he devotes a few chapters explaining what exactly defines kedusha, but in a few words, it refers to absolute unity with G-d Himself. Only one whose every thought and action is focused on Godliness has achieved kedusha.

We would think that such loftiness is expected only from an elite group, but surely not the benchmark for your average layman. Yet Rashi makes an astonishing comment – he says that Moshe was required to gather every single member of the nation; men, women, and even children, to communicate this mitzvah. That means that it is expected from each and every Jew. No matter who he is, he can and must achieve kedusha.

If the Torah expects such holiness from us, then rest assured that we all possess the ability to get there. The Jewish soul is a piece of G-d himself and in it is contained literally endless potential. We can do it if only we will believe in ourselves, strive ever higher, and never sell ourselves short.

Let us remember the parable of our tragic medical student, and feel the pain and sorrow of such wasted potential. Let us realize that the tragedy depicted in the parable is only a drop in the ocean next to a waste of our own spiritual capabilities. Let us understand what heights we are able to reach and use this understanding to bring our limitless abilities to fruition.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Avi · April 30, 2020

In This week's Parsha Acharei Kedoshim, God warns Bnai Yisroel of the potential of the Kodesh Kodeshim, the innermost chamber of the Temple. On Yom Kippur the Kohen Gadol only may enter, even then he is doing so at his own risk, if his mind wanders or his intentions waver he may not come out alive. Similarly on Yom Kippur we address two parts of ourselves, a part that is fit to enter the holiest places and parts of ourselves we would like to ditch. In this ceremony two goats are chosen, one is offered to God the other is driven into the desert to meet its demise.

We are warned not to make animal sacrifices away from the temple, we are commanded to clean the blood from our meats, and we are warned about illicit sexual relations. In this week's Parsha God tells us to emulate him, and the prohibition of idol worship and commandments of charity, honesty in business and equality in law are all constructed in the image of God. Finally we are given the commandment which may be most applicable to our human relationships. Love your fellow as yourself.

All these commandments seem to have a general theme. God warning of potential and the importance of boundaries. A sacrifice offered in the temple may free you of your sins but an offering made off the temple mount is an abomination. If not properly cleaned the very food that sustains us may make us ill. God is warning us of a pitfall in our human nature. When we see the potential for good it is in our human interest to ignore the negative potential. As adults we may take risks to accomplish our goals and sometimes knowing the potential for harm or failure may drive us not to try at all.

Baruch Hashem who gave us the Torah! By emulating God we see the dualities of our actions, and we can appreciate the intentions that make meaning in our lives and know that it is not about what we do but how we do it.

An "Extra" Mitzvah

April 23, 2021

One of this week’s parshiot is Parshat Kedoshim. It starts with a mitzvah that at first glance seems superfluous. After having been warned about forbidden relationships, seen the consequences of Nadav and Avihu actions, and right before more mitzvot relating to moral and spiritual growth we are commanded, “קְדֹשִׁ֣ים תִּהְי֑וּ“ (“You Shall be Holy”). Hasn’t that been the main point of the narrative and commandments spanning the last couple parshiot? What does this commandment add to our understanding of achieving the goal of holiness?

The Ramban asks a similar question on Rashi’s understanding of this posuk. Rashi states the command to be holy means, “הֱווּ פְרוּשִׁים מִן הָעֲרָיוֹת וּמִן הָעֲבֵרָה” (“To separate from forbidden relationships and from sin.”) The Ramban, citing other sources that refer to abstinence as a virtue unto itself, says this mitzvah must refer to something more. In his opinion, the mitzvah is referring to abstinence even from permitted temptations. He describes the “נבל ברשות התורה” (sordid person within the permissible realm of the Torah). This person indulges every pleasure to the point of excess. He is a glutton but only eats kosher meat, a drunkard but only drinks kosher wine, he is sex-obsessed but only for his wifes. While this character may seem to comply with the bare minimum necessary to be “Torah Compliant” his attitude and demeanor are themselves a violation. He is not using the regulations of the Torah as a tool to strive for holiness and closeness to God, but rather as a fig leaf to barely obscure his vile actions.

This mitzvah is teaching us an important lesson about how we achieve holiness. We can use the Torah and our observance as a form of moral licensing that allows us to indulge without restraint in the permissible. This perspective undermines the entire exercise of religious restriction and leaves us no holier than before. On the other hand, using our religious practice as a constant reminder and reinforcement of a lifestyle of moderation and self-restraint can result in us achieving greatness and even godliness. As the posuk concludes, “קְדֹשִׁ֣ים תִּהְי֑וּ כִּ֣י קָד֔וֹשׁ אֲנִ֖י יְהוָ֥ה אֱלֹהֵיכֶֽם׃” (“You shall be holy, for I, the LORD your God, am holy.”)

Shabbat Shalom

Scapegoating Ourselves

April 29, 2022

After Nadav and Avihu are killed for bringing foreign sacrifices we are instructed that only the high priest may enter the holy of holies on Yom Kippur. In this week's Parsha we are taught the laws of Yom Kippur and the process of casting away guilt.

The idea of scapegoating in the Jewish sense is to dramatize a dynamic that is so universal that in relating the scene the participants live it. In our ceremony lots are cast to determine which of two identical goats is "To The Lord" and which is "to Azazel". Some say Azazel means a hard rocky place others agree that it is the name of a bad angel but the simplest answer is that it means ez-goat azel-sent away, hence the latin translation scapegoat. Maimonides says "There is no doubt that sins cannot be carried like a burden, and taken off the shoulder of one being to be laid on that of another being. But these ceremonies are of a symbolic character, and serve to impress men with a certain idea, and to induce them to repent; as if to say, we have freed ourselves of our previous deeds, have cast them behind our backs, and removed them from us as far as possible." Maimonides believes we are not rational and as humans framing our spiritual goals with physical behavior helps us make a change. Whether it be casting crumbs into bodies of water or animal sacrifice.

As Jews, we are very familiar with guilt offerings but this particular offering distinguishes itself. Why do we use two goats? Because The Torah specifies two objectives: "On this day atonement will be made for you, to cleanse you. Then, before the Lord, you will be clean from all your sins" (Leviticus 16:30) the two distinct objectives are atonement and purification, atonement being for deeds and purification being for people. We are regularly given the chance to atone and assume responsibility, even if this is hard, purification is truly an opportunity to give us a clean moral slate. The goat that is sacrificed is "To The Lord" is for atonement and for acts we committed. The goat sent away "To Azazel" will make the long journey through the desert for our moral renewal.

In Gan Eden when we ate from the tree of knowledge we hid and blamed circumstances for our lust. In this week's Parsha we are taught to do the opposite "It was me, and I offer no excuses, merely admission, remorse, and a determination to change." These words are powerful and resemble the confessions the high priest makes on Yom Kippur on our behalf. In our renewed view of ourselves, we know sin is practically inevitable and preys on the spiritually weak. We won't have a Yom Kippur with nothing to atone for, by taking responsibility we invite the possibility of gaining higher perspective through watching our own growth and healing. By remembering the duality of or nature we remember the power of our yetzer hara can be captured and used as we choose. Being a nation that believes in one god, we believe that god is also captured by this duality feeling remorse and being torn in times of tough decision. This is why occasionally we must be purified. Fixing our problems is not enough we must free ourselves from sin's corrosive residue after correcting our behavior. Jewish scapegoating is the ultimate form of ownership of one's misdeeds.

The Self-Imposed Masters

Yosef · May 6, 2022

The Torah exhorts us 'Do not follow after idolatrous trinkets and do not make for yourselves molten gods.' At first glance, the verse seems redundant, but Rashi reveals the great lesson which is being conveyed. He states, 'At first they are nothing but worthless trinkets, but, if you follow them, they will end up being gods over you.'

Rabbi Yeruchom Levovitz explains this to be an all encompassing lesson, not limited to idolatry alone. There are a great many things in life which, in truth, are useless, powerless, and devoid of any importance. But if, for whatever reason, we decide to make these things a part of our lives, we very quickly become wholly dependent upon them.

Rabbi Levovitz cites a case from the Gemara concerning the first king of our nation, Shaul. When the prophet Shmuel was sent by Hashem to anoint Shaul as king, the humble Shaul fled, wanting nothing of the power and prestige of the throne. Still, when it was later decreed for Shaul to step down from the throne in deference to Dovid, he fought mightily to kill Dovid and retain his kingship. This is but one example of how honor, once despised and unwanted, could take of hold of even the greatest men, eventually ruling over them.

The same can be said of countless physical pleasures and conveniences. How many times have we scorned the overindulgence of friends and neighbors, only to later once indulge ourselves and become utterly dependent on what we formerly considered absurd?

The same can be said of many helpers and devices. One may be fine in school on their own, but once they find some brilliant friend who is ready to help, they suddenly find that they just can't keep up on their own.

Or how about the backup camera which I never missed until I got one – and now I'm handicapped without it.

I don't mean to say that every convenience is an evil, but we should at least be aware that every silly thing we let into our lives will, almost necessarily, become an imperative.

So let's think twice before we get involved in things we don't need, especially if they are out of our reach. And as for those things which already have taken hold of us, let us remember their true lack of value and realize that it is only by our decision that they gained mastery over us.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

We Rate Our Mitzvos

Yosef · April 28, 2023

Moshe was a good boy who lived in the Ramot section of Jerusalem. At the time of his Bar Mitzvah, Moshe heard an idea to choose one mitzvah in which to excel in and take to the highest level. This is one way to achieve greatness, because when one chooses one area in which he will take no shortcuts, eventually this will lead him to fix all of his flaws. Moshe, enamored by the idea, decided that his special mitzvah would be davening with a minyan (a quorum). He took upon himself never to miss a minyan and to work his life around this mitzvah, come what may.

For a few years, everything went smoothly. Sure, it wasn't always easy, but more or less Moshe was able to conveniently schedule life in such a way that his undertaking would be fulfilled. After a few years of never missing, though, Moshe faced a real test.

He had gone to a wedding with friends, missing his usual Maariv minyan, but planning to find a minyan at the wedding hall. Unfortunately he couldn't find one there, and resolved to go to a 'minyan factory' near his house as soon as he got home. He got carried away by the dancing and after a long evening finally boarded the bus home with a few of his friends. They chatted amicably, and in the midst of it all, it slipped Moshe's mind that he hadn't yet gone to Maariv.

He showered and finally climbed into bed, well after one o'clock in the morning, weary from a long day. As his head sunk into the pillow, he suddenly remembered that he hadn't yet davened Maariv. An average person would have managed a quick bedside prayer before retiring, but not Moshe. He had a special mitzvah, and he was committed to uphold it – no matter what.

He knew that the local minyan factory didn't run so late, so he decided that he would call a taxi and travel to the famed Zichron Moshe Shtiblach, quite a way from his house, where he was confident that minyanim would still be available. Moshe and his family were far from wealthy, so he pulled out his stash of Bar Mitzvah money to pay for the taxi – taking the whole stack just to be safe. He finally arrived at Zichron Moshe somewhere in the two's, and found, to his surprise, that it was empty. Not one person was waiting for Maariv. Dejected, he almost gave up, but as he took out a phone to call another taxi for the ride home, he had an idea. He asked the dispatcher to send him as many taxi's as he could. This company had four available, and from another few companies he managed to order a total of nine. They pulled up in front of the shul and Moshe instructed the irreligious Jews to please turn on their meters and come inside to help with the minyan. Nine taxi's would cost him the remainder of his assets, but to Moshe it was well worth upholding his special mitzvah. Each one managed to find some sort of Kippah, and Moshe led the services. For many of the drivers, this was the first time in a shul since their own Bar Mitzvahs. When they concluded the service, Moshe took out his stash and asked each driver to tell him the charge. One after another, however, the drivers declined payment and tearfully thanked Moshe for allowing them to be part of such a beautiful minyan.

This is an incredible story about the dedication of a young teenager and of the power of a mitzvah done to the fullest to touch the hearts of those far from Torah. But I heard an incredible lesson from this story which pertains to all of us on a daily basis.

Many times in life, we are given the choice between a mitzvah and some personal drive or desire. How we choose defines the value of all of our mitzvos. In effect, we personally rate each of our actions.

How much do you think every minyan that Moshe attends is worth? We can't really know, but it's worth more than his entire bank account and a whole lot of work in the middle of the night. He rated it as such.

But suppose we miss a minyan in exchange for a few extra minutes of shut-eye, to spare ourselves the inconvenience of cutting our supper short, or to some other minor deterrent. By doing so, we have unfortunately rated our minyan's value far below what it should be worth. After all, we ourselves have shown what silly things we are willing to trade our mitzvah out for. [This idea is brought out in the Ramban in this week's Parshah (18:5)]

So let's be ever cognizant of the value mitzvos have over worldly drives and pleasures, and let's make sure to retain the value of our actions and keep them at the premium they are truly worth.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Parshat

Emor

Whose Blood is Redder?

May 17, 2019

This week's Parsha contains the verse,

"ונקדשתי בתוך בני ישראל"

"and I will be sanctified in the midst of the Children of Israel"

From here the Sefer HaChinuch explains, we derive the mitzvah of kiddush Hashem, the imperative to sanctify the name of God. This sanctification is achieved through martyrdom, giving up our physical lives to stay consistent with our religious values. Despite this running contrary to the axiom to "live through the commandments," our sages identified three cardinal sins that are so foundational to our faith we must be willing to sacrifice our lives rather than violate them.

In the explanation of the cardinal sin of murder, the Sefer HaChinuch brings a Gemara in Sanhedrin that offers a logical metaphor as the source for the prohibition of killing another, even at the expense of one's own life. "Do you see that your blood is redder? Perhaps the blood of that man is redder." The Sefer HaChinuch explains this to mean,

The Sefer HaChinuch seems to be claiming that because of the infinite potential of a human we don't know what one individual is capable of achieving. Therefore, we cannot morally choose our own future over theirs. The next conclusion that he brings regarding an entire town (originally from a Gemara in Yerushalmi Terumot) seems to take this ideal and stretch it to its limits.

In a position that seems to run contrary to the Sefer HaChinuch's conflation of these two moral issues, the Ramach notes that the rationale of "whose blood is redder?" does not apply in the case in which townspeople must decide between handing over one person and being killed. He insists that under these circumstances, we can indeed determine which misfortune is graver, as whomever the people choose to hand over to the enemy would otherwise be killed along with the rest of them. As this decision does not require us to be selective, we can limit the damage being done.

What all of the opinions seem to reject, albeit to differing degrees is classical utilitarianism. The underlying assumption, "Nature has placed mankind under the governance of two sovereign masters, pain and pleasure" that forms the basis for utilitarian thinking reduces a human to their capacity to feel. There is much more to a human being, the infinite potential for closeness to god. Furthermore, this premise contains a duality that is alarming for Jewish thinkers. After all, we are the people that,

" מברך על הרעה מעין על הטובה ועל הטובה מעין על הרעה"

"bless for the bad just as for the good."

We don't see the good and evil as separate self-determined forces. They are both emanations from a single omnipotent god. Our commitment to unity extends even further as explained in Tanya, all Jews are one entity sharing a single spiritual presence. Connecting to this truth through our precepts and customs is how we achieve in this world. To view life as a zero-sum game of pain versus pleasure is to ignore the true point of existence, connection.

We have one sovereign master, and when we acknowledge and connect to the infinite potential in those around us, we sanctify his name.

Shabbat Shalom

A New Look at Mitzvos

Adam · May 8, 2020

There is a common Jewish phobia today known as Benchaphobia, or in English Graceaftermealsaphobia, the fear of eating bread and being required to recite the Birkas Hamazon.

It is the second most common fear amongst observant Jewry today, second only to Fleishophobia, the fear of being "fleishig" and unable to consume dairy for 6ish hours.

As much as I understand these fears, I'd like to explore an idea relevant to this time of year and hopefully help treat the first of these all-too-common phobias.

Today, (Friday still) is Pesach Sheni, the second Passover. It's a minor holiday on the Jewish calendar and today consists mostly of having a bit of matzah in commemoration of the Holiday practiced in temple times.

When the temple still stood, there was a law that one who was Tamei, (ritually "impure" for lack of a better English translation) as a result of contact with the dead was not able to bring a sacrifice. On Pesach, every Jew was required to take part in a Korban Pesach, or Passover sacrifice. Naturally, since some people would have died in the week preceding each year's Holiday and others would be required to bury them or attend the funeral, those attending would not be able to participate in the Korban due to their Tamei status.

Not to worry though, the Torah had these Jews' backs; there's a second shot at this one on Pesach Sheni one month later.

Interestingly, however, this mitzvah was introduced to the Jewish people only after the first Pesach when a group of Tamei Jews came to complain to Moshe.

"Lama Nigarah, why should we lose out?" they asked.

Moshe took the question back to Hashem and returned to reveal the law of Pesach Sheni, the make-up Holiday.

At a time when many of us might have thought, "Hey, I'm Tamei, I guess I can skip this Mitzvah this year," these giants came to lay their claims.

"What have we done wrong, that we should miss out on this mitzvah opportunity?"

I think there is a lot we can learn from this outlook on Mitzvos. Korban stems from the word Karav, to draw close. These Jews understood that they were indeed missing out. They saw every Mitzvah as an opportunity to draw closer to their creator and understood that the inability to do a mitzvah, even one that they were absolved from, was truly a loss.

"Why should we lose out?!"

My oldest, Yonason Ephraim, at six years old will frequently ask," Can I have bread at this meal? I want to bench."

I thought it was a ploy to have carbs but when I offered "mezonos bread", a sweetened kind of bread that doesn't require Birkas Hamazon, he turned it down.

"I want to Bench though!"

I suppose no-one has introduced him to Benchophobia yet. And I hope he never learns.

My plea for all of us is that we take this opportunity to exchange our glasses regarding Mitzvos.

May we always view them as an opportunity to connect. May we merit to return to the Holy Land, and to bring the Korbanos, drawing ever closer to the one above and to each other!

Good Shabbos!

Love, Adam

May 2022

This week's Parsha, Emor, "Speak," focuses on laws regarding the Kohanim and the Temple service -- a Kohen may not become impure through contact with the dead except for close relatives; a Kohen Gadol may only marry a virgin; a Kohen with a physical deformity may not serve; a deformed animal may not be sacrificed.

The second half of the Parsha turns to the cycle of Jewish holidays: weekly Shabbat, Pesach and the counting of the Omer culminating in Shavuot, Rosh Hashanah, Yom Kippur ten days later, and Sukkot's seven days followed by Shemini Atzeret. The Parsha concludes with the story of a man executed for blasphemy, and laws of compensation for injury and property damage.

Two themes run through this Parsha: holiness and priestly purity on one hand, holidays and communal ceremony on the other -- together forming the foundation of Jewish community.

2023

This week's Parsha, Emor, "Speak," focuses on laws regarding the Kohanim and the Temple service -- a Kohen may not become impure through contact with the dead except for close relatives; a Kohen Gadol may only marry a virgin; a Kohen with a physical deformity may not serve; a deformed animal may not be sacrificed.

The second half of the Parsha turns to the cycle of Jewish holidays: weekly Shabbat, Pesach and the counting of the Omer culminating in Shavuot, Rosh Hashanah, Yom Kippur ten days later, and Sukkot's seven days followed by Shemini Atzeret. The Parsha concludes with the story of a man executed for blasphemy, and laws of compensation for injury and property damage.

Two themes run through this Parsha: holiness and priestly purity on one hand, holidays and communal ceremony on the other -- together forming the foundation of Jewish community. Recognizing that we are each born into different roles goes a long way toward strengthening our communal ties, and it is the shared calendar of celebration -- Shavuot, Sukkot, and the rest -- that reminds us G-d is the source of our good fortune, binding and elevating an entire people together.

Festival

Lag Ba'Omer

Avi · April 30, 2021

On Lag baomer we celebrate the life of R' Shimon Bar Yochai, a contributor to the mishnah. Rabbi shimon bar yochai was a devout student of rabbi Akiva; together they studied talmud and torah against the decree of the roman emperor. Many of Rabbi Akiva's students and colleagues died martyrs for their judaism. After the death of the reigning roman emperor restrictions on jewish lives eased allowing for a few of rabbi Akiva's remaining students to gather and discuss diplomacy strategies with Rome going in the future. The three rabbis had different views, Rabbi Yehuda suggested that they be friendly with the new romanleader Rabbi Yose expressed no opinion and Rabbi Shimon remembering his martyred colleagues called for maximum defiance of the new government.

When the conversation was reported to Roman authorities Rabbi Yehuda became the Jewish ambassador to Rome and Rabbi Shimon was sentenced to death.

Escaping his life sentence Rabbi Shimon and his son Rabbi Elazar hid in a cave for 12 years sustaining themselves with carob fruit and water, there in the cave the father and son became great and wise sages. When there was a new shift in the Roman government Rabbi Shimon and his son left the cave when they saw fellow Jews plowing the earth he exclaimed "Imagine people giving up the sacred study of the Torah for worldly matters!" upon his utterance the fruits of the fields went up in smoke, a heavenly voice boomed "Have you come out to destroy My world? Go back to your cave!"

Rabbi Shimon and his son returned to the cave where they spent twelve months getting an attitude adjustment. When they reemerged they saw a Jew carrying two myrtle branches home on friday afternoon they asked the man what they were for and he explained they were to make his house fragrant for Shabbos Rabbi shimon impressed by this man's dedication told his son "See how precious the precepts are to our brethren!" Despite the attempts by the Romans, Jews still celebrated and identified.

When R Shimon saw one of his colleagues his friend began to cry. R Shimon was in very bad health from his years in the cave and his friend and colleague was worried. R shimon reassured him by saying he would never have reached such heights of piety if not for his years in the cave.

Rabbi Shimon taught and many came to hear him until there was another change in the Roman government Jews were once again being persecuted. Together with his fellow scholars R shimon organized a delegation to approach the Roman emperor.

The roman emperor spoke of his ill daughter. Rabbi Shimon, seeing the opportunity offered to cure his daughter of her illness. After a few sessions R Shimon was successful and was offered the kingdom in return. Rabbi Shimon ended the decrees against jews and returned to teaching and learning Torah.

Despite being a recluse for so many years Rabbi shimon is revered for his ethics and the way he treated others. Rabbi Shimon said "Man should rather jump into a fiery furnace than offend another in public." "To deceive anyone by words is worse than cheating him out of money." and "He who lets arrogance get the better of him is like the heathen worshipping idols." We celebrate his life on lag baomer observed on Thursday of this week. Good Shabbos!

Parshat

Behar

2018

This weeks parsha, Behar, opens on Moshe speaking with God on Mount Sinai. Specifically at this summit of spiritual experience God instructs us to safeguard the Shmita cycle. We are told that for 6 years we may work our fields and prune our vineyards but the seventh year, "the land shall have a complete rest a Sabbath to the Lord." The parsha continues with the complex agricultural restrictions surrounding Shmita. It explains that we may eat from the produce that continue to grow during this year, however we cannot sow new seeds or prune existing crops.

Rashi immediately ask a question. Weren't all the commandments given on Mount Sinai? Rashi explains that the Torah emphasizes that this mitzvah was given on Sinai to teach us that just as all of the complex details were taught as Sinia, so too all of the mitzvos had their technicalities and specifics explained at Sinai.

If the lesson from Shmita really is applicable to every other commandment, why would the Torah choose to teach it to us through Shmita? Why not have the first or last mitzvah as the example? Why not choose to teach us this lesson from Shabbos? What about Shmita is so fundamental to our mission as Jews that it meritted to validate all other commandments, and their minutia, as originating from the Revelation at Sinai?

Shmita is unique in that it commands a type of dedication to God that is more nuanced than any other. While Shabbos and Holidays offer us a similar respite from labor, this is a complete rest. We are forbidden from engaging with the material world except to fulfill our most basic needs. On Shmita we are allowed an opportunity to still go about our daily routine. We can travel, spend money, and engage in business, all of which are forbidden on Shabbos and Yom Tov. We are only prohibited from toiling in our fields for the sake of the next years harvest.

The Shmita year gives us an unparalleled opportunity to elevate our daily lives, our "mundane" routines. Instead of refraining from materialism to attain spiritual connection, we take spirituality and transform the temporal. But this process can only take place if we relinquish our sense of control and truly believe that everything comes from God. This perspective shift is achieved through the singular prohibition on cultivation. Our focus can no longer be about accumulating wealth and securing the next year's abundance. We have to accept that the future is in God's hands alone. For a full year, we are reminded daily that our efforts are not what sustain life and create wealth. We only exist and are sustained through the compassion and good that God bestows upon the universe constantly. Every aspect of our lives, every detail of our routine is only possible because God has given us the opportunities and resources to maintain it. The resulting humility enables us to appreciate and elevate all of the blessings God gives us daily instead of worrying about what will be.

Many of these same lessons are found during the revelation at Sinai. The giving of the Torah was the culmination of God's demonstration that his will controls the world. Our acceptance and performance of the mitzvos is an opportunity to transform every aspect of our lives into a testament to this fact. From when we wake up to when we go to sleep, we are constantly able to choose to do the right thing, creating a life of holiness and connection. Furthermore, the choice of Mount Sinai as the setting for this event shows that God can only share his will with those who make room for him. The Gemara teaches that God specifically came down to Mount Sinai instead of raising the mountain up. From this the Gemara learns that God himself descends to the humble and engages with them. When a person believes that their will determines their future, they cannot achieve the surrender necessary to make room for the will of God.

WIth this understanding, it is clear why Shmita was chosen as the mitzvah to teach us the profound message that every detail of every commandment came from God at Sinai. Every detail of Shmita is designed to give us the opportunity to relinquish our perceived control and make room for God in our lives. We don't have to make any drastic changes to our lifestyles yet they can become infused with a constant reminder that we are not running the show, we aren't the center of the universe. These are the essential prerequisites on which all other commandments rest. The commandments and all of their particularities and strigencies are an exercise in accepting that our will is not paramount and incorporating this awareness into every moment of our lives.

We should all merit to lead lives that are replete with this awareness and the resulting peace of spirit.

Shabbat Shalom

Avi · May 24, 2019

In this weeks Parsha Behar Bnai Yisrael are commanded the laws of shmita and yovel, property laws and the laws of charging interest.These are all defining characteristics of being a Jew in Israel farming or a Jew conducting business with another Jew.The law of not charging interest is cut and dry, "If your brother becomes poor, and his means fail, you shall support him [with a loan]. . . . Do not take interest and usury from him. . . . I am the L-rd your G-d, who has taken you out of the land of Egypt . . . to be your G-d. " of course being the tribe of lawyers and judges we are, klal yisrael relies on a "heter iska" to allow one Jew to profit from sharing funds with his fellow. This specific arrangement is set up as a rental in which the money received by the borrower never leaves the domain of the lender, there may be restrictions on what the money can be used for or there may not. This is distinctly different from a loan where a a sum leaves the hand of the lender and then is repaid in excess for complete inactivity. By setting up a loan through this process a lender is investing in the ventures of his fellow, even when the investor gives a lump sum his share rises and falls with his fellows endeavour. The restrictions put on the money in a heter iska allow a borrower to repay his lender for the favor of connecting their financial fates. Lending money to your fellow when he is failing is a mitzvah, we are commanded to help when we can.

Our arrangement with god is the same as an investor and borrower. Our sages teach us that God does all the same mitzvot we do. The midrash explains that unlike most kings who delegate and avoid direct involvement Hashem's very footsteps in the world set israel into action. God leads by paving the path, and we, his children follow suit.

At the same time other sources show that God rejoices in our mitzvot and in turn we kindle another facet of gods observance.

Why is god moved by our service? "If you sin, how have you affected Him? If your transgressions are many, what have you done to Him? If you are righteous, what do you give Him? What can He receive from you?" Why does god command us with restrictions? This is the lesson of heter iska. Your life is not a free loan! God does not run a body gemach! We are in business with God and encouraged to make the world a better place in return for the life we are granted.

Shabbat shalom, Avi

Freedom From Posession

Noah · May 20, 2022

This week's Parsha Behar lays out some of our most fundamental rights and responsibilities around land ownership and wealth accumulation. These laws are not merely dry and technical regulations but instead focus on the human experience of property exchange. The first mitzvah in the Parsha immediately illustrates this point. We are instructed, "When you enter the land that I assign to you, the land shall observe a sabbath of יהוה." According to the Klei Yakar, the Shemitta year is not about allowing the land to rest so it will be fertile in the coming seasons. If that was the case it would not be called a "Shabbath of Hashem."

The Klei Yakar explains that rather, it is "to implant the character traits of faith and trust in Hashem in the people of Israel. Hashem was concerned that perhaps when they would come to the land they would work the soil in a natural way, and when they would be successful they would forget Hashem and remove their trust from Him. They would think that 'their might and the power of their hand have made them this wealth,' and the world goes along in its natural way. They would think that the land belongs to them; they are the masters and no one else."

This theme is repeated throughout the Parsha. Our laws around property rights are not about protecting ownership as much as about fostering trust, realizing that Hashem truly owns everything and that property is meant to be used equitably. The next mitzvah, the Yovel, makes this point clearly.

"And you shall sanctify the fiftieth year. You shall proclaim liberty throughout the land for all its inhabitants. It shall be a jubilee for you: each of you shall return to your holding and each of you shall return to your family."

This year reverses many of the largest financial decisions people have made over the last 50 years and we start it by proclaiming freedom? This is one of the most interventionist economic policies in the Torah and it is justified with the same principle that is used to advocate for laissez-faire economics today.

I think this drastic change in perspective is deeply rooted in the character traits the Klei Yakar outlined above. Someone who subscribes to the philosophy that 'their might and the power of their hand have made them this wealth,' would see the Yovel as a limitation on freedom. They worked and toiled for material gain that all can be taken away! But someone who has had the 'character traits of faith and trust in Hashem' implanted in them may view this reversion to an economic mean differently.

When we have faith in Hashem and the people around us and the humility to realize that what we own is just on loan from Hashem, there is nothing more freeing than the opportunity to begin again. From this positive perspective, freedom from material things can be achieved whereas the disposition that the Klei Yakar warns about can only result in servitude to stuff.

While we see that trust in Hashem is created through Shemittah observance, where is our interpersonal trust derived from? The next verse states, "When you sell property to your neighbor or buy any from your neighbor, you shall not wrong one another." This warning is reinforced with the subsequent bans on usury and interest.

Taken all together we see that the Torah's perspective on regulating property ownership is designed to create an economic framework that is trustworthy, equitable and human-centric. This is a high bar to set but by following the Klei Yakar's advice to trust in Hashem and by conducting our own business fairly and faithfully we can always improve.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Parshat

Bechukotai

ROI

Adam · May 31, 2019

Every investor knows that there's one key thing to evaluate above all else when investing money and time. Return on investment, or ROI. If you have a good projected return on investment, it's worth figuring out all the other factors. But without it, it's better to walk away.

This week's Torah portion is very interesting from an investing standpoint. We have quite a few guarantees from Hashem if we "go in Hashem's laws, guard the mitzvos, and perform them."

We are promised rain in exactly the right times, ample crops that follow one another without delay, satiating bread that never leaves us hungry or wanting for more, as well as peace both internally in our society and from outside forces that may rise up against us.

Sounds like a good Return, question is, what is the investment?

Rashi jumps in immediately to ask the obvious question: "Guarding the mitzvos and doing them" would seem to refer to the positive and negative aspects of keeping mitzvos. Guarding means observing the negative commandments and doing them, the positive ones.

But what does "going in Hashem's laws" mean? Rashi says it refers to the toil in Torah learning.

The ROI on observing the Torah AND making time to learn and delve into its depths, yields the guarantee of all our physical needs being met and the societal security to enjoy the abundance we are granted.

Sounds like a pretty sweet ROI! Not only are we seeing the long-term return in the next world, we're guaranteed a healthy "cash flow" if you will, while we "build our nest egg."

And it doesn't stop there, we have the best insurance policy around to protect the abundance we're growing.

If you're a savvy investor, you probably want in on the deal!

But you'll see, it gets a bit more interesting in the next few verses. And you may get ready to call your attorney to review the documents on this one since the numbers just don't quite work out.

(Get your accounting brain on.)

Translation based on Rashi:

"...I will grant peace in the land; you will lie down to rest without fear, I will banish evil beats from the land, and no sword shall pass through your land (even to wage war against others). You will pursue your enemies and they will fall before you by their own sword. Five of you will pursue one hundred, and one hundred of you will pursue ten thousand, and your enemies will continually fall before you in supernatural ways…"

Did you catch the accounting problem? The numbers don't work out. If five are capable to take on one hundred, then one hundred should only be able to pursue two thousand, not ten! (Always read the fine print.

But before you get the attorney on the phone, let's look at Rashi's explanation.

He says one of the most important lessons we need to know in our generation, "this is teaching you that there is no comparison between a few who perform the Torah and many who perform the Torah!"

Sometimes we think that maybe our responsibility is to worry about ourselves. We'll take the deal because we see the obvious ROI's both in this world and the next. And if the next person doesn't see it or isn't interested, that's their business not ours.

NOT SO, says Rashi! The Torah is telling us that our own ROI is dependant on the number of people in the syndication! And it's an exponential gain, not a linear one. So we have to invest in changing the world, and helping others join us in our mission!

May we be granted the ability to internalize what we learned from Uncle Yonatan, who never thought it was enough that he was growing but looked for the exponential return from investing in all of us (and frankly, anyone who would listen, no matter their lot) and may we realize all of the returns in this week's Parsha along with the entire Jewish nation. Good shabbos!

Love, Adam

The Giver of the Torah

Yosef · May 27, 2022

With Shavuos, the day we received the Torah, fast approaching, I'd like to take the opportunity to share a beautiful thought about the Torah. I understand that this may be a paradigm shift for many, and I believe it is one which, when truly internalized, can change our whole relationship to Torah and to Hashem.

I once had the privilege to learn with a very special young man as he was finding his way back to Torah Judaism. He had grown up in an orthodox family, but had struggled mightily in high-school and eventually found himself in the streets while heavily drug-addicted. When we met, he was taking the first steps back into society and Torah observance, and it seemed that the most powerful impetus in his miraculous return was the realization that Hashem really loved him. Today, that confidence in Hashem's love is still carrying him, and he's made a complete turn-around, beyond anyone's wildest dreams. In fact, I am thrilled to have tickets to attend his wedding in just less than a month.

What I'd like to share with you, though, is one of the main reasons he had gone astray in the first place. He grew up doing the mitzvos, learning the Torah, and believing in G-d. There was just one problem -- he pictured G-d as a menacing ruler, waiting to zap him for the slightest infraction. As he grew older, the demands of a Torah life, viewed only as rules and regulations, became burdensome to the point that he stopped conforming and rebelled.

I'd like to dispel the myth to which he, and unfortunately many others, fell prey.

Hashem is not bound to our finite notions, nor can we perceive his essence within the constraints of a mortal mind. Our only understanding of Hashem, our only windows into who He is, is through the facets by which He interacts with people. In such context, we do find certain descriptions of Hashem in scriptures and from our sages, drawing corollaries to human conduct. For example, at the splitting of the sea Hashem is described as a mighty warrior, and often He is described as a father or a king. These are not descriptions of His essence, rather they are depictions of the different ways in which He relates to us at different times.

How would we expect Hashem to 'appear' at the giving of the Torah? I hope none of us imagine the picture we painted before of the menacing ruler, but what would be a more reasonable guess? Perhaps as a sage or a judge, teaching law and wisdom? Or maybe as a king or general, giving us our directives and marching orders?

Rashi tells us the answer which I don't think any of us would have guessed. Hashem revealed himself at Sinai as a 'zaken malei rachamim', a kind elder brimming with mercy. The Torah is not the law book of a judge nor the orders of a king, it is an outpouring of love and mercy. Every word of the Torah is dripping with kindness and goodness, like the advice of a loving grandfather equipping his grandchild with the means to achieve fulfillment.

This idea is found many times in the Talmud as well, as it often prefaces a quote from the Torah with the statement 'amar rachmana', or 'the merciful One says'. The Torah is the worlds greatest expression of Hashem's mercy and love.

If we can picture that behind every verse in the Chumash and every law of the Torah, there stands not an iron-fisted ruler, and not even a wise judge, but a loving and merciful zaidy, then the Torah will take on a whole new dimension. These are not just laws and statutes, but rather the keys to success and fulfillment. They are not just rules and regulations, but opportunities and steps to create a beautiful relationship with that Loving Elder.

So for the remaining days of Sefirah, as we count up to the great day of the receiving of the Torah, let us realize just what sort of event we are anticipating -- a climactic outpouring of love expressed by G-d through the giving of His Torah.

And it is you, me, and the rest of our people who are so fortunate as to be the recipients.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Parshat

Behar-Bechukotai

Waking the Paths of Torah

May 15, 2020

Bechukotai, the second of this week's parshiot opens with, אִם־בְּחֻקֹּתַ֖י תֵּלֵ֑כוּ וְאֶת־מִצְוֺתַ֣י תִּשְׁמְר֔וּ וַעֲשִׂיתֶ֖ם אֹתָֽם - If you follow My laws and take heed of My commandments." This vague message is the subject of many interpretations. What are we to learn from this posuk? Rashi offers the classic explanation, based on the midrash, AND TAKE HEED OF MY COMMANDMENTS -- "Study the Torah laboriously with the intention to take heed and to fulfill its teachings, as it is said, (Deuteronomy 5:1) "and you shall learn them and take heed to do them."

Many commentators follow in this same vein, interpreting the verse as a coming to emphasize the importance of both learning Torah and practically utilizing the knowledge by either teaching or practicing the mitzvot. The Ibn Ezra says it most succinctly: אם בחקתי. תשמרו ועשיתם. מצוה ללמוד וללמד ולעשות

AND TAKE HEED OF MY COMMANDMENTS- It is commanded to learn, to teach and to do.

But the Aderet Eliyahu drives the point home: ואת מצותי תשמרו. הלמד שלא לעשות נוח לו שלא נברא:

AND TAKE HEED OF MY COMMANDMENTS- The one who learns Torah but doesn't do anything with the knowledge, is better off to have never existed.

The marriage of intellectual achievement to real-world action is so fundamental that each alone is worthless. But why does this invalidate a person's entire existence? This can be explained by looking to the next verse, וְנָתַתִּ֥י גִשְׁמֵיכֶ֖ם בְּעִתָּ֑ם וְנָתְנָ֤ה הָאָ֙רֶץ֙ יְבוּלָ֔הּ וְעֵ֥ץ הַשָּׂדֶ֖ה יִתֵּ֥ן פִּרְיֽוֹ׃

I will grant your rains in their season so that the earth shall yield its produce and the trees of the field their fruit.

The apparent promise of prosperity to the pious is problematic. We know that the correlation between our spiritual advancement and material wealth is tenuous at best. What does it mean and why is this promise made specifically made in regard to the commandment of learning Torah?

The Chizkuni and the Bechor Shor explain that we receive bracha in the form of rain and produce for laboriously learning Torah because to the same extent that a cloud exists to fulfill its mission of providing rain, our mission is to plumb the depths of Torah understanding. Just as an apple tree bearing fruit has achieved the central goal of its existence, when we learn Torah and perform the mitzvot we connect to the central goal of our existence. This perspective also illuminates Aderet Eliyahu's sharp Mussar. One who has learned but not utilized their learning to teach or do mitzvot is akin to an apple tree that never bears fruit. Each year it grows larger and larger taking up more space and resources without ever fulfilling its essential purpose.

Now it is easy to get lost in this metaphor and think that like apple trees we are all supposed to produce the same fruit. The Or HaChaim lays this misunderstanding to rest, saying that the Torah has 4 paths (Pshat, Remez, Drush, Sod) and each path has 70 faces which each branch off into innumerable trails and ways. This is hinted to in the plural form of the words "paths" and "you (plural) shall walk." The Torah is commanding us to explore all of these paths and forbidding us from proclaiming that there is a single path for everyone.

This week's Parsha is calling us to do the laborious work of creating a unique relationship with the Torah and exploring it vigorously. Then we are instructed to make the insights we acquire to change the way we behave and to share them with those around us. This process is our fundamental purpose in this world and its results are the fruit we yield.

Iron Skies

May 12, 2023

In the first of this week's two parshiot we learn about the mitzvot of shmittah and yovel, our obligation to redeem our captured brothers, and our responsibilities to the destitute members of our communities. We are forbidden from charging interest within our tribe and commanded, "For your kin, no one shall rule ruthlessly over another."

These mitzvot share a strong tribal perspective that prioritizes the continuation of our communal identity over individual rights. Returning ancestral lands, freeing captives, and our unique responsibilities to Jewish slaves, are just some of the many mitzvot in the parsha that emphasize our communal identity over universal morality. These are the threads that make our social fabric distinctive.

This mix of mitzvot in parshat Behar is summarized by its final verse, "You shall keep My sabbaths and venerate My sanctuary, I am God."

Rabbenu Bechaya, quoting the Ramban, explains that this conclusion is a warning to us about selling a Jew to outsiders. Isolated from the community he is bound to stray from Judaism. The one who casts away a fellow is responsible for neglecting the communal mindset that is the foundation of our society.

This is the mindset that allows us to create sabbaths and sanctuaries.

Our second parsha paints the idyllic picture of what life can look like under these conditions. We are promised bounty "so that the earth shall yield its produce and the trees of the field their fruit"

and security "I will grant peace in the land, and you shall lie down untroubled by anyone; I will give the land respite from vicious beasts, and no sword shall cross your land." Finally, we are told that God will "establish [his] abode in your midst, and will not spurn you."

This is the promise of the Land of Israel. Our security is dependent on our internal cohesion. Respecting the individual sacrifices that we all make for the sake of our tribe can be a way of venerating God's sanctuary and inviting him to abode in our midst.

The opposite is also true, "And if, for all that, you do not obey Me, I will go on to discipline you sevenfold for your sins, and I will break your proud glory. I will make your skies iron..." And if this isn't enough of a warning to make us change, "And if you remain hostile toward Me and refuse to obey Me, I will go on smiting you sevenfold for your sins."

For months, we have found ourselves in the middle of one of Israel's largest internal conflicts. Both sides have taken to the streets in record numbers, some to voice their opinions on the issues, and many to confront and demean the other side. The situation feels intractable with each of Israel's "four tribes" digging in their heels and receding deeper into their respective corners. Each one casts the other as having malevolent intentions and an unwillingness to compromise. The rhetoric has become so pessimistic that newspapers from Yisrael Hayom to Haaretz have featured headlines about the prospect of a Civil War.

And then this week, the day after lag b'omer, all schools within 40 km of Gaza were closed until further notice. The arguing pundits on the radio were supplanted by interviews of displaced families punctuated by the command to immediately seek shelter. Video footage of the Iron Dome shooting down the latest barrage is everywhere. The external threat has temporarily quieted our internal conflict.

As we watch the news unfold, I am grateful that our skies are iron. I just hope it is enough for us to recommit to making this place a sanctuary for everyone, from the inside. Only then can we be assured that the divine will dwell among us.

Shabbat Shalom

Sefer Bamidbar

The Book of Numbers

Parshat

Bamidbar

Opposition at Sinai

Yosef · May 22, 2020

When Moshe ascended Mount Sinai to receive the Torah, he was met with fierce opposition. The angels made an uproar - "What is mortal man doing here?" These angels reside in the uppermost heavenly spheres and possess a level of holiness which man cannot even fathom much less rival. How could it be, challenged the angels, that Hashem's most prized possession should be given into mankind's unworthy hands?

Our great teacher Moshe was charged with refuting the claim. He grasped the heavenly throne, in an effort to gain divine inspiration, and took the stage. He said, "The Torah states, 'I am Hashem who took you out of Egypt' – did you descend to Egypt, were you enslaved by Pharaoh, why should the Torah be yours? The Torah further states to keep Shabbos – do you perform work that you can be commanded to rest from? It further says to honor one's parents – do you have parents to honor?"

But with all this, the angels were still unsatisfied. This needs an explanation. After Moshe's very compelling argument, we need to understand what the angels' position was in the first place. Didn't they know that all the mitzvos of the Torah are things relevant specifically to our world? Our teachers explain that the angels actually had a most powerful point – and that is that the Torah in its essence is far far greater than what meets the eye in its simple understanding. Only in it's most basic level does the Torah speak of earthly matters, but in its deeper levels of understanding it can be understood in the most esoteric ways. In fact, the Vilna Gaon said that in the first verse of the Torah he was able to perceive all that would ever happen in the course of history. The angels, therefore, claimed that to give the Torah to man would be to limit it to it's the most basic meaning and strip it of its deepest essence. Such could not be tolerated. And it is for this reason that the angels were unmoved by Moshe's first and seemingly compelling point.

And so Moshe moved on to the final and decisive defense. "The Torah states: 'You shall not commit murder, adultery, or theft.' Do you have any desires which you must quell? Do you have any evil inclinations with which you must do battle?" When the angels heard this they immediately backed down and bowed to mankind's right to the Torah.

This is an incredible story. The angels were alight with righteous indignation at the thought of degrading and devaluing the Torah to mortal confines, and for this declaration of human frailty they bowed their heads? Isn't the same answer they had to Moshe's first point valid here too? In the Torah's highest and purest form, these base prohibitions also take on an esoteric and exalted form.

The answer lies not in the nature of the aforementioned prohibitions, but in the point, Moshe made from them. Man's desires and inclinations, his greatest enemies, are in fact his greatest resource. It is precisely these limitations which equip him to bring the ultimate glory to his Creator. The angels are possessed of endless holiness and purity, as well as unparalleled proximity to G-d, and it is in this way that they bring honor to Hashem. But because they have no battles to fight and no challenges to overcome, they are unable to contest with man's service. It is specifically when we engage our lower elements and emerge triumphant when we are hampered by our frailty and yet stand strong, that we bring the ultimate honor to Hashem.

In these times of difficulty we are all met with a long list of struggles. Struggles with finances, struggles in health, struggles with our patience for family, struggles in our Torah learning and mitzvah observance, struggles of sadness. And the list goes on.

Let us not be defeated or demoralized by our struggles, but realize that it is precisely the struggles which made us the recipients of the Torah and which arm us with the ability to bring the greatest honor to Hashem.

Good Shabbos, Yosef

¹ This is a loose and partial translation of the Gemara Shabbos 88b.

June 2022

In this week's Parsha, Bnai Yisrael is counted -- all men of draftable age in one count, the Levites who perform priestly duties in a separate count. Priestly service originally belonged to the firstborn, but after the sin of the golden calf that responsibility passed to the tribe of Levi -- which is why firstborns must still be redeemed today through pidyon haben, a payment of five shekels.

There are practical reasons to count a nation: military strength, dividing responsibility for transporting the Mishkan. Nachmanides explains that rather than count each person directly, each gave a half-shekel and was counted that way instead -- teaching that it is not appropriate to count Jews directly. G-d promised that Bnai Yisrael would be as numerous as the sand of the sea and the stars of the sky -- innumerable. The half-shekel is described as an atonement for the soul, because being counted, even indirectly, places a person alone on a kind of pedestal before G-d, without the communal merit that ordinarily surrounds them.

King Shaul counted his people using shards of pottery and kid goats; King David's more direct census led to the deaths of 70,000. It is inappropriate to count Jews without a specified purpose -- and even today, some Jews in Israel are reluctant to take part in a census, citing this prohibition.

On Shavuot we complete the counting of the Omer -- the days leading to the giving of the Torah. As Jews who emulate G-d, we count the days and the people who matter to us. Uncle Yonaton bound our family together with Judaism and uplifted us all -- he strengthened our sense of community while also investing in each of us as individuals, preparing us to receive the Torah. Some of us still stay up all night learning; it is Uncle who taught us how.

June 3, 2022

In this week's Parsha Bnai Yisrael is counted. All men of draftable age are in one count and the Levites that perform the priestly duties are in a separate count. The priestly service was the responsibility of the firstborn males but after the sin of the golden calf the firstborns were stripped of their duties and the work was passed on to the tribe of Levi. This is why firstborns must be redeemed in a "pidyon haben" a transaction in which five shekels are donated to free the firstborn from their priestly duties.

There are many logistical reasons for counting a nation, knowing how strong the army is, and dividing up the responsibilities of transporting the Mishkan and all of its parts. God counts his people at some pinnacle moments throughout our journey. The completion of the Mishkan was one of these moments, God counted us to see if there were enough of us to earn his holy presence but he also counted us to determine our military might. Nachmanadies explains that rather than count us directly each person gave a half-shekel and that's how we were tallied. We learn from this that it is not appropriate to count Jews directly.

God made a commitment that we, Bnai Yisroel would be so numerous like the sand on the beach and the stars in the sky which shall never be measured or counted. God instructs us that the half-shekel is an atonement for our souls. The reason we need atonement is that even though we are being counted indirectly it is like we are alone on a pedestal before God. Without the merit of our communities, we need to do something extra to maintain our favor before God. when we daven with a minyan and engage in our communities we all bring each other up simply by pooling our strengths and merits. When we are counted however we are simply an individual as we stand before God. King Shaul counted his people using shards of pottery and later kid goats. King David was responsible for the death of 70,000 Jews. Some say this is because he counted the Jews directly others say he counted them indirectly but merely to pique his curiosity. It is inappropriate to count Jews for no specified reason. Today there are Jews living in Israel who are reluctant to take part in the country's census citing these prohibitions.

On Shavuot, we complete the counting of the omer. These are the days leading up to the giving of the Torah. These days have certainly been precious and as Jews who emulate God, we count the days and people who are important to us. Uncle Yonaton bound our family together with Judaism and uplifted us all. He strengthened our sense of community and he also invested in us as individuals he prepared us to receive the Torah. Some of us may stay up all night learning and reading it is Uncle who taught us how to do that.

Count Your Blessings

Yosef · May 19, 2023

While in the desert, Hashem commanded Moshe multiple times to take a census of the people. While this might be an understandable process for a mortal government, it is quite perplexing for a people led by Hashem and His prophet Moshe. Hashem, of course, knows every single person down to his most minute details, and he certainly knew how many they numbered, so what was the purpose of counting them?

The Ramban explains that the census was indeed not for the sake of those counting, but for the sake of the people being counted. As the people heard the final count -- over six-hundred-thousand battle-ready men, not to mention the women, youngsters, and elders -- they were astounded at the number. They remembered how, just a few generations back, their ancestors had arrived in Egypt with a mere seventy people. Now, as they bore witness to the tremendous proliferation with which Hashem had blessed them, they were overcome with happiness, gratitude, and the realization of Hashem's great love for them.

This was the purpose of the census; not so much to count the people as to get the people to count their blessings.

And this is the lesson that the census holds for all of us. Every person, no matter his situation, has an endless list of blessings to count. Rabbi Avigdor Miller used to say that if a person would know how many miracles take place when he just uses the facilities, he would write home each time to let his family know that the procedure was successful. And so it is with every breath of air, every step, and every meal. These things are well-known to be miracles of 'nature', but we tend to take them for granted, never stopping for a moment to count the blessings. But, if we would just get into the habit of thinking about the little things, we would find endless lists of things to be thankful for.

Such a habit would not only help us achieve perfection of character and the greatness of gratitude. It would not only help us build a beautiful and fulfilling relationship with Hashem. But it would start us on the path to a life brimming with happiness. A person who appreciates the myriad little miracles of life would never have a bad day, would never let life's inconveniences and troubles get him down, and would feel forever like the luckiest man alive.

No price would be too high for such a life of happiness, so what are we waiting for? Right away, let's learn the lesson of the census and learn to count every blessing. I guarantee that we won't regret it.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Festival

Shavuot

Torah – Our Link to Above

Yosef · May 16, 2018

For millennia, we have been known as the "people of the book". Our nation's greatest leaders are men who spend every waking moment poring over the worn pages of the talmud and it's commentaries. Yeshivas and Kollels across the globe are brimming with thousands of young men, many of our best and brightest, who spare no effort to gain some mastery of the Torah. Even our laymen can be found early in the morning or after a long day at work, fighting back their weariness in order to learn the Daf Hayomi (daily page of Talmud). The Torah is the lifeblood of our people, our defining point and the essence of our being.

As we approach Shavuot, the day on which the Torah was given to our people, let us try to understand a little bit of the Torah's overarching importance and why so many of our finest minds are prepared to give up everything else the world has to offer in the pursuit of Torah study.

If one were to say that Torah study is important because it is necessary in order to know how to perform the mitzvot, it would be understandable. As Jews, we have 613 mitzvot to fulfill, and without learning we wouldn't know what to do.

But such an answer would in no way justify the importance attached to Talmud study, which, more often than not, discusses far-fetched cases which have almost no bearing on the practical law. Moreover, the Talmud is full of arguments and discussions, each one leading to a single decision at the bottom line, yet we study even the opinions and thought processes which are eventually discarded in practical law. Also, the practical law is relatively simple -- do this, don't do that -- whereas the Talmud and its commentaries are exceedingly difficult. Why should we work so hard if it doesn't have any practical application?

While the deeper sources talk about the incredible and far-reaching effects of Torah study on every realm of creation and how it is the source code for the entire universe, I'd like to offer an explanation which is a little easier to grasp on our simple level.

As a nation, we are charged with being a G-dly people and with spreading godliness throughout the world. It is through us that the entire world is to be elevated and imbued with Hashem's light. It follows that the only way we are able to attain our purpose in life is if we can connect ourselves to G-d in such a profound way that His light will shine through us. This requires a very powerful and unique bond. Mere contact will never suffice to transform ourselves into conduits of His holiness.

So how are we to achieve such depth of relationship to the point that we reflect the light of Hashem? Just like any relationship, bonds are built through knowledge and understanding of the other. Torah is the words and will of the Creator. Through its study we can begin to know G-d, and thereby begin to form a true relationship with Him. Still, a casual knowledge of the Torah will get us no further than that - a casual relationship with Hashem. Hardly the depth and intimacy needed to radiate His own aura from within ourselves. To achieve such a profound bond we need to attain an acute understanding not only of G-d's edicts on a practical level, but of His entire way of thinking, so to speak. This is why every thought process of the Talmud deserves our full attention; because it allows us to understand His thought processes, as it were. Only through intensive study of every facet of Torah, can we become so connected to the Almighty that we will be able to cast His light on all of creation.

This is the reason that our nation has always been, the "people of the book". This is why our leaders are the Talmudic masters. This is why many of our best and brightest give their lives to the singular study of Torah. And this is why our laymen find it so necessary to make time in their schedules for intensive Torah study. We are in the pursuit of creating the most profound relationship with Hashem that can possibly be achieved.

So this Shavuot, as we celebrate the giving of the Torah, let us realize its overarching importance to our people and find its importance in our personal lives as well. Let us make an effort to increase our dedication and connection to the Torah, as well as our understanding of what Torah truly is.

Good Shabbos and Good Yom Tov,

Yosef

Never A Victim

Yosef · June 7, 2019

On Shavuot we celebrate the day that Hashem gave the Torah to Israel in favor of all the other nations of the world. The Midrash tells us that this was not just the way it had to be, but rather G-d offered it first to each of the other nations; only when they all declined did he give it to the eager Jewish nation. With a little speculation on the words of the Midrash, we can learn a very vital lesson for all of us to experience our own acceptance of the Torah this very Shavuot.

The following is a loose translation: When Hashem revealed himself to give the Torah, He went to all the other nations first. He went to the children of Esav, and they asked what is written in it. He responded that it says one may not murder. They said, 'Everything about us and our forefathers is murder.' He went to the people of Amon and Moav and told them that the Torah forbids immorality. They said, 'We are rooted in immorality as our people were born from the union of Lot with his daughters.' He went to the children of Yishmael and told them that the Torah forbids stealing. They said, 'Our forefather was a robber.' When Hashem saw all of this, he went and gave the Torah to Israel.

The Midrash spells out that the complaint of all the nations was not that they themselves could not part with their sinful ways, but rather that their predecessors were sinful. They said that this is the way we were raised, this is how our nation has lived for all time. We are therefore unable to accept the Torah and change.

We must understand that Hashem certainly knew that these nations were steeped in evil from their very origins, and yet he offered them the opportunity to have the Torah. This is a fundamental principle in Torah, that mankind is imbued with bechira, or the ability to choose between right and wrong. It doesn't matter how you were brought up, what your natural dispositions are, or what circumstantial pressures come your way. It doesn't even matter how big a ditch you have dug for yourself in the past. No matter what, you and only you are in control of your destiny, and you alone must bear the responsibility for your choices.

The other nations complained that they were victims of their circumstances. They fully understood the tremendous value of the Torah and [as another Midrash states] they longed to be able to receive it. Yet they were shackled by their perception of determinism. They said, 'We were born this way, we were raised this way, and there's nothing that we can do about it. The Torah is simply beyond our scope.' It was this fundamental mistake, the failure to recognize man's ability to rise above his circumstances, that caused them to lose out on the Torah for the rest of eternity.

But let us not think that this is just something for the history books. The giving of the Torah was not a one-time occurrence but rather a yearly event for every single Jew. On the day of Shavuot we are each charged with accepting the Torah anew, and the amount of Torah growth we can achieve this year will be according to the level of that acceptance. If we wish to merit the Torah this year, one of the lessons we must learn is that which the other nations failed in. There is no room to blame our shortcomings on anything outside of ourselves; we alone are in charge of our lives.

Uncle Yonatan lived this lesson with every fiber of his being. He had a million and one excuses to explain away a life of complacency and stagnation. He wasn't raised with Torah and he had to sacrifice everything in order to attain it. At every turn of his personal and religious life he faced tremendous opposition. He fell many times, and fell hard. But never did he give up, never did he say "I can't", and never did he stop fighting.

We must stop blaming our situation or our past mistakes for our present state. We must accept full responsibility for our future and realize that no matter which hand we've been dealt we are never victims of our circumstances. We must resolve to never be complacent but to constantly strive to reach beyond ourselves. In this way, may we merit to receive a heaping portion of Torah in the coming year, along with all the blessings that the Torah brings.

Good Shabbos and Chag Sameach,

Yosef

Opposition at Sinai

May 28, 2020

As a Nation we all stood under Mount Sinai, overcome with awe, we all accepted the Torah. God appeared face to face with us, as fire blazed our discerning consciousness left us, and our most primal instincts were inspired to accept the Torah. The Gemara explains that it is not for another thousand years that our covenant with God was sealed. A contract made under duress cannot stand, and under Mount Sinai, God held the mountain above our heads like a jar to an insect, as if to say accept the Torah or let this spot be your grave.

A thousand years later, after the destruction of the first Beis Hamikdash Jews are in exile and stand to be annihilated by Haman. No longer witnessing open miracles in the temple, God's hand is hidden but the Jewish commitment to God is as public as ever. Not a single jew in the Purim story assimilated out of fear, the resistance was strong and empowered Jews. How did this spiritual blackout change our relationship with God? What was the point in God's coercion at Mount Sinai?
