Our relationship with God is not based on reason. Nor is it based on our awareness of its existence. Our relationship with God is so rudimentary it resides in the very core of our souls. This is why we were "compelled" to accept the Torah, a conscious and thought out acceptance would not have covered that which is so elemental.

In the Purim story, we have the opposite situation, a seemingly godless exile and a nation that wants to destroy us. The awe is in the Jewish people who grow stronger and more united through said darkness. This polarity allowed for the contract that was drawn at Sinai to finally be sealed, now as sober people we embrace the Torah and its commandments, reflecting the light of God unto the nations in a dark exile. On Shavuot, we affirm that our coercion at Sinai was not against our will, but in complete harmony with our true desire.

Noah · May 14, 2021

Shavuot is uniquely enigmatic. While the other chagim have clear historical and agricultural significance written in the Torah, Shavuot is only explicitly related to agriculture. It is only through reasoning and the Oral Torah that we learn that the Revelation at Sinai occurred on Shavuot.

At the introduction to the giving of the Torah it is written, “In the third month after the Israelites had gone forth from the land of Egypt, on that very day, they entered the wilderness of Sinai” (Ex. 19:1). We know that Shavuot is the only holiday in the third month so we make the connection. But this implicit connection is very different than the explicit agricultural and historical connections made with Sukkot and Pesach. It is a connection that requires the Oral Torah.

Rabbi Sacks explains that the essence of Shavuot can be explored by investigating the holiday from the perspective of the Sadducees in the time of the second temple who didn’t recognize the validity of the Oral Tradition. There is a famous argument about when the counting of the omer starts, starting the countdown to Shavuot. The Sadducees interpret the verse requiring the counting of the omer to start on the “day after shabbat” as meaning Sunday, while the oral tradition understands Shabbat in that context to mean the first day of Pesach. The gemara recounts an exchange in which a Sadducee explains to R. Yochanan ben Zakkai why, according to them, Shavuot is always on a Sunday: “Moses our teacher was a great lover of Israel. Knowing that Shavuot lasted only one day, he, therefore, fixed it on the day after the Sabbath so that Israel might enjoy themselves for two successive days.”

From the Sadducee's perspective, this results in a holiday that does not have a fixed day. It would be hard to commemorate the Revelation at Sinai, a concrete event with a set date, with a holiday whose date fluctuates annually. Instead, Rabbi Sacks posits that the significance of Shavuot was much more closely associated with the harvest, the land, and entrance into the Land of Israel in the times of Yehoshua. This harvest-centric view is reinforced by the remark said to R. Yochanan ben Zakkai. After an exhausting harvest, all anyone could want in a long weekend to rest.

With the oral tradition, we were able to root this holiday in something much more eternal, the giving of the Torah. While the Sadducees all but disappeared at the end of the second temple period, our religion rooted in the Oral Tradition continues to thrive. When the process of a miraculous Exodus followed by arduous wandering in the desert leads to land, when that land is taken away so is the tradition built by those experiences. But when the culmination of our formation as a nation is in receiving the Torah, something divine and eternal that we can take with us anywhere, we are left with a much more resilient tradition. One that allows us to appreciate the land and the harvest but one that retains its meaning and significance even when we can’t.

Shabbat Shalom and Chag Sameach,

Noah

Parshat

Naso

Blessings

June 14, 2019

This week's parsha, Nasso, includes the commandment directed to Aaron and his son's to bless the children of Israel. The verse preceding the command says, "And they shall place My Name upon Bnei Yisrael and I shall bless them." The concept of human conduits for heavenly consecration is confounding. Why is it necessary for us to receive a benediction via the Kohanim when God is surely capable of bestowing it upon us directly?

To shed light on this question, we can look to the Malbim's explanation of the parsha. He brings down a dispute from Chullin over who, "and I will bless them" refers to.

R Yishmael says, when it says, "and I will bless them," it means the kohanim bless Israel and God then blesses the kohanim.

R Akiva says, it means that the Kohanim bless Israel and God responds with his approval.

The Malbim says that this argument is parallel to the argument in Brachos:

It is said: "Hear Me, stubborn-hearted who are far from charity". While both agree that the verse refers to the righteous, Rav and Shmuel, and some say Rabbi Yoḥanan and Rabbi Elazar, disagreed as to how to interpret the verse. One said: The entire world is sustained by God's charity, not because it deserves to exist, while the righteous who are far from God's charity are sustained by force, as due to their own good deeds they have the right to demand their sustenance. And one said: The entire world is sustained by the merit of their righteousness, while they are not sustained at all, not even by their own merit, in accordance with the statement that Rav Yehuda said that Rav said.

As Rav Yehuda said that Rav said: Every day a Divine Voice emerges from Mount Horeb and says: The entire world is sustained by the merit of Ḥanina ben Dosa, my son, and for Ḥanina, my son, a kav of carobs is sufficient to sustain him for an entire week, from one Shabbat eve to the next Shabbat eve.

On one side we have the position that the righteous who bring blessings into the world automatically receive from the blessing they bestow upon others. If this is the case, the bracha of kohanim makes perfect sense. They are given the opportunity to bless the people so they can be the first beneficiaries of said blessings. According to the second opinion, that the kohanim do not even necessarily benefit from the blessings they bring, what is the point?

This question can be resolved with a fundamental change in perspective. While we assume that the blessings are for the sake of the kohanim or based on their power the opposite is true. As explained by the Sefer HaChinuch, Birchas Kohanim is really an opportunity for the community to physically express their desire to be part of the divine source of blessing. The kohanim simply give us a forum to exercise our ultimate power, showing god we want to be blessed. This opinion seems to believe that the power to receive blessing isn't through being a tzadik or Kohen, but by simply seeking to be included in it.

This idea is even more powerful when put in the context of tefilla. It seems trivial to stand to before an omnipotent and incorporeal creator and pray that my car works. It seems delusional to believe that my paycheck is reflected in my shul attendance. But we see from the kohanim that davening isn't about demanding reality be a certain away, but opening ourselves up to be willing to see all the bracha around us even when it isn't. Rabbi Chanina ben Dosa received sustenance in a way that was, "concealed and required toil" according to Rashi. Despite this, he is credited with having some of the most powerful forces of prayer. Most notably, as recounted in Taanis:

On a Friday night, R Chanina ben Dosa noticed his daughter was sad and asked why. She responded that she had mixed up her vinegar with her olive oil and accidentally used the vinegar for the Shabbos candles. "Do not let it bother you. He who commanded oil to burn will also command vinegar to burn."

Sometimes we get so caught up in the difference between wealth and poverty, oil and vinegar, a working car and a lawn ornament, that we fail to recognize they all share the same source. To be blessed is to have this recognition and to believe whatever resources we are provided are enough to fulfill the will of God.

Shabbat Shalom.

Adam · June 5, 2020

"May Hashem bless you and safeguard you. May Hashem light up His face to you and be gracious to you. May Hashem lift His face to you and give you peace."

These words echo in every synagogue daily in Israel as the Kohanim bless the Jewish people. But interestingly, we use these same words to bless our children each Friday night. And just as the kohanim aren't allowed to give the blessing if they harbor ill feelings toward one of the congregants, so too we must focus our love as we bless our children.

At first glance, the blessing seems almost repetitive and strangely abstract. Light up his face? Lift his face? What is the deeper meaning?

Rabbi Fohrman suggests a beautiful understanding in regards to parenting that I believe we can use in every area of our lives. After all, when we're all grown up, we must still parent ourselves.

The first verse says Yevarechecha, may Hashem bless you. Blessing connotes expansion and multiplication. We find this all over the Torah when the word Bracha is used. One example is when the land will be blessed in the sixth year of the Shemitah cycle such that it gives forth 3 years of provisions. The numerical values of the letters in Baruch are 2, 200, and 20. All demonstrating multiplication.

This verse is connected to the growth of a young child even while in the womb as his or her cells multiply and continue growing exponentially while being safeguarded from the outside world. Parents are mandated to continue this with their children, boosting them and growing them while always protecting them from negative influences and dangers.

The second describes the uncontrolled joy we feel as we see our children live. Our faces light up at a smile, a giggle, a sound, and later an adorable one-liner they never even prepared. This joy must be communicated to our children. They should know the impact they make on us just by being themselves.

And the third part takes us into later life; "lift up" refers to being able to see "eye to eye" with them. To see them as separate and whole individuals who impact the world in their own right. If we have raised them up and properly performed the first two dicta, we should be looking at a healthy adult. One who is very clear and able to create healthy boundaries and who is capable of the unconditional self-love that leads to passing these blessings to yet another generation. We give them Shalom, Peace.

Peace amongst sameness is not true Peace. Shalom is only truly possible when there are differences. God is said to "Create Peace on High," referring to the plague of hail, when fire and water, possibly the most polar opposites, coexisted. Only when we allow our children to truly fly the nest, respecting their differences as they become their own unique selves (lift his face to you), do we reach for Shalom. Peace amongst sameness is not true Peace.

The words for loving-kindness, gemilas chesed, denotes this as well. Gemila means to wean. Chesed means kindness. True kindness is allowing for separation and difference. Giving in a way that allows the child or friend to grow and wean themselves, setting out on their own. True leaders create leaders, not followers.

May we all merit to experience the three-fold blessing ourselves, to pass it with love to our children, and to constantly be the light, lifting our own eyes up to see our families, communities, and the rest of the world. In such a way, may we experience the only true Shalom, heralding Mashiach speedily in our days!

Love from the Shtetl, Adam

Rotten Wealth

Yosef · May 21, 2021

A man once traveled to a far away island on rumors of the opportunity for great business prospects. He planned to stay for a year and hoped to make enough money to last his family a lifetime. Indeed, when he got to the island he found that his skill set was perfect for making it in the local economy; before long he had climbed his way to real wealth and was regarded as one of the richest men in town. However, wealth on the island wasn't measured in modern terms of gold and silver or dollars and cents. It was measured in animal fat, which was a precious commodity on the far flung island, necessary for all parts of their way of life. As the date neared for our friend to return home, he began to plan just how he would transport all of his newfound wealth back to his shtetl in Poland. Sufficiently brainwashed by the local society, he began to invest all of his funds in the most stable form of local currency -- animal fat. Finally the big day came and a great team gathered to pack all of the fat onto a huge ship. The man boarded last, and with a feeling of triumph and pride he dreamed of the admiration he would enjoy from his family and townspeople when they would learn of his new status.

The rest of the story is as obvious as it is tragic. During the voyage home, the ship began to reek of rotting fat, insects began to swarm the boat, and when he ultimately reached land there was nothing but rot and stench to show for his year of toil. He knew from home what was really considered wealth, but he had been blinded by the primitive ways of the island-folk into amassing a boatload of garbage in place of the wealth which could have lasted him a lifetime.

What a fool this man was! It's almost painful to think about such folly. And yet, says the Chofetz Chaim, this story is a parable to each and every one of us.

Our neshama comes from the highest heavens, where it knows with absolute clarity that Torah and mitzvos are the only thing worth collecting. It travels down to this world for but a few years on a very clear mission of earning the spiritual gains to last it an eternity. But then it gets to our world, to the primitive and short-sighted value system which enshrines fickle material gains that won't last more than a few years -- certainly not beyond the grave. We become busy amassing this new kind of wealth valued on the island. We chase endlessly after fun and good times, pleasure, honor, and material riches. We may even perceive ourselves as successful individuals, but all in the primitive lens of worldly pursuits.

One day each person's time will come to travel back home and return his soul to its maker. If we succumb to the brainwashing of the world around us, then we will have nothing to load onto the boat but our cars and estates, our selfies and travel visas, our steaks and sushi and wines. And the rest of the story is again as obvious as it is tragic. Those things are at best useless in the real world, and more often than not they begin to reek with the stench of sin. When we reach our destination we are in danger of facing humiliation and remorse in place of the wealth and honor which we had imagined.

This is the message of the verse in our parshah, 'and that which man will tithe to the Cohen will be his.' The Chofetz Chaim explains that the balances in all of our bank accounts, brokerage accounts, and other assets, are not ours to keep. The time will come oh too soon when we will not be able to take them home. At that time we will realize that the only money that is truly ours is that which we tithed to the Cohen and to tzedaka. Only mitzvos will truly 'be his'.

Let us each take a few minutes to ponder this story, connect to the feelings of embarrassment and regret that the man feels upon returning home, and then turn our focus inward to the parallel in our own lives. With such an exercise we could gain a great deal of clarity and focus in everything we do, and we could rid ourselves of so much of the pettiness of this world.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

The Angel's Giveaway

Yosef · June 10, 2022

From the Haftarah

Manoach and his wife had been married for many many years, but had not yet been blessed with children. One day, however, everything changed in a miraculous way. When she was in the field alone, a man with a G-dly countenance approached her and shared the most wonderful prophecy. She would soon conceive and have a son who would be a savior to the entire nation. This son, Samson, was to be a nazir from birth -- never to cut his hair nor drink wine. In fact, even she was to abstain from any grape products for the length of her pregnancy.

Just as soon as the man had come, he disappeared, leaving a very surprised, if delighted, woman to share the news with her husband. When Manoach heard the news, he prayed that they be privy to a second revelation, in order that he could hear first hand the prophecy and directions for raising such a child. In response to his prayers, the man of G-d again appeared and repeated his message. Manoach offered the man to partake of a celebratory feast, but the man declined, suggesting instead that he make an offering to Hashem. As fire rose up on the altar, the man, in truth an angel, joined the flames and rose heavenward.

The Torah closes the story with a puzzling statement: 'And the angel never again appeared to Manoach and his wife; then Manoach knew that this was an angel of Hashem.'

The glaring question is that if, after all the miraculous events, Manoach was still unconvinced of the identity of this 'man', how did he suddenly figure out that it was an angel when the man disappeared forever?

It would seem that in those times miracles were not so unheard of, and there were mortal men as well who could do the miraculous. As such, although Manoach was keenly aware of the godliness of his visitor, there was no clear indication of his immortality. Even as the man rose to the heavens with the flames, he still could have been a human capable of miracles.

So what blew the angel's cover? When he did something distinctly inhuman. For after being the bearer of such wonderful news, which human could withhold himself from coming back to enjoy the thanks and honor which were sure to greet him at the baby's bris milah? When the 'man' remained in hiding throughout all of the festivities of the baby's birth, Manoach finally knew that this was no mortal man.

We're not angels, but let's try just a little bit to fight back against this lowly human trait of being so inebriated with our own praise. The next time we perform a good deed, let's try as much as possible to hide it from others. If we achieve something impressive, let's try not to look over our shoulder for a pat on the back. And if we do someone a favor, let's do it for their sake and try to forget about the thanks we have coming to us.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Parshat

Behaalotecha

Second Chances First

June 21, 2019

This week's Parsha is Behalotecha, Israel is leaving their encampment at Sinai where they have been for almost a year. Before setting out Aaron is commanded to light the menorahs and the tribe of levi are assigned to temple duties in the Mishkan. We are also given the commandment for Pesach, G-d spoke to Moses in the Sinai desert ... saying: "The children of Israel should prepare the Passover [offering] at its appointed time. On the fourteenth of this month, in the afternoon ... in accordance with all its decrees and laws...."

After conveying the mitzvah of Pesach to Bnei Yisroel Moshe was approached by a few ritually impure people that would be unable to give a sacrifice on that day. "...Why should we be deprived, and not be able to present G-ds offering in its time, amongst the children of Israel?" And Moses said to them: "Wait here, and I will hear what G-d will command concerning you." God gave over the specifications for a second Pesach a month later for those who had been "in contact with the dead or out on a distant road" to rectify their failures.

Also in this weeks Parsha Bnei Yisroel complain to Moshe about not getting meat, forcing Moshe to setup a more elaborate system of hierarchy to function smoothly, thus losing the approval of his sister Miriam. After speaking negatively about him, Miriam contracts leprosy.

The idea behind Pesach Sheini - the second pesach is Teshuvah the practice of transforming negative experiences like "contact with the dead" into uplifting interactions with the living and Returning after wandering "out on distant roads" makes us homesick. Hashem initially commanded just the one Pesach, it took a small group of men to ask for an exception. Returning often seems automatic, less active than seeking something out, but the Torah teaches us the opposite is true, it takes 613 mitzvot to define God's will to add a channel for instant return detracts from the initial obligations. While the first Pesach is seven days long and leavened bread is forbidden, Pesach Sheni is observed one day and though the sacrifice offered is very similar, the distinctions between the times are clear. In the words of the midrash, They asked Wisdom, "What is the punishment for the sinner?" Wisdom replied: "Evil pursues sinners."

They asked Prophecy, and Prophecy replied: "The soul who sins shall die."

They asked Torah, and Torah replied: "He shall bring a guilt-offering and he shall be forgiven."

They asked G-d, and G-d replied: "Let him do teshuvah and he shall be forgiven." (Yalkut Shimoni, Tehillim 702)

In the realms of our wisdom and prophecy teshuvah cannot be a consideration. However through torah study and a relationship to the divine, we can carve the path for second chances to be granted, before transgressions are made.

Noah BenEzra · June 12, 2020

This week's parsha concludes with the paradigm of lashon hara, Aaron and Miriam speaking about Moshe. This saga begins,

וַתְּדַבֵּ֨ר מִרְיָ֤ם וְאַהֲרֹן֙ בְּמֹשֶׁ֔ה עַל־אֹד֛וֹת הָאִשָּׁ֥ה הַכֻּשִׁ֖ית אֲשֶׁ֣ר לָקָ֑ח כִּֽי־אִשָּׁ֥ה כֻשִׁ֖ית לָקָֽח׃ Miriam and Aaron spoke against Moses because of the Cushite woman he had married: "He married a Cushite woman!" What is the relevance of Tziporah's Cushite heritage in Aaron and Miriam's gossip about Moshe? Rashi citing Sifri fleshes out the story a little more, giving us a clearer picture of the interpersonal dynamics at play. Following the appointment of the 70 elders, there was a period of celebration. One evening, Miriam said regarding the new appointees, "Fortunate are these men, and fortunate are their wives!" to which Tzipporah replied, "Do not say 'Fortunate are their wives,' say 'Woe unto their wives!' For from the day that God spoke to Moses your brother, he has not had relations with me." This is where our Parsha continues the story, "Miriam and Aaron spoke against Moses about the woman"— about him separating himself from the woman. They accused Moshe of being prideful in his separation from Tziporrah, unaware that God had instructed Moshe to, "stay with me" following the revelation at Sinai, necessitating his celibacy.

With the story presented in its fuller form, it is still hard to understand why Tziporrah being a Cushite is the impetus for their Lashon Hara.

The Abarbanel offers an explanation,

הם אמרו שמשה בהפרדו מאשתו לא היה אפשר שיהיה אלא לאחת מג' סבות או לשלשתן יחד. הא' להיותם כושית כעורב כי צפורה היתה ממדין והיתה שחורה כי מדינים הם ישמעאלים שחורים מכח השמש וחומו ואולי משה נמנע ממנה לפי שאינה ישרה בעיניו. והב' מפני התבודדותו לנבואה והיותו מוכן אליה בכל עת. והג' להיות משה בטבעו ענו ומסתפק ומואס בפחיתות המשגל כי חרפה הוא לו. They (Aaron and Miriam) said that Moshe's separation from his wife could only be for one or all of these three reasons. First, Tziporra was black as a crow. She was a Midianite, and was black, as Midianites are Yishmaelites that are black from the power and heat of the sun. Perhaps Moshe wouldn't be with her because she was not proper in his eyes. Second, maybe he needs to be ready for prophecy at all times. Third, maybe his nature caused him to regard relations as a disgraceful act. The Abarbanel goes on to explain that these reasons were eventually rejected by Aaron and Miriam as well, but it was this line of reasoning that resulted in Miriam contracting leprosy.

In both Rashi's and the Abarbanel's explanations of these events, Aaron and Miriam making judgments based on extraneous evidence is the crux of the incident. In Rashi's retelling, the evidence is an offhand comment in a social setting, in the Abarbanel, it's Tziporrah's complexion. But it is clear why the Torah emphasized Tziporrah's ethnicity at the commencement of this episode. What could be more emblematic of the problems with making an unfounded judgment based on superficial reasoning than a racial justification for Moshe's celibacy?

In a similar vein, Pninei Halacha sites our Parsha not in dealing with classic Lashon Hara, but rather the obligation to judge each other favorably. פניני הלכה, ליקוטים ב א׳:י׳:ה׳ וככל שהאדם יותר צדיק, כך צריך יותר להתאמץ לדון אותו לכף זכות. ועל כך נענשה מרים הנביאה ע"ה, שלא דנה את משה רבנו ע"ה לכף זכות (במדבר יב, א-טז). And the more righteous a person is, the more he needs to strengthen his favorable judgment of others. As we saw with Miriam, she did not judge Moshe Rabbenu favorably. In keeping with the above teaching, the Talmud offers a homiletical interpretation of Miriam and Aaron's reasoning that applies the favorable judgment to them that they lacked for their brother. "על אודות האשה הכושית אשר לקח" וכי כושית שמה והלא ציפורה שמה אלא מה כושית משונה בעורה אף ציפורה משונה במעשיה "Miriam and Aaron spoke against Moses because of the Cushite woman he had married." Was Cushite her name? Certainly not! Tziporrah was her name! Rather she was called a Cushite because just as a Cushite has exceptional skin, Tziporrah had exceptional actions.

Especially now, we can take from the Talmud's perspective and learn to look past the cosmetic and make judgments based on character.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah BenEzra

Make it Personal

Yosef · May 28, 2021

When Aharon saw that all of the princes of Israel brought lavish offerings to Hashem in the end of the last parsha, he felt bad that he had not also done the same. To comfort him, Hashem told Aharon at the beginning of this week's parsha about his personal mitzvah of lighting the menorah in the tabernacle – a great mitzvah reserved for the high priest alone.

At face value, this story seems puzzling. If Aharon indeed felt bad that he hadn't brought the same offerings as the others, why was he just sitting around feeling down about it? There was no reason he couldn't get up right now and do the same, so why the feelings of dejection?

I heard a beautiful explanation from Rabbi Dovid Kviat zt”l. He explained that all of the princes were moved of their own accord to bring their offerings to Hashem; it was something that came from deep within and was a genuine expression of their inner love of Hashem and their excitement for His service. Aharon, on the other hand, was not personally inspired to bring such an offering. However, when he saw this great outpouring from his brethren, it dawned on him that this would really have been a very nice thing to do, and he longed that he too should be able to do such an act for Hashem. Why then didn't he follow suit? Because service of Hashem, and indeed everything we do in life must be genuine. If we do something just because others are doing it, then however praiseworthy the act may be, it is empty and largely devoid of meaning. This doesn't mean we shouldn't learn from the virtue of others, but before we act in kind we must be sure that we have already internalized the lesson and that we are acting according to our newfound personal inspiration – not just copying their external act.

I believe Uncle Yonatan was fond of relating a story in the Talmud, in which one sage said that he was like vinegar the son of wine in regards to his father. Meaning that his father was far greater than him. How so? Because his father waited a full twenty-four hours between meat and milk, whereas he only waited from one meal to the next [parenthetically this is the basis for the six hours prevalent in our days]. The question again is, that if he perceived this as a shortcoming, why didn't he do the same? It is certainly a safe assumption that this great sage was easily able to overcome the urge for that slice of pizza, so why not just wait a little longer to achieve the greater level. The answer is that the father did so out of a genuine fear of sin which drove him to separate so far from a chance of mixing meat and milk. The son, however, did not possess this level of fear, and although he could have easily replicated the behavior, it would have been devoid of meaning if not fueled by internal inspiration.

Let's aspire to achieve ingenuity in all that we do. Let us learn from others, but not copy them. Let us be virtuous not only in action, but in our hearts and minds. Let's drop our society's obsession with the external and make sure that our inner world duly reflects all the virtue that we display on the outside.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Avi · June 8, 2023

Parsha Behalotecha, means "when you step up." This weeks Parsha focuses on themes of leadership and social order and teach us a lot about living in a community. The Jewish people have been camped at Mount Sinai for nearly a year and God instructs Moshe on the procedures and formations for their departure.

Also in this Parsha, Pesach Sheini (a second passover) is instituted, because a group of Jews who were impure and unable to participate in Pesach petitioned Moses asking "why should we be deprived?"

On their journey through the desert the children of Israel became dissatisfied with their heavenly Manna. They complained to Moshe, and pheasants flew into the desert to provide meat. Moshe was overcome by his duties as leader and delegated smaller tasks to 70 elders who heard and dealt with the concerns of Israel. Miriam, Moshe's sister speaks ill of his leadership and develops leprosy from her sin. All of israel waits for her recovery and Moshe prayed.

Our leaders can never make everyone happy all the time. Moshe demonstrates resilience as a leader who fights on behalf of his nation perhaps even when their requests are lofty. A good leader always knows when to delegate tasks because they are overwhelmed. The idea of having a second passover demonstrates that Judaism has an open door policy seeking to give evryone every opportunity to benefit and participate. These are the traits of the Jewish community that make us stay strong und united. It is important to remember how grateful we are and recognize the devine intervention we benefit from daily. Shabbat Shalom!

Parshat

Shelach

Shlach

Shira · June 28, 2019

This week's Torah reading is Parshas Shlach, literally translated as 'Send'. The Jews are in the desert after receiving the Torah and are heading toward the Land of Israel. As they are traveling, doubt begins to bubble to the surface and questions plague their minds. What if the land is not fruitful? What if the inhabitants prove to be too difficult to fight and conquer? They express these fears to the leader, Moshe, who answers, "send for yourselves spies so they may check out the land and see that it is good." Moshe had hoped that when they saw how comfortable he would be with them investigating the land, they would gain confidence that there is nothing to be afraid of. However, that was not the case, and a representative from each tribe was tasked with infiltrating the communities and evaluating the strength of their soon-to-be opponents. While traveling the Land of Israel, they encounter a family of giants. The spies make a very interesting statement. They say, "we were in their eyes like grasshoppers". They bring this back to the rest of the Jewish people in their report, stating that the men who live there are so big and strong, it would be impossible to defeat them. I'd like to explore an interesting matter. The spies contend that in the eyes of the giants, they were as small and insignificant as insects. But how would they know how they appeared to the giants? We actually learn that the mountainous men in question were busy arranging a funeral and didn't spare a glance at the representatives of the tribes. The verse continues with the words, "and so we were in our own eyes". Once they believed that this was how the giants saw them, that was how they then saw themselves. The mind has a way of convincing ourselves that we know how other people perceive us. We assume that others have a certain judgment of us and feel we are confined by those conclusions. We give them credence and an ability to confine us to those barriers.

There is a solution though. When we realize that we don't know and we learn not to care what other people think about us, we remove those shackles and allow our wings to fly over the gates that jail us in. There is someone who we were privileged to see putting this into practice. Uncle Yonatan truly did not care what others thought of him. He wanted to be comfortable. He wanted to do the right thing. He wanted to push himself to be better. And he never once checked if anyone was watching. May we all learn to live lives that are not dictated by what society expects of us, but what we know is right in our hearts.

Good shabbos, Shira

Yosef · June 19, 2020

The story of the spies is one of the most painful in the history of our people. We were on the brink of entering the Holy Land while still under the leadership of Moshe, and our sages tell us that had he brought us into the land we would have immediately achieved the purpose of creation. Had Moshe been at the helm as we entered the land of Israel, the temple would never have been destroyed and the Jews would never again have tasted exile. All of that was snatched literally out from under our noses with the sin of the spies. Tragedy of tragedies.

But perhaps the most troubling part of the whole story is that we received permission from the Almighty to send them. Admittedly it discouraged, but Hashem did acquiesce to the nation's request to send spies. How could our omnipotent Father, fully knowing the destruction which would result from such an initiative, have given us the go-ahead?

Rabbi Moshe Feinstein offers an incredible explanation that is highly relevant to each of us in our own lives. And, as with all good Torah answers, he prefaces with another question.

The generation of the Exodus was privy to the most incredible and undeniable revelations of G-d's power in all of history. They saw the ten plagues, which demonstrated Hashem's absolute mastery over all aspects of creation. They themselves marched through the Sea of Reeds upon dry land. And they personally witnessed the giving of the Torah at Sinai. These are all the cornerstones of emunah, or Jewish belief, which provide the base for Jews throughout the ages to believe in G-d and His Torah. And they didn't merely hear these things from their fathers, as is the case for the rest of history, but rather they saw it with their own eyes. We would hence expect that they would be the paragon believers in Hashem and His boundless power.

But countless stories in the Torah show a lacking in their emunah that begs an explanation. Let's take just two examples. The first is the episode in last week's parsha, when the Jews, unsatisfied with the angelic food called Manna, demanded to be fed meat. Our sages tell us that this was not a simple craving for a steak, as they had plenty of cattle from which they could have eaten. It was rather a challenge – to see if Hashem could really supply them with meat or if He was r"l limited to Manna. After all, they had seen, they were somehow still unconvinced that this feat was within heavenly reach.

Likewise, right here in the story of the spies the Jewish people were scared of a war with the nations of Canaan. They had seen Hashem disrupt every facet of the very formidable Egyptian society, all without the Jews lifting so much as a finger. They witnessed as the entire might and majesty of Egypt drown miraculously in the Sea of Reeds. And yet somehow all of this didn't equip them with the belief that He could just as handily quash the armies of Canaan.

How are we to understand such a seemingly incongruent worldview?

The answer, says Rabbi Feinstein, lies in the miraculous fashion by which they came to believe in G-d. Because they had witnessed such open miracles which left no room for doubt, and because they were served emunah on a silver platter, they therefore never had the need to invest their own thought and feelings into that belief. When we receive knowledge or belief without having a personal stake in them, they remain on the surface and never penetrate to the depths of our identities. As such, their emunah was limited to exactly that which they had seen, but anything beyond their prior knowledge was clouded in uncertainty. They knew Hashem could provide Manna because they had seen it, but perhaps meat functioned differently. They knew Hashem could defeat a single, albeit mighty King, but they were unsure about the prospects of the many Kings of Canaan.

This state of belief could not possibly remain intact throughout the generations; they would have to spread their own wings and work on their own emunah in order to give it an element of permanence. It was for this reason that Hashem allowed the Jews to send spies, because after they would fall they would finally need to work on themselves and acquire their own well-founded belief which would persevere even in areas which they had not yet witnessed. In their teshuvah process, they would need to realize their error and invest in themselves to become confident of Hashem's ability to conquer the nations of Canaan. Only in this way would the Jewish nation be able to remain strong throughout all the ages.

May we learn the lesson of the spies and commit to put our whole selves into everything we learn, never accepting knowledge on a silver platter but rather working to fully acquire and incorporate our learning into our deepest selves. And especially in the arena of belief in G-d, let us not be satisfied with the superficial lip-service which is so prevalent, but toil always to make it part and parcel of who we are.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Avi · 2021

In this week's Parsha Shelach, Moses sends a representative from each tribe of Israel into the land of Cannan. After a forty day expedition through the land the twelve men returned with a giant cluster of grapes and one oversized pomegranate. The land of Cannan was lush and bountiful! Ten of the men warned that the land was as dangerous as it was bountiful, saying that the inhabitants of the land were giants and warriors. Only Caleb and Joshua believed the land could be settled as Moshe instructed; the rest of the spies all believed that the Cannanites were stronger and more powerful than them.

The people of Israel wept that they wanted to return to Egypt so God decreed that they would not enter the holy land for forty years. In response some Jews stormed the border only to be met by Amalekites and Canaanites on all sides.

The laws of Nesachim are given in this Parsha. These are the laws regarding meal, wine and oil offerings. We are also given the Mitzvah of Challah, removing a portion of bread for sacrifice. At the end of the Parsha we are told the story of a wood gatherer who desecrated Shabbos and was put to death. We are commanded to wear tzitzit on our garments to remind us of all the Mitzvahs constantly.

Good Shabbos,

Avi

Baby Steps

Yosef · June 24, 2022

When the spies came back from their tour of the Land of Israel they brought an ill report. Gathering the people, the spies began to viciously discourage them from continuing into the land. They told of giants and fortified cities, saying that their small nation would never be able to overcome such enemies. At that point, Kalev stood up and silenced the crowd, convincing them that there was hope and that with Hashem's help they would indeed conquer the land. Yet his accomplishment was incredibly short-lived. In the very next verse, the spies repeated their message and won back the hearts of the people, ultimately succeeding in turning them against Moshe and G-d.

From our perspective, it would seem that Kalev accomplished nothing. His efforts were heroic, but ultimately a failure. That short moment where he refuted the spies had no lasting effect and was forgotten almost as soon as he finished speaking. Yet we find that Hashem took great pleasure in Kalev's actions, even granting him the holy land of Hebron (the burial place of our forefathers) as a personal estate.

Why is it that Hashem saw this as such a great deed when we see it as no more than an act of futility?

I'd like to answer with a parable from real life:

Picture a mother as she watches her precious baby turn over for the first time. She is overjoyed! Whooping from excitement and smiling ear-to-ear, she calls her various relatives to share the news. "The baby turned over!" In the course of the child's development, this scene repeats itself, again and again, the first smile, the first word, the first step, and each time the mother's joy and excitement are boundless.

Why? Doesn't she know that these are standard things that almost every child achieves; small achievements in the scale of things?

The answer is that when it comes to your child every small milestone is reason for celebration. Because of our overwhelming love for our children, even a small step in the right direction brings us immeasurable happiness.

We are all children of the Almighty. In fact, our sages tell us that Hashem, in his infinite capacity to love, views each and every one of us as his only child. We should know that just as we are overjoyed with the smallest improvement that our children achieve, so too Hashem derives immense joy from even the smallest steps we take to become better people.

This is why Hashem so appreciated Kalev's short-lived accomplishment. Yes, Kalev only saved the people from sin for a tiny amount of time, but for that minute the Jews reaffirmed their loyalty to G-d. They fell back down a minute later, but for that moment they made a baby step in the right direction and our loving Father derives immeasurable joy even from our baby steps. So much so that Kalev, who facilitated that baby step, was deserving of great honor and the priceless gift of Hebron.

Often, we have a desire to improve, but we tell ourselves that we can never be good enough anyway. We let these thoughts lull us into complacency, thinking that if we can't achieve perfection we might as well just stay how we are. Let us think of our own children's baby steps and how proud they make us, and let us know that Hashem is all the happier with our smallest achievements. Every small improvement is worth millions to our loving Father. Let us empower ourselves to eat the elephant one bite at a time and step by baby step we can become better and bring G-d and ourselves so much pride.

I believe it is obvious how such an outlook will not only encourage us to make those baby steps, but in the end, all those baby steps will add up to true greatness and we will truly accomplish things we never dreamed we could.

Good Shabbos, Yosef

Count Your Blessings

Yosef · June 16, 2023

The spies returned from touring the land of Israel with a stunning report, as far as the bounty was concerned. They carried samples of the fruit of the land; fruits which required multiple men just to lift. And they told of a land literally flowing with milk and honey, a land lush and beautiful beyond belief.

But then, they made an abrupt about-face, declaring unequivocally that the plans to invade must be forsaken. True, it was a beautiful land, but the inhabitants were incredibly powerful giants living in fortified cities.

לא נוכל לעלות – 'We cannot go up.' It is too dangerous, too difficult, and we are too weak and frail.

Only Yehoshua and Kolev dared stand up to the spies' bleak forecast, as Kolev responded עלה נעלה כי יכול נוכל, 'We shall rise up, for then we will surely be capable.' His point was clear. We are capable of exactly how much we perceive ourselves to be. If we give up in the night, we'll never see the light of day, but if we will only choose to fight then, with Hashem at our side, there is no limit to what we can accomplish.

The message of the spies, their call of despair and hopelessness, is not a one-time event in history, but the oft-repeated attack of the yetzer hora, the evil inclination, which takes many forms throughout the generations.

At a great many junctures in our lives and in our national history, we face tests and trials which seem insurmountable. We may see the opportunity for greatness contained in overcoming the challenge, but we lose hope of being able to rise to the occasion. There's a voice, mocking and derisive, telling us lo nuchal la'alos – you can't, you won't, and you shouldn't even try.

Our job is to recognize the yetzer hora of the spies again rearing its ugly head, and to identify that this is nothing new, but the age-old cry of lo nuchal la'alos. And as we distinguish exactly who the source of that voice is, we are empowered to respond with the secret that Kolev once entrusted us: alo na'aleh ki yachol nuchal.

We can. And we will. If we'll only endeavor to try.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Parshat

Korach

The Tyrant's Takeaway

Noah · June 15, 2018

The parsha begins, "And Korach took..." This verse is our introduction to arguably the most infamous event during the travels of our forefathers. In this week's parsha, Korach incites a mutiny against Moses and the leadership he has appointed. He takes special issue with the granting of the priesthood to Aaron. Joining Korach and his cronies are 250 distinguished members of the community, who offer the sacrificial incense to prove their worthiness for the priesthood. The earth opens and swallows the mutineers, and a fire consumes the ketoret-offerers. Among the many questions raised by this chapter in our history, the commentators are puzzled by the first verse. They point out that the verb "took" is not connected to any subsequent article. What could Korach possibly have taken that could explain the coming coup that he would attempt? Furthermore, how did this taking enable a single rabble-rouser to ensnare the leaders of his generation in his divisive scheme?

To understand this and any large scale political situation, we must consider the broader context and mindset in which the Israelites found themselves. During their desert adventure, they were a people constantly on the move. Their lives were left entirely in the hands of God, they had very little control over their futures and they constantly complained about the uncertainty of what was to come. From wishin for chicken to the subversive spies, the Israelites desert misdeeds have a lack of trust and reliance on God as a uniting factor at their cores. They projected unpredictability and instability onto every opportunity and gift given to them by God. Despite having all their needs miraculously taken care of, just the feeling of uncertainty was enough to lead them to err numerous times in the face of the greatest revelation in history. It was amidst this struggle that Korach rose to power. Korach's ascent did not occur in a vacuum, he certainly was not the first person to question Moshe's leadership but somehow his protest ignited the populace to an unprecedented extent. The mindspace and attitude of the generation is one of the first explanations of how Korach was so successful.

In conjunction with this background, Korach waged his rebellion in exceptional ways. While others may have complained about Moshe and the leadership quietly and in private, Korach challenged the institution loudly, publicly, and with derision. While other firebrands were at the fringes of society, Korach was a distinguished and wealthy individual with social standing. These factors greatly increased the reach and appeal of an otherwise questionable message. The commentators point out that while Korach was driven by the desire to be Kohen Gadol his argument against Moshe and Aaron undermined the entire value of this position. Korach railed against a corrupt institution while secretly coveting the very office he claimed was corrupt. How did the greats in the generation fall for this illogical ruse and how did Korach arrive at the point he was comfortable with maintaining this cognitive dissonance?

The Talmud explains that the verse, "Riches hoarded to the misfortune of their owner," refers to Korach. The Talmud explains that even though he had unlimited material means, they could not help him. This statement can be taken on its face value to mean that his wealth could not bail him out of the trouble he caused. Or, the Talmud could be indicating that his wealth couldn't help him achieve the spiritual status he so desperately desired. From this perspective it was greed that drove him to threaten the foundation of his nation. Korach was accustomed to receiving recognition and respect related to his riches. The suggestion that spiritual status is distinct and cannot be purchased drove Korach to relinquish everything in pursuit of this goal. It is understandable that greed blinded Korach but we are still left to wonder how his supporters couldn't see through his transparent scheme.

There is one explanation of Korach's taking that can help answer this final question. Rabbi Eliyahu Monk explains that Korach took the feelings of envy and resentment of his followers. The outrage he expressed was not his own, rather he just echoed the sentiments of each disadvantaged group around him to garner their support for him to push his greedy self-serving agenda. This dishonestly was so effective because there were bits and pieces of legitimate complaints. Each faction could hear what they needed to continue believing Korach was their advocate, fighting tirelessly on their behalf against an oppressive system. They only saw a wealthy charismatic leader that was fearless in the face of authority. Meanwhile, Korach didn't have any honest moral objections to the system, he was driven by only his own greed and honor.

There are many practical lessons to be learned from this incident. Had the Israelites been more content and secure, they might not have been susceptible to scapegoating and panic. Had Korach appreciated his good fortune and wealth he may have been satisfied with his lot in life and not obsessed over what he couldn't have. But perhaps most importantly, had Korach's comrades remained critical of his leadership and genuinely listened to his platform and ideals they may have realized that he was not an advocate but a conman.

We should all merit to be content, appreciative and always critical of authority.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Avi · June 22, 2020

In Parshas Korach Bnei Yisroel are at odds with each other, Moshe's and Aaron's authority and priesthood are being upended by a radical new rebel who makes claims to that very same authority and priesthood. When Uncle Yonaton taught me for my Bar Mitzvah he stressed the following point immensely, If you would have been there it would have been hard for you to know it was even a rebellion, Korach was learned and at times in history his tribe had legitimate claims to priesthood.

His claims caused so much tumult that God instructed Moshe "Take a staff from each of the tribes' leaders and write each one's name on his staff. Write the name of Aaron on the staff of Levi and the man whom I shall choose, his staff will blossom."

Moshe placed each staff before God in the Sanctuary. On the next day behold, the staff of Aaron was blossoming: it brought forth blossoms, produced fruit and bore ripe almonds.

The Rebbe says there are two types of miracles, confrontational ones that bypass nature and harmonious ones that work through nature. The extra words at the end of the Posuk show us how this staff blossomed, bloomed, and bore ripe almonds, going through all the stages an almond branch would have still on the tree.

Miracle in hebrew or "Nes" means aloft or elevated. The regularity of nature makes for "laws" and these laws bind us to the "natural order".

The truth however is otherwise, humans are created to do more, to change nature and improve the status quo, Change the way things are, and uplift our existence for future generations. Miracles are displays of Divine power, when witnessing a miracle we see past the "natural order" we are inspired to test our own limitations and question societal norms.

One may think a confrontational miracle is somehow more divine, but a miracle that works through nature has the power to rewrite nature and make our world permanaty uplifted.

Good shabbos!

-Avi

Misguided Righteousness

Yosef · June 11, 2021

Following the story of the spies, which ended with a decree to wander in the desert for forty years, a rebellion began to rise. The rebel group was led by Korach, a very great man and a close cousin of Moshe himself. He gathered a group of followers and plotted, in the name of righteousness, to dethrone Moshe. In his mind, Moshe had abused his power and he truly believed that Hashem Himself would choose him in place of Moshe. In fact, he was so confident of Hashem's approval that he knowingly brought an incense offering which carried a heavenly death sentence if brought by anyone other than Hashem's personal elect. We all know the end of the story; the ground opened up and swallowed Korach and his entire assembly, thus abruptly ending the rebellion and restoring authority to Moshe.

I once heard an intriguing question. We know that the Jews in the desert were sustained by the manna which fell outside their tents each morning. This manna didn't fall by chance but was carefully placed in varying proximity to each person's tent. It's location depended on one thing only – the spiritual standing of the recipient. The more righteous one was, the closer it fell, and vice versa. Let's imagine Korach's experience. Before he initiated the rebellion, the manna surely fell right outside his door, as befitting such an incredibly great man. But with each thought or action he took against Moshe, the manna fell further and further away. After such a clear sign that his mission was against the will of Hashem, how could he possibly still have thought that he was acting in the name of G-d?

Rabbi Chaim Kanievsky, he should live and be well, gives a chilling explanation. Of course Korach realized that Hashem was upset with him, but he was so blinded by self-righteousness, that he couldn't possibly believe that his mission was evil. To uphold his facade as the champion of good, he chose to believe that Hashem was chastising him for not fighting hard enough! As the manna fell further and further from his tent, he only redoubled his 'holy' efforts to overthrow Moshe.

We see here a terrifying portrayal of just how much one can be blinded if his worldview is somewhat skewed. Such a person can be the flag-bearer of all that is evil, and never for a minute doubt that he is a servant of G-d. Even the purest of intentions, when improperly guided, can lead a man to destroy himself, to destroy his family, and to bring the whole world down with him.

And the examples of such are endless. Each of the greatest atrocities in history were committed in the name of righteousness. The crusaders, Hitler, Stalin, and all the greatest villains lived out their lives and indeed died believing all the while that they stood for the good of humanity. They lacked not good intentions, but the values and wisdom to properly guide those intentions.

Of course, on our own we are almost doomed to such a fate. One cannot pull himself out of the pit, and it is nearly impossible to perceive our own faults. For this we must subject ourselves to the timeless knowledge of the Torah and to the guidance of our Rabbis and teachers.

Let us not repeat the greatest mistakes of history, but rather learn this lesson now. Let us recommit to learning and acquiring a proper set of values, to seek guidance from those greater than us, and to never stop questioning the sincerity of our own intentions.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

July 1, 2022

This week's Parsha is Korach, in this familiar story of rebellion, Korach and his 250 statesmen challenge Aharon's priesthood, claiming, "why take all the holiness for the Kohanim when the whole nation is holy?" Moshe suggests a test: both sides offer incense, and whoever's is accepted holds the priesthood. Before the test, Moshe tells G-d, "I have not taken a donkey from a single one of them, and I have not harmed a single one of them."

At first glance, Moshe seems to be defending himself -- but the rabbis explain the opposite: Moshe, secure in his own credibility, is instead admitting he could have done more to win Korach's crew over. He had never interacted with them personally, never done business or asked favors -- and he acknowledges to G-d that this very distance may have contributed to the rebellion.

Moshe's reaction teaches us how to respond when people around us make poor choices -- if we bear some responsibility for them, that may include accepting some share of the blame. It is easy to see people make bad decisions and conclude they are simply bad people; it is much harder to examine the circumstances that led them there.

Judging people favorably is a core tenet of Jewish practice, even toward those who oppose us. Moshe teaches us not to avoid such people, but to intertwine our lives with theirs -- to understand what led them to believe as they do, and, if we hold any influence, to try to improve their circumstances. By judging our enemies favorably, we humanize them, dampen tension, and address issues before they become disasters.

Three Lives, One Soul

Noah · June 24, 2023

Rabbi Elazar Ha-kappar said: envy, lust and [the desire for] honor put a man out of the world.

Korach incites a mutiny against Moshe for granting his brother Aharon the priesthood. Together with Moshes' inveterate foes Datan and Aviram and another 250 distinguished members of the community they offer a sacrifice of incense to Hashem to prove their worthiness for the priesthood. The earth swallows the leaders and those offering the ketoret (incense) are consumed by fire.

While this incident may seem like a unique occurence in our peoples history the Shelah HaKadosh reveals a much deeper historical pattern that runs through this event. He teaches that this conflict stretches back to the first two brothers, Kayin and Hevel.

According to Rav Huna in the Midrash (Bereshis Rabbah 22:7) what were they [Kayin and Hevel] arguing about? R' Huna said: an extra twin sister was born with Hevel. This one said: I will take her because I am the first born. The other one said: I will take her because she was born with me. Because of this "…Kayin rose up against Hevel his brother and slew him."

Referencing this interpretation of the original conflict between brothers, the Shelah explains that Kayin had to go through three separate incarnations to recitfy the three fundamental spiritual components of a human, his נפש, his רוח, and his נשמה. The three incarnations are, the Egyptian that Moshe slayed, Yitro - Moshes' father in law and Korach.

The first incarnation was retribution for Kayin's killing of his brother out of lust and envy. Rashi teached us that the Egyptian Moshe saw brutally beating a slave was motivated out of lust for the man's wife. Here, Moshe in his capacity as Hevel's incarnation was able to carry out divine justice by killing the Egyptian the same way he had been struck down by his brother. The Shelah explains that this is hinted at in the verse ויך את המצרי. The numerical value of ויך, when you add one digit for the word itself, equals the numerical value of הבל, i.e. 37. The word המצרי, equals the numerical value of משה, i.e. 345.

The next incarnation was as Yitro. Yitro deferred to Moses by giving him his daughter Tzipporah as a wife. The Shelah explains She was the reincarnation (גלגול) of the extra twin that had been born with Hevrl, on whose account Kayin had slain Hevel out of jealousy. By giving her over to Moshe, here too divine justice is served.

Finally we have the namesake of our parsha. Korach now started a quarrel as he simply reverted to the pattern in which his original ancestor Kayin had acted. Moses then killed him (i.e. caused his death), fulfilling the Torah's commandment that if someone has shed innocent blood, his own blood will be spilled by human hand. We must understand that verse is telling us that the very person who had been slain, will in due course slay his murderer. This is why we find Moshe, who was in reality the re-incarnation of Hevel, slaying Korach who was the re-incarnation of Kayin.

How do these incarnations differ from eachother? It seems that each one moves deeper toward the underlying motivation that caused the original conflict between the brothers. The violent outcome is fixed by slaying the Egyptian, the lust and jealousy that drove Kayin to kill Hevel is addressed by Yitro giving his daughter to Moshe. What does Korach teach us about the core catalyst that caused the calamity?

After Kayin's offering is denied by Hashem, according to Targum Yonatan, Kayin came to the conclusion that, "There is no Judge or Law." This was the final justification that enabled him to commit fratricide. Korach represents this same ultimate rejection of a divine will. The Shelah explains, "Korach also denied the prophetic powers of Moses, that the Torah originated in Heaven and that the earth had been created because of the merit of Moses, as we have been told בראשית, בשביל משה, שנאמר וירא ראשית לו"

While Kayin's jealousy and lust had already been rectified through the previous lives, the ultimate denial of a creator and orchestrator was the final thread that had to be mended. The Shelah explains that this is alluded to through the juxtaposition of Korach's uprising and the Mitzvah of Tzitzit.

"The reason that the Torah reports the quarrel of Korach immediately after having taught us the laws about ציצית is explained by Rashi, who says that ציצית teaches the unity of G–d, His existence, and that He created the world ex nihilo. It also teaches that the origin of Torah is in Heaven, that it is not man made. It teaches that there is a system of reward and punishment which operates in this world. Concerning ציצית the Torah says "when you see Him i.e. אותו, etc." There are eight threads above the blue thread and eight threads below the blue thread which is threaded through the corner of the garment. Subsequently we attach five knots, each one being a double knot making a total of ten. Thus we arrive at a total of sixteen threads and ten knots, the numerical value of the Ineffable Name of God. This name teaches that He is, was, and will be, and that He is the Cause of all Existence.

It this understanding that allows us to remain in the world. The real world, the world with meaning and consequences and justice. Without it we are victims to lust, jealousy and the endless pursuit of honor. With this understanding we can come closer to emulating Moshe and eventually inheriting the world to come.

Parshat

Chukat

The Inevitable Purpose of the World

Yosef · July 12, 2019

The Israelites cried out for lack of water in the desert, and Moshe and Aharon were commanded by G-d to speak to a rock, whereupon water would gush forth from the rock. There was a certain confusion (which is the subject of debate and is not the topic of our discussion) and they hit the rock instead of merely speaking to it. 'Hashem said to Moshe and Aharon, "Because you did not believe in me to sanctify me in the eyes of Israel, therefore you will not bring the nation into the land which I have given them."'

We find here a fascinating concept which Uncle Yonatan was very fond of discussing. Our great leaders were charged here with passing up the opportunity to sanctify Hashem's name. Rashi explains that if they would have spoke to the rock, the people would have understood that if a rock obeys the mere command of G-d, then certainly they too must heed His word alone. By hitting the rock, they watered down the lesson, implying that it is only necessary to obey G-d in order to avoid punishment. Because of the exalted level of Moshe and Aharon, this was considered for them a very grave sin and they were denied the right of entry to the Land of Israel.

The incredible thing to note is how the Torah sums up the event. 'These were the waters of Meriva with which Israel challenged G-d, and He was sanctified through [the waters].' How was Hashem sanctified through the waters? Rashi tells us that it was through the punishment of Moshe and Aharon itself. He explains that when Hashem enacts judgment with His holy ones, His name becomes revered and feared. We are meant to realize that if even such lofty individuals sometimes fall short of G-d's expectations, then how much more so must we never be satisfied with our accomplishments. We must constantly be working on ourselves as we strive ever higher. That lesson itself is a great sanctification of His name.

This means that they were faulted for failing to sanctify Hashem's name and thereby they were punished. What happened through their punishment? Hashem's name was sanctified.

Rabbi E. E. Dessler explains that the purpose of the world and all that is in it, is to bring about the sanctification of Hashem's name. That is the purpose of each mitzvah and the central point of the entire Torah. We are charged with causing this sanctification through our every action. But say that one loses track of his mission in the world and he decides to sin against G-d, thereby defiling His name. Has he thwarted the divine plan? Not at all. All he did was to choose not to align himself with the will of G-d, and not to play an active and willing role in picture. As such, he will be punished, in this world or the next, and in this way Hashem's name we be sanctified just the same. The will of Hashem will be fulfilled no matter what, we can only choose what kind of role we'd like to play. We can proactively sanctify Him through His service, while reaping endless reward for ourselves, or we can break ourselves against His will, sanctifying Him through our very suffering.

Choose wisely.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Surrender of the Mind

Yosef · June 18, 2021

The Red Heifer, or parah adumah, was a special sacrifice brought to Hashem, which had a function of purifying those who are tamei, or ritually impure. Why was this sacrifice specifically a cow? Rashi answers with a parable to a maidservant in the king's palace. When the maid servant's son sullied the palace, they fetched his mother to clean up the mess. Hence, when the Jews sullied the world with the sin of the golden calf, they called its mother the cow to clean up the mess and atone for their sins. Then, Rashi proceeds at some length to point out all the correlations between the golden calf and the red heifer, and how each part of the parah adumah was designed to atone in some way for the sin of the calf.

We could take Rashi's comments at face value and understand that the entire link between the two parts of Torah are no more than the fact that a calf is the son of a cow, but a thinking Jew has to realize that there must be something deeper going on here. What is the true relationship between the two, and how is the red heifer an atonement for the sin of the calf?

The first thing we need to understand is the essence of the golden calf. What was the purpose of the calf, and which shortcoming was expressed in it's creation? The Beis Halevi famously explained that the golden calf was not a simple idol being worshiped as if it were G-d Himself. Such an understanding is simply untenable, being that each and every member of the nation had experienced the revelation of G-d's presence at Sinai. Rather, he explains that the calf was an attempt to create something not unlike the eventual tabernacle. We understand that the service in the tabernacle was a form of service to Hashem, with the tabernacle serving merely as a point of connection between the people and G-d – it certainly was no substitute for Hashem. Such was the Jews plan with the golden calf. It was their understanding that through the calf they would be able to connect with Hashem, similar to the way the golden cherubs on top of the ark served as a point of connection.

So what was their mistake? Nothing more than the fact that Hashem hadn't decreed as such. When the command would later come to build the tabernacle with all that was in it, it was truly not so different from the calf – only that the will of Hashem made it a mitzvah.

A deeper understanding of this concept was presented by Rabbi Avigdor Miller. He explained how the Jews had always been a nation guided by their intellect, which they came to rely on very heavily. In fact, it was just their own sense of right and wrong which led them to face off with the entire world on the subject of idol-worship. But at Sinai, Hashem asked them to give themselves completely to Him. As we say in the Shma, 'with all of your heart, with all of your soul, and with all of your possessions'. While their wealth and even their bodies were something they were prepared to give over to G-d, they found it very very difficult to subjugate even their minds. All of a sudden, the people of the mind were asked to become the people of the book. They were charged with making their minds into Torah minds; minds which were filled only with the word of G-d. This was no small task and no easy sacrifice. Still, they accepted and vowed to give over their most prized possession, their intellect, to Hashem.

This is the subtlety which was behind the sin of the golden calf. The nation had just stood at Sinai and accepted the Torah, including the momentous agreement to even think only according to the Torah. Then, as their leader Moshe tarried on his descent from Sinai, they began to panic, falling back on their natural tendency to use their heads on their own terms. They used every bit of their formidable wisdo to devise a way to fill in for Moshe with a connection point – and they arrived at the conclusion that the calf would best serve their purposes. Their intent was good, their logic was solid, but they had reneged on their agreement, following their minds to do something not condoned by the Torah.

In this light, we can understand the depth of connection to the parah adumah. The red heifer is considered the quintessential example of a mitzvah which is beyond the scope of our understanding. While it is a cleanser which purifies those who are impure, it conversely affects those who are pure by rendering them impure. This is something we do not understand; we must surrender our intellect and act purely in submission of Hashem's command. Such a show of relinquishing our allegiance to mortal logic in the face of divine law is an effective teshuvah for the sin of the golden calf, and it is for this reason that the parah adumah is correlated with the golden calf on every level.

The takeaway for us is the need to realize the frailty of human thought and the shakiness of mortal values and morals. We need only take a quick look outside to see all the depravity being paraded about as the epitome of virtue, and to realize how lost we are when left to our own ideas of right and wrong. This ought to stir a deep unrest in man, and it should leave him searching for a truly infallible source of thought and reason. A source found only in the Torah. We should happily surrender our minds to think Torah thoughts, with the knowledge that only thus will our minds be freed from all the biases and agendas of the world. Only by submitting our minds to the Torah can we really tap into the power and intellectual honesty that mankind can attain.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Recognizing the Source

Yosef · July 8, 2022

This week's parshah includes the painful story of Mei Meriva, when Moshe and Aharon sinned by hitting the rock to produce water instead of talking to the rock as commanded by Hashem. This seemingly minor infraction cost our great leaders their lives and the right to lead the nation into the land of Canaan, and much has been said to explain exactly the nature of this sin and why it was treated with such harshness.

I'd like to share a penetrating explanation from Rav Yeruchom Levovitz, whose deep words I will attempt give over.

Our sages tell us that one who says things over in the name of it's source brings geulah, or redemption, to the world. On the surface this seems difficult to understand. When one hears a nice thought from his friend and tells it over to another, it is simple decency to quote the source instead of taking the credit for oneself. To do otherwise is called plagiarism and, at least in writing, is even against the law. How is this a ticket for the ultimate redemption?

To understand, let's preface with a perspective on what geulah is really all about. We've said many times in this forum that the ultimate purpose of creation is to bring about kiddush Hashem, the sanctification of Hashem's name. In golus, or exile, Hashem's mastery of the world, as well as His power and honor, are concealed. There is a thick smokescreen of nature which hides Hashem's involvement and threatens to fool us into thinking that we run the world. Geulah, however, is the full revelation of Hashem's might and omnipotence, when all of His glory becomes crystal clear and undeniable. Geulah, then, is the ultimate manifestation of kiddush Hashem and the climactic culmination of the purpose of the world.

On a deeper level, one who says something in the name of it's source, doesn't just mean one who avoids plagiarism. It is a deep-seated trait of disgusting any undeserved credit and an exalted level of humility wherein a person is able to take himself and his ego out of the picture and realize that even his 'own' accomplishments are truly rooted in what he was handed from others.

To illustrate, a master of this trait is might be one who delivers a blockbuster performance in front of an enamored audience thousands strong, yet remains unimpressed with his own accomplishment. As the crowd cheers, he, in his heart of hearts, feels no surge of pride and no pleasure at his glory. Instead, he is thinking that his charisma is God-given, his prowess is the product of his teachers, his wisdom is from his parents, and so forth. Everything which would make a lesser man feel pride, evokes in him nothing but gratitude. Every greatness which he possesses is ascribed to those above him, with no sense of ego to steal any of the limelight.

Because geulah is the ultimate manifestation of Hashem's glory, it is only this person devoid of any ego and self-interest who can be the vehicle for the kiddush Hashem of geulah. Anyone else would cloud the picture by taking some of the credit for himself. As he would lead the people to salvation, he would feel some modicum of haughtiness and grandeur, thereby diverting some of the glory to himself instead of pointing it all to it's true source as the honor of Hashem.

Moshe was the paradigm of one who attributes everything to its source. While he was the greatest man that ever lived, he took no credit for himself. It is Moshe who said ונחנו מה, 'what am I'. It is Moshe who is hailed by G-d Himself that the most humble of all men. Moshe knew who he was, but he took no credit for his achievements, sourcing everything back to Hashem. As such, Moshe was the quintessential vehicle to bring the ultimate geulah, and it was he who was charged with bringing the Jewish nation into the land of Canaan. Had Moshe been the one to lead them in, our sages tell us the world would have reached its purpose and the temple would have never been destroyed. He was the only person armed to bring about the full and complete geulah because he was the one who could do so while sourcing everything to Hashem and enacting the ultimate kiddush Hashem.

Thus, when Moshe was commanded by Hashem to speak to the rock and he veered from the source and did something of his own, this was a catastrophic glitch in the master plan. By doing something, however minuscule, that was not a perfect representation of the Source, he had lost the ability to bring about the complete geulah. No longer was he able to be the transparent agent who could focus all the honor on it's source. By straying from the Source, he had lost the ability to be the bringer of geulah, and for this reason he was denied the position of bringing the nation into the land.

We are standing on the brink of geulah, with Moshiach just waiting to step over the threshold and usher in the final redemption. Each and every one of us can and must bring that great day by exerting ourselves in Torah and mitzvos, but we must do so with an emphasis on ascribing everything to the Source. We have to strive and fight to accomplish all that we can, but when we are met with success we have to realize that it's not thanks to us. By doing so, we can diminish our egos and become the transparent people capable of evoking kiddush Hashem and the ultimate geulah.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Parshat

Balak

The King's Treasury

Yosef · June 29, 2018

A merchant arrived by boat in a distant land and hired one of the porters to bring his bags to the inn. A short while later, the porter arrived, out of breath and dripping with sweat. He asked for payment, but the merchant confidently responded, “You didn't bring the right bags.” Outraged, the porter asked how he could possibly know such a thing when he hadn't even seen the packages yet. The merchant answered calmly that his bags were small sacks full of diamonds and other precious gems – if he had brought the correct packages there was no way that he would be sweating so much.

This story is a parable from the great Maggid of Dubno, in explanation of the following verse (Isaiah 43): “It is not to Me (G-d) that you are calling, for Israel has grown weary.” The verse begs an explanation; what does weariness have to do with calling out to G-d? But with this parable we can understand the message. Hashem told the Jewish people that His service is meant to be a pleasure. Learning Torah and doing other mitzvot is like collecting diamonds, service which we should be itching for the opportunity to fulfill. If “Israel has grown weary” then clearly their service of G-d has taken the wrong approach and “it is not to Me that you are calling.”

If we would only realize the tremendous opportunity afforded to us with each of the Mitzvot, we would never grow weary no matter how much we work. Picture a man who has just been granted twenty-four hours in the King's treasury. He is allowed to keep anything he gathers in that time period, but when the clock runs out the game is over. You can imagine how hard he would work, not resting for a moment to eat or to sleep. For twenty-four hours he would be the hardest-working individual in the world. But if you'd ask him how he's doing, would he respond “Oy! This work is so difficult!” Of course not! He understands his incredible good fortune and would revel every second that he was allotted.

Every Mitzva is an opportunity to achieve greatness of character, closeness to G-d, and immeasurable rewards, as well as attain true happiness. Mitzvot can be hard work, they can be demanding, and they can even be expensive, but if we would realize what an opportunity they are, we would never grow weary from the service of Hashem.

In this week's Parhsa, Bilam praises Israel with the following words: “I did not see toil in Israel.” Isn't this is a puzzling way to praise someone – it sounds more like an insult, calling them lazy and unwilling to work?
