The Ohr Hachaim explains the phrase to mean that even though the Jews expend great effort hard work in the service of G-d, it does not seem to them to be a toil. They realize the great value of Torah and mitzvot and thereby they are ecstatic about their good fortune and relish every opportunity spend in the King's treasury.

I'd like to close with a true story about Rabbi Chaim Kanievsky, he should live and be well. Rabbi Kanievsky is famed as one of the greatest Torah scholars of our generation and one who does not waste a single second; he is constantly engrossed in Torah. When he was a young man, one of his peers noticed that although he'd come to the Kollel (study hall) with bloodshot eyes every day, clearly running on very little sleep, he never once dozed off while learning. One day, however, a man approached Rabbi Kanievsky with a newspaper clipping – something important but not Torah related. The Rabbi began to read the newspaper clipping, and within seconds his eyes closed in exhaustion. This is our point in case. The Rabbi was completely exhausted from hard work and lack of sleep, yet he was so excited about the Torah that he never found it wearying. Only when he tried to focus on something relatively mundane did his fatigue finally catch up with him and knock him out within seconds.

We all were privy to see Uncle Yonatan's great passion for anything Torah related. He never tired for a second from learning something new. He would walk miles to do a mitzvah, and in prison he would prepare for months in advance to be able to once eat matzah or sit in a succah. His great love for G-d and his Torah made him indefatigable. “Mighty waters could not extinguish his love.”

May we all merit an appreciation of the great treasure that every mitzvah truly is and thereby experience all the joy that Judaism has to offer.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Noah · 2019

Bilam is standing on a mountain overlooking a portion of the Jewish camp in the desert, Balak alongside him, hoping this third attempt will finally produce a curse. Instead, the words "Mah Tovu" emanate from Bilam's mouth -- praise so enduring it was written into our siddur, mandated to be said upon entering a synagogue. What do Bilam's words teach us about the essence of a Jewish community?

Rashi explains that Bilam noticed the entrances to the Jewish tents were offset, so no one could see directly into a neighbor's home -- a Jewish community is modest. Rav Hirsch reads "Tov" in the moral sense: regardless of a home's outward appearance, its real value lies in its spiritual atmosphere -- a Jewish community makes spirituality its focus. The Gemara in Sanhedrin explains that Bilam had originally intended to attack our shuls and study halls, understanding them as the centers of communal spirituality -- a Jewish community values its shuls, yeshivas, and schools.

The Ohr HaChaim reads the blessing's imagery -- "brooks stretching out," "gardens along the river," "aloes planted by G-d," "towering cedars" -- as describing four types of people who make up a community: those who spread Torah across the land, those who teach near its center, those who study purely for its own sake, and those who support Torah study financially though not scholars themselves. All are described as deeply rooted in sanctity -- a Jewish community is rooted in sanctity, no matter which role each person plays.

Why would Balak think this particular mountain, this particular vantage point, would finally produce a curse? Perhaps because a curse is simply the choice to notice the negative and hope for its continuation. When Bilam finally saw the Israelites as one impenetrable community, he could find nothing to curse at all.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

US vs. THEM

Noah · July 7, 2020

In this week's parsha we learn about the infamous curses of Billam against the Jewish people. This conflict is archetypal of the us versus them mentality that has taken root around the world. First, King Balak and the people of Moab begin having anxiety about the rapidly growing neighboring Jewish population that is consuming large amounts of resources, "as the ox licks up the grass of the field." This anxiety results in monumental cooperation between historical rivals, Moab and Midian, and true fulfillment of "the enemy of my enemy is my friend." With this alliance established, וַיִּשְׁלַ֨ח מַלְאָכִ֜ים אֶל־בִּלְעָ֣ם בֶּן־בְּע֗וֹר פְּ֠תוֹרָה אֲשֶׁ֧ר עַל־הַנָּהָ֛ר אֶ֥רֶץ בְּנֵי־עַמּ֖וֹ לִקְרֹא־ל֑וֹ לֵאמֹ֗ר הִ֠נֵּה עַ֣ם יָצָ֤א מִמִּצְרַ֙יִם֙ הִנֵּ֤ה כִסָּה֙ אֶת־עֵ֣ין הָאָ֔רֶץ וְה֥וּא יֹשֵׁ֖ב מִמֻּלִֽי׃ [Balak] sent messengers to Balaam son of Beor in Pethor, which is by the Euphrates, in the land of his kinsfolk, to invite him, saying, "There is a people that came out of Egypt; it hides the earth from view, and it is settled next to me. Let's unpack the Balak's reasoning for hiring this hitman. First, the posuk mentions the "land of his kinsfolk" again stressing the sense of relatedness that now existed between these nations. Next, the posuk mentions, "to invite him." Rashi explains that this is a hint to the large cash payment that Balaam received for his willingness to act against this perceived threat. Balak now mentions the Exodus from Egypt. The Aderet Eliyahu asks why would Balak mention a time of Israel's incredible triumph while trying to rally the troops against them? What is the connection between this and the incitement Balak is attempting? The Kli Yakar explains that this was to make it clear that we were not a settled people, but migrants. This made the Jewish people seem more vulnerable and Balak's suspicion blinded him from even noticing the incredible triumph that was their departure. But agitating against the vulnerable isn't always enough to convince others to join your cause. Rashi explains;

עם יצא ממצרים [THERE IS] A PEOPLE COME OUT FROM EGYPT — and if you ask: What harm does that do you? If you are like the questioner in Rashi and aren't convinced yet, Balak will now roll out his final justification for cursing a nation. "It hides the earth from view." In Hebrew this phrase literally means, cover the eyes of the earth. Rashi explains that this is an allusion to Sichon and Og, the giants that we conquered in the previous parsha. Sifsei Chachamim says that Rashi isn't sticking with the simpler meaning that the Jewish camp was massive because we know it covered only 12 mil, a relatively small area. The problem wasn't really about overpopulation or resource consumption, but because they saw that we took away something they relied on. The giants are referred to as the eyes of the world because Balak relied on them for protection like his own eyes. The insecurity that our success provoked was the final justification for Balak's enmity toward the Jewish people.

In the end, we know that Balaam was not successful. The many merits of our communal structure, its modesty, and commitment to holiness, saved us from the impact of these curses. Still, we have to learn from this attempt against us. We have to learn to identify the patterns of "us versus them." It starts with the consolidation of a silent, previous fragmented, majority. This group, motivated by economic anxiety, has a leader promise riches while directing this worry toward a vulnerable minority. This minority fits the accusation as they have already caused insecurity by threatening the integrity of GIANT systems of oppression. Sound familiar?

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Adam · June 25, 2021

This week’s Parsha contains within it one of the deepest missing pieces in our generation’s treatment of one another.

Let me give you a brief overview of the events:

King Balak calls Bilam to curse the Israelite nation. After a brief back and forth between Bilam and G-d, and then Bilam and Balak by way of messengers and emissaries he agrees to come on the condition (supplied by G-d) that he will only say the words Hashem tells him to.

Bilam arises and saddles his donkey and begins his journey. Shortly thereafter, the donkey stops midway down the path and chooses an off-road route for she can see a fiery angel of “G-d guarding the way. Bilam gives her a kick to set her straight and she returns to the path only to flee the other direction, this time pinning his leg to a wall. Bilam beats her again.

Now the angel stands directly in the middle of the path blocking the entire way through causing the donkey to lay down mid-journey. Furious, Bilam strikes a third time and immediately G-d allows the donkey speech, an open miracle.

““What have I done to you that you have beaten me these three times?” asks the donkey.

“You have made a mockery of me! If I had a sword with me, I’d kill you,” Bilam replies.

“Am I not the ass that you have been riding all along until this day! Have I been in the habit of doing this to you?”

“No.”

At this point G-d allows Bilam, too, to see the angel and he bows deep until the ground.

The angel then admonishes Bilam, “Why have you beaten your donkey? It was because of me that she shied away these three times. And had she not, it would have been you that I’d killed and allowed her to live.”

These are the verses (very slightly paraphrased).

Rashi on this final verse allows us to understand the meaning of that last statement: and allowed her to live: But now that she admonished you and you had no response to defend yourself, I have killed the donkey lest onlookers later say, “That’s the donkey that admonished Bilam.” Such is the regard G-d has for human dignity.

Many point out how far this regard goes: Despite all possible gains to be had from others experiencing this open miracle of a talking donkey, in order to save the honor of one of the most morally decrepit individuals of the time, G-d takes the life of the donkey.

I think we can learn immensely from meditation on this point. Anyone we are coming across is surely not as big a villain as Bilam and surely has nowhere near the evil intent behind anything they say or do.

We have an opportunity to emulate G-d, to open the possibility for others to save face, to remove incriminating statements from the public view, and to show our regard for the sheer humanity of those we encounter despite even their obvious errors in action, judgement, or approach.

How great are the tents of Jacob?!

I love you all and miss you!

Adam

Recognizing the Source

Noah · July 15, 2022

Bilam is standing on a mountain overlooking a portion of the Jewish camp in the desert. A frustrated Balak is alongside him, hoping he can deliver a curse on his third attempt. Instead of the prophetic words, Ma Tovu, emanates from Bilam's mouth, extolling the virtues of a Jewish community. This blessing would go on to be included in the siddur (possibly a first for a non-Jewish/Israelite author) and be mandated to be repeated upon entering the synagogue. What do Bilam's words teach us about the values and essence of a Jewish community?

מַה־טֹּבוּ אֹהָלֶיךָ יַעֲקֹב מִשְׁכְּנֹתֶיךָ יִשְׂרָאֵל׃

How fair are your tents, O Jacob, Your dwellings, O Israel!

כִּנְחָלִים נִטָּיוּ כְּגַנֹּת עֲלֵי נָהָר כַּאֲהָלִים נָטַע יְהוָה כַּאֲרָזִים עֲלֵי־מָיִם׃

Like palm groves that stretch out, Like gardens beside a river, Like aloes planted by the LORD, Like cedars beside the water;

The most famous explanation of these verses is found in Rashi. He says that Bilam was noticing that the entrances of the tents in the Jewish encampment were offset, obscuring any direct view into the neighbor's abode. A Jewish community is modest.

מה טבו אהליך. עַל שֶׁרָאָה פִתְחֵיהֶם שֶׁאֵינָן מְכֻוָּנִין זֶה מוּל זֶה

In the same vein as Rashi, Rav Hirsch explains that the posuk means Tov in a moral sense. While our homes may differ in appearance the real mark of value in the Jewish home is the spiritual atmosphere. He goes on to explain that Ohalecha is referring to more humble dwellings and Mishkenosecha to more lavish ones. Bilam is commending us for making spirituality the central focus of our lives regardless of the material situation we currently find ourselves in. A Jewish community makes spirituality its focus.

The Gemara in Sanhedrin teaches that from Bilam's blessings we can ascertain what his true intentions were. The Gemara says that because Bilam wanted to attack our shuls and study halls his curse was flipped to Ma Tovu Ohalecha Yaakov. Bilam understood that these locations are the centers of communal spirituality and that spirituality itself was the core of the Jewish community. This is why he selected it as his first target. The Sforno explains that the wording Bilam used reflected a deep understanding of the influence our shuls and study halls have on our community. He comments on the unusual plural formulation of the word Tovu saying that it indicates these places are not just of supreme value to those who frequent them but their very existence is a benefit to the whole community. A Jewish community values Shuls, Yeshivas, and Schools.

Finally, the Jewish community is comprised of individuals. To understand who these people are the Orach Chaim says we need not look farther than the first verses of Bilams blessing. The first type of person is the people who go across Israel year after year to spread Torah knowledge. They are alluded to by the phrase, "brooks which stretch out." The second is people who make headquarters near the temple mount and judge and teach. They are like, "gardens along the river." The third, study Torah entirely for its own sake and are "Aloes planted by G'd." Finally, there are those who provide financial support for people who are studying. The Torah calls them "towering cedars" because although they do not have the fruits of their own Torah they are still mighty spiritually. The Orach Chaim concludes that "the entire blessing is meant to describe all of the Israelites as deeply rooted in sanctity, even the section of the population which devotes itself to the pursuit of material goals in order to share the results with the Torah students." A Jewish community is rooted in sanctity.

Why would Balak think that on this mountain Bilam would perform differently? Why was the Bilam's power to curse dependent on the vantage point from which he observed the nation? The answer may be that Bilam's curses were merely observations. A curse is a choice to notice the negative and hope for its continuation. When Bilam saw the Israelites we were one impenetrable entity. When our communities function as they should, "He sees no sin Jacob no woe for Israel."

Shabbat Shalom

Noah

The Capital of Israel

Yosef · June 30, 2023

Bilam, someone infamous for the profound potency of his curses, tried multiple times, with many different tactics, to curse the Jews as they marched toward the land of Canaan. Although his employer, Balak the king of Moav, only wanted the curse to keep the Jews from invading his land, Bilam wickedly decided to do away with the Jewish problem once and for all -- shooting for their total annihilation.

Hashem, however, had different plans. He forewarned Bilam that even if he were to attempt to follow Balak and curse the people his efforts would be unfruitful -- his mouth would only pronounce the words placed there by Hashem. This arrangement resulted in some of the most beautiful blessings our people have ever received being bestowed upon us by our arch-enemy. Still, our sages say that from the blessings we can infer that the intended curses must have been the same point reversed.

Given these two points, that Bilam was only interested in our complete destruction and that all of his blessing mirrored the intended curse, it is rather puzzling to understand one of the most famous verses in his liturgy. He says, 'How good are your tents, Yaakov, and your tabernacles, Yisroel.' The sages explain that these are references to the shuls and the Yeshivos where Jews unite in the study of Torah. According to the principle above, we can infer that Bilam must have tried to curse the Yeshivos and to hamper our ability to learn Torah. This is a surprising attack from one seeking blood alone. It would be like Hitler neglecting his gas chambers in favor of ensuring that the Jews shouldn't speak of Torah in the barracks.

What did the Yeshivos have to do with Bilam's dastardly scheme?

Rav Elchonon Wasserman zt"l explained with a war analogy. When two countries go to war, there may be a great many battles fought and a great many casualties suffered. One country may far exceed its enemy when it comes to individual battles won, but that still doesn't define the victor. At what point can they claim victory? When they conquer the capital city. When the capital falls to the enemy, the war is over. There may still be remaining towns to overtake and battalions to vanquish, but these are just the finishing stages -- the war has already been won.

Bilam knew that the Yeshivos, the halls of Torah learning, are the capital of the Jewish nation. Were he to focus only on the physical lives of the Jews, he knew that he would never be able to claim victory. Only by targeting our lifeblood, our core, and our capital fortresses of Torah, could he achieve his ultimate goal of annihilation.

If the wicked Bilam understood the centrality of Torah learning not only for our spiritual existence but for our physical vitality, then we, the Chosen Nation, must certainly learn the lesson as well. So, let's redouble our efforts in the pursuit of Torah learning; through personal study, through teaching, and through supporting our institutions of Torah. In this way, and only in this way, will we succeed in rebuilding the glory of our nation as it finds it center in the capital fortress of Torah.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Parshat

Pinchas

With God on Either Side

Adam · July 6, 2018

In this week's Torah portion we find a very strange phenomenon. Not unusually, God tells Moshe (and Aaron's son, Elazar) to go count the Jewish people. But unlike every other time they are counted, this time each family is called twice. The verses go through the 45 different families that stemmed from the 12 tribes and calls each one by name like so: “the sons of Shimon, according to their families: to Nemuel, the Nemueli family, to Yamin, the Yamini family, to Yachin, the Yachini family…”

Suffice it to say that the repetitiveness needs explanation. Every single letter in the Torah is there for a reason. There are no extras. So when the Torah repeats 45 family names with no additional information being given over, we must pause.

Fortunately, Rashi tells us what’s going on: The nations of the world where laughing that the Jewish people took such pride in there familial heritage. Tribal affiliation and family heritage travels through the patriarchal line and the nations would tease, “What are the chances that any slave nation could know their fathers?”

Would not the Egyptians sleep with any woman they pleased?

“How is it possible,” they laughed, “that the Jewish people know their fathers’ ancestral heritage?”

Therefore, Rashi says, God places each and every name in between the two letters of the Divine name to testify upon them that they retained the sexual purity that befits a nation of royalty.

This is easier to see in the Hebrew, but each name was called with a Hey before it and a Yud after, the two letter name of God, Y-ah.

An example: “To Yamin, family of HaYamini, the Yaminite.”

The trait we are discussing here is called yesod, or foundation, and actually refers to all relationships and relating between people. (It's not by accident that the Torah uses the term “to know” to refer to relations between a man and wife.)

I think I understand finally this year, why this specific counting of the Jewish people is the one where God places his name on each family. We have been counted many times since we left Egypt but suddenly now this becomes necessary. If you remember, the story that bridges last week's Parsha and this week's, was the one of the Midianite princesses that came promiscuously into the camp to entrap the Jewish people.

Simultaneously, we become aware of the Jewish people engaging in idol worship. Not just any idol, but actually the most disgusting idol we know of. (The service was to defecate in front of it.) Just after the Jewish people begin serving this idol, known as Baal Peor, a plague breaks out in the camp and many of the Jewish people begin to die.

The Nesivos Shalom points out that the plague begins due to idol worship but it only ends after Pinchas, Elazar's son, drives a spear through Zimri, a Jewish tribal leader, and Cozbi, the Midianite princess, whilst they are engaged in an act of sexual immorality before the camp.

We see an interesting and strong value placed on sexual morality here. The sin of being with a midianite woman is a debate among the sages. Some hold that it is only a rabbinic prohibition and others holds that it is a Torah prohibition. But everyone agrees that the Torah prohibition of idol worship falls under the three sins one must give their life not to transgress. And lying with a non-Jewish woman isn't even agreed upon by all to be a Torah prohibition! How could this act of Pinchas which seems so clearly related to the promiscuity that was entering the camp, put an end to a plaque that befell the Jewish people due to idolatry?!

The answer, our sages tell us, is that the Jewish people have an internal knowledge of God that is unshakeable. And according to the kabbalists, when the evil inclination seeks to destroy the relationship between a Jew and God, he knows that he cannot strike at that relationship directly. He must go through the conduit of sins of sexuality. (Rashi actually tells us in last week's Parsha that the Jewish people began worshipping Baal Peor only after the midianite princesses pulled the idols out of their tunics and said they would only lie with the Jewish people if they first served the idol.) When Pinchas called attention the underlying problem, the plague came to an end.

So I think that now, after this story of Pinchas, God seeks to remind the Jewish people that we have pure roots. That we needn't worry what any of the nations say. That we do have an ancestral heritage that is passed through our father's tribal identity. And that just like they figured out how to stay pure and endure the slavery they were faced with, we too can stand in this challenge.

The Arizal says that this is the central challenge of our generation. To work on Yesod, sexual morality and relationships. May we merit to keep ourselves distant from sins, and to relate to one another in a Torah-true and healthy way, and to thereby be in touch with our innate knowledge of the Creator and build an intensely loving relationship with the One!

Good Shabbos!

Love, Adam

Adapted and expanded from some shiurim by Rav Moshe Weinberger, Shlita

PS, missing y'all already

When to Act

July 26, 2019

In this week's Parsha Pinchas, A grandson of Aaron is responsible for single-handedly halting a plague and defending the morals of Bnei Yisroel when he stabs an immodest Midianite and her prince husband Zimri for publicly flaunting their intimacy. Pinchas famously is struck by Zimri's immodesty with his wife in public. Pinchas may have seemed to act on impulse, but the Gemara tells us the opposite. Instead of jumping into action Pinchas looked to his elders, Moshe and Aaron were equally taken aback by the scene. Pinchas respectfully reminded Moses that he himself taught the principle that one who behaves as Zimri may be executed by the zealous. At which point Moshe ordered Pinchas to carry out the killing, all in one instant.

Why is Pinchas credited for doing something required by law in such an informal fashion and What made Moshe forget his own doctrines?

When destiny beckons, and there is a situation that we personally can see to resolution an obligation falls upon us to do so. This is Pinchas's test. Moshes inability to draw quick conclusions, set the stage for Pinchas to act rash. If Pinchas would not have looked to his elders and just went ahead he would surely be a murderer. If he looked to his elders and saw that they were speechless and remained speechless the plague would have never lifted. Pinchas knew his place as a young man and his place in a royal family involved in carrying out divine procedures. This was far out of his job description but asserting himself with the oversight of his superiors is what made his trial a success. The Lubavitch rebbe reminds us that when we see the people we look up to most speechless, overrun or out of breath, these are the times our involvement is most meaningful.

Pinchas (2020, Daughters of Zelophehad)

Avi · July 16, 2020

The daughters of Zelophehad were Mahlah, Noa, Hoglah, Milcah, and Tirzah; they lived in the fortieth year after the exodus from Egypt. Shortly before the Jews entered Israel it was announced that a lot would be cast for each man in a tribe to be assigned a plot of land to be passed to future generations through the male lineage. Zelophehad from the tribe of Menashe, aside from being a dead martyr, had no male lineage. His five daughters had no inheritance, they were bold enough to raise the issue with Moshe. "Our father died in the desert... he died due to his own sin and he had no sons... Give us a landholding among our father's brothers!"

Moshe brought their case before God. God spoke to Moses saying "The daughters of Zelophehad have spoken correctly. You shall certainly give them a landholding among their father's brothers, and transfer their father's inheritance to them."

Zelophehad means dark shadow, which is a fitting name for a man who died for his own sin. Nomatter how he died the sages agreed he did it with good intentions. Some say he was part of a band of Jews who after the sin of the spies, despite being warned, took Israel by storm only to be slaughtered by Amalekites and Cananites. Another opinion says he is the Shabbos wood collector who overheard teens saying Shabbos no longer applied in the desert. In response he publicly broke Shabbos and was executed for doing so in a gastly attempt to teach by hypocrisy.

With their father dead, the woman had no choice but to embolden themselves, forcing Moshe to publicly hear their demand for a stake of land. After it was granted to them they faced another struggle, because of their position as Jewish women, their children would absorb the tribal identity of their father. This forced the 5 daughters to marry within their tribe, they duley married their cousins. Only 15 years later an heiress gained permanent stature in the law allowing daughters with bequeathments to retain their own land.

Unlike their father who is remembered solely for his martyrdom, these women embody resilience and the ability to get what they need. They would never self-destruct to be heard, and did not have the power to overstep their bounds in an attempt to take what they needed by force. This is why God heard them directly and allowed their struggle to make lasting change. The daughters of Zelophehad represent Jewish women's love for Israel, their names have become common in modern Israel, because they represent resilient members of the historically disenfranchised. We are living in a period of unprecedented nationalism, this rise causes for many to feel misplaced and uninvited in places they previously knew as home, this Parsha shows us that going rogue or radicalising to the death does not help gain traction, rather reacting to needs as they come and demanding change in public spaces is how we go about changing the status quo.

Pinchas (2022, Yehoshua)

Avi · July 21, 2022

In this weeks Parsha Moshe appointed his successor, Yehoshua Bin Nun. While he is certainly a great person, what were the outstanding characteristics that allowed him to fill such an important role?

The role of a leader is to ignite people and to keep their passion for life and learning burning strong. The most important way to achieve this is through consistency. Just like a pot of water on the stove if removed and returned will never come to a boil, we cannot grow and become greater people without constant dedication.

About Yehoshua, it is written that he was "A lad, who did not depart from within the tent." Even from his youth, he knew that to achieve greatness he would have to dedicate himself constantly to learning. He never took breaks or vacations and soon was burning brighter than any of Moshe's other students.

We see this same value of consistency reflected in the korban tamid, the daily offering. It was responsible for atoning for all of our sins. This is because a consistent effort can overcome any odds.

Yehoshua, like Moshe, would have to lead his people to new heights and inspire them to persevere through trying times. Using his character of consistency, Yehoshua would be able to accomplish this massive endeavor.

July 2023

This week's Parsha is Pinchas, a difficult Parsha dealing with conflict between Jews and Midianite influence. It includes laws of land inheritance among the tribes, Moshe's formal appointment of Yehoshua as his successor, and additional sacrifices commanded for Shabbat and festivals.

Pinchas is himself a difficult figure -- rewarded for publicly killing a tribal leader engaged in an open affair with a Midianite woman, he receives G-d's covenant of peace and priesthood, a fitting reward that reminds us to value peace above all.

A census counts 601,730 men between twenty and sixty; land is divided among tribes and families accordingly. The daughters of Tzelophechad -- with no brother to inherit their father's portion -- petition Moshe directly, and G-d instructs that their case be incorporated permanently into the laws of inheritance: "The daughters of Tzelophechad have spoken correctly."

The daughters of Tzelophechad represent Jewish women's love for Israel; their names remain common in modern Israel today, representing resilient members of a historically disenfranchised group. Unlike their father, remembered mainly for the manner of his death, they are remembered for resilience and the ability to ask, publicly and persistently, for what they need -- showing us that reacting to real needs as they arise, and demanding change in public, is how the status quo actually changes.

Moshe tasks Yehoshua with leading the people into Israel, and the Parsha ends with a full accounting of the additional offerings for Shabbat, Rosh Chodesh, and each festival of the year.

Shabbat Shalom!

The Zealot

July 7, 2023

This weeks Parsha is Pinchas, it is a difficult Parsha that deals with conflict between Jews and the influence of Midianites. It also includes laws regarding the division of land among the tribes and within a family. Moshe officially appoints Yehoshua as leader after his death and God commands israel to offer extra sacrifices on Shabbat and Yom Tov.

Pinchas is a difficult figure, he is rewarded for publicly murdering a head of tribe who was making a skeptikal of his affair with his Midianite paramour. God blesses him with a covenant of peace and priesthood. This is a fitting blessing that reminds us to value peace overall.

A census is conducted and 601,730 men between twenty and sixty were counted. Moshe is instructed on how to divide land between tribes and within families. The daughters of Tzelaphchad appeal to Moshe. As daughters of a man with no son they had nolrgal heir from their generation. Moshe heard their plight and incorporated their exception into the Torahs laws of inheritance.

Moshe tasks Joshua with leading the Jews into Israel. The Parsha ends with a list of all the Musaf offerings brought on Shabbat, Rosh Chodesh (first of the month), and the festivals of Passover, Shavuot, Rosh Hashana, Yom Kippur, Sukkot and Shemini Atzeret.

Shabbat Shalom!

Parshat

Matot-Masei

Refuge and Reconciliation

July 13, 2018

This week’s parsha introduces the concept of the cities of refuge. These 6 cities are designated as safe havens for those guilty of manslaughter that are fleeing from the relatives of the deceased. Law allows for immediate relatives of the victim of an accidental killing to pursue the killer until he reaches these cities, at which point he is once again protected by law. The only way for the manslaughter ensure that he is not murdered in retaliation is for him to remain in the city of refuge until the death of the Kohen Gadol.

Many facets of these laws are not easily understood. First, by establishing these cities the courts are effectively sanctioning extrajudicial manhunts by grieving families. Second, why does the Kohen Gadol’s death so directly impact the fate of manslaughters? The relationship between the Kohen Gadol and the manslaughters became so intimate due to this connection, that the Talmud explains how the mother of the Kohen Gadol would deliver food to the cities of refuge in hopes of preventing the enclave from praying for the death of her son!

In response to the second question, we see two main answers. The first is brought in the Talmud. “A venerable old scholar said, I heard an explanation at one of the sessional lectures of Rava, that the High Priest should have implored divine grace for the generation, which he failed to do.” Here, we are taught that the Kohen Gadol is responsible not only for the explainable preventable mistakes of his community, rather he is responsible for divine grace as well which would have prevented such awful accidents from happening.

The Rambam offers another explanation entirely. “A person who killed another person unknowingly must go into exile because the anger of “the avenger of the blood” cools down while the cause of the mischief is out of sight. The chance of returning from the exile depends on the death of the High Priest, the most honoured of men, and the friend of all Israel. By his death the relative of the slain person becomes reconciled; for it is a natural phenomenon that we find consolation in our misfortune when the same misfortune or a greater one has befallen another person. Amongst us no death causes more grief than that of the High Priest.” This explanation entirely neglects the Kohen Gadol’s responsibility for the spiritual circumstances that allowed for a calamity to occur. Instead, the Rambam offers a natural explanation of consolation and safety. As we understand too well from the national tragedies we have lived through, people come together and reconcile in an unparalleled way during times of grief. The Rambam seems to indicate that this force is so strong it can pave the way for forgiveness even between a killer and the surviving family.

These two explanations seem to have little to do with one another. While the Talmud focuses on a spiritual reality that impacts physical events, the Rambam seems to offer a brief lesson in psychology. To understand how these relate, we simply have to consider each from a different perspective of grief. When any death touches a family, it feels preordained and supernatural. For relatives whose grieve for accidental death, it can feel as though their loved one was taken for no reason. It can feel like they were robbed of their rightful conclusion to their spiritual journey and it was instead cut short. With these people, the Talmud empathizes. It validates their feelings and passes the blame away from the deceased.

Meanwhile, the killer himself had no intention of taking life away from this innocent man. The death undoubtedly weighs heavily on his conscience and is source of pain and grief to him as well. With the killer, the Rambam empathizes. His explanation acknowledges the very real hurt that was caused by the killer while contextualizing it and allowing room for time and history to mend.

Through these explanations we can answer our first question as well. The laws about Cities of Refuge aren’t designed to allow or prohibit behavior. We are not trying to reprogram the human being that feels loss, anger and pain. Our laws are designed to make room for the full human experience. When an accidental loss occurs our legal system empathizes with all parties, the result of which is a City of Refuge connected to the High Priest.

Moo · July 31, 2019

This week's Parsha, Matot-Masei, discusses the way in which the land was split up between the twelve tribes. Gad and Reuben owned many cattle and therefore asked for land outside Israel, specifically on the eastern bank of the Jordan.

This request angered Moshe and he discouraged the entire nation from settling outside of Israel. In order to compromise, the tribes of Reuben and Gad decided to leave their families and cattle behind in fortified cities, and all their men would proceed into Israel with the other tribes and contribute to the conquest of the land.

After the land was conquered, the men from these tribes joined their families and cattle again on the other side of the Jordan. Moshe accepted the offer from Gad and Reuben, and the two tribes along with half of Menashe settled on the eastern bank of the Jordan.

The rest of the land of Israel was divided among the remaining nine and a half tribes by lottery. G-d appointed a representative from each tribe to further divide the given land between the tribes' members. Gad and Reuben's decision to live outside of Israel was met with anger from Moshe similar to the anger he expressed when hitting the rock which caused G-d to forbid Moshe to lead the Jewish people into the Land of Israel.

Why was Moshe not punished for his strong response to the tribes of Gad and Reuben? Rav Kook explains that Moshe's anger in this situation was justified. We learn from this that even if there is a reasonable argument for settling outside of the Holy Land, discouraging the Jewish people from making aliyah is following in the footsteps as the infamous spies.

While Rav Kook explains that Jews should aim to live their lives in Israel, a Tosafot in Gemara (Menachot 37a) quotes a Midrash which gives us another outlook on our roots and connection as Jews.

The Midrash goes as follows: a man with two heads married a woman with one head and they bore both a one-headed and two-headed child. When the children's father died, the two-headed child came before King Shlomo and demanded a double portion of the inheritance.

The wisest of all men covered one of the heads of the two-headed son and began pouring boiling water over his other head. When the covered head bellowed in pain, it was evident that the two heads were part of one person.

This story suggests a metaphor for the Jewish people that we have a number of "heads". Each head has developed its own way of thinking which detaches them from the other Jews. While on the surface this may seem true, once "boiling water" is poured over a Jewish community, instinctively, Jews from around the world will feel the pain and sorrow of their fellow Jews.

Spending a week in Poland and six weeks in Israel I have seen how true this is. In Poland visiting Auschwitz, Birkenau, and Majdanek I felt the "boiling water" and the pain of the 6 million Jews who were murdered. A week later when I arrived in Israel, I felt the joy and love of a country filled with Jews, connected by our religion, culture, and language. Israel is a melting pot of Jews from everywhere, and there is a place for any Jew. Every city in Israel has its own culture, as it did when the tribes were divided, but somehow we are all connected and linked. Being a Jew is a brother and sisterhood, and it is especially felt in the Holy Land.

Aspiring for Redemption

Yosef · July 29, 2022

The Ibn Ezra, about the mitzvah not to covet your friend's possessions, famously explains the tactic to avoid jealousy with a parable to a peasant and a princess. The peasant, in his overalls and boots, covered in mud and grime, living in a straw hut, would never even dream of marrying the King's daughter. As her entourage passes through his city, replete with a magnificent marching band, beautiful stallions, and royal chariots, he feels no twinge of longing to have her hand in marriage. Why? Because the princess is so far removed from the peasant and such a shidduch is so far from the realm of reality that his mind doesn't even travel down that route – it is simply unthinkable.

So too, says the Ibn Ezra, when we see something that our friend has, we have to realize that it has absolutely nothing to do with us. If we are decreed to have that thing we will have it and if not we will not, and our friends' possessions are completely and totally out of reach. With such an understanding, we, just as the peasant and the princess, will be rid of any and all feelings of jealousy.

This is a well-known concept and something which logically makes perfect sense. Jealousy is truly not something based on rational thinking, and we all know that it gets us nothing but heartache. Yet still, if we are driving a 20-year-old clunker that has been at the mechanic every other week and we suddenly see our neighbor driving a sparklingly new and beautiful car, it is tremendously difficult to put the above parable to work and avoid any tinge of jealousy.

However, for some reason, when it comes to spiritual matters, where we are supposed to covet the virtues that we see in others, we are all very quick to dismiss our friends' achievements as beyond us. If our friend is incredibly kind, we immediately assert that he is a tzadik by nature. If he is more learned then he is simply a genius. If he is more diligent then he is a machine. The bottom line is, that we are very content to define our friends as the princess and ourselves as the pauper, without the slightest twinge of envy, in flawless execution of the Ibn Ezra's teaching.

Rav Aharon Kotler zt"l explains that this complacency and lack of ambition in matters of spirituality is one of the primary reasons that we have such a hard time mourning the destruction of the Beit Hamikdash.

When the Beit Hamikdash stood, every Jew lived with a pristinely clean slate. If one stumbled into some sin, he would immediately bring a korban (sacrifice) and be cleansed. Our relationship with Hashem was tangibly real, as we witnessed constant miracles and Hashem spoke to us through his prophets. Our Torah learning was with a level of understanding and retention that we cannot begin to fathom. And the list goes on and on. [As an aside, for one who wishes to gain a more complete picture of the differences between then and now, I would highly suggest going through the section of the Chazan's Repetition for Yom Kippur immediately following 'Mar'eh Kohen'.]

That was in the times of the Mikdash. But today, we are sullied beyond belief, buried under years and even generations of sin. When we try to daven and connect with Hashem, we often feel so distant that we have trouble even concentrating on the words. We flounder without knowing exactly what we are meant to do in the world, never hearing the clarion call of the prophets. We struggle mightily to make headway in our learning, and after hours of exertion we cannot reach the toes of even our recent predecessors.

We are so hampered and limited in every facet of spiritual achievement precisely because we are in exile, so how could we not mourn our loss? The answer is complacency. We have grown so accustomed to our challenges and we are so ridiculously content with our predicament, that we don't even wish for more.

To change this outlook will certainly not be easy, but in truth this is a lifeline for all of us who struggle to feel a sense of mourning.

Allow me to explain. Another reason that we have such a hard time time mourning is because we never experienced the Beit Hamikdash. We are like a child who's father passed away before he was ever born. He doesn't have any knowledge of who his father was, so he naturally cannot connect to the pain of his loss. We are likewise born into the exile, and not just us but our parents and grandparents for so many generations. So, naturally, we have a terribly difficult time relating to our loss.

Our spiritual struggles, however, are no strangers to us. If we involve ourselves even minimally in self-improvement and spiritual pursuits, then we are keenly aware of our shortcomings. We can certainly understand and relate to these hardships.

As such, it should be much easier for us to work on our spiritual ambitions, and to wish and pine for the ability to achieve those ambitions. We can then use this as a springboard to mourn the entirety of our loss, and ultimately, through our tears, to merit the rebuilding of the Beit Hamikdash and our final redemption.

Good Shabbos, Yosef

Sefer Devarim

The Book of Deuteronomy

Festival

Three Weeks / Tisha B'Av

Wake Up

Yosef · July 10, 2020

This week, we began the 'three weeks' of mourning for the Beis Hamikdash. These begin with the fast of Shiva Asar B'Tamuz, and conclude with the fast of Tisha B'Av. Throughout this period, there are many rabbinically ordained practices of mourning; creating the feeling that the loss of Beis Hamikdash is as painful to us as the loss of a close relative. Most of these laws are ascending in their severity as we draw closer to Tisha B'Av, with the lowest level for the remainder of the month of Tamuz, a higher level with the onset of the month of Av, yet another level with the week of Tisha B'Av, and culminating with the fast and heightened affliction of Tisha B'Av itself. Curiously though, there is one exception to the rule of a gradual increase; we start off the very first day with a fast although no fasting is required for the rest of the interim. What is the meaning of this break in the order of things?

Let's face it. Mourning the Beis Hamikdash is perhaps one of the hardest things for us American Jews to properly perform in the entire Jewish calendar. As hard as we try, the loss of the Beis Hamikdash is just not something we easily relate to. It seems so distant and so far from reality.

I believe one of the reasons we find this so difficult is because we are so comfortable here in 'exile'. Throughout our entire sojourn amongst the nations we have been met with hardship, persecution, and poverty. These last few decades in America have truly been a time of blessing and prosperity, not only in a material sense but also in our ability to freely practice our Judaism. And yet, this greatest of blessings carries with it a very difficult test – the test of complacency.

Our ancestors had no problem relating to the pain of exile; they felt it keenly with the hunger in their stomachs and the hatred of their neighbors. It was clear as day that Poland, Turkey, Russia, and Egypt, were not our homes, and so our fathers longed with all their hearts for Moshiach and the rebuilding of the Beis Hamikdash.

Not so here in America. We may know intellectually that as Jews we belong in Israel of old, but it is very far from our hearts. In our state of security, affluence, and societal acceptance, we feel much too comfortable and much too at home in America. We feel as if we belong, and this is a terrible mistake. Yes, we should feel a tremendous amount of gratitude to America, and yes we should care and even pray for the welfare of the the country, but it is not our home and not our place. We belong in a much higher plane; in a society governed by G-d Himself and among a people driven exclusively towards spirituality. If we don't feel this way and we don't long for more, then we are in need of a real wake-up call.

That wake-up call is what our sages intended by all the customs of the three weeks. We begin with minor things such as not listening to music and not cutting our hair. These disrupt our normal way of things to a certain degree, but we can easily shrug them off and go on with life. So then we step it up a notch; during the nine days of Av we cannot bathe in hot water, wear freshly laundered clothing, or eat meat. These are pretty hard to ignore and really demand a certain amount of our attention, but they still do not encompass the entire day and one could go about most of his days without thought about what is going on. But on Tisha B'Av even the most callous and unthinking individual is compelled to realize that something is wrong. He cannot eat, drink, sleep in his normal way, sit on a chair, wear shoes, and so on. This puts a complete halt on life as usual and all but forces us to wake up, at least for the day.

I think that perhaps this is the purpose of starting the three weeks with a fast. Our sages wanted to shake us form our reverie and disrupt things in a way that couldn't be missed right from the get-go. Once we're awake from the fast of Shiva Asar B'Tamuz, we can then ease into things with gradually more severe practices, while still taking their message.

It would seem that this year, besides for our sages enactments, we are being given a healthy dose of divine assistance in this area. Hashem has made it easier than ever to realize that all is not well, that we do not belong here, and that there is so much more to long for.

First, Hashem brought every world power to its knees with a tiny virus which has forced us to realize that we don't have things under control. He saw to it that so many things that we take for granted were suddenly cast into doubt. Can we get whatever we need? Can we go where we want? Can we rely on our medical expertise? Questions which we might have answered in the affirmative are suddenly greeted with a very loud 'No!'

And then we experienced the riots. Here in Los Angeles we happened to have box seats on the event, so my experience may be a little more extreme then your memories, but the bottom line is the same. On May 30th, just after Shavuos, I sat in my house listening to absolute pandemonium outside. Smoke filled the air, explosions sounded rapidly, cars wizzed by, sirens and helicopters were everywhere. I tried leaving my house to daven Maariv a block away – but I quickly ran back inside as something exploded nearby. In the morning we saw the damage everywhere. Nearly every store for miles had been smashed and looted, and the city had a curfew for the rest of the week.

Who would have thought that here in America I would be scared to walk out my front door? We're so used to our security and comfort, but now that was stripped from us. We suddenly see clearly that we are not safe and secure here; that all is not well.

How much more do we need to wake us up? How much will it take to shake us from our complacency?

Let's not wait any longer, but take the medicine and use the opportunity to engrave in our hearts the message of the three weeks. Let us feel the gaping hole in our existence, truly pray and hope for redemption, and ultimately merit our final homecoming.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Our Lifesource

Yosef · July 2, 2021

This week was the first of the 'Three Weeks', during which we mourn the destruction of the Beit Hamikdash and the exile which has now stretched on for thousands of years. Each year, I grapple with the task of properly feeling the sense of pain, loss, and longing which ought to be felt by every Jew. And every year it evades me...

Unfortunately, I sense that I am not alone with this shortcoming – it seems to be a universal problem, albeit a problem that is only a generation or two old.

Our sages tell us (Taanit 30b) that anyone who mourns Jerusalem will merit to see her joy (with the redemption), and anyone who does not mourn Jerusalem will not merit to see her joy. So when we wonder why it's taking so very long for Mashiach to come, we really must direct the question inwards and ask ourselves why it's taking us so long to mourn for our loss. Our lack of feeling is not a problem which we can simply make peace with; it is something we must rectify if we ever want to dig ourselves out of the pit into which we have fallen. We must do everything in our power to feel, to mourn, to long, and to cry.

The tabernacle was called the Mishkan, and the Midrash explains that this is a form of the Hebrew word mashkon, which refers to a collateral taken in place of a debt. When the Jewish people strayed from the path of Hashem, we racked up a heavy debt – one that could only have been paid with a destruction too great to speak of. Instead of destroying the people, Hashem destroyed the tabernacle, and the *mishkan *thus served as the collateral for our people, as Hashem took it in our stead.

The question is that such a collateral seems to run contrary to Torah law, which delineates exactly what a debtor is and isn't permitted to take as collateral. The Torah states that one may not take anything which the borrower relies on for his livelihood. If he is a farmer you can't take his cow, if he's a miller you can't take his mill, and if he's an Uber driver you can't take his car.

When the Beit Hamikdash stood, the life of every Jew centered around it. Three times a year, our entire people made a pilgrimage to the site to reconnect and draw spiritual sustenance for the rest of the year. On a daily basis, the priests were busy all day performing their service on behalf of the entire people. If anyone accidentally sinned, he would immediately go to the Beit Hamikdash to repent, bring an offering, and walk away cleansed.

With the destruction of the Beit Hamikdash, we lost our spiritual livelihood. Since that time, we have so much trouble connecting to Hashem, performing mitzvos, avoiding sin, praying, learning, and everything in between. How could Hashem take such a central source of life as collateral – wasn't it at least as essential as the farmer's cow?

The answer again places the question squarely on our shoulders. It is true that a debtor may not take one's source of livelihood from him, but if the person himself doesn't consider it essential that is a different story. The debtor doesn't need to convince him of its necessity.

So too, if we would have perceived the Beit Hamikdash as something we could not live without, Hashem would never have taken it from us. But if we felt that it was nothing more than extra credit, if we felt like we could get on fine without it, then it was within our power to declare it unnecessary to us and to offer it for collection.

But it isn't too late. If we would sit down today and make a serious reckoning of our situation and what we are missing, we could again declare it essential and demand it back. So let's think just how short we fall in our service of Hashem, just how hampered we are in our Torah learning, and just how hard it is for us to overcome our evil inclination. Let us realize that almost all of our hardships are direct ramifications of the exile, and how vital the Beit Hamikdash really is to our lives. And when we truly feel that such a life is not a life and that we just cannot go on like this, let us turn to Hashem and ask for our collateral back.

We just cannot live without it.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Why We Cry

Yosef · 2023

When the Israeli Defense Force regained the Old City of Jerusalem and the Western Wall, it was an incredibly emotional moment. Many soldiers who had fought in the liberation laid their heads on the Wall and wept. One soldier, Dani, very far removed from Jewish observance, watched in silence at first -- then broke down sobbing louder than anyone. When a friend asked why he was crying, Dani answered, "I am crying because I don't know what there is to cry for."

These are the "three weeks" leading to the fast of Tisha B'Av, the saddest day of the year, when we mourn the destruction of the Beit Hamikdash, the exile of our people, and the distance between us and the Divine Presence. Once, this mourning pierced every heart; today, we fast but count down the hours to a glass of orange juice, sit on the floor but complain of discomfort, recite the Kinot but rarely feel a genuine tear.

One reason for this estrangement is that we simply never knew any other existence -- we never felt the ecstasy of intimacy with G-d in the Beit Hamikdash, never witnessed its open miracles, so we lack any frame of reference for what was lost, much like a person born blind cannot truly grasp what color is, however vividly it's described to him.

So we must try mightily to glean whatever sliver of understanding we can -- and when, inevitably, we fail to fully comprehend the scale of the loss, let us at least recognize the desperate situation that failure reveals. And let us cry because we don't know what there is to cry for.

Good Shabbos, and may we merit celebrating this coming Tisha B'Av with the rebuilding of the Beit Hamikdash, Yosef

Our Father’s Lashes

Yosef · July 21, 2023

There once was a prince, the only son of a great and mighty monarch, who began to stray from the good ways of his kindly father. In his youth, his rebelliousness was relatively harmless, but as he grew the problems grew with him. His father warned him many a time, but to no avail. Left without a choice, the king threatened to beat the prince if he would not change his ways. Unfortunately, the threat wasn’t good enough and soon the king had no choice but to order lashes upon his beloved son’s back. The king begged and pleaded with his son not to make him repeat the order, and prayed that this one-time measure should succeed in saving the future of his child. But still, the prince wouldn’t break. He continued in his ways, and was beaten again. Time after time, the story repeated itself; the beating, the prayers and entreaties, and the subsequent brazenness on behalf of the prince, who refused to right his wrongs. Once, after yet another flagrancy, the king took a merciful look at his son. The prince, once strong and handsome, was now black and blue from the beatings, thin, pale, and limping. The king realized that his son wouldn’t be able to survive another beating, but at the same time he couldn’t allow the misdeeds to go unpunished. He couldn’t just allow his son to go lost.

After some thought, the king had an idea. If his dear child couldn’t take the beatings, then the king would bear the lashes on his own back. The prince would watch the beating, knowing that those blows were meant for himself, but the king would accept them out of love for his son. In this way, the king hoped, his son would finally capitulate, unable to cause such suffering to his great father…

Rav Shimshon Pincus explains that this is a parable to our present-day exile of the twenty-first century. In the days of old, the children of Israel, the only son of Hashem, began to stray. He warned us and foretold of the punishments which would come upon us if we wouldn’t correct our ways. He sent us leaders and prophets which pleaded on His behalf that we repent, but to no avail. Finally, we forced Hashem’s hand and He ordered us to be beaten and punished, hoping to save us from going lost. The beatings we suffered were harsh and painful, much worse than we can possibly conjure up in our imagination. The destruction of the Beit Hamikdash with rivers of Jewish blood. The expulsions and libels, the Inquisition, the Cossacks, the crusades, and the holocaust. And these are just a drop in the ocean.

We suffered them all, and perhaps we made slight amendments, but ultimately, we stood brazen and bold – unwilling to change.

Today, we live lives of freedom and prosperity. Things aren’t perfect, but we are a very far way from the tortures and torment which our people have lived through. When we read stories of the holocaust or the inquisition, we can barely muster the courage to read on, and we certainly can’t imagine being able to withstand such trials. We wouldn’t last for a minute in the face of what our ancestors stood up to.

We are the prince who, after generations of beatings, can no longer survive the blows. We are weak, tired, and worn; scarcely mustering up the strength to withstand our small tests.

Our loving Father cannot hit us any more, because one more blow would finish us off. But He also cannot allow us to stray without consequence. So, He has taken the whip off of our backs and placed it, so to speak, upon His own. Instead of striking us with the Nazi inferno, He strikes himself with the millions of Jews who don’t even know His name. And instead of striking us with the tortures of the Inquisition, he strikes himself with so many of his children who have gone lost amongst the nations.

The question is how we are going to react. Will we breathe a sigh of relief that we are no longer suffering and continue in our ways? Will we sit by in comfort as our Father suffers for our deeds and allow Him to continue bearing the brunt of our actions?

Or will we finally say enough is enough? Will we finally decide that we cannot be the cause of our Father’s pain, and thereby commit to better ourselves?

Will we be the ones to say, “Daddy, take the rod off Your holy shoulders – we’re coming home now.”

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Parshat

Devarim

Lessons on Leadership

Avi · July 20, 2018

On the first day of the month of Shevat, five weeks before Moshe’s passing on, the nation is assembled before their elderly judge and leader. Moshe begins the repetition of the Torah and gazes across the desert, recounting the events that have led them all here. The forty year traveling period was full of shortcomings and iniquities. Moses recounts that he needed help judging the nation and could not do it alone. Moshe reminds the people that they passed through a great and fearsome desert but not without the trial of the spies whose complaints misled and scared the nation causing the generation of the desert to die. Only their children had the opportunity to enter the holy land. “Also against me,” Moshe admits, “God was angry for your sake,” saying, ”you too shall not go in.” Moshe then recounts the nations who would not let us pass through their land. Moshe tells us of his successor Joshua and urges us to not be afraid - Hashem will fight with us.

Rewriting the past and giving rebuke is heard way too often from those in positions of power. When a politicians agenda cannot come to fruition, rather than owning up to it he may rewrite his initial commitment, saying his agenda was different from the beginning. This opens the door to blame shifting and gives a chance for all stakeholders to reconsider the ground on which they stand. This chaotic unhealthy leadership is so directly rooted in self esteem and general approval ratings.

Not so with Moshe. At the time of the spies, Moses said, “the idea was good in my eyes.” Then we were decreed to die in the desert because of how the spies reported the land. Now after the fact, Moses still says, “the idea was good in my eyes.” Isn't this political suicide! We do not see this ownership today because leaders are not invested in the people. In truth, Aa leader’s core job is learning to trust his followers by being honest to them when assessing the road ahead. Politicians who go back on their word forget that admitting that their idea was bad shows conviction, not weakness. While Moshe does take his time to rebuke, it is measured out and well directed. The verse shares that Moses rebuked the Jewish people after he had smitten Sichon, king of the Amorites and Og, king of Bashan. To find conviction in rebuke, the Lubavitcher Rebbe teaches to make a physical offering or to serve the one you wish to rebuke. Joshua, Himself one of the spies, is Moshe’s closest disciple. The talmud says that Moshe is like the sun and Joshua like the moon, absorbing and reflecting Moses’s brilliant rebuke through all hours of the night.

Shabbat Shalom, Avi

Why We Cry

Yosef · July 24, 2020

When the Israeli Defense Force regained the power of the Old City of Jerusalem and the Western Wall, it was an incredibly emotional moment. Many of the soldiers who had fought in the actual liberation laid their heads on the wall and began to weep. Standing there was one soldier, Dani, who was very far removed from Judaism. For a minute he watched his comrades in silence, unmoved by the significance of this religious landmark. But suddenly he too broke down, sobbing bitterly, until his cries rose above those of his religious companions. Dani's friend knew that he lacked any connection to Judaism and asked with surprise, 'Why are you crying?' To which came Dani's profoundly tragic response, 'I am crying because I don't know what there is to cry for.'

This week there will be the fast of Tisha B'av, the saddest day of the year. It is on this day that we mourn the destruction of the Beit Hamikdash, the exile of our people from the Holy Land, and the distance which stands between us and the Divine Presence. In shuls and homes throughout the world, we will sit on the floor, remove our shoes, and recite some of the most haunting and sorrowful liturgy ever written.

In times not long gone by, the mourning of Tisha B'av was felt deeply by every member of our people. Tears flowed freely from every eye, and our bereavement pierced every heart even more sharply than the death of a loved one.

And yet today, our hearts of stone are all but impervious to the calamity which has befallen our people. We fast, but we count down the hours until we can grab that glass of orange juice. We sit on the floor, but we moan and groan from discomfort and celebrate when we finally are able to sit back up on our couch. We recite the Kinot, but the words seem far-away and empty, rarely eliciting a lone teardrop from a wary eye.

One of the reasons that we are so estranged from the proper spirit of Tisha B'av is the fact that we never knew any other existence. We never experienced the ecstasy of such an intimate connection to G-d. We never felt the purity of one who had brought an offering and cleansed his past misdeeds. And we never witnessed the open miracles which abounded in the Beit Hamikdash.

Bottom line, we have no idea what it is that we are missing.

We are akin to a child who is born without any sense of sight. If we would try to explain to him what a colorful, vibrant, and brilliant world there is, he would never understand. He simply lacks any frame of reference for such things. So are we, born into a world of spiritual darkness. We can talk all day about the times of old, but a true and accurate perception of such loftiness is as unimaginable to us as color is to the blind man.

So, of course, we must try mightily to glean whatever sliver of understanding we can from all the wealth of information on the subject. But when, inevitably, we fail to comprehend, let us realize the desperate situation we are in. And let us cry because we don't know what there is to cry for.

Good Shabbos and may we merit celebrating this Tisha B'av with the rebuilding of the Beit Hamikdash,

Yosef

The Covenant

Noah · August 5, 2022

This week we start the final book of the torah, Devarim. Originally named "Mishneh Torah" or the repetition of the law it contains a seemingly odd mix of both new and repeated laws, historical recollection and anticipation for entering the land of Israel. Despite this varied mix of subject matter, Devarim has a form that is uniquely concrete as proven in the general dispute about the chronological order of the Torah. While from Bereshit through Bamidbar there are ongoing debates over the significance of juxtaposions of events as they may not have occurred in the sequence described, everyone agrees that in Devarim "Homiletic interpretations from juxtaposed verses" are valid. This unanimity suggest the structure of Devarim is of great significance.

Rabbi Saks explains that recent archeological discoveries from before the time of Avraham have uncovered covenants between rival nations in the region. These treaties adhere to a consistent template comprised of 6 parts. Rabbi Saks explains the structure of these covenants,

[1] They began with a preamble, establishing the identity of the person or power initiating the covenant. This was followed by [2] a historical prologue, reviewing the history of the relationship between the two parties to the covenant. Then came [3] the provisions of the covenant itself, the stipulations, which were often stated in two forms, [a] general principles, and [b] detailed provisions. There then followed [4] a provision for the covenant to be deposited in a sacred place, and read on a regular basis. Next came [5] the sanctions associated with the covenant, namely the blessings that would follow if it was adhered to, and the curses that would occur if it is broken. Lastly, there is [6] a statement of the witnesses to the agreement -- usually the gods of the nations involved. The entire book of Devarim is structured as an extended covenant, on precisely these lines.

Rabbi Saks then continues to map each of the section of Devarim into this ancient arrangement:

  1. Preamble

Dev. 1:1-1:5

Announces place, time and person initiating the covenant that follows: Moses on behalf of God.

  1. Historical prologue

Dev.1:6-4:49

Moses recapitulates the history that has brought them to where they are, mostly recalling the events described in the book of Bamidbar

  1. Stipulations

[a] chs. 5-11 [b] chs. 12-26

[a] general provisions: Ten Commandments, Shema, etc. Recapitulation of events surrounding the making of the covenant at Sinai. [b] specific provisions: the details of the law, with special reference to how they are to be carried out by the people as a whole in the land of Israel.

  1. Deposition and regular reading

27, 31

The law to be inscribed on stone (stele) at Mount Ebal; the Torah written by Moses and placed in the ark; to be read in public at a national assembly by the king every seven years.

  1. Sanctions: the blessings and the curses

28

Ch. 28 states the blessings and curses; 29-30 the actual covenant renewal, together with a statement that even if the people break the covenant and the curses come to pass, return, teshuvah, is still possible.

  1. Witnesses

Dev. 30:19 -- 32:1

"Heaven and earth" (Dev. 4:26, Dev. 30:19, Dev. 31:28, 32:1), "This song" (Dev. 31:19)

What emerges from this analysis is an understanding of Devarim as an expertly crafted legal document with a couple monumental changes that define the covenant we have with God. The first such modification is that it is a treaty between a nation and God, the first time this political formula was used for a religious purpose.

The next signficant departure from the covenants before it is that all of Israel is included in the agreement. Similar contactual relationships were traditional negotiated and agreed to by the ruler alone. In Devarim we are reminded that all of Israel is included in this story and party to this arrangement.

As Rabbi Saks explains, "This is covenant politics, a unique form of political structure based not on a hierarchy of power but on a shared sense of history and destiny. It is a moral politics, dedicated to creating a just and gracious society that honours the dignity of all, especially the downtrodden, the poor, the powerless and the marginal: the widow, the orphan and the stranger."

As we read these parshiot and explore this covenant this structure can help guide our understanding and deepen our appreciation for the final book of the Torah.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Parshat

Vaetchanan

Eternity

Yosef · July 27, 2018

This Friday marks the first yahrtzeit of our beloved Uncle Yonatan. It is hard to believe that it's been a full year since he took leave of us, as his memory is so real and vivid. For many of us the wound is still raw, but more importantly, the memories are still very much alive.

Much was said at the funeral about the fact that it took place on the festive day of Tu B'Av, the fifteenth day of the month of Av. Also notable is the fact that Uncle passed away in the week of Shabbos Nachamu, or the Shabbos of Consolation. And, as Uncle always taught us, it would serve us well to understand how his passing corresponded to this week's Parsha, Va'Etchanan.

Let's start by understanding this Shabbos of consolation. The last few weeks have been the saddest in the Jewish calendar. Beginning with the 17th of Tamuz and culminating with the 9th of Av, this is the time of intense mourning for the destruction of the Temple. But on the Shabbos right after Tisha B'Av, we make an about-face and it is suddenly a time of consolation. What is the consolation? Aren't we still here in exile just as before?

The Chasam Sofer (1762–1839) explains that the very fact that we are still mourning the Temple's destruction is the biggest reason for consolation. If we would have stopped mourning and it would have passed from our memories, then it would essentially be lost forever, never to be rebuilt. The fact the we are still mourning, some two-thousand years later, means that the temple and what it stood for lives on in all of us. Our dedication to its ideals and lessons gives it eternal life and guarantees that one day it will again stand tall.

Uncle may no longer be with us in physical form, but his memory and all that he gave us is etched forever in our hearts and minds. The indelible mark which he made upon us and on the world gives him eternal life. Perhaps this is the reason his memory is so vivid to us – because we continue to embody his ways. Just like the temple lives on, so does he live on.

Now I'd like to address Tu B'Av, and why I see it being so appropriate as the day Uncle returned his soul to his Creator. You see, the 15th of Av is a day which is supposed to mark a renewed dedication to Torah study. Because the nights, which are considered the main time for study, begin to become longer from here on out, this is the time when everyone is expected to redouble their efforts in Torah learning. Uncle loved the Torah with his whole heart and soul. There was nothing he wanted more than to be free to study Torah day and night without any other responsibilities. Alas, between work and all the other things which one must attend to, he didn't have nearly as much time as he would have wanted. We are taught that, in the world to come, the righteous sit together with God in the heavenly Yeshiva, plumbing the depths of the Torah. On Tu B'Av, Uncle Yonatan was finally able to ascend to the heavenly Yeshiva, where he is able to constantly rejoice in the study of Torah, finally unhindered by all the distractions which are the lot of mortal men.

This means that not only here does he live on in a more figurative sense within the hearts of those who loved him, but in the next world he lives on in a very literal sense. There he has finally achieved the goal towards which he strove for all his life – closeness to God and to His Torah. There he exists in a plane of spiritual ecstasy doing what he loved most.

And this ties us back to our Parsha. The verse says (4:4) “And you who cleave to the Almighty are all living today.” Our deeper sources tell us that this does not only refer to those who were then alive whom Moshe was talking to, but rather it is a sweeping statement that applies for all time. Those who are connected to God, the omnipotent Source of all life, are connected to eternal life. By attaching to The Source, mortal man can lift himself above his mortality and indeed live forever.

So on Uncle's yahtzeit, let us understand and take consolation in the fact that Uncle lives on, both here in our hearts and minds, and in the next world, as one connected to the divine. And let us also learn the lesson of eternity, learning how we too can live forever by teaching and giving to others, as well as connecting ourselves to God.

Have a wonderful Shabbos of Consolation,

Yosef

Passing the Reigns

August 16, 2019

"Let me cross over," Moshe implored God as the people of Israel camped on the eastern bank of the Jordan, "and let me see the good Land." But God refused. Moshe, who had taken the people out of Egypt, who had climbed Mount Sinai to receive the Torah for them and returned to teach them the word of God, who for forty years tended to their hungers and thirsts, their doubts and complaints and rebellions—Moshe was to die and be buried in the plains of Moab, and his disciple, Yehoshua, was to lead a new generation into the Promised Land.

But upon closer examination, Moshe prayer was at least partially answered. Moses asked to cross over and to see the Land; God refused the first part of the request and granted the second. "Ascend to the top of the summit," God said to Moshe, ". . . and see it with your eyes; for you shall not cross this Jordan."

This week's Parsha contains the heartwrenching culmination of the saga of the greatest leader we have ever had. This confounding conclusion and its accompanying teachings reveal the essence of the Moshe Rabbenu. Why was he barred from leading the nation he built into the next phase of their existence? Why did it require God's interference to end his imploring? Why did we have to lose the leader we loved?

The simple answer is that Moshe sinned by failing to obey God's instructions. When the people thirsted for water, God told Moshe to speak to a certain rock and it would bring forth its waters. Instead, Moshe struck the rock. As a result, God informed him that both he and Aaron would not be allowed entry into the Land but would perish in the desert. (See Numbers 20:1-13.)

This event revealed a facet of Moshe's leadership that was incompatible with the next chapter of the Jewish nation. Moshe carried the nation from its birth to this point. Just as an infant cannot understand language and must be physically reprimanded when endangering itself, so was Moshe's approach to Israel's spirituals follies. Similarly, Moshe did not allow Israel to face the consequences of its actions. When we sinned he shouldered the burden and prayed for us. When we complained he provided. Part of this leadership style was rooted in Moshe's selflessness. His direct unfiltered connection to God could make him aloof and unrelatable, hence Aaron's role as a more human interface, but this was the only person that could handle Egypt, Mount Sinai and 40 years of wandering. He was the only vessel pure enough to both receive and transmit the Torah to the generations that would follow. Only the complete absence of id exhibited by Moshe could allow enough room for a nation to be conceived and delivered.

The next stage of Israel's development would necessitate a leader that was accessible and empathetic to the mistakes we would surely make, leaving the nest and settling the land. For Moshe to be that leader he would have to dial down the intense relationship with God he had devoted his life to forging. With this understanding, God's single command to Moshe after his protracted appeal makes much more sense. "It is much to you; do not continue speaking to Me about this matter further" (Deut. 3:26).

Yehoshua's leadership is compared to the moon and Moshe's to the sun. Moshe's brilliance will always be the source of our connection but other leaders will arise to reflect it in ways that are practical and applicable. Yehoshua will be tasked with sustaining the flame lit by Moshe and ensuring it ignites another generation to serve God.

On Uncle's 2nd Yahrzeit it is clear that he was our Moshe. Through his unique intensity and selflessness, he brought us the Torah. He carried us through the beginnings of our religious development and brought each of his children to the edge of their promised lands. Like Moshe he got to see it with his eyes, the families being brought up with his influence, the Torah homes established because of his passion. And like Moshe, he didn't get to enter the land. He was denied a chance to see the grandchildren living his legacy come to bar and bat mitzvah. He never saw all the fruits of his labor.

But in his absence, we are each accountable to be Yehoshua. To lead our respective families by example and reflect Uncle's light onto the next generation.
