While we may never reach the same spiritual and intellectual heights he did, we have the opportunity to build sustainable futures on the Torah he gave.

Shabbat Shalom

Why We Cry

Cali · July 31, 2020

This year, we have all seen loss and devastation. There is a global pandemic, governments seem to be crumbling, and we are just starting to see the beginnings of an economic downturn. Times are hard and I find myself feeling helpless and asking myself, "how will anything ever get better?"

For me, the months of isolation were hard and feeling connected to my community and Judaism felt even more difficult. When I read the first line on Eicha this year, it seemed eerily relevant to the months past and I realized that this Tisha B'Av hit a lot closer to home than I could have ever imagined.

"How solitary lies the city that was once so full of people!" (Eichah 1:1)

I am so blessed to be part of such an awesome community. We have friends, women's Torah classes, shul, and everyone is ready to help each other out. But, in the past few months, this community moved online and I found that it was much harder for me to connect. It made me ask myself, why does Hashem throw these hurdles at us, and what makes him so sure that we will actually make it out the other side?

In Yirmiyahu 31:28 it's written: "and just as I was watchful over them to uproot and to pull down, to overthrow and to destroy and to bring disaster, so I will be watchful over them to build and to plant—declares the LORD." Yirmiyahu saw the destruction and devastation before it happened, and yet, he also saw that we would have the strength and resilience to rebuild.

But how was Yirmiyahu so sure? Rabbi Sacks [1] asserts that this is because of Hashem's promise in Vayikra 26:44, "Yet, even then, when they are in the land of their enemies, I will not reject them or spurn them so as to destroy them, annulling My covenant with them: for I the LORD am their God."

I think we can see from these two sources that destruction is inherently tied to rebuilding and that Hashem is ultimately in control. So I was wrong, now isn't a time to "why is this happening," but rather, it is a chance to ask ourselves "where do I have room to improve." Even if we can't see the positive side of the situation in the moment, things will get better and we will become stronger and more resilient than before.

Shabbat Shalom! Cali

The Grasshopper Dvar

Adam · July 23, 2021

It's hard to find the words to start this Dvar Torah. It's hard to believe it's been four years without Uncle Yonatan. I feel the loss like it was yesterday.

I'm going to break the rules and go off of the Parsha for this week, instead revisiting one of Uncle Yonatan's favorite Dvar's. The one that I take with me most often. The grasshopper one. :) Hopefully we can uncover another layer this year.

I have spent many hours delving into the depths of the story of the spies and it was particularly on my mind this past week due to Tisha B'Av which has its roots in the story of the Spies, or the Meraglim, as the Sages called it.

To recap, Hashem told Moshe to send spies to the Land of Israel to bring back a report "If it is good or bad; fortified or unprotected, etc." Moshe does just this. He selects the leader of each of the 12 tribes and sends them on a reconnaissance mission. They return and give a "bad report" about the land saying that the fruits are huge, the cities are heavily fortified and the people there are giants. "We were in our eyes like grasshoppers and so we must have been in their eyes." The Jewish people listened to the report and cried that night, beginning the cycle of mourning that still has a hold on this date in our history.

I heard an amazing new idea that I'll attempt to excerpt before we return to Uncle's favorite part. (Full version here: https://www.alephbeta.org/playlist/tisha-bav-origin-story) In the interest of brevity, I will skip the bulk of the question answer format that drives these observations.

The story of the Meraglim relates directly to the story of Yosef and his brothers. We find the same unique words that link these two portions together.

Early in the story of Yosef, he went to spy on his brothers His father's request. This immediately followed a bad report that he gave Yaacov about his brothers and the repeating of his dreams which increased the jealousy and bad feelings that were already rampant in the family. As he was approaching, they saw him from afar and decided what they would do with the "dreamer", ultimately throwing him in a pit and selling him to some spice traders on their way to Egypt. (The same word "dibbah" is used to describe bad reports in both stories).

Rabbi Fohrman's question on both of these stories is what the intention of God was in sending the spies and what the intention of Yaacov was in sending Yosef? Were these both not reconnaissance missions doomed to failure? Didn't Moshe ask them to see if the land was good or bad? Was this some sort of trick question? Were they not supposed to answer honestly? How could these stories have ended well?

He posits a very deep and important truth To make sense of these stories:

We are not supposed to ignore the "negative" parts of a person when we view them. We are allowed to see fault. But we are meant to view the person as a whole being. And we should understand that many traits are package deals. The giants who were so threatening to the spies were only giants due to the fruits being so prolific, nutrient dense, and bio-available. The problem came in when they passed judgment on the entire package as not worth claiming. But noting the fortification of the land and the military aptitude of the current residence was part of their mission. And they did nothing wrong by noting the bad along with the good. The problem was when they allowed the bad to overshadow the good that was inherently part of the package.

With this in mind, the goal that we have toward each other should be to view each other objectively. We must understand that the same creativity that expresses itself as disorganization also creates the seeds of incredible creative change. That the flexibility we enjoy in loved ones being easy-going about rescheduling might come along with some lack of timeliness.

I think through this lens, Uncle's favorite Dvar about the grasshoppers allows us to reflect the same idea inward. The Spies saw themselves as grasshoppers and only once they were viewing themselves that way, did they assume that they were also being viewed by others in that manner. We tell the world what to think about us. Everyone of us gives off the vibrations of our own self judgment. The more that we can view ourselves as a mixed bag and appreciate the gifts that Hashem uniquely gave us, the more we can build on the positive aspects of ourselves, mitigate the negative effects of those same traits, and ultimately appreciate the sublime beauty of those around us in their technicolor glory!

I love you all so much! You make Yonatan proud! See yourselves as the Awesome lights that you are!

"Be excellent to each other!" And "Party on Dude's!"

Good Shabbos!

Adam

August 2022

In this week's Parsha, Moshe tells the children of Israel that he will not enter the Land of Israel -- he will only see it from a distance. Continuing his recap of their journey, Moshe recounts the Exodus and the giving of the Torah, overcome by its splendor: "Has there ever occurred this great thing, or has the likes of it ever been heard? Did a people ever hear the voice of G-d speaking out of the midst of fire... and live?"

Moshe predicts that Jews will, in the future, turn away from G-d and be scattered among the nations -- and also that they would ultimately search Him out and return. The Parsha concludes with the Shema and the Ten Commandments, along with the mitzvot to love G-d and study His Torah -- the words we bind to our arms and heads with tefillin, and affix to our doorposts with mezuzot.

Idol worship posed a threat to Judaism that is hard for us to understand today; when it began with Enosh, it served a very tangible purpose -- his generation believed in G-d, but worshipped constellations as intermediaries capable of warping His will, so that even a punishing drought could be undone by an idol, making it impossible for G-d's judgment to actually reach them. The evil of idol worship is that it removes the point of life -- growing closer to G-d through self-perfection. If we don't suffer through a drought, we may never improve our watering systems. Idol worship does not exist today in the same form it did five thousand years ago, but many things today still keep us from confronting reality.

Shabbat Nachamu

Noah · July 28, 2023

This week is Shabbat Nachamu, our first Shabbat out of the three weeks and nine days and immediately following Tisha b'Av. At a time when our spirits have been crushed by reliving the horrors our people have endured, we are given this Shabbat as a consolation. Our calendar is arranged so we always read Parashat Vaetchanan and its accompanying haftorah this week and can find solace in their words of unity and comfort. This is also the Shabbat right before Tu b'Av, a holiday characterized by intimacy with God to the extent it is compared to Yom Kippur, and so joyful that the Mishnah writes "the daughters of Jerusalem would go out in white clothes to the vineyards and dance." It was when, on multiple occasions, the individual tribes of Israel reunited and started building families together again. It is an auspicious day to get married and it has many segulot related to finding a spouse. We have barely removed our sackcloth and washed the ashes from our hair and we are already expected to don our finery and find our dancing shoes. What is the power of this Shabbat that gives it the ability to serve as such a seamless bridge from tragedy to triumph?

While Tisha B'Av represents our recognition of the impermanence of even our deepest treasures, Tu B'Av represents our appreciation for the things that are eternal. We mourn the temples and the exiles on Tisha B'Av but celebrate God's forgiveness and the establishment of new Jewish families on Tu B'Av.

This theme is found deeply throughout our Parsha. Moshe reveals that he will not be permitted to enter Israel but will only be able to observe the foundation of our undying connection to the land from afar. There is a profound sense of loss brought on by Moshe's departure but also a deep sense of hope in the establishment of our eternal homeland.

Moshe also explains that we will sin and experience exile. Again we are struck with sadness at how fragile and temporary our connection with God can be. But Moshe also reassures us that we will forever continue to seek God and do teshuvah. Providing us another eternal consolation for the loss of something ephemeral.

We also repeat the Ten Commandments, our immortal code of conduct, and the Shema, declaring our continuous unity as a people.

This Shabbat is our chance to feel the deep void left in ourselves after reflecting on our losses. It is a chance, while we are still raw, to try and zoom out and see the bigger picture. We lost a temporary temple but we never stopped seeking god, just as Moshe promised. We faced internal schisms and divisions yet we always managed to create families together. We have been exiled and tormented at every turn but we still declare our unity as a people. This contrast between sorrow and hope enables us to feel both our anguish and our excitement more intensely. It is this emotional dichotomy that gives Shabbat Nachamu its incredible power.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Parshat

Eikev

August 13, 2017

While in the desert, the Children of Israel were sustained by the miraculous "manna", a bread that fell from the heavens to the door of each individual's tent. In fact, this manna possessed whichever taste the one eating it wished for, and it sustained all of their needs for the entire forty-year period in the desert.

But what does this have to do with us? How is it relevant to the world as we know it, when bread doesn't fall from the sky, but is earned through hard work and effort? The Torah itself addresses this question and tells us that more important than that which it sustained our fathers is the message which it imparts to us for all time. "In order to let you know that not on bread alone is man sustained, but rather on whatever is decreed by the mouth of G-d shall man be sustained." The lesson for us is that G-d has many ways to sustain us. Yes, the natural order ofs things is that bread is earned through hard work; it doesn't fall from the sky. And yes, the natural order is that food sustains, while other things do not. But in truth, even that is really just a decree from on high. Nature is just what we call the heavenly decrees which we observe on a reliable basis. It serves as a mask to allow one free will and the choice to disbelieve.

This reminds me of a story from the Talmud which Uncle Yonatan loved to tell. Rabbi Chanina ben Dosa was extremely poor, and one week his family had no oil with which to light Shabbos candles. His daughter told him of the predicament, and he calmly told her to light the wicks with vinegar. When she questioned him that vinegar does not burn, he responded, "He who said that oil should light will say that vinegar shall light." And that's how it was. Rabbi Chanina, who truly believed with all his heart that the entire world and its "laws" are just a projection of the will of the Almighty, was thereby not bound by those laws.

For those of us who aren't on Rabbi Chanina's level to totally discount nature, we must play by the rules and relate to the world as it is masked by nature. But it isn't all or nothing. Even as we work within the framework of nature, G-d will bend the rules to the extent by which we see His hand behind it and grant us extra divine assistance.

This week has been incredibly difficult for all of us. Uncle Yonatan's passing leaves us with a gaping hole in our lives and our hearts -- a hole which cannot possibly be filled. Let us realize, though, that death is not an accident, and the cause of death, be it what it may be, is not a blind "law of nature", but rather an expression of the will of G-d. There is a Master Plan and everything is being done by the hand of G-d. I know Uncle Yonatan could be in no better place than in the hand of G-d.

The learning should be a merit for Yonatan ben Avraham Avinu a"h

Questions and comments can be sent to freedmanyosef@gmail.com

Growth and Heeling

Adam · August 3, 2018

This week is Shabbat leading into the month of Elul, the month immediately preceding Rosh Hashanah and the High Holidays!

This week's parsha, Eikev, begins with a very important verse: “And it will be, Eikev Tishm'un, as a consequence of your heeding these laws, and your guarding and fulfilling them, Hashem your God will guard for you the covenant and the kindness which he swore to your forefathers.”

These two Hebrew words, Eikev Tishm'un, are translated by Rashi, the foremost commentator of the Torah, to encompass two things. Eikev literally means heel. But figuratively in this verse, it means as a consequence of, or as a result of. Tishm’un means your listening or your adhering to. Thus the translation utilized above.

But in his first comment to this week's Parsha, Rashi says that Eikev also encompasses its literal meaning. Every word in the Torah is intentional and important. So we must delve into the usage that God felt necessary to convey with the specific words He did.

Rashi says, “If the light commandments which a person (has a tendency to) tread on with his Eikev, with his heel, you will heed, Hashem will guard for you…”

The use of the word heel is there to adjure us to pay specific heed to the small things, the small mitzvos which we may deem less important.

So many times in life, we look to be grand, to be a hero. We try to think of the big ways with which we can make an impact and we try to jump high and fast in our relationship with God. But Rashi tells us an imperative lesson to take account and look to the small things.

With this period leading into the High Holidays, when we all look to improve ourselves and make resolutions, we read an imperative lesson in doing so.

Uncle Yonatan, I believe, was a king of this. We know that with the intensity and brilliance he had, he was well aware of flaws, both in character traits and Torah observance. But he had internalized the message that growth happens slow. And every one of us who gained from his reconnection to Hashem knows that it took years. And that he knew where he wanted to be every step along the way. For most of us, cognitive dissonance makes true, lasting growth very difficult in these moments because we are so far from who we wish to be. But as Yonatan's example showed us, we can reach it.

You may have heard of the Chinese Bamboo. Like any plant, growth of the Chinese Bamboo Tree requires nurturing – water, fertile soil, sunshine. In its first year, we see no visible signs of activity. In the second year, again, no growth above the soil. The third, the fourth, still nothing. Our patience is tested and we begin to wonder if the daily watering and effort will ever be rewarded.

And finally, only in the fifth year, is there visible growth. And what growth it is! The Chinese Bamboo Tree can grow 80 feet in just six weeks.

But in all honesty, does the Chinese Bamboo Tree really grow 80 feet in six weeks? Did the tree lie dormant for four years only to grow exponentially in the fifth? Or, was the little tree growing underground, developing a root system strong enough to support its potential for outward growth in the fifth year and beyond?

Very often the big leap we seek may be beyond our current abilities but there is a small step, which if we build small steps upon small steps, we can use our heels to push us to new horizons that we may not even currently consider possible. And we can build from our heels up, the foundation upon which we can become the Giants we are capable of being.

As we enter this auspicious and meaningful time of year, let's choose small, reasonable baby steps that we can stick to each day and we will merit that “Hashem will guard the covenant and the kindness,” both parts of our unbreakable relationship with Him until we can merit the arrival of Mashiach, together!

Much love,

Adam

2019

This week's parsha is "Eikev," meaning "because." Moshe continues his closing address to Bnei Yisrael, telling them that as long as he has known them they have been sinners -- recounting the golden calf and Korach's rebellion -- before reminding them of their eventual forgiveness and the giving of a second set of tablets. Moshe reassures the people that G-d's forgiveness is bountiful, and "that man does not live on bread alone, but by the utterance of G-d's mouth does man live."

Moshe describes the land of Israel they are soon to enter -- flowing with milk and honey, blessed with the seven species -- and warns of the danger ahead: to destroy the idols worshipped throughout the land, lest the people become haughty and believe "my power and the might of my hand have gotten me this wealth." Through devotion to mitzvot we are brought near to G-d and all His blessings; through idol worship and forgetting G-d in our lives, we may face famine, drought, and worse. Moshe speaks of the coming of Moshiach and the resurrection of the dead.

We have all faced trials in our lives that, once we come through them, seem impossible in retrospect -- and it is this that inspires us to reach new heights. The sages quote G-d as saying, "Open for me a door as small as the point of a needle, and I will open for you a door as wide as the entrance to the Temple."

At the end of a journey it is good practice to recount what we might have done differently, and to name the mercies that carried us through despite the tumult. We are commanded to take the Land by force while simultaneously commanded not to celebrate our own strength or our enemies' downfall -- teaching us that even our most uniquely human capacities are seen through by G-d, and that even our bodily functions are driven by something beyond us for which we cannot take sole credit.

Reflection and Appreciation

Avi · August 8, 2020

This week's parsha is "eikev", meaning "because". Moshe continues his closing address to b'nei yisrael, telling them that as long as he has known them they have been sinners. He recounts times like the golden calf and the rebellion of Korach. Moshe goes on to remind bnei yisrael of their recompense and the delivery of a second set of tablets. Moshe reassures his people that God's forgiveness is bountiful and "that man does not live on bread alone, but by the utterance of God's mouth does man live."

Moshe begins to tell of the land of Israel which they are soon to enter, flowing with milk and honey and blessed with seven kinds. Moshe warns of the danger ahead, commanding to destroy the idols worshiped throughout the land, lest they become haughty and believe that "my power and the might of my hand have gotten me this wealth." Through devotion to mitzvos we are brought near to Hashem and all his blessings, but if we worship other gods and fail to see Hashem in our life we may face famine, drought and the most frightening of natural consequences. Moshe tells bnei yisroel of the coming of Moshiach and the resurrection of the dead.

We have all seen trials in our lives, when delivered through them we look back and believe that was not humanly possible, this drive inspires us and leads us to reach new heights. The sages quote G-d as saying, "Open for me [a door as big] as the point of a needle, and I will open up for you [a door] like the hall in the Temple."

At the end of our journeys it is good practice to recount the things we would have done differently and name the mercies that inhibited our success despite tumult. We are commanded to take Israel by force while simultaneously being commanded to not celebrate our physical strength or the downfall of our enemies. This shows us that even the most uniquely human activities are seen through by God. Even our bodily functions are driven by something beyond us and we cannot take credit.

Shabbat Shalom, Avi

The Potency of Prayer

Yosef · July 30, 2021

This week's Parsha is called Eikev, which has a dual translation. In the simple form it is rendered 'because' and thus the first verse reads that because of our keeping of the mitzvos we will merit a lengthy list of blessings. But Rashi tells us the hidden meaning of the word here, which can also be translated as the heel. If we keep the mitzvos that one normally tramples on with the heel, that is how we will merit those blessings.

Which mitzvos is Rashi referring to here? Rabbi Moshe Feinstein (quoted in Kol Ram) explains that the intention is those mitzvos which we grow up from a young age to pay little attention to. Those mitzvos which we have trained ourselves to disregard, are precisely the ones which we need to pay extra attention to, and those are the ones which we can take to the bank with just a little effort.

One of Rabbi Feinstein's prime examples is the mitzvah of praying with concentration. Most of us pray on a regular basis, even three times a day and, for the men, even with a minyan. But how many of those prayers are said with the proper level of concentration and how many are said by rote – barely even knowing which words our mouth is spouting. In fact, the Talmud states that prayer is one of the things which stands at the pinnacle of the universe and which people treat cheaply. Is there a better example of a mitzvah which is trodden upon? And what can we do to rectify the situation?

In response to this problem, I'd like to share a very powerful explanation of the first three brachot of the amidah, which I believe have the potential to uplift and empower the rest of our prayers.

The Rashba (one of the great early talmudic commentators) explains that the first three brachot are designed to underscore the difference between requesting from mortal man and beseeching from the Almighty. Each of the three brachot is pointed towards a specific facet of the uniqueness of prayer.

First, when one asks his fellowman for something, there is usually some element of reciprocity, wherein either the person you are asking from already owes you something, or at least that he stands to benefit from you in the future. The average request is not made for a complete freebie. Not so when we pray to Hashem. Hashem owes us nothing and He lacks nothing that He could hope to gain from us in the future. Thus, when we ask from Hashem we must realize that we are asking without any merit whatsoever – it is a request for pure and complete divine kindness. This is brought out in the first bracha, where we state that Hashem helps us 'in memory of our fathers', He is the 'One who bestows pure and good kindness', and 'He who does for the sake of His name, out of love'. With these praises, we recognize that there is no reciprocity here – just a humble servant beseeching his Master for undeserved goodness.

Secondly, when requesting from a mortal, one understands that it may very well not be in his power to fulfill the request. This is not necessarily the address where he can hope to have his needs fulfilled. However, when beseeching Hashem we recognize that there is absolutely nothing which is not within His power to grant. This is brought out by the second blessing, which declares Hashem the One who revives the dead. In this blessing we state that Hashem is completely unbound by the shackles of nature – He can make a total reverse of nature like the revival of the dead, and certainly any other need is simple in comparison to that. Hashem is most definitely the address where we can come to for any and every need.

The third fact about a mortal benefactor is that whoever this person may be, he is not the only hope. If the first person turns down the request it's not the end of the line; there are others on the list who may still be able to help. With Hashem, though, it is not this way. If one asks Hashem for something and is turned down, there is no backup plan and nobody else who is able to help. One absolutely must receive the blessing of G-d for him to be able to carry on. If Hashem will not help him, then rest assured that nobody else can. This is the third blessing, where we name Hashem 'the Holy G-d' or הא-ל הקדוש. Kedushah, holiness, in a Torah sense means something or someone who is separate and removed from the rest of the world. In this context, it is a description of Hashem's quality of Oneness, referring to how He stands far above any other power in creation; alone in His ability to run the world.

If we would say these three blessings with such an understanding, I believe it would change the entire form of our prayers. By using the beginning of the amida to realize and understand the power and importance of what we are about to do, it would give another whole edge to our prayers. So next time we begin to daven, let's utilize the first three blessings to revitalize our entire prayer. Let's remember how much we need to beg, seeing as we are asking without any merit of our own, and how important such requests are, seeing as He certainly can help whereas nobody else can without Him.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Gratitude – The Key to it All

Yosef · August 19, 2022

And now, Israel, what does Hashem your G-d ask of you? Just to fear Hashem your G-d, to go in all his ways, to love him, and to serve him with all your heart and all your soul.

This is a thoroughly perplexing verse. It is a short list of all the loftiest levels of spiritual achievement; things which one must strive his whole life to attain, and they are prefaced with the word 'just'?!

The Gemara (Menachot 43b) makes a seemingly unconnected drash from the beginning of the verse. Instead of reading מה, what, does Hashem ask, we read מאה – Hashem asks of you to make one hundred brochos, blessings, each and every day.

This is a very nice ideal on its own, but does it have anything to do with the rest of the verse?

Rabbi Avigdor Miller z"l teaches that it does. One hundred brochos is the way to achieve all those loftiest levels in the easiest way. In effect, מאה, meaning the hundred brochos, is what puts the מה, the ease, into all the holy achievements listed at the end. If one will only focus on the key of one hundred brochos, this will bring him quite easily to all the rest.

How so? Certainly not by hastily muttering a blessing under our breath while simultaneously stuffing the doughnut down our throat. And probably not by reciting Shmoneh Esrei while daydreaming about our summer plans either.

Rabbi Miller explains that brochos are really a portal into living with Hashem at all times. When making a blessing on a delicious fruit, or a scrumptious baked good, or a savory steak, we are ascribing all of our enjoyment to Hashem. When we daven, beseeching Hashem for all of our greatest needs and deepest desires, we are affirming that every facet of our life depends on, and is being sponsored by Hashem. When we make a blessing after using the facilities, we recognize the great miracles of our bodily systems and, again, we recognize that it is all the kindness of Hashem.

But this is just the beginning.

As we continue to make brochos and think about what we are saying, truly thanking and asking Hashem for everything, we are becoming new people. We are becoming thankful people. We are training ourselves not to take everything for granted, but to be aware of every good thing which we enjoy.

Let's take an apple for example. Yesterday, we ate an apple with no thought at all, mumbling a quick brocha and devouring our snack. Today however, we made a thoughtful brocha, thanking Hashem for the sweet-tasting, healthy apple. The next day, as we become more appreciative, we realize another dimension. Wow, it's not just delicious and nutritious, it even has that satisfying crunch. And so on. We notice that, in addition, it's beautiful! And it's the perfect size for my hand. It comes ready-made, right off the tree! It's got this incredible protective wrapper (the peel) which keeps it fresh and juicy! Hey, and you can even eat the wrapper! And the more we work on our blessings, on being thankful, we find more and more facets to every pleasure in the world. Our apple eating experience has transformed. Besides providing us with loads more enjoyment than it previously did, it has opened our eyes and our hearts to the love and kindness of our creator. Our relationship with Hashem has begun a new chapter.

And it's not just apples. There are hundreds, no, thousands of such kindnesses which we take for granted every day. Think what life would be like if we appreciated every breath of air. Every movement of our limbs. Every chirp of a bird, every flower, every cool breeze. Each of these things consists of a million miracles, each is a manifestation of endless kindness from above.

If we would just realize these things we'd never have a bad day in our lives. And if just a hundred of these miracles would elicit a heartfelt 'thank you' to Hashem, we would be elevated, almost automatically, to untold heights.

To fear Hashem? With such a clear perception of His limitless power, and our dependence on Him for so many miracles, how could we not fear Him?

To love him? Is there any more natural feeling for one who bestows such goodness upon you?

To go in his ways and to serve him with all our heart and soul? As we marvel at every facet of his creation all day every day, aren't we compelled to act in his glorious ways and serve him to the very best of our abilities?

So let's make an effort to unlock the secret of one hundred brochos. There's no better investment for this world and for the next.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Gratitude is an Attitude

August 4, 2023

Parsha Eikev touches on some values that are truly at the core of Judaism. Eikev means “because” and opens with Moshe’s closing address to Bnai Yisroel. Moshe reiterates the promises of land and wealth God swore to our forefathers. Moshe continues with rebuke, reminding us of the sin of the golden calf, Korachs rebellion and the sin of the spies among other transgressions.

Moshe calls Bnai Yisroel rebellious yet in the same breath reminds them of Gods forgiveness. Moshe reminded them that for forty years God sustained us in the desert with manna and that man is not sustained on bread, but on the utterance of Gods mouth. Moshe describes Israel as flowing with milk and honey, and the center of divine providence. Moshe commands them to destroy the idols of foreign gods. God warns them that they cannot become full of themselves and believe that their weath and success is a broduct of their own strength and might.

A major passage that we recite daily is found in this Parsha, the second paragraph of Shema describes the rewards we receive when we follow Gods commandments. It also describes the adverse outcomes of not following commandments. Famine exile and supernatural disaster.

Once there was a troop of climbers who train for years in various conditions at higher and higher altitudes so that one day they can summit the highest peak. After years of training they embark on their journey, they walk and climp for months, the weather is severe but their gear protects them. One day they finally reach the peek and as they appreciate the awesome view a very small boy walks comfortablly out to them in his pajamas. Perplexed, the climbers ask the boy how he got there and the boy simply replies “ I was born here.”

It is easy to think that our accomplishments are ours and that our wealth is earned, and that we are deserving of the good that the world sends our way. But as Jews we know we are born on top of the mountain. We are imbued with a profound gratitude for our creator and we constantly remind ourselfs that our strength is given to us by God.

Parshat

Re'eh

Tithes for Social Capital

Noah BenEzra · August 9, 2018

This week’s parsha introduces us to the concept of Maaser Sheni, or the second tithe. Judaism's rich agricultural tradition and laws form the backbone of our shared society. While we value the open market and self determination in the planting and harvesting processes, laissez faire economics were already recognized as a failure in many respects. From providing parity and sustaining spirituality to harboring humility and strengthening social bonds, our complicated cycles of tithes mandated the positive behaviors the invisible hand failed to value.

Some commandments form the basis for a welfare state. Leket, Shikheha, and Pe’ah or the forgotten ears of grain, forgotten sheafs, and the corners of the field all were earmarked for the poor. Additionally, the Maaser Ani or tithe for the poor was due in the 3rd and 6th year of the cycle and also distributed among the less fortunate. The 7th year in the shmita cycle forgave debtors and the Yovel or Jubilee year restored everyone's ancestral land. We see many different tools being implemented to ensure the Jewish economy is one that provides for all, regardless of individual wealth.

Next, we have sets of laws that are designed to protect and support our religious leaders. The Kohanim and Leviim were our teachers, rabbis, and scholars. They were responsible for the soul of the Jewish people. They ran the temple, the cities of refuge, and were our spiritual doctors, diagnosing our ailments and recommending teshuvah. The Torah acknowledged that as central as they were to our lives and survival as a people, their pay wasn’t great. For this reason we are commanded to give Teruma and Maaser Rishon, priestly portions.

Finally, we are commanded to bring first fruits to Jerusalem on the pilgrimage holidays. This is meant to remind our nation that its success and bounty is only by the gift of God. Without this reminder, an affluent society can become overly materialistic and self absorbed. Leaders can quickly forget their role is to serve and not to be served. These are the first signs of a civilization in decline. So the Torah remedies this threat as well by commanding us to take our first fruits and dedicate them to God.

With this understanding, we can observe how each halachic nuance in our agricultural laws is meant to compensate for ways that the market inevitably fails to provide for its participants. That's why the law of Maaser Sheni in this week’s parsha is so difficult to comprehend. Unlike the other donations and tithes described above, Maaser Sheni does not benefit anyone except the individual himself. While first fruits were accompanied by liturgical declarations and ceremonial pomp, Maaser Sheni can be used for almost anything, as long as it is consumed within Jerusalem. What hole in the agricultural economy could this law be addressing?

The Rambam in the Guide for the Perplexed explains, “As he was not able to use it (Maaser Ani) otherwise than by way of eating and drinking, he must have been easily induced to give it gradually away. This rule brought multitudes together in one place and strengthened the bond of love and brotherhood among the children of man.”

From the Rambam’s explanation we are able to see that Maaser Sheni combatted an incredible threat to peaceful society, individualism. The great driving force of a free economy is individual identity and the right to self determination. As much as this is an engine for the expansion of our economy, the quest for individual achievement comes at a massive cost to the common good and public spirit. As we become more focused on ourselves and our lot, even while paying our other dues, we lose the social capital on which our society is built. This is the brilliance of Maaser Sheni. We are commanded to flood the streets of Jerusalem with open hearts and full pockets. Once there, we have the opportunity to lose ourselves, willingly, in the needs and desires of the whole. This experience rejuvenates the connections that form communities and ensures we can not recede into icey isolation.

These lessons ring even more true in the post industrial age. As the costs of capitalism become more apparent through rising income inequality, sharp declines in religious practice, and a government that increasingly only legislates in its own interest, we must heed the warning of this week’s parsha. We cannot let individualism consume our communal consciousness. Now, more than ever, we need to come together in joy and celebration and strengthen the bond of love and brotherhood among all of mankind.

Shabbat Shalom,

Noah BenEzra

Only You

Yosef · August 31, 2019

It was just before Sukkot during the second World War, and bombs were falling everywhere. Fear reigned. But the Brisker Rav, Rabbi Yitzchak Zev Soloveichik, had something else on his mind. He was totally absorbed in finding Hadasim for the chag of Sukkot; not just kosher ones, but the finest Hadasim available. Someone asked the Rav if the dire situation didn't warrant a slightly lower standard than usual. Wouldn't it be okay this year to use regular kosher Hadasim and forgo the added stringency of finding the most beautiful ones?

Rabbi Soloveichik gave an incredible response which is pertinent to all of us, in the best and worst of times. What if Hashem would tell just one person, that he wants just one thing, right now. And that one thing would be to find beautiful Hadasim. Of course in such a case everyone would make it their business to fulfill the command, no matter the circumstances. After all, G-d asked a personal request of me, right now.

Why do we have trouble doing the mitzvot in the face of adversity? We don't think of the mitzvot in the frame of reference of G-d Himself asking. We don't feel personally responsible because all Jews were commanded, not just us. We don't realize the importance of the specific mitzvah in front of us because there are so many other mitzvot. And the command isn't current but rather from thousands of years ago.

It is for this purpose that we say the following every day in the Shema: "And this thing that I command you today shall be upon your heart." 'This thing,' as if it's the only single mitzvah. 'That I command', that G-d Himself is the one talking. 'You,' as if you are the only subject of the command. 'Today,' as if I just commanded this very day. We have to view the Mitzvot as current and personal objectives which we received today. Then His service will be real and relevant, and we won't easily shrug off such a specific request.

This, says Rabbi E. B. Finkel, is the message contained in the beginning of our Parsha. "Behold (single tense) I am placing before you (plural tense) today a blessing and a curse." Why does the verse begin with the single tense of seeing, as if it was said to a single person, wasn't this said to the entire assembly together, as the verse finishes in the plural? The answer is that even though it was said to everyone together, Hashem wants us to know that in His eyes every person is treated as an individual. He wants us to know that every mitzvah is not only a national objective, but the personal responsibility of each and every individual. As the omnipotent G-d, the significance of a single person is in no way mitigated by the existence of others doing the same thing.

Let us realize our very personal connection to G-d and to each of His mitzvot, let us realize the importance and vitality of our own service, and let us thereby redouble our efforts and performance, no matter the circumstance.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Greatness Unveiled

Yosef · August 14, 2020

'See that I am placing before you today a blessing and a curse.' Famous words, but they seem to be grammatically incorrect. 'See', or Re'eh in Hebrew, is written in the single tense, whereas 'before you', lifneichem, is written in the plural form.

The Rebbe of Alexander explained with a powerful one-liner. He said it in Yiddish, but we'll do our best to translate without losing the flavor. 'Things are presented before everyone, but only a select few see the opportunities.' Thus the presentation is before the plural you, but the seeing is written only to an individual.

All of us receive endless invitations and opportunities to become great – the hard part is being the individual who sees, realizes, and capitalizes on that which is presented.

But why is it so difficult to see? What is this blindness which impairs most of the world from tapping into their potential? And how can we avoid the pitfall and count ourselves among the few possessed of vision?

The Netivot Shalom (Parshat Va'etchanan) says in the name of a certain tzadik that when, at the end of his life, he looked back over the years, he found an incredible thing. He saw then that all the periods which had seemed to him as the 'good years' were really very simple and unaccomplished. On the other hand, those times which had seemed dark and treacherous, when he felt like he was faltering, those were in fact days of tremendous growth and achievement.

Why the paradox? It would seem that greatness is built through hard work and striving against the darkness. Greatness doesn't come from places we'd expect it, from times when everything is going right and we feel like we're on top of the world. Rather, greatness is something which blossoms from times of trial, from times when things aren't going the way we'd like when we feel like we just can't overcome.

Those trying times are the 'presentation' which is afforded to everyone, and which precious few are able to see. And perhaps with that realization, we can understand why some of us are opportunity-blind. When we find ourselves face-to-face with a situation we dread when all decks seem stacked against us, how do we react? Do we roll up our sleeves, take the bull by the horns, and brace ourselves for victory? Or are we so busy feeling upset about the situation, so scared of suffering defeat, and so wrapped up in self-pity that we check out and turn a blind eye to greatness being presented to us?

If you identified with the latter thought process, then I think we've identified the problem. But how do we combat it?

An Israeli soldier was once captured by terrorists and was being tortured for information. He was a very good soldier and was committed not to disclose anything, but as the days wore on and the torture became worse and worse, his stamina began to wear thin, until one night he decided he just couldn't take it anymore. He made up his mind that tomorrow he would tell them everything. But later that night he overheard the most peculiar thing. His captors, thinking they were out of earshot were conversing in Hebrew! Immediately he realized that these were no Arabs, but fellow members of the IDF. They were testing him. As soon as the truth hit him, he was filled with new strength – if this was just a test then he would pass, no matter what it took. He stood strong, and before long he was thrilled to be told the truth and to find himself promoted to the highest echelons of the army.

If we would only ingrain in our hearts that all the tribulations that come our way are none other then tests and opportunities to propel us to new heights we too would be filled with the power and strength to carry on and overcome. So let us change the lens through which we view every challenge. Let us not waste our time being upset by the situation, but recognize it for the treasure trove that it is. Let us not be scared by the chance of failure, but thrilled by the opportunity for true greatness. And let us never be wrapped up in self-pity, but filled with gratitude for each stepping stone that is sent our way.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Avi · 2021

In this weeks Parsha Re'eh Moshe gathers the children of Israel and places before them a blessing and a curse, a blessing that will come from fulfilling God's commandments and a curse for abandoning the commandments. One of the commandments given this week is regarding kosher meat consumption which has a complex history starting with Adam in the Garden of Eden.

Upon his creation Adam was taught the ways of the world by God, "Behold, I have given you every seed-bearing herb which is upon the surface of the entire earth, and every tree that has seed-bearing fruit; it will be yours for food." In the beginning humankind ate from the land and survived with no meat.

Several chapters and over 1600 years later, after surviving the great flood Noach is instructed by God, "Every moving thing that lives shall be yours to eat; like the green vegetation, I have given you everything." And meat became part of our Noach's diet.

It may sound from this progression of events that God's initial plan was for us not to eat meat. However the Torah tells us to celebrate Shabbat by eating meat which implies that meat is encouraged and honorable.

Rabbi Yosef Albo, author of Sefer ha-Ikkarim suggests that God's first plan was for humans not to kill and eat meat. In his view killing animals is a furious and cruel act, by engaging in such behavior we become engrained with these negative traits. The people in the first generations that were not permitted to kill and eat meat failed to recognize the difference between themselves and the animals around them. This led to the denigration of society into violence and corruption. They failed to see themselves as Godly creations and neglected their responsibility to uplift the world. This chaos is what God sought to destroy with the flood.

When Noach stepped off the Ark there was a new deal. Noach was not only permitted to eat meat but obligated to sacrifice it to God. This reminds us that we are beyond animals and responsible for the world in ways beyond our survival. Meat is a luxury and eating it gives us a taste for material lust, the rules around meat in Kashrut are there to lessen that materialistic drive.

Eating meat is a luxury and that is why the sages tell us that an empty minded person has no business eating meat. The sages also instruct us not to eat meat on an empty stomach, better to start with bread.

Good Shabbos,

Avi

Wealth Before Wishes

Yosef · August 11, 2023

While in the desert, camping around the Mishkan, the Jewish people were commanded only to eat meat that was part of a korban, a sacrifice to Hashem. Since everyone lived in immediate proximity to the ‘House of G-d’ it was a slight to Hashem’s honor to eat meat without offering its blood and the like to Him first. However, when the people were to come into the land of Israel and were no longer going to be in direct contact with the Mishkan, Moshe gave the word that basar taavah, meat not part of any korban, would become permissible.

What’s interesting to note is the preface which the Torah gives to this amendment: ‘When Hashem will broaden your boundaries with wealth¹ and you will desire to eat meat, you may eat meat as you wish.’

What does wealth have to do with the desire to eat meat?

Rashi comments that the Torah is teaching us derech eretz, which means etiquette or simple common decency. What’s the common decency? That one should not desire meat unless he has first been granted the wealth to obtain such delicacies. If one cannot afford meat, he shouldn’t even have a desire for it. It’s not in his dreams or his aspirations – it is simply something outside of his reality. This is why the Torah prefaces the desire to eat meat with the blessing of wealth because until wealth comes his way, he has no business with such desires.

For our purposes, we must point out the obvious that meat is only a parable. In our days, thank G-d, I’m confident that everyone reading this can afford meat on at least some sort of regular basis. However, The broader lesson is perhaps more pertinent today than ever.

The entire American culture is based on longing for things out of our reach. Every advertisement, every social media post, and so many of our conversations revolve around the things we cannot afford. Be it that picturesque vacation in paradise, that bright and flashy new car, or that dream house big enough to fit three of your own, we are perpetually programmed to dream and desire every unattainable indulgence known to man.

But here we find an eye-opening window into the stark contrast between the timeless truth of the Torah and the distressing degeneration of Western society. Here we learn that the very baseline of human decency in the Torah’s view is something antithetical to our whole ‘American’ way of life. In the Torah’s eyes, the desire for material things beyond our grasp is a violation of the most basic human principles. One who harbors such aspirations is lacking in his status as a contributing part of society.

I want to stress that Rashi is not telling us about lofty levels of piety but about simple ‘derech eretz’, the most basic definition of human morals and conduct. He is teaching us the playbook for successful living and the path to a life of fulfillment. This is the inescapable truth; it is up to us only to decide whether we would like to face that truth and introduce it into our thought patterns. We need to retrain ourselves to stop all of those wants and desires at their very root before they have a chance to settle in our hearts and minds. And as we learn to desire only those things which are already within our means, we will find a much happier and more fulfilled existence.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Parshat

Shoftim

Noah, Cali, and Moony · August 25, 2017

This week's Torah portion concludes with an intriguing law. This is the law of the Eglah Arufah. When a body is found abandoned on the outskirts of town, we learn that the leaders of the nearest city are obligated to sacrifice a calf as an atonement offering. They must slaughter the calf and proclaim, "We have done our duty, we did not spill his blood nor did we witness what occurred." After this process, either the elders or the priests of the city request forgiveness from god, saying, "Absolve your people and do not allow the guilt for the blood of the innocent to remain among your people."

The commentator Rashi questions why the Torah is requiring a statement of innocence from the elders and leaders. Do we seriously suspect that the elders of the community committed this murder? Rashi answers that when the elders say that they did not commit the murder or witness what occurred, what they are really saying is, "We did not see him leave without food or escort, we did not know that he had needs that were unfulfilled." The implication from Rashi is astounding. This explanation indicates that had they seen the person leave without adequate provisions they would be responsible as if they had committed the murder themselves!

This obviously raises the question that this law seems to put responsibility for the death on the hands of a governing body that shouldn't be culpable. Why should the elders of a town take responsibility for something that occurred outside of their jurisdiction, especially when they took no part in committing the act? Why must they profess their innocence when they were not involved in the incident and had no idea that it had even taken place. The ordeal was not in their community, instead it occurred outside of their city limits.

The Netziv answers this question by explaining that this ritual is designed to start a dialog among the townspeople and their leadership. When the community learns of what has happened they will immediately mobilize to ensure this never happens again. The shock and horror of learning that a soul was lost in their midst will surely lead them to consider how these situations can be prevented. Through this dialog they will take productive steps to become a closer more attentive community. The Netziv is clearly explaining the reason why a ritual to raise awareness is necessary, but why the large emphasis on communal responsibility?

The Lubavitcher Rebbe learns a tremendous principle from how the Torah views communal responsibility in the case of the Eglah Arufa. He says that this law is meant to teach us that a person never has the ability to say, "This is outside of my control therefore it doesn't concern me." He teaches that immediately when we become aware of something, the awareness alone creates responsibility. In the case of the Eglah Arufah it was the responsibility of the townspeople and the elders to watch over and protect their fellow, to ensure his safety. They were responsible to be aware and concerned for each other, always. From here it is clear that avoiding awareness of that which occurs in your surroundings can not absolve you from guilt. Here too, the elders were unaware of what occurred and although they did not know, they still required repentance.

In this difficult time, still amid Uncle Jonathon's Shloshim (30 Days of Mourning), we must remember that our tremendous loss need not be in vain. Now that we have completed our ceremonies and rituals and engaged in the dialog of shiva we are left to see what will really change. When hardships befall us in the coming year will we remember that we do not have the right to throw our hands up and say, "What could I do? How is this my responsibility?" Did we harness this time of mourning to repent and look inside of ourselves to see what are we can do to enrich and extend the lives of those around us? Are we asking the hard questions about our community involvement? Will we start showing up and taking responsibility for our family, friends and neighbors? Now is when we need to really assess how we are using our loss as an impetus for lasting meaningful improvement. And finally, as hard as it may be, we must remind ourselves that tragedy has the ability to inspire transformation and that from this adversity we will emerge more aware.

Shabbat Shalom from Israel, Noah Cali and Moony

The learning should be a merit for Yonatan ben Avraham Avinu a"h

Guarding the Gates

Avi · August 17, 2018

This week we learn all about the Jewish court of law and responsibilities of the community, law enforcement, and judges. The parsha is called Shoftim, meaning judges. The parsha includes measures to prevent favoritism and corruption and the laws of witnesses during testimony. crimes are investigated, the evidence is examined and at least two witnesses are needed for a conviction.

Moshe tells us that in each generation there will be those entrusted with enforcing and upholding the law. We are instructed to take their words to the tee, not “swaying left or right”. We are taught the legal bounds of sorcery and are reminded of the prohibition of idolatry. The parsha teaches us how to appoint a king and how a king should hold his reins. Also stated is that in the case of accidental murder we are to establish cities of refuge.

In times of war, there are many exemptions for the family man. One who has planted a new vineyard, built a home, or is newly wed is exempt so they may enjoy the fruits of their labor. A man who is “afraid or soft-hearted” is also exempt from battle. We are obligated to negotiate for peace before engaging in battle and are warned not to cause damage for the sake of damage. We know “man is a tree of the field” so we are forbidden to lay to waste a fruit tree even in a siege. If a body is found in a field the elders of the surrounding towns visit the location and take preventative measures so it does not happen again.

When Israel was founded there was a general expectation that a Sanhedrin would be formed. A group of seventy scholars who knew the law and could administer it without favoritism. Who will take the position? Who will quit their day job? The entrepreneur in the room said, “For the right price, I’ll do it!” It is very hard to find people not susceptible to bribery. When judging, even being a friend of a friend to a witness or convict leads us to believe you can no longer be impartial. Bribery "blinds the eyes of the wise and perverts the words of the righteous." We are not far from it. Every day we may be pulled off of our good and straight path because of our friend rationalization, we can convince ourselves we are impartial but our relationship bears testimony to the fact we are not. When we go out of our way for friends we are putting them before our responsibilities which is nice but we don't want judges doing this. Each and every one of us is a judge and with the high holidays approaching it would be nice to see an impartial representation of our personal growth.

“Place judges and officers over your gates." as humans we have seven gates. Two nostrils two ears two eyes and one mouth and we are in control of keeping those gates. A well-kept gate makes it is easy for the right things to get in and hard for the wrong things to get in. the senses are the gate to our soul and when they are happy so are we.

A famous story about a little old rabbi in a small shtetl. His disciple was told to meet him at his front door and not leave until the Rebbe makes it to the door. The disciple waited and it began to snow, he waited as the moon rose up and around, and in the morning the little old rabbi invited him in. his student freezing and wet said, “Rebbe I need help with my temptations” the rabbi responded and said "you see how I kept you waiting all night? This is my house — I decide when you come in, not you…

This is how you control your primal nature. Don't let it sneak up on you, you are in control you can tell it to come, go or wait. we are taught that a wise man learns from every man. This does not mean to accept every man, it means to take a lesson from every man. Something that accepts from everyone is a waste receptacle. Something that takes a lesson from everything is a whole recycling facility, filtering and sorting raw material, to go through the necessary changes to reemerge as a better-updated product.`

Make sure your relationships are more than just good deals, strive for growth with others and always put your best foot first, do not stray from your calling and we will see a Sanhedrin built with the coming of Moshiach speedily and in our days!

Good Shabbos, Avi

September 6, 2019

This week's Parsha begins with the dictum to appoint judges and police in all of our cities.

It follows with more commands of how to create judicial systems in communities.

The wording of the verse is literally, "place for yourself (singular) judges and officers in all of your (singular) gates."

The sages derive an interesting additional idea here. Using the singular "lecha", your, and "shearecha", your gates, seems to obligate each person individually. The problem is, this is a communal command. How can each individual accomplish this?

The answer lies in another understanding of "gates". Each person has gates in their bodies. Our eyes allow new information and images into our minds. Our ears bring in music, which influences our emotions, and allows us to bond and form connections with others. Our mouths give us the ability to experience all of the nuances of flavor Hashem has placed in the world and to put out thoughts into words that can change the world.

The sages tell us that on another level this command is telling us to create accountability systems for ourselves. To place judges and officers in our own personal gates. To judiciously examine what we take into our minds, bodies, and souls and to take actions that only let in what will help us to flourish and to only put out that which changes the world around us for the better.

If we take the time to examine and judge what we consume and speak about, and guard the gates between ourselves and the world around us, we can reach the closing of the Parsha and "do what is right in the eyes of Hashem."

An Upside Down Reality

Adam · August 21, 2020

I have seen the world turn upside down the past year perhaps more than any other time in my life. There may have been crazier personal battles and difficulties but to see the world flip and the increasing gap between head, heart, and action has been whiplashing.

More and more seems backwards and upside down and it's becoming increasingly difficult to gain clarity on issues from public health to economics and mental health to politics.

I ventured onto facebook for a couple of minutes today for the first time in months and felt uneasy the entire time. The more I read, the more of a sinking feeling I felt in my stomach. The difficulty for me is in seeing the emotional schism growing between family, friends and acquaintances. Gone are the times of civil discussion of disagreements. I am becoming very aware of the grip of each of our beliefs about life and searching for connection with loved ones across the divide of views and judgements about where we should be headed and even where we are, seems an increasingly dangerous option.

Enter this week's Parsha. No, not about guarding our mouths in these times, a bit further in we have a discussion about what to do when there are disagreements.

Read these words carefully: "If a matter of judgement is hidden from you… you shall go up to the place which Hashem your God has chosen… and to the judge who will be in your days… and you will do according to the ruling they tell you… and you shall be careful to do according to all they have told you… do not deviate from what they tell you right or left."

Rashi tells us: "in your days," even if the leaders in your generation are not what you had in previous generations, you must listen; you only have the leaders that are in your days.

He continues: "left or right," even if they tell you about right that it is left and about left that it is right.

I know this is the perfect opportunity for a political joke but I'll spare you.

Why would we listen to someone telling us the polar opposite of what we can clearly see with our own eyes?! Rashi didn't say "if they tell you that you made an egregious error and have the data backwards." He said "if they tell you left is right and right is left!"

I think there are deep secrets here both in understanding the Torah's view and in understanding each other.

Have you ever been on the phone giving directions to someone? "Turn right at the Walgreens," you say only to eventually realize they've driven ten minutes in the wrong direction. You've driven the route hundreds of times, you know the right way, but they were coming from the other direction. Right is only East if you're facing North.

Our perspective makes all the difference when we're trying to judge right and left. The thing is, it makes just as much difference when we're trying to judge right and wrong and true and false. Depending on where we're standing, the same information can mean a completely different thing.

I find this crucial when seeking to understand the position of anyone disagreeing with my judgment. I have to remember that everything they've done until now influences their view on the "data" and even influences where they will source their "data" from. They may have made a u-turn at some point in life which changed all future lefts to rights and vice versa. The weirder thing is, I might be the one turned around. Empirical truth becomes harder to verify and trust.

So aside from a healthy loving understanding that we might see the same facts from different angles resulting in different conclusions, we have two other tips I see in these verses right off the bat.

We can seek out the leaders "in our day" and we can always seek to be "going up to the place Hashem has chosen."

If we focus on our own growth as the goal, seek qualified leaders in our generation, and remember that we might be turned around, I think we can grow closer even with everything going on around us.

I love and miss you all so much,

Have an uplifting Shabbos,

Adam

September 2022

In this week's Parsha, Shoftim, Moshe lays the groundwork for a Jewish judiciary. "Justice, justice shall you pursue." Moshe explains how to carry out laws without bias, how to investigate crimes, and the requirement of at least two witnesses to convict.

Moshe tells Bnai Yisrael that every generation will need people entrusted to interpret the law and issue rulings -- "you shall not turn away from the thing that they say to you, to the right nor to the left." It is important to have people in our lives whose rulings we do not feel the need to constantly re-litigate.

Shoftim also includes prohibitions against idol worship and sorcery, laws for appointing a king, cities of refuge for accidental killers, and the laws of war -- including exemptions for men who have recently built a home, planted a vineyard, married, had a child, or are simply soft-hearted. We are commanded to offer terms of peace before attacking a city, forbidden to destroy wantonly, and forbidden even to cut down a fruit tree.

The Parsha concludes with eglah arufah, the procedure for an unsolved murder near a town -- underscoring the responsibility of community leaders to examine and change whatever could have prevented the crime or led to a better outcome.

Parshat

Ki Teitzei

Yosef · September 1, 2017

In this month of Elul, the period of preparation before the Day of Judgment on Rosh Hashanah, we are tasked with the lofty work of teshuvah (repentance). It is in these days that the entire Jewish nation sets out to better our ways and truly return to our Creator with all our hearts. Shaarei Teshuvah, the monumental guide on repentance, explains the incredible potency of this great gift called teshuvah. In essence, once one has sinned, he has caused tremendous damage in all of the upper spheres; damage which should not be rectifiable. In the natural world there is no delete button -- a deed cannot be taken back and its consequences cannot just be forgotten. Yet the Almighty, in His infinite kindness, gave us a way out. He changed the very material of existence and created a back door, as it were. A device through which He considers "that our change of heart and annulment of will for our actions are like a retroactive nullification of the action itself" (Shaarei Teshuvah). This means that we have the incredible power to truly rectify our misdeeds and all of the negative consequences resulting from them, in totality, as if they never occurred. [As an aside, it must be noted that this is only effective regarding sins between man and G-d; sins against our fellow man cannot be forgiven until we attain the forgiveness of those whom we have harmed.]

Now that we understand the great power of teshuvah, it behooves us to learn how exactly the teshuvah process is realized, and how we can get there. Here too, the Shaarei Teshuvah, enlightens us with a three-step regimen for the most basic level of teshuvah. The first step is viduy, which means admitting our sins. Before we can do anything else, the first step must be to take an honest and thorough accounting of our lives and realize where we fall short. This is no easy task; we are all blind to our own shortcomings and it takes hard work and brutal honesty to see through our own facades. However, comprehending this great power and understanding the benefits of teshuvah can give us the much-needed motivation to complete this task. The second step is a firm resolve to better our ways. This is where we begin the "annulment of will" from our previous misdeeds. Only if we can accept upon ourselves to discontinue our actions can we truly say that we're sorry. Lastly, once we are already on the road to bettering our ways, we must look back at the damage we have caused and truly feel remorse and regret. This is the last step in the "annulment of will" which is the essence of teshuvah. Not only have we changed our ways, but we actively wish that we had never committed the sin.

For most of us, the second step of resolve to better ourselves is the hardest part of teshuvah. In our human frailty, we find it very difficult to make any iron-clad changes in our lives. To make matters worse, we are unsure where to begin. We have so much that needs improvement and we can't change it all at once, so we are inclined to just throw in the towel and give up before we even start.

Where should we begin and how can we possibly get to where we need to be by the time Rosh Hashanah will be upon us?

The Alter of Kelm had a very enlightening thought based on this week's Parsha, about the ben sorer u'moreh, the wayward son. This young boy has set off on the wrong path. He steals money from his parents in order to buy and consume huge amounts of meat and wine. Despite being reprimanded and even lashed for his misdeeds, he continues in his ways. The Torah's sentence is astounding; it says that in such a case the boy should be put to death! For what? Thievery and gluttony? Since when do such things merit a death sentence? The Talmud (Sanhedrin 71b) answers that he is killed not for his actions now, but because of where he will inevitably end up. Such a boy is on the path to become a true bandit and even a murderer. Therefore, the Torah says that he should better die innocent now than die guilty later. This is very revealing of the Torah's perspective on life -- that it is not an end unto itself but rather just a way to earn reward in the Next World. Hence, it is merciful that the boy's life on this world be shortened, in order that he not lose out in the World to Come.

From this, the Alter of Kelm drew an analogy to each and every one of us on Rosh Hashanah. He pointed out that usually we would assume that the divine attribute of mercy would call for one to receive positive decrees whereas the divine attribute of justice might call for a negative one. In the case of the wayward son, however, even the attribute of mercy agrees that he should be killed -- after all, it is in his best interest to not be allowed the chance to sink deeper into sin. Unfortunately, this is not a predicament exclusive to the wayward son. Anyone who is on the way down and descending ever deeper into the quagmire of sin, would have a similar judgment. It would be better that he die innocent than guilty. This is a terrifying and very dangerous situation to be in, because if the trait of mercy calls for one's demise, to whom can he turn?

On the other hand, we can find a great deal of comfort from this very Parsha. If one is not yet perfect but he's climbing the mountain, albeit at his own pace, then he has the opposite judgment. For him, G-d wants to grant him life in order that he have time to reach the top. In order that he should be able to become a truly meritorious individual and he not perish now in his still sullied state.

This, then, must be our plan of action. Yes, we may be very far from perfect, and yes, we may have too many shortcomings to possibly tackle at once. But perfection is not what is required of us. All we need is to do our very best and to be on our way up. If we can take upon ourselves one or two small things that we will do better -- small but absolutely rock solid -- then we will be on our way and, with G-d's help, we will merit the time we need to keep climbing.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

The learning should be a merit for Yonatan ben Avraham Avinu a"h

Questions and comments can be sent to freedmanyosef@gmail.com

The Innocence of Youth

Noah · September 13, 2019

This week's parsha introduces us to the Ben Sorer u Moreh, the rebellious son. The Torah tells us:

(18) If a man has a wayward and defiant son, who does not heed his father or mother and does not obey them even after they discipline him,

(19) his father and mother shall take hold of him and bring him out to the elders of his town at the public place of his community. (20) They shall say to the elders of his town, "This son of ours is disloyal and defiant; he does not heed us. He is a glutton and a drunkard."

(21) Thereupon the men of his town shall stone him to death. Thus you will sweep out evil from your midst: all Israel will hear and be afraid.

What is the concept of the Ben Sorer o Moreh adding to our Elul? What are we supposed to take from this before Yomim Noraim? Now is the time for us to start our Teshuva process and Ben Sorer U Moreh seems to teach us that there is a point of no return. On the surface, the Torah appears to say that a minor can become so immersed in sin that he cannot fix his ways.

There is an oft-quoted opinion in the Gemara that there never was a ben sorer u moreh and that this mitzvah exists solely for educational purposes. But this makes even less sense. Why would the Torah bother with a mitzva that seems to run counter to its ethical code if it was also unrealistic? What lesson are we meant to learn?

To better understand how this works we need to define exactly what is happening.

According to the Ibn Ezra the primary sins of the ben sorer u moreh are being a:

(2) drunkard; someone who drinks excessively, to the extent that he gets drunk. Such a person resembles a follower of Epicuros, for he seeks from the life of this world only pleasure, through all kinds of food and drink.

(1) glutton; is a general term, denoting someone who permits himself everything that he desires.

The Ibn Ezra's comments make it clear that there is an underlying attitude of a ben sorer u moreh. An obsession with pleasure and the lack of any boundaries seem to define this mindset.

Next, we must understand the justification for the killing:

The Mishna explains that the community kills the child for his own sake. "Better he dies innocent than live to be guilty. He will take his stand on the crossroads and rob people, and in some way or other make, himself liable to the death penalty. Says the Torah, "Let him die innocent of such crimes, and let him not die guilty of them."

The Mishnah is explaining that in the Ben Sorer u Moreh's current state he is blameless for the spiritual situation he is in. His family and community were responsible for him until now and their failings contributed to his deviation from the path. Like an infant, he does not bear the responsibility for his actions.

We are born with limitless potential. Our choices throughout life limit our ability to make further choices. While this is true in concrete matters like marriage and children, it is also true about our bechira (free choice) muscle. As we achieve higher levels in our service of God we strive to make our previous errors unthinkable to the improved us. Like a baal teshuva who, after 10 years of keeping kosher, finds no urge to enter the Mcdonalds he passes on the way to work, the same is true to the opposite extreme. When we choose to make the wrong decisions over and over again, our ability to make the right decision is further and further constrained.

The Ben Sorer u Moreh was allowed to reach a point in his obsession with physicality that even if he had a hirhur teshuva and wanted to change, his bechira muscle wouldn't be up to the task. While he could still connect to the arouse the deepest part of his soul, this connection would not be enough to raise his nekudas habechira to a point where he could live a productive life in service of God.

This point is further illustrated by a Gemara:

They said about Elazar ben Durdayya that he was so promiscuous that he did not leave one harlot in the world unvisited. Once, he heard that there was one in a city overseas who was the most expensive. He took a purse full of money and went and crossed seven rivers to reach her. When he arrived, she let out a breath and said: Just as this passed breath will not return to its place, so too Elazar ben Durdayya will not be accepted in repentance, even if he were to try to repent. This statement deeply shocked Elazar ben Durdayya, and he went and sat between two mountains and hills and said: Mountains and hills, pray for mercy on my behalf, so that my repentance will be accepted. They said to him: Before we pray for mercy on your behalf, we must pray for mercy on our own behalf, He said: Heaven and earth, pray for mercy on my behalf. They said to him: Before we pray for mercy on your behalf, we must pray for mercy on our own behalf. He asked the Sun and moon, Stars and constellations and they gave him the same answer. Elazar ben Durdayya said: Clearly the matter depends on nothing other than myself. He placed his head between his knees and cried loudly until his soul left his body. A Divine Voice emerged and said: Rabbi Elazar ben Durdayya is destined for life in the World-to-Come.

The Gemara explains that since he was attached so strongly to the sin, to an extent that transcended the physical temptation, he couldn't live even after doing teshuva.

I know I have a bit of ben sorer u moreh inside of myself. I engage in excess and lack the boundaries I should. I have been warned by my parents and my community and I have failed to change. What this week's parsha is telling me is inspiring but difficult. Like with R' Elazar ben Durdayya teshuva is always possible! But teshuva doesn't make things easy. It doesn't automatically turn me into a tzaddik and make all of my previous challenges vanish. As long as my life and values are rooted in excessive physicality the journey back to Hashem gets longer and harder and, left unchecked, can prevent me from ever reaching my potential. But while this is true, this Yom Kippur as soon as we realize the matter only in our hands, we can crawl into the fetal position and cry out "I want to be a child again! I want to return to my limitless potential untarnished by my mistakes!" And god willing a divine voice will answer.

Good Shabbos

Noah

Free Bird

August 20, 2021

This week's parsha includes the mitzvah of Shiluach Haken, the commandment to scare a mother bird away from the nest before taking its eggs.
