Should a bird's nest appear before you on the way, on any tree or on the earth, chicks or eggs, and the mother resting on the chicks or the eggs: You shall not take the mother with the offspring. You shall send away the mother, and take the offspring for yourself so that it be good for you, and your days be long.

This is one of the few mitzvot in the Torah that carries an explicit reward with it. This distinction makes it a central point for exploring the Torah's take on theodicy, or how God allows evil to exist in the world. While we are instructed to do many commandments without any defined reward and are encouraged to "be not like servants who serve the master in the expectation of receiving a reward but be like servants who serve the master without the expectation of receiving a reward," here we are given instruction with clear compensation.

Our standard approach to reward and merit is easier to integrate into the world we see around us. There are many beautiful benefits that come with following the Torah that is obvious and evident. It is clear that there are many rewards to be earned in this world. But at the same time, the abstractness of this promise makes the impoverished Torah scholar more understandable, God never promised him riches for following the Torah and he still enjoys the manifold other natural benefits that accompany his pious lifestyle. But with the mitzvah of Shiluach Haken, where there is a defined and explicit promise attached to the commandment, this can get much more complicated.

The gemara in Kiddushin recounts the event that drove Elisha ben Abuya to heresy: It occurred that there was one whose father said to him: Climb to the top of the building and fetch me chicks. And he climbed to the top of the building and dispatched the mother bird and took the young, thereby simultaneously fulfilling the mitzva to dispatch the mother bird from the nest and the mitzva to honor one's parents, but upon his return, he fell and died. Elisha ben Abuya was among the greatest sages of his generation and his faith was shattered by this event. What is the solution offered by his contemporaries? Rav Yosef said: Had Elisha ben Avuya, interpreted [the promise of long life] homiletically, as referring to the World-to-Come, he would not have sinned.

The only way for us to confront the randomness and lack of justice that we see all around us is to have faith that it is part of something bigger. While there are positive natural consequences of living an upright and faithful life, it is never a guarantee for prosperity or physical longevity. We must abandon our notion that moral accomplishment and material success are linked otherwise we risk being driven to heresy.

The Uncashed Check

Yosef · August 25, 2023

A poor man once approached a well-known philanthropist in shul to solicit his help. The man begged and pleaded, tears dripping from his eyes as he told of his terrible plight. His children were starving, his heat had been shut off, and his landlord was threatening to evict them. The wealthy man, kind-hearted and generous, was moved by the tale and reassured the beggar that he would take care of everything. He promised to give everything the man needed to get back on his feet – everything. There was just one caveat. The philanthropist’s policy was to only give sizable donations from the security of his own home, so he asked that the poor man meet him the following morning when a generous check would be waiting for him.

The following morning, the rich man waited in his study for his expected visitor, fully prepared to make all of the beggar’s dreams come true. But the clock ticked on and on until eventually the rich man could wait no longer, and he went on with his day’s affairs.

That night, the scene repeated itself, with the poor man pouring out his heart and pleading for mercy. The philanthropist was a little confused, but again assured the man that he would take care of everything if he would only visit in the morning. But alas, in the morning the wealthy man again waited as long as he could, but eventually had to go.

Over and over the same scene unfolded, until it reached the point that the philanthropist had no time to listen to the pleas. He knew already that the beggar could not be expected even to pick up the check of his salvation.

The Chofetz Chaim explains that this is a frightening depiction of our very own actions time after time. Not once but three times a day, we stand and pray to Hashem to help us improve. Especially in these days of Elul, we ask him to help us repent, to help us learn Torah, and to help us conquer our yetzer hora (evil inclination). Sometimes, in a moment of truth, we are even moved to tears, overwhelmed by our predicament and at a loss as to how we can continue.

At those times, Hashem, our most kind and generous benefactor, is moved and decides to give us all the help we desire. He ‘writes out the check’ and has all the divine assistance we could possibly want waiting for us. There’s just one caveat – we are expected to come and pick up the check. We have to wake up the next morning and apply ourselves to our learning, to our repentance, and to our spiritual battles. If we would just show up and do our part, we would find all the doors wide open for us, but we unfortunately neglect our post. Like the poor man in our story, we just can’t get ourselves to do what we have to do. And so, again, we cry and we plead, and again Hashem preps everything for us. But if we won’t show up, we cannot collect the handout and we are left in the same predicament as always.

So this Elul, let’s buck the trend. Let’s continue to pour out our hearts in the quest for spiritual success, but let us follow the request by completing our end of the deal. Let us make sure to do our part by redoubling our own efforts in the service of Hashem, and we can rest assured that, with tenacity, we will receive all the help that we need to succeed.

Good Shabbos, Yosef

Parshat

Ki Tavo

Avi · September 8, 2017

Ki Tavo opens with the mitzvah of Bikkurim, upon entering Israel there is a commandment to bring the first ripened fruits of your field to the Beit Hamikdash (Holy Temple) and declare gratitude for all that Hashem has done for you. The parsha continues to speak about the laws of Levite's tithes and the recitation of the blessings and curses proclaimed on Mount Gerizim and Mount Eival. The parsha concludes with rebuke and warning of illness, famine, drought, and poverty if we abandon the Torah.

Every morning, as we wake up, we say Modeh Ani "I offer thanks to You, O living and everlasting king, for having restored my soul within me; great is Your faithfulness." The Rebbe uses these simple twelve words of the Modeh Ani to delve into the nature of the omnipresence and all-pervasiveness of Hashem, and the principle of "perpetual creation." The Rebbe wondered how can we offer our thanks in our beds, while wearing pajamas, still groggy and without even a sip of coffee. Wouldn't our gratitude be greater after a period of preparation and contemplation? Why are we so meticulous to say this the moment we resurface?

Throughout a day we experience many different levels of consciousness dictated by specific times and settings. Throughout the day the conscious mind filters out stimuli and issues commands to the body. At night our command center diminishes to a place where fantasy supersedes logic, sense supplants thought, and awareness is replaced by a more elemental form of knowing. Certain truths withstand these fluctuations; our faith in hashem, his centrality to our being, and the depth of our commitment to him. These things we know absolutely, through all stages of consciousness.

What we know with the very essence of our being, remains even when plunged into the deepest recesses of slumber. In fact, the opposite is true; while awake we must wade through the vast complexity of our reality to arrive at truth, while sleeping we enjoy a deeper more intimate awareness of our innermost persuasions. The Modeh Ani is placed at a crucial moment, on the threshold of morning wakefulness. While emerging from the midnight dream of repose we are blessed to express some of our innermost creedences.

Bikkurim like Modeh Ani is a declaration of indebtedness and gratitude to hashem. In the 26th chapter of Deuteronomy, the Torah instructs: It shall be, when you come in to the land which the L-rd your G-d is giving you for an inheritance, and you will possess it and settle in it that you shall take from the first of the fruits of the land . . . and place them in a basket; and you shall go to the place that the L-rd your G-d will choose to rest His name there. In his proclamation, the bikkurim-bearing farmer goes on to recount the story of our liberation from Egypt and Hashem's gift to us of "a land flowing with milk and honey," The Talmud and the Sifri disagree on when Bnei Yisrael began to bring the Bikkurim. Fourteen years elapsed between the jews entering israel and the land being properly divided. The talmud states that the verse specifies to bring bikkurim "when you come into the land . . . and you will possess it and settle in it" this teaches us that the first fruits of the land should be presented to Hashem only after the land has been fully conquered and divided amongst the tribes.

On the other hand, the Sifri puts emphasis on the beginning of the phrase "and it shall be when you come into the land" to imply that bikkurim be brought immediately upon arrival in Eretz Yisroel. The Sifri bases this of the word "vehaya"- "and it shall be" this is indicative of an event taking place immediately. Because Bikkurim must be brought by a farmer who rightfully owns his land, according to the Sifri, Bikkurim were brought by the family of Yitro who inherited jericho, the first settlement conquered by Yehoshua. Yitro gained his estate by aligning his fate to that of Bnei yisroel.

The Talmud's conception of bikkurim is that true appreciation is only reached through fully understanding and living through the goodness we are grateful for. Without a grasp of each intention that is put into a favor one cannot properly even describe the magnitude of what they've received.

The Sifri puts higher value on praise that arrives with the dawn of our profound transition. Believing that the awe inspiring nature of a burgeoning experience is greater and more wondrous than the praise of a seasoned veteran.

Crossing the borders between slumber and wakefulness, desert and zion, expectations and the good life are paramount experiences. Times of transition are opportunities to specify who you are and what we want to be, we are blessed every morning with a prayer to say what we already know, my soul is boundless and I am lucky to have a loving king to return it to me in the morning and take it from me at night. May Hashem's presence return to Jerusalem speedily and in our days.

-Avi

The learning should be a merit for Yonatan ben Avraham Avinu a"h

Questions and comments can be sent to freedmanyosef@gmail.com

Uphold the Torah

Noah · August 31, 2018

At the end of this week’s parsha, we find the pasuk, “cursed is the one who doesn’t uphold the words of the Torah.” This warning is provided to the Jewish people at the of the series of blessings and curses that were given at Har Grizim/Har Eival. At its face value, the curse seems to apply to anyone who disregards any of the commandments. However, the Ramban interprets this curse much more specifically. He learns, based on a Gemara Yerushalmi in Masechet Sotah, that this curse is directed at a chazan that does not do hagbah, or the lifting and displaying of the Torah. Many poskim use the words of the Ramban to prove that this mitzvah is of biblical origin or, “close to biblical.” Let’s understand more about the origins, importance, and variations of this commandment.

Traditionally done by Sefardim at the beginning of Kriyas HaTorah and Ashkenazim at the end, this ceremonial display is normally accompanied by congregation pointing, often with their pinkies, at the spinning text. The Gemara in Masechet Soferim is the primary source for this custom.

מסכת סופרים יד:יד

מיד גולל ספר תורה עד שלשה דפין ומגביהו ומראה פני כתיבתו לעם העומדים לימינו ולשמאלו ומחזירו לפניו ולאחריו שמצוה לכל האנשים ולנשים לראות הכתב ולכרוע ולומר (דברים ד) וזאת התורה אשר שם משה לפני בני ישראל

[The Chazan] Immediately unrolls the Sefer Torah, revealing 3 sections of parchment which he shows to the assembled congregation to his left right front and back. [This is because] it is a mitzvah for all of the men and women to see the writing and to bow and say, “That is the Torah that Moshe placed before the Children of Israel.

The Shulchan Aruch quotes this Gemara almost verbatim when presenting the Halacha. The Mishnah Berurah does not argue and only adds that it is beneficial for a person to see the letters of the Torah as the kabbalists write that it pulls a great light onto the person.

Immediately these sources contradict the prevailing minhag. Very rarely do we see people bow during this part of the service, instead pointing has taken over which is not mentioned at all. Most authorities hold there is a commandment to at least bow the head, but the Ramo mentions the minhag recorded by the Maharil to prostrate oneself fully during hagbah. If this was such a foundational part of the service, why is it not practiced nowadays?

Rav Asher Weiss brings the Shiltei HaGiborim on Kiddushin 14b quoting the Riaz to justify the practice of not bowing in today’s age:

קידושין ל"ג ע"ב:

וחייבים לעמוד מפני הס"ת "אם מפני לומדיה עומדים מפניה לא כ"ש" ונראה בעיני שלא אמרו אלא לעמוד מפני התורה ולא להשתחוות לה ולא נמצא בכל התורה שמשתחוין אפילו לארון הקדש

One is obligated to stand before a Sefer Torah. "If we have to in front of those who learn it, all the more so before it" (Kiddushin 33b). But it appears in my eyes they only said to stand, but not to bow and in the whole Torah we never saw an idea to bow, not even to the Aron Kodesh.

R’ Yaakov Castro מהריק"ש however, disagrees:

א. אין כונת הריא"ז אלא שאין חיוב השתחויה אלא חיוב עמידה בלבד אבל ודאי ראוי אף לשיטתו לכרוע.

The Riaz was only implying that there is not an obligation to prostrate oneself in front of a Sefer Torah but it is still a fitting thing to do.

The Birchei Yosef also disagrees with the Riaz saying:

והברכי יוסף כתב דהריא"ז מיירי רק ברואה ס"ת עובר, ולא אמרו חכמים אלא שחייב לעמוד ולא לכרוע, אבל בשעה שרואה את הכתב אכן נהגו לכרוע וכך ראוי, עי"ש

The Riaz’s Psak only applied in a case where a Torah was passing, not when the writing was held visible. In such a case one certainly should bow.

Rabbi Asher Weiss accepts these refutations and says that today we should still be bowing during this auspicious time. This sheds light onto the lost custom of bowing but where did pointing come from and why the pinkie?

The Me'am Lo'ez quoted by R' Chayim Falaji point to a Medrash on Shir HaShirim as the source:

שיר השירים רבה ב:ד

“וְדִגְלוֹ עָלַי אַהֲבָה"

אָמַר רַבִּי חוּנְיָא לְשֶׁעָבַר אָדָם מַרְאֶה אִיקוֹנִין בְּאֶצְבָּעוֹ וְהָיָה נִזּוֹק, וְעַכְשָׁיו אָדָם מַנִּיחַ יָדוֹ עַל הָאַזְכָּרָה כַּמָּה פְּעָמִים וְאֵינוֹ נִזּוֹק, וְלֹא עוֹד אֶלָּא, שֶׁאָמַר הַקָּדוֹשׁ בָּרוּךְ הוּא וְגוּדָלוֹ עָלַי אַהֲבָה

The Pasuk says, “And his banner of love was over me.” The Medrash explains, “In the past, if a person displayed an image of the king on his finger he would be punished. Now a person places his hands on the king's insignia many times and is not hurt. Moreover, Hashem declares, “Vdiglo Alai Ahava.” Do not read the verse “vdiglo” meaning banner but rather “vgodlo” meaning pointer finger.

From here we see that we show appreciation for our ability to serve God by pointing at the Torah. But this scriptural source for applies specifically to the pointer finger.

A second source is offered as well:

תנא דבי רבי ישמעאל שלשה דברים היו קשין לו למשה עד שהראה לו הקב"ה באצבעו ואלו הן מנורה וראש חדש ושרצים מנורה דכתיב (במדבר ח, ד) וזה מעשה המנורה ראש חודש דכתיב (שמות יב, ב) החודש הזה לכם ראש חדשים שרצים דכתיב (ויקרא יא, כט) וזה לכם הטמא ויש אומרים אף הלכות שחיטה שנאמר (שמות כט, לח) וזה אשר תעשה על המזבח:

Gemara in Menachos 29a says: 3 things were called “Zeh” because it was difficult for Moshe to understand God's directive. Menorah, Rosh Chodesh, Sheratzim. In all these cases the Rabbis taught that Zeh means Hashem “pointed at the object with his finger.” So too, when we say V’Zos Hatorah we should point.

Like the previous source, this analogy of God pointing is understood to be referring to a metaphorical pointer finger. The scriptural significance of using the correct finger is such that R’ Chaim Kanievsky addressed this question and said that the original Minhag was with the index finger and that it is correct. There are other variations of Hagba behavior that have deeply rooted sources but none of them seem to advise pointing with the pinkie. So where did this custom originate? I could not find a definitive answer but someone who did a bit of research on the subject claimed that the reason people used their pinkie was that their pointer and thumb were reserved for snuff/snus during services and pointing at the Torah with a tobacco covered finger was deemed disrespectful.

With this deeper understanding of Hagbah, we should all merit to pull the spiritual light mentioned by the Mishnah Berurah. Our practice allows us to become a part of the chain of great sages that have upheld the Torah throughout the generations. And through the lens of even this individual mitzvah, we can understand that all of the holy debate is the greatest fulfillment of upholding the Torah imaginable.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Natural Consequences

Avi · September 20, 2019

In this week's Parsha, Moshe addresses the nation outside the land of Israel, instructing them to bring baskets of their first fruits to Jerusalem, as offerings of gratitude for the priests and for the poor. Moshe reminds us we are the chosen people and therefore we are the people who chose God, he reminds us of God's covenant and our inheritance of the land of Israel. Then we are rebuked, reminded of our shortcomings and their repercussions, The final verse of curses reading; And G-d will bring you back to Egypt in ships, through the way about which I had said to you, 'you will never see it again...'"

When we were brought out of Egypt there was a general understanding that it was a one-way flight, in fact before the splitting of the sea Moshe declares "Don't be afraid! Stand firm and see Gd's salvation that He will wreak for you today, for ... you have seen the Egyptians today, [but] you shall no longer...see them for eternity.'" This is what the sages interpret as a direct commandment and not just a prophecy. This is the first time we are commanded not to return to Egypt.

The second time we are commanded not to return to Egypt is in Parsha Shoftim in the description of a Jewish king. A Jewish king is only allowed a few chariots. Egypt has a world-famous horse market, and Egyptians put their trust in chariots and their armed riders. Obviously a Jewish king may not, he should have a humble fleet and trust in God, because the temptation may lead him to want to return to Egypt the very place he was brought out from.

Finally, at the end of our Parsha, our commandment comes as a threat. If you don't fulfill mitzvos I will ship you back to where you came. there is a clear escalation, our first warning in celebration of our departure- let it be permanent to avoid future suffering. The second warning is in response to horse envy,-how could we come to idealize the place that enslaved us? The third warning finally a threat- if you neglect the Torah and its commandments, God says I will put you where I commanded you not to be, and return you to a land of slavery and temptation.

It is needless to say that our Father in Heaven is a strong believer in natural consequences. If we insist to undo our redemption by ignoring our commandments we will come to regret our rescue and wish we were back in Egypt.

Good Shabbos, Avi

Avi · September 4, 2020

This week's Parsha is Ki Tavo meaning "when you enter". Moshe Tells the nation about the laws of Bikkurim - first fruits. The Torah instructs us to put the fruits in a basket and bring them to the Beit Hamikdash "Israel is a land of wheat, barley, grapevines, figs and pomegranates; a land of oil olives and date honey." For a farmer to make sure he brought the first of these fruits to the beit hamikdash he had to be very diligent and designate the first bud in a way that he could tell it apart from the rest of his harvest.

The seven species coincide with the seven Midot, Just like a farmer's fruits our character traits must be cultivated or else they may overgrow with tumbleweeds. In the process of cultivating Midot we learn about who we are and what we love. like a farmer we can take pride in not only sustaining ourselves, but in filling our neighbors with what we have so much of. In turn the farmer knows even in the best of times he cannot live on his single crop, it is of high importance for him to make relationships and learn about other crops. While it is important to recognize our strong suit, often our most powerful trait turns into our biggest downfall. This is why we are instructed to put our fruits in a basket, we display our whole character, all of our traits, the untamed ones along with our very favorite.

In carrying the basket to the Bait Hamikdash we see everyone else with their baskets of huge pomegranates and wheat that are little and yellow, or sweet dates and bitter olives. Through this experience we will be proud of our niche and have plenty of inspiration to move outside our niche.

All Good Things

Yosef · August 27, 2021

In the middle of a long and harrowing list of the curses which will befall the Jewish nation during our sojourn in the exile, the Torah spells out for us the reason for the curse. 'Because you did not serve Hashem your G-d with joy and a good heart from a position of all good things.' Simply rendered, this is saying that if, when showered with all good things, we lose sight of Hashem, then the good will be taken away. Hashem showers us with so much blessing and he expects that we will use those blessings as catalysts to grow in our love and fear of G-d. We are meant to utilize the myriad presents which Hashem gives us to recognize Him more and to redouble our efforts in His service.

Too often, though, all the blessings and good of this world just serve as distractions that stand between us and Hashem. When that happens, our loving Father takes away the blessing and delivers a slap to get us back on track and refocused on the true purpose of life.

This lesson is certainly true and important, but Rabbi Meir Simcha of D'vinsk takes it one step further. He explains that, when read in a different light, the Torah is actually coming to reveal what exactly it was that led us astray. The reason that you did not serve Hashem, says the Torah, is because you derived your 'joy and good heart' from 'all good things'. Your sense of happiness and fulfillment came as a result of your physical possessions and earthly pleasures. This is not the Jewish way of life. Quite the opposite; a Torah Jew finds 'all good things' sourced in his 'joy and good heart'. He may not have much in the way of physical possessions or pleasures, but his internal happiness makes him lack nothing. In effect, he indeed has 'all good things', but it's not confined or decided by how much stuff he has, rather by the wellsprings of happiness that he finds deep inside his soul. Like it says in Pirkei Avot, 'Who is truly rich? One who is happy with his lot.'

When we make the silly mistake of thinking that our success and happiness in this world must be defined in terms of worldly achievement and good times, we have already begun our descent away from Hashem. This is a hoax, and we all know it to be a hoax, yet somehow the whole world runs on this mistake. If you ask anyone in an honest moment if material possessions and pleasure seeking is able to bring happiness, I don't believe you will find a single intelligent person who will answer in the affirmative. But just the same, you'll find him a minute later chasing the American dream like all the rest. And you'll find me too; each in our own way and to differing degrees. It would seem like our knowledge of this falsehood is still not clear enough to govern our behavior.

But this lifestyle is completely antithetical to the service of our Creator. As long as we perceive, consciously or not, that happiness comes from worldly pursuits, they will always stand in the way of our true purpose in life. Torah learning will be a challenge because it takes away time from recreation and fun. Charity will be a challenge because it diminishes our net worth. Shabbos will be a challenge because we can't earn any money on Shabbos. And the list goes on and on. Such a worldview will inevitably curtail our service of G-d, besides for the fact that we will never achieve the imaginary dream which we so covet.

If we want to be able to live a life of godliness and of true happiness, then we need to deeply internalize and integrate the Torah paradigm of 'wealth' into our lives. We need to realize that it's not stuff that brings happiness, but happiness which reveals to us how much blessing we truly possess. Under that light, the pursuit of happiness doesn't stand in the way of our service of Hashem, but encourages it as a source of inspiration and fulfillment. And, as a bonus, when material success will no longer pose a threat to our Torah observance, there's no reason our loving Father shouldn't grant us a healthy dose of worldly blessings as well.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

The Free Ride

Yosef · September 1, 2023

This summer, my family and I took a wonderful trip to visit family in Denver. Of course, a major highlight of the trip was visiting the Rocky Mountains, where we spent two beautiful day trips. Since we were a large group, it was important to us to find free or inexpensive trips, and we were thrilled to find two such free experiences in the Rockies.

The first was Red Rocks, a world-famous venue with the most incredible rock formations colored a deep red hue, as its name would suggest. There we found a public porch big enough for our entire party, with stunning views of the park, complete with tables and chairs; but for some reason it was completely empty – as if reserved for us. We set up camp and enjoyed dinner there, wondering aloud about how we had chanced upon such a dining experience completely free of charge.

Our next trip was to Breckenridge, where there is a free cable car to the top of the mountain. Everyone enjoyed to views and the ride, again marveling at the fact that we could do this for free. From there we drove to Blue Lakes Trail, a place of otherworldly beauty and splendor. My words cannot possibly describe the magnificence of this mountain lake and waterfall, sitting almost as high as the timberline, where we spent two unforgettable hours in ecstasy. And again, we felt fortunate to be enjoying all of this completely free of charge.

A wonderful vacation. Great times. And life goes on.

Back home in Los Angeles, I was reminded of a statement from Rav Yisroel Salanter that got me thinking. He said that this world is like a very expensive hotel. If one drinks a cup of coffee at home it costs a few cents, in a cheap restaurant it’s a dollar or two, in a fancy coffee house it might cost five dollars, and in a world-class hotel it could cost ten. Why do the prices differ so much? Because you’re not just paying for the coffee, but for the whole setting – the ambiance, the décor, the service, the music, and so on.

If the ambiance in a hotel elevates the coffee to ten dollars, how much does Hashem’s world add to the price tag? Let’s go back to the coffee you’re drinking in your kitchen or your back porch for a few cents. All around you there is sweet, life-giving oxygen. There are birds chirping in the background and a gentle breeze rustling through the assortment of trees. Perhaps your children or grandchildren are playing in the background, and pictures of your family grace the walls. The sun might be shining brightly, or perhaps there’s a fresh blanket of snow all around. Your hands effortlessly lift the coffee and your lips adjust perfectly to the curvature of your mug. And of course, we all understand that this extensive list hasn’t even scratched the surface.

So, let’s revisit the question. How much is that cup of coffee worth, backdrop included?

Priceless. Truly priceless.

That ‘simple’ cup of coffee is worth more than any mortal fortune. In fact, Rav Yisroel says that if we would spend our entire lives serving the Creator of all that goodness, we could never repay Him for even that one cup of coffee. He warns that with one worldly pleasure we are at risk of consuming the entire reward waiting for us in the next world!

This is a very alarming statement from one well-qualified to make such assertions. For one pleasure? What then is one to do?!

And if this is true of a ‘simple’ cup of coffee in my own back yard, what kind of bill does a dinner overlooking Red Rocks rack up? Or an afternoon at Blue Lakes? Or a cable car in Breckenridge? There truly is no such thing as a free ride, and these things are far far beyond our means.

Thankfully, Rav Yisroel didn’t leave it up to us to devise a plan of action; he finished off the hotel analogy with a lifeline for all of us. If one is on a business trip and the company puts him up at the hotel, he can enjoy all of the amenities without a worry about the bill. After all, he’s working for the company and all of his expenses are on the house.

This is our lifeline. If our lives are centered on doing the will of our Creator, if we see ourselves as ambassadors for Hashem, if our ultimate purpose in life is Torah and mitzvos, then we are working for the company. As an emissary of Hashem, we can enjoy all the pleasures which we cannot begin to afford, and it’s all on the house.

And perhaps we can add an additional idea. We certainly should strive for Rav Yisroel’s ideal of living our entire lives focused on the service of Hashem, but if we fall short, I think there is a more case by case tactic that may help us. Say that one isn’t working for the company, but he receives a sample in exchange for writing a review. This would also be free of charge, since his enjoyment is of interest to the manufacturer. If we can utilize the enjoyments of life as vehicles to increase our gratitude to Hashem and as catalysts to renewed and reinvigorated service of Hashem, then each individual pleasure is of interest to Hashem, and is thus provided free of charge.

So this Elul, let’s make sure we’re working for the right company by centering our lives around Torah and mitzvos. Let us seize every pleasure, from the daily joys to the one-time experiences, as a means of growing closer to Hashem. And as such, let us truly enjoy life, while secure in the knowledge that it’s all on the bill of the Almighty.

Good Shabbos,

Yosef

Parshat

Nitzavim-Vayelech

Unity and Redemption

Avi · September 9, 2018

Parshat Nitzavim is full of subtle concepts regarding the unity of israel, our future redemption, and free choice. Especially mentioned in this parsha is the practicality of Torah and mitzvot. “For the mitzvah which I command you this day, it is not beyond you, nor is it remote from you. It is not in heaven . . . It is not across the sea . . . Rather, it is very close to you, in your mouth, in your heart, that you may do it.

This comes to teach us many things about the right mindsets and behaviors a jew should have. in a slighter way this statement implies that the Torah is in the comfort of your own home, can be heard on the street, and is breathed into you by an angel. In other words the torah is in your heart weather you like it or not and mitzvot can be performed in ones sleep. Of course these things are true, however as a jew in exile it is easy to forget about all the active mindsets really is and how it allows for a higher standard for living.

Rabbi Yeshua ben Chanania said, “once a kid got the best of me, I was walking on the outskirts of town when I met him. Upon asking for directions he said, “this is short and long way , and this way is long and short.”

Rebbe Chanania took the “short and long way” and as the town grew closer he looked up and found himself in a thicket of private orchards and gardens. He had to retrace back to where he found the boy. The Rabbi asked the child didn't you say this is the short way? The boy responded - “did I not tell you it is also long?”

While Rosh Hashanah is around the corner, jews around the world wait for the book of life to be opened. Our lot will be up for reconsideration and our opportunities and success for the year will be scribed in stone. Right now we can make a personal revolution, there will be time to reflect and polish our image in the coming days. In doing this we must take a lesson from our R’ Chanania who once took the “short long way” it is a basic tenant in chassidus that the mind corresponds to our human attributes and heart to our pulsing blood and our animal spirit (or spirit animal). By prioritizing these two functions we make the single biggest stride into our good life.

Because of this innate superiority the mind has over the heart, upon learning godly concepts and seeing them in the world our mind opens the gates for the heart to experience these enhanced more complicated feelings. As we age and learn more about our world we teach our heart to feel more about the world. We can accept a bigger picture now that we are all older.

This is only made possible when we put our mind before our heart. In the story the Rabbis heart was leading the way, This shortsightedness led him to a sign that he is impatient mind could read. When we try to go the boys first “short long way” we usually don't realize until we find a NO TRESPASSING sign. Sometimes we will find our simple lessons in hindsight, only after tedious repetition. This Rosh Hashanah instead of sneaking through the orchard or even asking the property manager to make an exception. Own up, trace and retrace find the child inside you - and shake him. Ask him, Didn't you tell me this was the short way‽

This year we have the opportunity to visualize a successful future, organize our priorities and prioritize organization, use the scaffolding set out to us by our teachers and begin living the good life.

Shana Tova, Avi

Creepy Crawlies and Confession

Noah · September 14, 2018

This week is Shabbos Teshuva, the Shabbos between Rosh Hashana and Yom Kippur during Eseres Yemi Teshuva. It is a holy and awesome time with tremendous potential for repentance. With the strong themes of Teshuva that surround us, we should review exactly what it is.

The Rambam explains that there are four essential components to this process:

• The transgressor stops doing the transgression and removes it from his thoughts.

• He resolves that he will not do it again in the future …

• He regrets [that which he did] in the past …

• He verbally expresses his wrongdoings to God and makes a verbal resolution for the future.

• Regarding wrongdoings against one’s fellow man: one must reimburse whatever he owes him, and ask the other person’s forgiveness.

The Rambam explains that:

“He who confesses by speech but has not his heart's consent to abandon his erstwhile conduct, behold him, he is like one taking an immersion of purification and in his grasp is an impure creeping thing, when he knows the immersion to be of no value till he cast away the impure creeping thing.”

This parable to a immersion with a bug has become a common idiom within the Torah world but the Rambam does not use it in the vernacular, he is understood to mean it quite literally. If this is the case, we must investigate the laws regarding one who immerses with a creepy crawly to better understand the role of confession in the Teshuva process.

There are two ways to understand the spiritual mechanics that prevent this immersion for having its purifying effect. The first, presented by Rabenu Yona and affirmed by R’ Chaim Soloveitchik based on a Gemara Yerushalmi, is that this immersion never works at all and is unable to interrupt the original impurity bestowed by the creepy crawly. According to this understanding, the immersion has no purifying effect whatsoever. The Rokeach however, disputes this understanding. He holds that in the same manner that the offerings of the wicked maintain their status as offerings, this immersion is still an immersion. The external problem of there being a creepy crawly does not affect the purifying power of immersion at all, it just immediately returns the subject to a state of impurity.

To return to our original case of confession without sincere intent to abandon the sin, we can see two different understandings emerge. According to Rabbenu Yona and R’ Chaim, the confession in such a case is not even considered a confession, while the Rokeach’s understanding validates this as confession albeit one that doesn’t result in full Teshuva.

What are we to do? Leaving our old ways behind, turning a new page, starting fresh, is so hard. Deep down we long to return to our destructive habits, to our impulses and desires. When we stand in reverence on Yom Kippur do we need to be concerned that because we have not yet learned to silence our Yetzer Hara our Teshuva is at stake? How can we ensure that our confession is sincere while acknowledging that truly acquiring our “heart’s consent” can be easier said than done?

To explain the value of verbal confession even before the heart’s tacit consent is acquired, the Yad HaKetana points to another Halacha in the Rambam. Regarding men who refuse to issue divorces to their wives, the Rambam writes that the courts, “beat him until he says he desires to give the divorce.” There is a concept that unless the divorce is given out of free will it is invalid. How does this restriction fit with the Rambam's advice to coerce this bad actor? The Rambam explains that deep down every Jew has the burning will to do what is right. In this case, the man is just being overcome by his evil inclination, something external to his own will. When the courts are beating him into a better mindsight they are not changing his will, rather they are helping him reveal a deeper more sincere will that he has lost connection to.

This same concept applies to our verbal confessions and our heart’s consent. Sure we may still face an inclination to return our sin, but this is not a product of our true will. This is an externality that is merely preventing us from realizing our higher convictions and commitments. From this perspective, even if our hearts are still attached on some level to our past misdeeds this is not a product of our will. Therefore, our verbal confessions hold even more value. The confession transforms our soul's silent yearning to improve into a real physical expression of regret and longing. The confession becomes a tool to expose our deeper pure will and help draw it to the surface.

This Yom Kippur, we are going to hear the same negative voices in our heads who question our ability to change. They ask us if we are really ready to abandon the indulgences we have grown to depend on. They will tell us that our efforts to return are insincere as we still desire our old lives. These voices will convince us that they are us, that they represent our will. At these moments we need to remember the Rambam and Rabenu Yona as they offer the best retorts. Rabenu Yona reminds us to tell this voice that even if we do still long for our old ways, this does not invalidate our confession. And the Rambam reminds us to tell the evil inclination that you are not me! I have a soul that longs for closeness and eternal life, I may get distracted but this spark is truly my defining characteristic. With this in mind, may we all merit to silence the prosecutors on this coming Yom Kippur and have a Gmar Chasima Tova.

Shabbat Shalom, Noah

Shira · September 27, 2019

This week's Torah Portion, Nitzavim, has a beautiful paragraph referring to where Torah can be found.

It clarifies that it is not a the top of a mountain nor at the bottom of the sea; it is actually very close. Torah exists in our hearts and minds.

To put in perspective how revolutionary this concept is, I'd like to point out that until not very long ago, everything took a lot of work. Fields had to be plowed to allow room for the seeds to be planted. Water had to be drawn from wells and fetched from streams and rivers. Everything necessary for physical survival was very difficult and took a lot of time and energy.

After exerting so much just to sustain daily life, it's very plausible that one might say that he has no time left to focus on spirituality. Only when all of his needs are taken care of, he may begin to connect to Hashem.

But we all know how that goes. The day never grows more hours and our workloads only increase as we age. We may face the terrifying possibility that years have passed and our souls are woefully undeveloped.

So Hashem tells us: do your work. Tend to your fields. Don't delay connecting and praying, because you can do both at once.

Follow the laws of gardening, keep your thoughts pure and holy, pray while you work. Don't separate the spiritual and the physical because you live in a world that is made of up of both and your body itself is the meeting of these two realms.

Our Rabbis teach us that if you do holy things even while you need to be in a mundane world, even the mundane will become holy.

Uncle Yonatan was a living example of this. He constantly had conversations about science that morphed into questions about Jewish Law. He took walks with prayers on his lips. He found a way to make every aspect of his life elevated, and we know that in Heaven he is enjoying an elevated place near The Holy Throne of Hashem.

The Inspiration to Return

Yosef · September 11, 2020

"And it will be, when all of these things befall you, the blessing and the curse which I have placed before you… and you will return to Hashem, your G-d…"

As we near the end of the month of Elul, the time allotted us to do teshuva and return to Hashem, it is high time that we all make a serious reckoning of how we are fulfilling our mandate. The Torah tells us two of the ways in which our Loving Father aids us in reaching this lofty goal.

The second is one that is easily understood. When a person finds himself on the receiving end of ‘the curse which I have placed before you’, it can be a powerful impetus to change his ways. After a large degree of suffering, there usually comes a point when we tire of pointing fingers, blaming happenstance, and feeling sorry for ourselves. There comes a point when we decide to accept responsibility for our misfortunes and focus inwards towards fixing our faults towards Heaven and towards our fellowman.

The first, however, is less understood and less commonly taken advantage of, but no less effective. That is when we look around us and see an abundance of ‘the blessing which I have placed before you’. When we realize the overwhelming blessings which we enjoy in every facet of life, there is also usually a point when the thinking individual is forced to acknowledge that it is not his intellect and talents which have got him where he is, but rather the hand of G-d. When this reality comes into focus, it is also meant to bring us to teshuva. Whether it comes from a feeling of gratitude, or whether from a feeling of embarrassment; when we stop to think about the tremendous debt which we owe to Hashem it moves us to reach higher.

On Rosh Hashana, we stand at a crossroads of unspeakable trepidation and of equally thrilling opportunity. The entire year which lies before us will be decided on this greatest of days. Our future and the future of the whole world hangs in the balance. There is simply too much at stake to be taken lightly.

This, my friends, is true every year. But this year the message has never rang more clearly. Who can ignore the chilling outcome of last Rosh Hashana? Are we blind to the implications of ‘the curse which I have placed before you’? How many must perish, how many must suffer, and how much must fall into disarray for us to finally get the message? Looking back at the awesome power of last Rosh Hashana’s decision should be plenty to inspire us to teshuva.

And at the same time, I think all of us who are privy to be sitting in our homes - safe, healthy, and comfortable, must be struck by the great ‘blessing which I have placed before you.’ Across the globe, millions have passed away before their time, tens of millions have lost their livelihoods, and still, more are living in a constant state of turmoil, loneliness, and fear. If we can look at our lives and find that we have escaped, to whatever degree, any of these problems, shouldn’t we be overcome with the debt we owe to Hashem? Shouldn’t a feeling of gratitude propels us to cross fire and water in the service of our Great Benefactor? Shouldn’t we be terribly ashamed of our shortcomings in the face of His benevolence?

This Rosh Hashana, we have been equipped to respond to the call of Teshuva unlike we have ever been before. Let us seize the opportunity, tap into the ideas mentioned in our Parshah and highlighted by current events, and return to Hashem with our entire being.

Good Shabbos and K’tiva Vachatima Tovah,

Yosef

2021

Nitzavim means "Ones Standing" in this week's Parsha Bnai Yisroel is reminded of the unity of Israel, the future redemption, the practicality of Torah, and our freedom of choice. With the high holidays approaching, these verses remind us of our covenant with God.

"You are all standing this day before the Lord, your God the leaders of your tribes, your elders and your officers, every man of Israel. your young children, your women, and your convert who is within your camp both your woodcutters and your water drawers." The unity of Israel is bound not only by the authority of leaders but by familiar ties and working together. These relationships help hold us accountable to our covenant with God.

Moshe forwarns Bnai Yisrael of the exile and destruction that will grip us if we abandon our covenant. "You will return to the L-rd your God... If your outcasts shall be at the ends of the heavens, from there will the L-rd your G-d gather you . . . and bring you into the Land which your fathers have possessed." Raging wildfires, hurricanes and coronavirus have destroyed so much but our covenant with God is still intact. Though we may be scattered our interconnectivity is beyond that of any other era. We are responsible to fix for the destruction we see.

"For the Mitzvah which I command you this day, it is not beyond you, nor is it remote from you. It is not in heaven . . . It is not across the sea . . . Rather, it is very close to you, in your mouth, in your heart, that you may do it." The practicality of Torah is highlighted in this Parsha. This lesson is especially meaningful before the high holidays when we may feel inadequate or unworthy. Torah is an earthly endeavour for those who stand among us.

Choosing Torah is as easy as choosing life. "I have set before you life and goodness, and death and evil: in that I command you this day to love God, to walk in His ways and to keep His commandments . . . Life and death I have set before you, blessing and curse. And you shall choose life." Soon we will gather with our communities and stand before God in judgment, may we all be inscribed in the book of life and blessed with a year of community, a year free of wildfire smoke, hurricane winds and coronavirus. May we be blessed with the insight to see Torah everywhere so we may love God and choose life.

My King, My Beloved

Yosef · September 23, 2022

Rosh Hashanah, besides for being the Day of Judgment, is also a day of Kingship. In fact, the machzor is almost devoid of any mention of our personal actions, and is rather focused on declaring and recognizing the Kingship of Hashem. The message here is that the way to earn a favorable judgment is not so much by examining our past deeds as by aligning ourselves and our futures with the Kingdom of Hashem. To the degree that we can, today, lift ourselves up and and join the ranks of the servants army of the King, to that degree will we merit a year of life and prosperity. Of course, past deeds will not be forgotten and it is imperative that we do repent and fix ourselves, but that is not the focal point of Rosh Hashanah but of Yom Kippur.

On that note, I'd like to share a perspective of what the Kingdom of Hashem is, and how it differs from the mortal kingdoms of yore.

While we tend to think of monarchy as a system catering to the king's benefit, with him tending to the people only for the sake of his own betterment, this cannot possibly be the system of divine kingship. Firstly, as the Omnipotent Creator of the universe, Hashem lacks nothing and does not at all need us to be His servants. Secondly, we find that the relationship of the Jewish people with Hashem is additionally described as 'אני לדודי ודודי לי', 'I am to my beloved and my beloved is to me.' This adds a dimension of marriage to the relationship we have with Hashem, as dodi refers to a spouse.

Marriage is unlike any other partnership known to man. Business partners, employers and employees, investors and the like are also partners, but they partner on a much more superficial level. Let's take the business partners for example. Each cares to earn his own living and to lead his own life irrespective of his partner. Only that this one is business minded and this one is artistically gifted, so they join forces to run their business. They are unified only in what they do, in the execution, but in their deeper wills and aspirations, they are as separate as strangers.

The partnership of marriage, however, is on a whole different plane. A husband and wife are partners in their very essence. They seek not their personal good, but the good of the couple. When one is in pain, it pains their spouse, and when one meets success it is the success of both. Their bond is not one of action and execution, but one of self and soul.

And so is the union of the Jew with Hashem. We don't just serve Hashem in action, and He does not just provide us what we need in order to serve him. Our very essence and our very lives are rooted in and centered around the same thing. That purpose which we are mutually centered around is the revelation of Hashem's glory in the world. It is for this purpose alone that we exist, making it our personal destiny and deepest desire (though we may sometimes allow distractions to stand in the way of that desire).

This perspective has a profound impact on how we relate to the world and the circumstances which come our way. Even someone who is a loyal Jew, focused on the service of his Creator, could make the following mistake. When a circumstance comes his way, especially a hardship, he is liable to understand that the circumstance came for it's own purpose. Only now that it is here, he understands the need to do his best to serve Hashem even in the circumstance. In light of the above, however, this perspective is flawed. It would be true of a business type relationship where the action, in this case the revelation of Hashem's glory, is the extent of the relationship. But because our union with Hashem is like that of marriage, it means that the circumstance is part of man's essence as well. The circumstance came for the express purpose of allowing him the opportunity to reveal G-d's glory in this moment, and thereby achieve his own fulfillment.

If we would see the world through such a lens it would rid us of a lot of the frustration which comes with life's ups and downs. If we would view the roller-coaster as our personal destiny designed specifically for us to affect our exact purpose in the world, then we would find every twist and turn playing to our own hand. And ultimately, when we feel like we are exactly where we want and need to be at all times, we are much better equipped to achieve success than when we feel like we are merely fighting off the misfortunes of life.

This Rosh Hashana, let us perceive how our King is part and parcel of ourselves, and thereby align ourselves fully with His kingdom. And as we continually strive to make ourselves one with the Kingdom of Hashem, may we all merit a year of life and prosperity.

Good Shabbos and Shana Tova, Yosef

Hakhel and Teshuva

September 30, 2022

This week's parsha details the mitzvah of Hakel. The King of Israel is instructed to convene the entire community on the first day of Sukkot after a shemittah year, "Gather the people—men, women, children, and the strangers in your communities—that they may hear and so learn to revere your God and to observe faithfully every word of this Teaching." The King would ascend to a platform in front of the assembly and would read from the Torah written by Moshe. The Sefer HaChinuch describes the reason for this gathering," The talk of all the nation – men, women and children – would then be: "Why have we assembled for this large gathering?" And the answer would be: "To hear the words of the Torah—our essence, glory and pride!" This would lead them to praise the Torah and speak of its glorious worth, and implant within their hearts a desire and motivation to study and know G-d. Thus they will merit the ultimate good, and G-d will rejoice in His creations."

As this year is a Hakhel year the mitzvah has special significance now. But its relevance is even more pronounced today as we are preparing for Shabbat Shuvah. In his commentary on the Mitzvah of Hakhel the Kli Yakar reveals that this mitzvah is actually the culmination of the entire teshuva process that we started in Elul and that escalated on Rosh Hashanah as we entered the 10 Days of Repentance. What can Hahkel teach us about this process and how can we use it to achieve more complete repentance?

The first day of Sukkot, when Hakhel is performed, is called "the first day of accounting for sins." This is based on a parable brought by the Midrash;

There was a province that owed tax to the king and the king went to collect it. When he was within ten miles, the leaders of the province went out to praise him, so he canceled a third of the tax; within five miles, the middle class of people from the province went out to laud him, so he canceled another third; when he entered the province, all of the residents of the province - men, women and children - came out and celebrated him, and he canceled it all. He said, 'What is past is past. From now, let us start a new reckoning.'

So too on the eve of Rosh Hashanah, the greats of the generations fast, and the Holy One, blessed be He, cancels a third of their iniquities; and from Rosh Hashanah to Yom Kippur, the select individuals fast, and the Holy One, blessed be He, cancels another third of their iniquities; and on Yom Kippur, everyone fasts - men, women and children. And from Yom Kippur to Sukkot, all of Israel is involved in commandments. This one is involved with his sukkah, that one is involved with his lulav. And on the first day of the festival, all of Israel stand in front of the Holy One, blessed be He, with their lulavs and etrogim for the sake of the Holy One, blessed be He. And He says to them, "What is past is past. From now, let us start a [new] reckoning."

The Kli Yakar further explains, "It is well known that Hakhel is necessary for Teshuva, as the Nation of Israel, when they are gathered together become a single unit and can turn toward God in communal repentance. Only between Rosh Hashanah and Yom Kippur is an individual's repentance accepted by God, the rest of the year only communal requests will be accepted."

From these observations alone we can learn a lot more about how the Teshuva process is supposed to work and where our efforts should be focused. We are currently in a rare time of personal introspection and improvement. This is the week that we renew and return to ourselves, so when the time comes we can congregate as a renewed community. What does that work look like? The Sefer HaChinuch's description is perfect, "To hear the words of the Torah—our essence, glory and pride! To praise the Torah and speak of its glorious worth, and implant within their hearts a desire and motivation to study and know G-d." This is our time to personally work on recommitting ourselves to the Torah and its values. To remember that living a righteous life is the ultimate pride and to acknowledge this righteousness is in us, it is our essence. We can find new motivation in our religious pursuits and rekindle our desire to be close.

Once we have done this work individually we will be able to enter the period after Yom Kippur when we are too busy with preparations for Sukkot to have time to sin. This gives us a chance to experience a taste of the righteousness we have worked to develop a deep yearning for. Then altogether we have a chance to go into the streets as one, men, women, and children to praise the king. If we pour our hearts into this teshuva process he will respond, "What is past is past. From now, let us start a [new] reckoning."

Shabbat Shalom

In the Heavens & Beyond the Seas

September 8, 2023

During the month of Elul, our spiritual focus sharpens as we prepare for the High Holy Days. As Rosh Hashanah, and Aseret Yemei Teshuva quickly approach, our attention is directed toward repentance. The Torah portion Nitzavim - Vayelech speaks directly to this theme of returning to God and the accessibility of teshuva.

In this parsha, Hashem issues a stern warning, cautioning us about the consequences of our actions. However, there is an underlying message of hope. Hashem assures us that, despite our wayward paths, "You, however, will again heed Hashem and obey all the divine commandments." This promise is a beacon of encouragement, reminding us that no matter how far we have strayed, sincere repentance is always an option, and Hashem welcomes us back with open arms.

The Torah reinforces this by stating, "Surely, this Instruction which I enjoin upon you this day is not too baffling for you, nor is it beyond reach. It is not in the heavens, that you should say, “Who among us can go up to the heavens and get it for us and impart it to us, that we may observe it?” Neither is it beyond the sea, that you should say, “Who among us can cross to the other side of the sea and get it for us and impart it to us, that we may observe it?" Here, the Torah emphasizes that the path to righteousness and connection with the Divine is not a complex or unattainable one. It is not concealed in the heavens, requiring extraordinary efforts to reach it. Instead, it is right before us, waiting for us to embrace it.

In Eruvin, our sages expand upon this message using the examples from the verse above. Rava teaches that “It is not in heaven” means that Torah is not to be found in someone who thinks his mind is above the Torah and he does not need a teacher and similarly it is not to be found in someone who expands his mind over it, like the sea, i.e., he thinks he knows everything there is to know about the topic he has learned. Rabbi Yochanan says “It is not in the heaven” is meant to teach us that Torah is not attainable by the haughty and “Nor is it beyond the sea” means that it is not to be found among merchants or traders who are constantly traveling and do not have the time to study Torah properly.

This teaching sets the bar for our teshuva back to the Torah. While it is an approachable path there are some basic prerequisites. We must humble ourselves intellectually, relinquish our pride, and commit time and effort to the process. The Torah is most accessible to those who humbly seek knowledge, set aside consistent time for growth, and recognize that learning is a lifelong journey.

As we navigate the profound season of Elul, we must internalize these teachings. We should approach our preparations for Rosh Hashanah with humility and a commitment to learning and growth. Teshuva, as highlighted by our Torah portion, is within our reach, and the wisdom of the Torah is accessible to those who seek it with sincerity. In the coming year, may we draw closer to Hashem through our genuine repentance, and may our Torah study be infused with humility and a deep desire for spiritual understanding.

Festival

Rosh Hashanah

Yosef · September 19, 2017

We now stand, quite literally, on the brink of the world. On Rosh Hashanah they are inscribed, and on Yom Kippur they are sealed. On Rosh Hashana, the world was recreated from scratch. All that we have taken for granted in the past is no longer a given. Rather, if we wish for a good and prosperous year it has to be rewritten now, and it is up to us to decide what will be written.

But how? What is the secret to achieving a positive judgment and a year full of blessing? The answer, we are taught, is teshuvah. Simply translated, teshuvah means repentance. But a more accurate translation would render it "returning". Teshuvah means returning back home, back to our loving Father in heaven.

I heard a beautiful story about a rebellious teenager, Sam, who ran away from home; shirking his responsibilities at the family farm in order to explore the world. Sam's father was livid with his son's behavior, and vowed that if he chose to leave home he would never be welcomed back again. Sam stuck to his guns and set out, with just a few coins to his name. He loved the adventure and for a while he was content scavenging for food and sleeping in the streets. The freedom thrilled him and he found it worthwhile to live the lowly life of a beggar, so long as he could do as he pleased. But as the months went on, Sam began to feel homesick. He missed his mother's warm meals and the security of his father's home. He longed for his parents embrace, but he remembered his father's threat all too clearly, and he feared that he'd never again be welcome back at the farm. In desperation, Sam penned a letter to his mother describing his regret and his wish to come back. He asked that if his father was willing to let him return, they should tie a white handkerchief around the apple tree in front of the house. If he would see the handkerchief as he rode by on the train, he would know that he was wanted back home.

As soon as he saved up enough money, Sam boarded the train and set off towards the place he had once called home. As he neared, Sam began to shake with trepidation. He willed there to be a white handkerchief on the tree, but he was too scared to look. Not knowing what to do, Sam closed his eyes and asked the man sitting next to him to tell him what he saw. As they passed, the man clapped him on the back and said, "Son, there are white handkerchiefs tied around every branch of that tree."

We may have gone astray, forsaking the warmth and comfort of G-d's embrace in order to chase after our wants and desires. We may have wandered far from home, sullying ourselves in the muck of our sins, all in the name of freedom. But despite it all, on Yom Kippur every Jew longs to finally pick himself up and return back home, back to our loving Father's embrace. And lest we fear that we are no longer welcome, G-d has hung white handkerchiefs around every branch of the tree. He gave us the entire month of Elul, Rosh Hashanah, the Ten Days of Repentance, and Yom Kippur -- forty days imbued with the tremendous power to cleanse and lift ourselves up. He is begging us to come back and has opened the door wide for anyone who's willing to take the invitation.

As we're looking to do teshuvah, it is certainly imperative to repent for our individual sins as enumerated in the Yom Kippur prayers. However, the basis of our teshuvah must be in its most literal sense - returning to our loving Father, making Him part of our lives, and living with Him on a daily basis. This is much like the relationship with any loved one. When you share a relationship, you keep in touch. You communicate on a regular basis, in order to keep your loved one involved in your life. So must we try to build a relationship with G-d Himself.

This is what teshuvah means at its most fundamental level.

This may sound lofty at first, but it's really not that hard. When you're running late and you need a parking spot, take a quick moment and ask G-d to help. When you're making supper, ask Him to make it delicious. If you're taking a test at school or an assessment at work, He can help you do your best. And when you find that spot or perform well, don't forget to say thank you. This is a very practical way to rebuild a relationship with our Creator, to truly make Him part of our lives and thus fulfill the most basic element of teshuvah.

May we all merit a complete teshuvah, and thereby may we be blessed with a Shana tovah umetukah. A sweet new year filled with all the blessings that come from being home, within the embrace of our loving Father. [Inspired by the writings of Rabbi Shlomo Wolbe and Rabbi Yissochor Frand.]

Good Shabbos and Good Yom Tov,

Yosef

Noah · September 19, 2017

As I stand at the threshold of the incoming year I am overwhelmed with disappointment. Reflecting on the past year I remember how many times I made commitments that went unkept. I recall the rush of excitement and pride in imaging how different I would be this year and how much I would change for the better. But looking in the mirror on Erev Rosh Hashanah morning the face staring back at me looks frighteningly familiar. Here I am, in countless ways the exact same person I was last year. I am still struggling against the same character defects, fighting the same desires and caught up in the same old vicious cycles. So many of the improvements that I was committed to making in my life have not come to fruition. These feeling are not conducive to a holiday atmosphere and suck the aspiration out of me.

That's why fully understanding how Rosh Hashanah is both a festival and the day of judgement requires a huge perspective shift. If the attitude described above was what Rosh Hashanah was meant to elicit, our liturgy should be full of groveling, pleading, and begging for divine forgiveness. But instead, the lengthy Rosh Hashanah service contains almost no references to the personal judgment that is occurring. It is focused on the state of our world as a whole, declaring that there is a God that is aware of everything, and that through his continued sustenance of this world he demonstrates his deep love and concern. We focus on the aspect of Kingship in the sense that a true King loves and does what's best for his people. This is the first tip that the judgment that occurs on Rosh Hashanah is much deeper than the judgement we are used to seeing.

The Torah reading for Rosh Hashanah offers more clues into the nature of the day. We read about Yishmael pleading to be spared. The Torah says that, "God heard the voice of the boy where he was." The redundancy of the phrase "where he was" is meant to teach us a crucial lesson. It clearly does not refer to Yishmael's physical location, rather it is coming to say that God heard the voice of the boy in that moment. The future conflicts that would arise between his progeny and the Jewish people did not weigh into the equation, nor did his previous actions. God did not compare Yishmael to who he was last year or to his future self to determine his fate. From this the Talmud learns that a person is judged only according to his actions in the exact moment of his judgement. Our Rosh Hashanah is not about looking to the past or the future but it solely about reclaiming the present.

The importance of our present is also found in the name of the holiday. Despite being called, "the birthday of the world," Rosh Hashanah is not celebrated on the day the world was created. Rather it is observed from the day that mankind was created. If the preexisting world, already colorful and mountainous, blooming with life and illuminated by a sun moon and stars, wasn't enough to merit commemoration, what did humans add to the mix that was so special? The answer is found in the defining characteristic of a human. This is our ability to make choices, to exercise free will. Free will is our method of interaction with the present, there is no ability to choose or decide the future or the past.

For these reasons, it is apparent that Rosh Hashanah's purpose is to gauge our intent and not our actions. After another long, taxing year are we curled up moping around in our own self-pity or have we dusted ourselves off, cleared our heads and filled ourselves with and excitement for the year ahead? Are we going to allow that failings of the past, and the fear of the future to define our decisions and destiny, or can we harness this opportunity to start anew?

This change in perspective perfectly explains why our day of judgement comes before our day of atonement. As each year passes we find ourselves caught in some of the same vicious cycles. When we allow our free will to be bound to the past we end up unable to make real lasting change. We become comforted by our vices and begin to lose our will to fight against them. We cannot ask for forgiveness and try to atone before we have freed ourselves from the shackles of the past. The twelve step program beautifully conveys this point. To escape a destructive habit the twelve steps require "Coming to believe that a Power greater than ourselves can restore us to sanity" way before "Making a list of all persons we have harmed, and becoming willing to make amends to them all." Rosh Hashanah is when we state that we believe there is sanity that will be restored to our world and that we no longer have to engage in behaviors that are harmful just because we have in the past. Only after this process can we begin to make amends, the culmination of which will be Yom Kippur.

Mishlei famously states that the righteous fall seven times and rise again. This is the attitude that I'm exchanging my dread for. Upon entering Rosh Hashanah I am going to remember that I am not defined by the times I fell last year but by the fact I have risen again to celebrate the start of a new cycle. Once again I have the opportunity want to be better, to want to make the right choices and to escape the pitfalls along the way. I still believe that the entire creation exists to help me grow and that sanity is attainable in this world. When I look in the mirror I will see the same hopeful face that imagined a better future and will be proud that I am that same person, resilient and strong, ready to fight the same battles with renewed vigor.

Shana Tova U'Metuka and Shabbat Shalom, Noah

The learning should be a merit for Yonatan ben Avraham Avinu a"h
