The Desperate Beggar

Yosef · September 18, 2020

In the High Holiday prayers, we say “כדלים וכרשים דפקנו דלתיך”, “like the poor and the destitute we knock on Your doors.”

Rabbi Shalom Scwhadron zt”l asks that the word dafaknu used here doesn’t connote a soft rap at the door, but rather a powerful knock – to bang mightily. This would seem to be out of place with the reference to a beggar, who normally knocks lightly out of embarrassment and humility.

Rabbi Schwadron’s answer will change the way you see Rosh Hashanah forever. Yes, a beggar usually knocks softly, but there is one exception. If he is so destitute that he is literally about to die of starvation, when he feels that his life is on the line and he cannot survive without the help of the homeowner, then he doesn’t tap – he bangs down the door!

We must realize that on Rosh Hashanah our lease on life is over. We were given the past year, with all the blessings we enjoyed, but they have zero bearing on tomorrow. Tomorrow is a new year, and everything requires a new judgment and allocation. If we were healthy last year, it has absolutely no reason to continue further. If we had enough to eat, a roof over our heads, and the rest of our basic needs, there is no guarantee for the future. In short, we need to realize that there is no such thing as status quo from before Rosh Hashanah to after Rosh Hashanah.

In this light, we are not just paupers begging for a handout from the Almighty – we are standing at the brink of death and should be banging heaven’s doors down in desperation. The way we knock is with our prayers, with our tears, and with our yearning to go higher.

But the same is true on the side of good. Rosh Hashanah is a time of unlimited possibility. All our constraints and all the tests which stood against us last year are about to expire. If we struggled with our finances last year, there is no reason to assume it must be that way tomorrow. If we suffered from bad health, from difficult relationships, or from any other hardship in the past, that doesn’t lock us in for the coming year. On Rosh Hashanah we have a clean slate which is ready to be filled with untold blessings.

So, let us see things for the way they really are – without taking anything for granted – and truly break open the gates of heaven with our Rosh Hashanah prayers. In this way, may we all merit to be written and sealed in the book of life and to a year of all things good.

Shanah Tovah Umetukah,

Yosef

September 14, 2023

As the month of Elul comes to an end and we approach Rosh Hashanah, we are filled with a sense of dread. Elul is an acronym for the phrase in Shir Hashirim, "Ani Le'dodi Ve'dodi Li" -- "I am for my beloved and my beloved is for me." In a month associated with fear and strict justice, it seems strange to address G-d as our beloved -- why does G-d even need to judge us? Why celebrate?

G-d, by definition, is perfect and complete; His judgment is not for revenge, but for our benefit. On Rosh Hashanah we remember that G-d cares about everything we do, say, and think -- and that this personal attention is itself proof of love. As any relationship counselor knows, indifference, not conflict, is the true sign of a lack of love; G-d shows no indifference at all.

This is laid out in the Rambam's thirteen principles: the tenth, that G-d knows the actions, speech, and thoughts of every person and does not ignore them, and the eleventh, that G-d rewards and punishes accordingly. Why does the Rambam need both principles separately? In theory, G-d could have built a world where reward and punishment happen automatically, without His constant awareness required. The Rambam insists on the tenth principle specifically to teach that G-d prioritizes His relationship with us over the mere mechanical fulfillment of mitzvot.

On Rosh Hashanah we celebrate how much G-d loves us, and the strange privilege of standing before a Judge who is both full of mercy and strict in judgment. We do not know why G-d allows bad things to happen to good people, but we are reminded of Avraham and the binding of Yitzchak, and of the sacrifices Jews have made throughout history to sustain our relationship with G-d.

Festival

Yom Kippur

Yom Kippur – Enjoy the Ride

Yosef · October 4, 2019

Yom Kippur. The holiest day of the year. The day set aside by G-d, in His infinite kindness, to forgive us for all of our misdeeds.

On this day, every Jew, no matter how estranged, somehow finds himself drawn to a house of prayer. But why is that? What is it about Yom Kippur that is so much more attractive than all the other wonderful days on our calendar? It certainly isn't about the delicious food, the spirited singing, or the beauty of family which we enjoy every other holiday. So what is it that gives Yom Kippur such a magnetic pull on the Jewish soul?

The answer, says Rabbi Shimshon Pincus, lies in the words of Maimonides: '[On Yom Kippur,] the very day itself atones.'

Rabbi Pincus explains that this is the fundamental difference between Yom Kippur and every other holiday. Every other holiday is a time of heightened opportunity for us to tap into the power of the time. For instance, Pesach is a time where we can tap into the spirit of redemption, Shavuot is a time where we can tap into the holiness of Torah, and Rosh Hashanah is a time where we can tap into the Kingship of G-d. But all of these are mere opportunities and times of divine assistance -- we alone are responsible for the heavy lifting.

Not so Yom Kippur. On Yom Kippur, the day itself atones. Meaning that the holiness with which Hashem Himself imbued the day works on its own.

However, there are certain things we must do to make ourselves available for that divine intervention. Teshuvah, repentance, is like the ticket to gain entry. We must improve something, we must take some steps in the right direction. We have to show that we're interested in being helped. Next, we have to refrain from the five restrictions of Yom Kippur -- eating, drinking, anointing, wearing leather shoes, and marital relations. These also are to open ourselves to receive the incredible heavenly assistance knocking at our doors. By loosening our hold on the physical world, we enable ourselves to be lifted up to the immense spiritual realms offered to us on this awesome day.

But, all of these laws are no more than prerequisites to allow the power of the day to act on us. The incredible power of the day is Hashem's doing alone. This is why even the most estranged Jew feels a tug on his heartstrings. We don't need to connect for the day to work. Hashem is reaching out to us and lifting us heavenwards -- on His shoulders, not ours.

This is the tremendous power of Yom Kippur, and it's this power that makes it so pivotal a day in our personal lives. When Hashem is doing the lifting, anything is possible.

Imagine a gigantic steel beam, one-hundred feet long and weighing several thousand pounds. When the beam is sitting on the ground, an army can't move it. But suppose that beam is suspended from above by a crane. Now, all that colossal weight is like nothing, and the beam can be easily maneuvered with no more than a hand.

Such is the Jewish condition on Yom Kippur. We are suspended, as it were, high above the ground. Lifted by Hashem himself out of the gravitational pull that holds us down a whole year. All of the weight of past mistakes and poor self-control which roots us firmly in our stagnant state is suddenly of little consequence. Floating above ground, we can turn ourselves around with minimal effort, so that we land exactly where we'd like to be.

It doesn't matter how far from home we find ourselves today. On Yom Kippur, it's not up to us, but to Him. Let's just open ourselves to His mighty hand and allow ourselves to be hoisted up and out. To become the new people that we always wish to be. And while we're enjoying the ride, let's make sure to adjust our position while the going is easy and start our new lives on the right foot.

Good Shabbos and Shana Tova,

Yosef

The Teshuva Lifeline

Yosef · September 22, 2023

Yom Kippur is the one of the greatest gifts ever given to mankind. It is the day described as an ocean of compassion, the day when Hashem purifies and forgives us for all of our misdeeds, the day that we are elevated from the level of mortal man to the heights of the angels.

There is just one caveat; one precondition which must be met in order to gain entry to such an awesome power of love and compassion. Yom Kippur atones for those who repent and do teshuva alone – if one fails to do teshuva, he is left untouched and unaffected by the holiness of the day. In fact, the Meshech Chochma comments that should one fail to do his part in unlocking all the treasures of Yom Kippur, this itself is the greatest prosecution against him, as he is thus charged with willingly confirming a year’s worth of sin when he could have so easily had it erased!

As such, it is of paramount importance to realize that going to shul, reciting the entire machzor, and beating our chests, is still insufficient to gain the atonement of Yom Kippur. We must each sit down by ourselves and go through some internal work which isn’t formally printed in the machzor. We must each find our way to teshuva and thereby gain access to the wonders of the day.

So, what is teshuva? In its most basic form, teshuva is a three-step process of regret, verbal confession to Hashem, and a concrete affirmation not to repeat the deed.

Regret is relatively easy to attain. Sure, it requires a certain amount of learning, since we cannot regret something while unaware of its folly. And it also requires an awareness of Hashem’s greatness and the necessity to obey his word. But, each of us according to our own level, can achieve some level of regret.

Confession is also not too difficult, as it is the one part of the teshuva process which is included in the machzor. The only things to be careful with is to understand what we are saying, and to know that not everything is included in that list. If we know of a specific sin, we should add it in our personal confession outside of the formal prayers.

But now we come to the hard part – the avowal not to repeat our actions. This is where too many of us throw up our hands, since, unfortunately, our list of shortcomings is impossibly long and most of our failings are things deeply entrenched in our natures. How are we supposed to honestly declare that we will never return to our sins?

Thankfully, Rav Yisroel Salanter, founder of the mussar movement, has thrown us a lifeline. He explained that nothing in the heavenly court is black and white, and we cannot assume that one who transgresses a specific sin is simply defined as a sinner until he rids himself of it entirely. Rather, every misdeed can be split into many different parts, and a teshuva even on a single part can already define a person as one who has done teshuva and thus earned entry to Yom Kippur.

Let’s take Rav Yisroel’s own example – Shabbos. If one is not careful with the laws of Shabbos, we might have assumed that so long as he isn’t a full-fledged ‘Shomer Shabbos’, he hasn’t done teshuva and his efforts are yet unfruitful. But in reality, his Shabbos observance, or lack thereof, is a much more nuanced issue where individual actions are divided based on the difficulty involved. Maybe he drives to work, cooks lunch, and plays a dumb game on his phone. The easiest one to avoid would be the game, since it’s relatively uninteresting and there are many forms of permitted entertainment to engage in instead. Next would probably be lunch, as eating is necessary and not frivolous like the game, but it also could be avoided with just a little forethought. Work might be the hardest since he sees it as necessary for his livelihood and dropping it would take bigger arrangements and a larger-scale lifestyle change. As such, the most serious transgression is the game; since it is the easiest to avoid, its performance is the worst show of apathy and the biggest disrespect to Hashem.

As things rank higher in terms of difficulty, they rank lower in terms of severity, and vise versa.

Rav Yisroel exhorts us to start small and take the easiest part of our sins to fix. On that smallest, most limited area, each of us is indeed capable of change. Since it doesn’t carry any special lure, we can honestly endeavor to change once and for all. And, through that minimal effort, we will have actually fixed the most severe part of our sin. With time, such baby steps can subtly chip away at every level of the sin until, eventually, we can rid ourselves entirely of it.

So, let’s all grab hold of the opportunity being given to us. Let’s not throw out the baby with the bathwater, assuming that without perfection we may as well give up, but rather let us take the baby steps which can unlock such spectacular results. And, as we all strive towards the highest teshuva we can reach, one step at a time, may we all enjoy the bounty of compassion and atonement being offered to us on Yom Kippur.

Good Shabbos and a Shana Tova to everyone,

Yosef

Festival

Sukkot

October 4, 2017

"And there arose not since a prophet in Israel like Moses, whom G-d knew face to face; [who performed] all the signs and wonders which G-d sent [Moses] to do in the land of Egypt... [who equaled] that mighty hand, those great awesome things, which Moses did before the eyes of all Israel." These are some of the Torah's final remarks before it is rewound and its cyclical ascent into Sukkot and Simchat Torah is celebrated, here at basecamp 5778. Rashi comments that "those great awesome things, which Moses did before the eyes of all Israel" - is actually a reference to the splitting of the tablets at the event of the golden calf.

This begs the question, to Hashem which takes precedence, the torah or the jewish people? Are people created in order to carry out the Torah or is the Torah here to enrich our lives? Or are they an indivisible whole? When Moshe came down from Har Sinai with tablets in hand and witnessed the golden calf, options lay before him, decisions, opportunities to make an example and set the record straight. In that split moment when Moshe threw down the tablets he made a decision to bind the jewish people to our commitment to Torah by making them intrinsically reliant upon eachother. If Moshe would have delivered the tablets at the event of the golden calf the burden of being a Jew alone would have killed all of us. This does prove our separation from gentiles greater, even in the middle of our midnight mutiny extravaganza, Torah in the most literal sense saves us. If we are not up to snuff the Torah is in a million pieces, this infers the value of the soul to endure over the Torah.

Of course without the Torah and this pinnacle decision we would not have a base for assessing the value of human life, making the Torah a key with many corresponding locks, and the jewish people a lock without key.

They are two sides of the same coin the soul cannot be fully harnessed without Torah and Torah cannot be fathomed to its depths until souls search it and observe it as shattered. Therefore, the ultimate Torah was created at the moment our input was received, when fate was changed because we woke up late. The ultimate Torah is the one we study and tweek, teach and discover, read and yell. The torah that is made by interactions, the Torah we wear, eat, in the Torah we go.

Everything we are and receive is in "quantized packages". Truth blessings and wisdom are infinite but we are not. Like Hansel and Gretel we are lucky to find morsels carving out our winding narrow path. Even the tablets were quantized, ten commandments if you do them great, if not then not. When Hashem saw Moshe mourning the tablets it states, "You have done well by smashing the tablets. For now you will receive a Torah that you may extend wider than the sea." With this second tablets came a platform for the jewish people to manipulate the torah from the inside, the Oral Torah.

If there is one thing sure to put you ahead in life, it's failure, it's exactly like success only instead of leading to wealth and wholeness, with failure you shrink. You fall inside yourself and see yourself in shattered disarray and think this will not make due, I'm leaving! Now you escape and join the infinite and discover what was there the whole time. Both the broken and complete tablets were placed in the arc, so to in your heart there is a set of each. When you find the infinite where will you put it? Your regular vessel won't hold it and neither will the the broken one. This is the nature of humans that trumps angels we can exist fully here and partially there we embody the dualities of the broken and whole. That is why it is our responsibility to make the invisible visible and bring heaven to earth.

The learning should be a merit for Yonatan ben Avraham Avinu a"h

Questions and comments can be sent to freedmanyosef@gmail.com

The Sukkah Song

Adam · September 23, 2018

Sukkos falls in an interesting place on the Jewish calendar. We just completed the Days of Awe and approached God from a place of fear and awe, looking to our Father and King and begging to be able to renew our relationship with him. We looked at our past mistakes, spoke them out verbally and resolved not to return to our prior misdeeds.

But we also did something very strange for Jews to do. We removed ourselves from physicality. We even say the verse of “Baruch Shem” aloud on Yom Kippur because we are like the angels for that day. Just as the angels exist in a spiritual plane alone, we stop eating, drinking, washing, and engaging in marital relations. These are all things that connect us to the physical world. And for one day we remove ourselves from the physical world and we stand in prayer all day long, connecting to the spiritual alone and building a bond with our Creator.

But now, a mere five days later, we utilize a different approach. Generally Judaism doesn't promote abstinence from the physical. Rather, we utilize the physical and elevate it to the spiritual plane. Sukkos is extremely physical. We take our entire bodies outside and into the Sukkah. We make Kiddush on wine and we eat and drink in celebration of the special bond we have.

Rabbi Shimshon Pincus brings an amazing explanation of the difference between our approach during the Days of Awe and Sukkos. He says that our avodah, our mission with the upcoming holiday, is to return to the deep relationship we have with the Creator but this time out of pure love. We have an opportunity to experience an even deeper connection than we could on Yom Kippur: to live within the embrace of the Sukkah. To take all of the physical and utilize it to fall in love with God again. Perhaps we could call Sukkos the Days of Love.

We just read Parshat Haazinu which is one of the ten songs in the world. Nine have been sung and the tenth will be sung with the arrival of Mashiach. If we read the parsha, however, it doesn't seem like the greatest song. There are some very difficult passages talking about the Jewish people disconnecting from God and chasing “other gods”. How could this be a quintessential song? Our sages say that what makes something qualify as a Shira, or song, is specifically that it encompasses the difficulties as well as the good. A true song is not all margaritas and basking on the beach. It's hiking a towering mountain, traversing difficult terrain, and arriving at the tallest peak to experience the breathtaking view at the top. It's the sublime mixture of seemingly incongruent emotions and experiences.

I think that's why we have songs surrounding this time of year. We need the mistakes we made in the past, the difficulties we faced, the awe that we ignited in our hearts, the fear of damaging the one relationship in our lives that matters more than anything else, and the love that we can experience with the Almighty all together to sing Shira.

May this Sukkos bring every one of us closer to each other and through that, to our Creator. May we all be able to experience teshuva me’ahava, returning out of love. And may we merit to bring the final song into the world with the coming of Mashiach!

I love you all,

Adam

Lessons of the Lulav

Noah · October 11, 2019

The Mishnah in Succah tells us:

לוּלָב הַגָּזוּל וְהַיָּבֵשׁ, פָּסוּל. שֶׁל אֲשֵׁרָה וְשֶׁל עִיר הַנִּדַּחַת, פָּסוּל. נִקְטַם רֹאשׁוֹ, נִפְרְצוּ עָלָיו, פָּסוּל.

"A Lulav that was stolen or that is completely dry is unfit for use in fulfilling the mitzva of the four species. The Lulav of a tree worshipped as idolatry [asheira] and a Lulav from a city whose residents were incited to idolatry [Ir Hanidachas] are unfit. If the top of the Lulav was severed or if the palm leaves were severed from the spine of the Lulav, it is unfit."

While on a simple level these appear to be halachic minutiae, their placement as the introduction to the mitzvah of lulav indicates a deeper significance. We are all familiar with at least one of the many metaphorical comparisons between the Lulav and a Jew's approach to divine service. These similes are seen in the species sensual characteristics, the sweet-smelling and tasting Etrog represents the tzaddik with both Torah and good deeds, the fragrant but tasteless Hadass represents those with good deeds but devoid of Torah, the Lulav has taste but no smell, symbolizing those who study Torah but do not possess good deeds, and finally the Aravah has neither taste nor smell, symbolizing those who lack both Torah and good deeds. In our action of binding together these different species, we acknowledge and unify Jews throughout the entire spectrum of divine service.

Alternatively, the species represents each of our human faculties that can be dedicated to divine service. The Etrog our hearts, the Lulav our spines, the Hadass our eyes and the Arava our mouths. Again the symbolism is deeply tied to a Jews approach to engaging with God.

B'lvavi Mishkan Evneh explains that this symbolism does not stop with the broad strokes of just what species to choose. He explains that the metaphor permeates deep into the halachic framework of the Lulav as well. The Mishnah in Sukkah is teaching us about the criteria for the acceptable service of God.

The first halacha disqualifies a stolen lulav. This is analogous to the inauthentic service of God. The Zohar teaches that Lulav comes from a combination of the words "lo" ("to him") and "lev" ("heart"), to show that "one's heart must be in his jurisdiction." In other words, a person's heart should not be swayed by the hearts of others, especially in relation to our way of serving Hashem. When we shuckle harder because the person next to us is, take on a chumrah that is not personally meaningful because it's the community fad, or mimic the behaviors of our Rabbis without understanding their significance, we are effectively offering a stolen service to God.

The second is a dried Lulav. This is our limp and lifeless Pesukei Dezimra the morning after a late night. It is our contentment with letting things dry out, rather than continuing to bloom.

The subsequent two disqualifications are Lulavim that were already dedicated to or used for Avodah Zara. When we use Torah to justify our own desires, when we rationalize rage with religion, we take a Lulav from the Asheria and shake it before God. Although it may look perfect on the outside, God isn't fooled. When we allow ourselves to be swept away by the current of pop culture and media we engage in public idol worship like the Ir Hanidachas. When we shake the Lulav and perform mitzvot are we thinking about the stock market or the impeachment inquiry, or are we focusing our effort and attention on spiritual pursuits?

Next, come physical abnormalities and deficits. A headless lulav has no direction. It can no longer hope to grow higher, to achieve more. An attitude without aspiration is not compatible with divine service. A Lulav with severed leaves is invalid. Rashi explains that this refers to a Lulav where the leaves have become separated from the spine so that there is nothing connecting the leaves to the source. Technically, the leaves could be tied to the spine or may even be able to survive independently without the spine. However, the Gemara learns from this that the Torah requires that the leaves of the Lulav must obtain their nutrients and energy from the spine, in order for the Lulav to be deemed valid for the mitzvah. Like the Lulav, our mitzvos and Avodas Hashem have to be connected. What source is connecting us to these actions? Do we feel that our mitzvah performance is the essence of our lives? Or are we just forcing ourselves to do the mitzvah while still remaining detached?

Coming off of Yom Kippur we are all granted a fresh canvas on which to paint another year of godly service. Sukkot is the time that we can choose our palette. Are we going to use bold meaningful colors to create a work of authentic expression, or are we going to choose the same dull and uninspired hues as the year past?

We should merit to learn the lesson of the Lulav and bring connection and intention to our Avodas Hashem this year.

Shabbat Shalom and Chag Sameach,

Noah

Where Do You Really Live?

Yosef · September 20, 2021

Once, a wealthy American businessman had the opportunity to visit the Chofetz Chaim (1838-1933), the sainted leader of the Jewish people, at his home in Radin, Poland. When the businessman entered the tiny and decrepit shack, he could hardly believe his eyes. He took in the sparse and rickety furniture, the bare walls, and the general lack of any and all creature comforts. Could this really be the house of the greatest rabbi of the generation?!

Unable to contain himself, the man turned to the Chofetz Chaim and asked incredulously, “Where is all of the Rabbi's furniture?”

The Chofetz Chaim smiled and turned the question right back, “I don't see your furniture either. Where is all of it?”

Now the man became confused, thinking he had been misunderstood. “My furniture? I am just passing through here, but in my estate back in America I have all the finest things that money can buy.”

“Ah,” said the Chofetz Chaim, “I, too, am just passing through here. It will just be a few short years here in this world, after which I will arrive at my true destination – the world to come. Since I'm just traveling, I see no need for more than the bare minimum to furnish my home.”

This is a perspective which would serve each of us very well, to whatever degree we are able to internalize it. And while such is no easy task, we have been given the holiday of Succos to help us learn the lesson, hands on.

The Talmud sums up the mitzvah of sitting in the Succah for us in one sentence: 'Leave your house of permanence, and live in a temporary hut.'

This means much more than the external action of walking out the door and setting up your folding chairs. There is a paradigm shift here which we are expected to internalize and make part of our lives.

Leave your prior notion that the place where you reside is here in this world. Let go of the misconception that material achievements, pleasures, and comforts are the purpose of life. Instead, live with the mindset that your residence in this world is nothing more than temporal; no more than a means to merit your eternal abode of the world to come.

For a week, we are afforded the priceless opportunity to live the ideal by actually abandoning the sturdy walls and thick roof of our house in favor of our oh so temporal Succah. But we can't just sit back and relax, because when the week ends we are expected to take our newfound perspective back into our houses. This is no easy task, so we have to get busy using our minds throughout Succos to make the message of the Succah part of our psyche, in such a way that it will last even without the external assistance of the Succah.

Have a wonderful Yom Tov,

Yosef

Festival

Shmini Atzeret / Simchat Torah

October 11, 2017

As Yom Kippur came to an end a little over a week ago, I found myself in a mix of emotions.

Elated by the closeness I felt from moving through Rosh Hashana, the Ten Days of Repentance and the culmination of it all at Neilah, the extra prayer on Yom Kippur.

Awe at the realization of how fragile our lives are and how great and vast God is.

Gratitude at having made it through the process once again.

And sadness that it would be a full year until I would stand here again.

But a moment later, I remembered that we weren't all done yet. We still had Sukkot only 4 days away. Sukkot is called by the sages the "Time of Our Happiness" and I have always felt the easiest connection to Simcha, or happiness, on Sukkot.

My mind began to fill with memories of singing and dancing in the Sukkah. Eating and sleeping each day under the canopy of pine, bamboo, or palm branches depending on where I spent the Chag. And I felt the sadness leave. Replaced by hopes and longing for the Sukkah we would soon inhabit.

But what is the internal work that we do on Sukkot? It seems hard to create happiness. The Days of Awe may be for many, the easier holiday to connect to emotionally.

Rabbi Shimshon Pincus said that there's a very deep concept in this progression from the Days of Awe to the Time of our Happiness.

In the Torah, building takes place in units of ten. And seven represents intrinsic completeness. God used ten sayings to create the world and ten plagues to remove the Jews from Egypt (and Egyptian-ness from the Jews). And we have ten days between Rosh Hashana and Yom Kippur. This is time to do Teshuva, which as Uncle Yonatan frequently reminded us means to return more than to repent. But this process is a ten day process indicating to us that it is a building process. That we must start somewhere and slowly raise our level of Fear and Awe of the Almighty and thereby develop within ourselves the will to return to him. The more we focus on His vastness and our smallness, His power and our fragility, the more we build toward this experience of Teshuva. This is what the sages call Teshuva Me'Yirah, or Returning/ Repenting out of Fear/ Awe.

The holiday that follows is Sukkot, a seven day holiday (for Noah and Cali at least). Seven, says Rav Shimshon Pincus, is the number of completeness. Seven days in the creation of the world, seven days in a week and seven thousand years of human history. The uniqueness of sevens is that the full experience is encapsulated in every moment. The potential is there to experience the entirety of what is contained, throughout the entire process. And Sukkot is all sevens. Seven days of the holiday, seven pieces in the Lulav and Etrog set we shake (one Etrog, one Lulav, two Aravot, and three hadasim), seven directions that we shake toward (front, right, back, left, up, down, and center), etc.

What can we experience during Sukkot? Returning from Love, or Teshuva Me'ahava. This is a higher level of Teshuva in which we, out of complete love and devotion, return to Hashem because we value the relationship that we have damaged. And this level of Teshuva has the power to turn our misdeeds into merits. It may sound like magic, but through the lens of relationship it actually makes perfect sense. When a husband and wife quarrel, and then fix things up out of love and devotion to each other and value for the relationship, they create a level of closeness which could only exist because of their previous misdeed. So to, when we come back to Hashem out of love for Him and the desire to be close again, our mistakes become a positive part of that process, retroactively.

So for seven days, we can at any moment step into the closeness that is love from the Almighty and we needn't build up to get there. We must only choose to experience it.

But these seven are also almost at an end. And we move into a new holiday.

Shmini Atzeret, the Eighth day of Gathering.

So what do we have to experience in this day? What does eight represent?

The sages say that Hashem gave us this additional holiday because He said, "It's difficult for me that you are going away." Can you believe? It's hard for Hashem that we are going back to "regular" life! Back to school, back to our jobs, back to the day-in day-out running around.

So He gave us one more day to celebrate together! During Sukkot, the nations of the world are represented in the sacrifices, for they too are His children and He wants a relationship with them as well. But on the eighth day, Hashem says, "It's just for you and me. No Shofar, no fasting, no Lulav and Etrog, no sukkah, no matza. Just you and me."

Before we go back to life we get one more day that is exclusively for connecting and experiencing our relationship with The One.

With one difference that Uncle Yonatan first taught me. Eight is the number that transcends the natural. All of nature exists in patterns of seven. He could have given you a hundred examples. But the number eight transcends what's possible in the natural world. It is a time when we can experience a closeness that can only exist between God and the Jewish People. When we take a Torah in our arms, and use our voice and our body to call out to Hashem and connect. When we can ask Hashem for anything because nothing is too much for him. And we can experience the true culmination of the last two months of preparation.

May we all merit to experience Hashem's closeness to us and find the way to reach out to Him and give Hashem a hug. Through the Torah and through the other Jews around us.

Good Yom Tov, Chag Sameach, Good Shabbos.

The learning should be a merit for Yonatan ben Avraham Avinu a"h

Questions and comments can be sent to freedmanyosef@gmail.com

A Father's Love

Yosef · September 28, 2018

This coming Monday is the holiday of Shemini Atzeret, followed by Simchat Torah on Tuesday. What's interesting to note is that although these holidays follow immediately on the heels of Sukot, they are actually a seperate holiday. The most apparent difference is that on these days we stop shaking our lulavs and sitting in the Sukah. In fact, the biblical source for these days is from the words, והיית אך שמח, "and you shall be only happy". From here we learn that these days have no specific mitzvahs, like the matzah of Pesach and the lulav of Sukot. These days are days of only happiness.

But there is a greater significance to letting go of our precious lulavs and etrogs. The Sefer Hachinuch tells us that the underlying reason for the Four Species which we take on Sukot has to do with our agricultural roots. As farmers, the harvest season in which Sukot falls is naturally a time of joy, when the people finally reaped the fruits of their hard labor. It is at this time of joy that we are commanded to take the Four Species, a sampling of that harvest, and direct our joy heavenwards. In this way, we come to recognize that all of our bounty comes from Hashem and it is to Him that our thanks are due. This means that Sukot stems from an external, physical joy, which we are redirecting to the service of our Creator.

Shemini Atzeret, on the other hand, comes from the Hebrew word עצור, meaning to hold back. The Medrash tells a parable about a king who throws a grand party for his entire kingdom. The party stretches on for a week and suddenly it is time for everyone to return home. The king's own son also turns to leave when his father, overcome by affection, begs him to stay back one more day for just the two of them to enjoy some quality time together. On this day all the pomp and grandeur of the party are dismissed, they would only serve as a distraction from the deep love that the two share.

Hashem is the King, and Israel is his precious son. The seven days of Sukot are the party for the entire kingdom, during which we bring seventy offerings corresponding to the seventy nations of the world. At the end of the festival, everyone turns to leave, when Hashem is overcome by emotion, as it were, and he begs his precious children to hold back (עצור) just one more day for just the two of them together. On this day, all the ceremonies of the lulav and the sukah are suddenly extraneous. There is no need for external sources of celebration, but rather they would serve only as distractions from that overwhelming affection between us and our Father in heaven.

This means that the joy of Shemini Atzeret is a joy that comes from within, fueled by the open affection Hashem shows us by requesting us to stay with Him. As a result of that love, we reach unprecedented levels of joy and closeness to G-d.

This Shemini Atzeret, let us realize just how much our loving Father longs for a relationship with us. Let us realize that Hashem has not only forgiven us for our past wrongdoings on Yom Kippur, but he so desires to rekindle the deep and profound relationship we once had. Let us feel the warmth of His affection and use it to fuel us on to greater levels of joy as well as a recommitment to Hashem and His Torah.

Chag Sameach,

Yosef

A Personal Celebration

October 18, 2019

This week we finish our celebration of Sukkot, by celebrating Simchat Torah, "On the eighth day shall be a holy convocation to you… it is a holding back (atzeret)." A "holding back" is explained in a parable. A king throws a feast and many guests come, as guests begin to get full and head for the door the king holds back his closest friends. On this holiday we dance with the Torah, into the night.

The celebration of Simchat Torah is different than the celebration of Shavuot while both are to appreciate the value of Torah. On Shavuot we celebrate the Torah delivered on Mount Sinai, the tablets crushed by Moshe in response to the golden calf. We stay up all night with our community studying the nitty gritty details of cases, laws, and rituals so we do not make the mistakes of our ancestors. All of this to recognize the intrinsic value of learning Torah.

On Simchat Torah we celebrate a more personal Torah, after going through the high holidays and being forgiven for the golden calf Moshe descends with new tablets. Instead of the thunderous revelations of Sinai these tablets are delivered after Yom Kippur, when we are taken anew. After yom kippur we are again bound to our brothers, and embraced by the sukkah with our four species in hand. Hashem sees us having so much fun eating and walking to shul and shaking our lulavs he invites us to stay one more day. On Simchat Torah we appreciate that God took us from Egypt and gave us the Torah. We dance in circles because we know through the Torah God manipulates our lives on the most personal level.
